=== D1: Agentic Architecture - Foundations ===


--- Agents, Workflows, and Chatbots — What's Actually Different ---

1
Hi and welcome to our first lesson. In this one we're looking at what actually makes a system
2
agentic. We'll compare agents, workflows, and chatbots, figure out why an LLM by itself isn't
3
an agent, and explore how tool use closes that gap. By the end you'll know how to tell these
4
architectures apart and when each one makes sense. Let's jump into it. Start with the vocabulary
5
because these terms get mixed up constantly. An agentic system is one where the language model
6
autonomously pursues a goal. It perceives context, selects actions, invokes tools, and iterates until
7
it's done. A deterministic workflow is different. The developer writes the control flow at design
8
time and the model just fills in content along the way. An autonomous agent goes further still.
9
It operates without step-by-step human direction, making its own planning decisions to hit a high
10
level goal. Autonomy exists on a spectrum from fully supervised to fully autonomous, and most
11
real systems sit somewhere in the middle. A conversational chatbot looks powerful but its
12
architecture is actually quite narrow. It receives your message and generates a reply. That's the
13
complete loop. There's no mechanism for the chatbot to open a file, call an API, or remember anything
14
outside the current context window. This isn't a criticism. Chatbots are the right tool for a huge
15
range of conversational tasks, but they are fundamentally stateless text generators. The
16
moment you want the system to take an action in the world, you've left chatbot territory. You need
17
tool use and you need something deciding when and how to use those tools. The comparison that trips
18
people up most is workflow versus agent. In a deterministic workflow, a developer writes every
19
step, every branch, every loop before the system runs. The LLM might be involved at several points,
20
generating summaries or filling in structured outputs, but it never decides what happens next.
21
That's fixed. An agentic system flips this. The control flow emerges at inference time.
22
The model looks at its current context, decides which tool to call, observes the result, and then
23
decides what to do next. That runtime decision making is the defining characteristic. It's also
24
what makes agents harder to audit and higher risk. Here's a question worth sitting with. If LLMs are
25
so capable, why aren't they agents by default? The answer is that a language model's only output
26
is tokens. It generates text. It has no native ability to read a file, execute code, query a
27
database, or observe anything that happens outside the current inference call. There's no persistent
28
memory, no side effects, no feedback loop. We call this the action environment gap. The model can
29
describe what it would do, but it can't actually do it. Closing that gap requires a runtime layer
30
that takes the model's structured output, executes the requested action, and feeds the result back
31
into context as an observation. So how do we actually close that gap? The answer is a building
32
block. Anthropic calls the augmented LLM. Take a base language model and enhance it with three
33
things. Retrieval, tools, and memory. That combination is the foundation every agentic
34
system is built from. What makes it powerful is that the model uses these capabilities actively.
35
It generates its own search queries when it needs information. It selects the right tool for the job,
36
and it decides what's worth remembering across steps. The model isn't a passive text generator
37
anymore. It's directing its own access to information and actions. Once you see the
38
augmented LLM as the base unit, everything else in agent design is about how you wire these units
39
together and control what they're allowed to do. We can break the agentic loop into four pillars.
40
Perception. The model reads its context, which includes messages, injected data, and results from
41
previous tool calls. Selection. It decides which action or tool to invoke given the current goal
42
state. Execution. The runtime calls that tool and captures whatever it returns. And iteration. The
43
result is fed back into context as an observation, and the model plans its next step. This loop
44
continues until a termination condition is met. Either the goal is achieved, a limit is hit,
45
or an error requires escalation. Understanding this loop is fundamental to designing, debugging,
46
and auditing agentic systems. Claude's tool system is the practical implementation of
47
everything we just described. You define tools using a schema, a name, a description in natural
48
language, and a parameter specification. When Claude is generating a response and determines
49
a tool is needed, it emits a structured tool call output rather than plain text. The runtime
50
intercepts that, executes the actual function or API call, and returns the result to Claude as an
51
observation in the next context window. One thing that surprises practitioners, the description field
52
is the most important part of the schema. Claude selects tools based on how well the description
53
matches the task. A vague description means unreliable tool selection. Three more patterns
54
are worth pinning down. Tool use is the general capability. Anytime a model emits a structured call
55
that the runtime executes. Prompt chaining is a specific workflow technique where the output of
56
one LLM call becomes the input to the next. It looks agentic because it involves multiple model
57
calls, but the sequence is fixed by the developer. The model doesn't decide to chain. The developer
58
does. The action environment gap is the conceptual label for the boundary between what the model
59
generates and what actually happens in the world. Tool use is the bridge across it. These
60
distinctions matter because they clarify the actual risk surface of a given system design.
61
So when should you actually build an agentic system? The honest answer is less often than
62
you'd think. If the steps to complete a task can be written out in advance, a deterministic
63
workflow is usually the better choice. It's predictable, auditable, and easier to test.
64
Agents shine when the path to the goal can't be fully prescripted, when the system needs to adapt
65
based on intermediate results. But higher autonomy means higher risk. Agenic systems can take actions
66
with real-world consequences. Mistakes can propagate across multiple steps before anyone notices.
67
The CCAF framework addresses this with minimal footprint principles
68
and human-in-the-loop checkpoints, which we'll cover in detail later in the course.
69
So let's pull this together. What makes a system agentic is that the model controls the flow at
70
inference time. Chatbots respond, they don't act. Workflows are deterministic, fixed at design time.
71
Agents are dynamic, with the model selecting tools and planning on the fly.
72
Tool use closes the action-environment gap, and that covers what makes a system agentic.
73
In the next lesson, we'll look at tool use as a core mechanism that enables agency. I'll see you there.

--- Tool Use as the Foundation of Agency ---

1
The capability that takes an LLM from generating text to actually doing things in the world is tool use.
2
We'll walk through the full lifecycle of a tool call,
3
look at how description quality affects selection reliability,
4
and draw the line between a developer-scripted tool sequence and a model-driven agentic one.
5
Let's get into it.
6
Before we trace the lifecycle, let's lock in the vocabulary.
7
A tool definition is a schema you pass to the model at inference time.
8
It has a name, a natural language description, and a parameter specification.
9
The model reads this to figure out what the tool does and whether it applies to the current task.
10
A tool call is the structured output the model emits when it decides to use a tool.
11
It's not plain text. It's a formatted object containing the tool name and a JSON argument object.
12
An observation is what comes back after the runtime executes the call.
13
It gets injected into the next context window, and the model uses it to plan its next step.
14
These three concepts form the spine of every agentic interaction.
15
Let's make this concrete at the API level, because the roundtrip is specific.
16
When Claude decides it needs a tool, the response comes back with a stop reason of tool use,
17
and the content includes one or more tool use blocks.
18
Each block names the tool and carries the arguments in JSON.
19
That's Claude's half of the exchange.
20
Your half is to run the tool and send back a tool result block that references the same call.
21
One distinction lives here. For client tools, the ones you define, your application executes the call.
22
For server tools, like web search, Anthropic runs the tool for you and returns the result directly.
23
Either way, that tool result becomes the observation Claude reads on its next turn.
24
This exchange is the atomic unit of the whole loop, and it repeats many times in a single task.
25
Here's the distinction that separates a workflow from an agent, stated as precisely as possible.
26
In a pre-scripted workflow, the developer decides, at design time, which tools run and in what order.
27
The model might fill in the parameters, but it never chooses the tool.
28
The sequence is fixed.
29
In a model-driven agentic system, the model selects tools at inference time,
30
based on what it's trying to accomplish right now.
31
The sequence isn't known in advance.
32
It varies across runs, based on context and intermediate results.
33
That runtime selection is the defining characteristic of agentic behavior.
34
A system where the developer controls the tool order is a workflow, full stop.
35
Tool description quality is one of the most underappreciated factors in agentic reliability.
36
When Claude needs to decide whether to use a tool, it reads the description, not the implementation.
37
If the description is vague, like processes data, Claude has no reliable basis for selection.
38
If the description is precise, like queries the inventory database by product skew and returns current stock count,
39
Claude can accurately judge when the tool applies.
40
The practical rule is to write descriptions a non-engineer could act on.
41
Describe what the tool does, what inputs it expects, and what it returns.
42
Don't describe how it works internally.
43
Poor descriptions are one of the most common root causes of agents calling the wrong tool
44
or skipping a tool they should have used.
45
It helps to think about tools in functional categories.
46
Data access tools let the model read from the world,
47
fetching a web page, querying a database, reading a file, or pulling from a knowledge base.
48
Computation tools let the model do things that pure text generation can't.
49
Running code in a sandbox, parsing structured data, or executing a calculation.
50
External API tools let the model write to the world, sending a message, creating a calendar event,
51
updating a record, or triggering a downstream workflow.
52
These categories have different latency profiles, different failure modes, and very different security surfaces.
53
Knowing which category a tool falls into is the first step in assessing its risk.
54
The observation phase is where the agentic loop earns its name.
55
A plain pipeline executes steps in sequence and stops.
56
An agent looks at what came back and decides what to do next based on that.
57
A successful tool result confirms progress. The model moves forward.
58
An error triggers replanning.
59
The model might retry with different parameters, try an alternative tool, or decide it needs to escalate.
60
A partial result might prompt a follow-up tool call to gather more information before proceeding.
61
This dynamic response to real-world feedback is what makes agentic systems more capable than fixed
62
pipelines for open-ended tasks.
63
It's also what makes them harder to test, because the behavior depends on runtime observations that
64
aren't always predictable.
65
Three runtime concepts worth knowing by name.
66
Tool result injection is the process of taking a tool's output and appending it to the conversation as a message,
67
so the model can read it in the next context window.
68
Parallel tool calls happen when the model emits multiple tool call outputs in a single response.
69
The runtime can execute independent tools concurrently and return all observations together, which reduces latency.
70
The tool call loop is the full iterative cycle.
71
Call, execute, observe, respond, repeat.
72
Most agentic frameworks implement this loop automatically,
73
but understanding it explicitly helps you set appropriate stop conditions and timeouts.
74
Without a well-defined termination condition, an agent can loop indefinitely.
75
Let's make this concrete with examples across tool types. Web search.
76
The model calls a search tool, the runtime queries a search API,
77
and the model receives a list of ranked results as an observation.
78
File read and write.
79
The model reads a document, generates edits, and writes the updated content back to disk via
80
separate tool calls. Code execution.
81
The model writes a Python snippet, a sandboxed interpreter runs it, and standard output is returned
82
as an observation.
83
Each of these has a distinct failure surface.
84
Search tools can return stale or irrelevant results.
85
File writes can overwrite data if the model misidentifies the target.
86
Code execution can have unintended side effects if the sandbox is too permissive.
87
Designing safe tool configurations is a core part of agentic system architecture.
88
Pulling this together, tool use turns LLM's text output into real-world action.
89
The lifecycle goes from structured output through runtime execution to observation returned to context.
90
Description quality drives selection, so write for the model.
91
Prescripted sequences are workflows.
92
Model-selected sequences are agentic.
93
Observations enable replanning.
94
And that wraps up tool use foundation.
95
I'll see you in the next lesson.


=== D1: Agentic Architecture - Task Decomposition & Planning ===


--- Perception, Reasoning, Action, and Observation ---

1
Let's talk about the agentic loop, the core cycle that drives every agent.
2
We'll walk through all four phases, perception, reasoning, action, and observation,
3
see how they connect to one another, and look at a real example to make it concrete. By the end,
4
you'll have a clear mental model of how agents actually work. Let's get into it.
5
Before we dig into each phase, let's lock in three terms you'll use constantly.
6
The agentic loop is the repeating, perceive-plan-act-observe cycle. It's not a one-shot
7
call and response. The model reads its context, picks an action, runs it, gets a result, and then
8
decides whether to keep going or stop. Tool use is what makes the action phase real. The model emits
9
a structured call, the runtime executes it, and the result comes back as an observation.
10
And dynamic planning is why the loop is powerful. The model isn't following a fixed script.
11
It's updating its plan at every turn based on what it just learned. These three ideas together
12
define what makes a system truly agentic. Perception is the first phase, and it's
13
deceptively simple. The model reads everything currently in the context window. That includes
14
the original instructions, the conversation history, any documents loaded at startup,
15
and, critically, every tool result that's accumulated so far. The model uses all of
16
that to figure out where it is in the task, what's been done, what information is available,
17
what's still unknown. Think of perception as the agent taking stock before it decides its next
18
move. If the context window is the agent's working memory, then perception is the act of reading from
19
that memory. It's not passive. The model is actively identifying relevant signals against
20
the background of everything else in context. Reasoning follows perception. Once the model
21
knows what's available, it decides what to do next. This is where dynamic planning shows up.
22
The agent isn't running a fixed workflow where step three always follows step two. It's weighing
23
what it just perceived against the original goal and asking, what action would move me closer to
24
a solution? Sometimes the answer is obvious. Sometimes the agent has to work through ambiguity.
25
The model might recognize that a prior search returned incomplete data and decide to refine
26
the query before proceeding. The key point is that reasoning is adaptive. If the situation has
27
changed since the last iteration, the plan can change too. That flexibility is exactly what
28
makes agentic systems more capable than simple pipelines. Action and observation are two sides
29
of the same coin, so let's look at them together. In the action phase, the model emits a structured
30
tool call. It might call a search, API, run a code interpreter, query a database, or write a final
31
response. The tool call is the moment the agent reaches out to the external world. Observation is
32
what comes back. The runtime executes the tool, gets a result, and injects that result into the
33
context window. Now the agent has new information it didn't have before. That observation is what
34
the next perception phase will read. These two phases are tightly linked. Every action should
35
produce a meaningful observation, and every observation should inform the next decision.
36
The context window is the agent's working memory, and it's worth understanding how it fills up over
37
a session. At the start, it holds inputs, the system prompt, the user's request, any initial documents.
38
As the loop runs, actions accumulate. Tool calls pile up in context. Observations accumulate, too.
39
Every result the runtime returns gets appended. In intermediate reasoning, the model's planning
40
thoughts adds to the total. This is why context capacity matters for agents. A long-running agent
41
can fill its context window if it makes many tool calls without summarizing or pruning.
42
Understanding this structure helps you design agents that manage their working memory effectively
43
rather than running out of room mid-task. The cyclic nature of the loop is what makes agents
44
capable of complex, multi-step tasks. Let's trace the data flow. Perception surfaces what's in
45
context for the reasoning phase. Reasoning produces an action selection based on that view.
46
The action generates an observation that gets returned to context, and then perception reads
47
that updated context at the start of the next iteration. Each phase's output is the next phase's
48
input. This isn't a linear pipeline, it's a feedback loop. That feedback is what allows an
49
agent to course correct, to retry when a tool fails, to revise its approach when the data it
50
expected turns out to be different from what it actually received. Let's connect these loop
51
mechanics to a few more precise terms. An agentic system is the full architecture, the model, the
52
tools, the runtime, and the loop that ties them together. In-context memory is the state the agent
53
carries during a session. It's immediately accessible, but it's also ephemeral. When the
54
session ends, or if the context gets truncated, that memory is gone. That's a design constraint
55
you need to account for. A tool result is the structured data returned after a tool call.
56
It's what turns an action into an observation. Knowing these terms precisely matters because
57
when you're reading documentation, debugging an agent, or discussing architecture, everyone needs
58
to be working from the same definitions. Let's make the loop concrete with a research agent
59
example. The user asks the agent to summarize recent findings on a topic. Perception. The agent
60
reads the query and notices it has no prior search results in context yet. Reasoning. It decides to
61
run a search. Action. It calls the search API tool with a targeted query. Observation. The runtime
62
returns a list of article summaries and injects them into context. Now the loop restarts. Perception.
63
The agent reads those results and notices one source looks highly relevant. Reasoning. It decides
64
to fetch the full text of that article, and on it goes. Each iteration makes progress toward the goal
65
and the agent's plan evolves based on what it finds. The agent loop is the foundation of
66
everything we'll build in this course. Perception reads context. Reasoning selects the next action.
67
Action invokes the tools, and observation returns results. The context window holds all of it as
68
ephemeral working memory, and then the cycle repeats, adapting at every turn until the task is done.
69
That covers the four-phase agentic loop. In the next lesson, we'll look at loop control
70
covering stopping conditions, iteration, and escalation. I'll see you there.


=== D1: Agentic Architecture - Multi-Agent Orchestration ===


--- Loop Control — Stopping, Iterating, and Escalating ---

1
Welcome back. This lesson is about loop control. How you tell an agent when to stop, when to try
2
again, and when to hand the problem to someone else.
3
We'll cover termination conditions, turn budgets, infinite loop risk, and the distinction between
4
iteration and escalation.
5
These are reliability concerns just as much as performance concerns, and every production agent
6
needs them. Let's dive in.
7
Before we talk about stopping, let's name the loop we're controlling. Anthropic describes the agent
8
loop as three phases that blend together and repeat.
9
First, the agent gathers context. It pulls in whatever information it needs to figure out the next
10
move, whether that's reading files, searching, or reviewing prior results.
11
Second, it takes action. It uses a tool to actually do something in the world, querying a system, or changing state.
12
Third, it verifies the work. It checks the result against the goal to see whether things went as expected.
13
Then the whole cycle repeats. Gather context, take action, verify work again and again until the job is done.
14
Everything we cover in this lesson is really one question layered on top of that loop. When should
15
this gather, act, verify cycle actually stop?
16
Loop control is the discipline of answering that question well.
17
Before we go further, let's pin down the language. A termination condition is the explicit criterion
18
that causes the loop to stop.
19
Without one, the loop has no reason to end. A turn budget is a hard limit set in the orchestration
20
layer on how many iterations the agent is allowed to run.
21
It's a programmatic guard, not a hint. An escalation is the controlled handoff from the agent to a
22
human operator or fallback system when the agent can't make further progress on its own.
23
These three concepts work together. The termination condition tells the agent when it's done. The
24
turn budget tells the runtime when to force a stop.
25
And escalation is what happens when neither of those is enough to resolve the situation cleanly.
26
There are four main reasons an agentic loop should stop. First, task complete. The agent has
27
produced a final output and there's nothing left to do. This is the happy path.
28
Second, error threshold reached. If the agent has hit too many consecutive failures, continuing
29
rarely helps and usually makes things worse.
30
Third, budget exhausted. The turn limit or token budget set by the orchestrator has been reached.
31
The loop halts programmatically regardless of task state.
32
Fourth, stop signal. The orchestrator or user explicitly tells the agent to stop. All four of these
33
have to be handled. An agent that only checks for task completion will keep running through the
34
other three scenarios without stopping.
35
Infinite loops are one of the most common failure modes in agentic systems. They happen in a few
36
ways. The simplest is a missing stop condition. The agent never decides the task is complete, so it keeps going.
37
Another is always failing tools, a broken API call that returns an error every time, retried without
38
limit. The most subtle is circular reasoning, where the model revisits the same decision point
39
iteration after iteration without new information to break the cycle.
40
None of these are exotic edge cases. They're predictable failure modes that show up during normal
41
development. The fix in every case is the same. Explicit termination conditions and hard caps on
42
iterations, baked in before the agent runs, not added after something goes wrong.
43
Turn budgets are the primary programmatic defense against runaway loops, and there's a key design
44
point here. They belong in the runtime layer, not in the model. You shouldn't rely on the model to
45
count its own iterations and decide to stop.
46
The orchestrator tracks iteration count. It enforces the limit. When the budget is exhausted, the
47
runtime halts execution and surfaces whatever progress was made up to that point. Setting a
48
reasonable max iterations limit before the agent starts is standard practice. What's reasonable depends on the task.
49
A simple lookup might need 3-5 iterations. A research task might need 20. The point is that the
50
number is chosen deliberately, not left open-ended. Bounded loops are auditable. Unbounded loops are liabilities.
51
Progress versus spinning is a distinction worth spending time on, because spinning looks like
52
activity without actually being it. An agent making progress produces a new observation. Each
53
iteration that advances toward the goal. Each tool call returns different data. The scope of the
54
problem narrows. Something gets done.
55
A spinning agent, on the other hand, repeats similar actions without new results. Tool calls come
56
back with the same errors. The plan doesn't change. The context window grows, but the gap between
57
the current state and the goal state doesn't shrink. The agent is generating tokens and consuming
58
API calls without actually moving forward. Recognizing this pattern is the first step to stopping it.
59
Detecting spinning in practice requires deliberate instrumentation. The simplest check is to compare
60
successive observations. If two consecutive tool calls return nearly identical results, that's a
61
signal. Tracking repeated tool calls with identical parameters is another approach. If the agent is
62
calling the same search query three times in a row, something is wrong.
63
You can also monitor the gap between goal state and current state across turns. If that gap isn't
64
closing after several iterations, the agent is likely stuck. None of this detection has to be
65
complex. Even a simple counter that flags when the last N observations were identical is enough to
66
catch most spinning cases before they run up your API bill.
67
When the agent hits a problem, it has two options. Iterate with new information, or escalate to a
68
human or fallback system. Iteration makes sense when there's new data to work with, a different
69
approach to try, or when the error is recoverable. Maybe the first search query was too broad. A
70
more targeted query might get better results.
71
Escalation makes sense when the agent has reached its authorization boundary, when it lacks the
72
information it needs to proceed, or when a human judgment call is required. The important thing is
73
that the boundary between iterate and escalate is explicit in the agent's design. Escalation isn't
74
failure. It's a designed safety valve. An agent that escalates gracefully is more trustworthy than
75
one that keeps cycling and hoping things improve.
76
Loop control is a reliability concern, and it helps to understand exactly why. Runaway agents can
77
exhaust API quotas and generate unexpected costs. Agents that don't escalate when they should can
78
make unrecoverable decisions, taking actions the user would have stopped if they'd known. And agents
79
without defined exit conditions are hard to reason about, audit, or debug. Proper termination
80
conditions make agent behavior predictable.
81
When something goes wrong, you can look at the logs and understand exactly why the agent stopped,
82
what it was trying to do, and where it got stuck. That auditability is what makes it possible to
83
improve the system over time. Reliability isn't just about what happens when things go right.
84
So every agent needs a defined exit condition before it runs. Cover all four stop scenarios. Task
85
complete, error threshold, budget exhausted, and stop signal. Use turn budgets to enforce limits
86
programmatically. Watch for spinning. Build a clear escalation path for what the agent can't resolve.
87
In the next lesson, we'll look at breaking complex goals into actionable subtasks. I'll see you there.

=== D2: Tool Design & MCP - Tool Design Fundamentals ===


--- When Workflows Outperform Agents ---

1
Welcome. In this lesson, we're looking at when deterministic workflows outperform agentic systems.
2
Agents get a lot of attention, but for a wide class of problems, a well-designed workflow is cheaper,
3
faster, and easier to maintain. Let's build the vocabulary and intuition you need to make that
4
call confidently. Let's define the key players. A deterministic workflow is a fixed, developer-authored
5
sequence of steps. Control flow, conditional branches, and loops are all specified at design
6
time. Nothing surprises you at run time because you wrote every decision in advance. An agentic
7
system is the opposite. The LLM decides which actions to take, in what order, to pursue a goal.
8
You don't fully control that sequence. And prompt chaining is the simplest workflow pattern,
9
passing one call's output directly as input to the next. It's linear, testable, and easy to debug.
10
These three definitions are foundational for everything that follows in this lesson
11
and throughout the course. The core trade-off is straightforward. Every architecture decision
12
comes down to one question. Does this task require runtime judgment? Workflows trade
13
adaptability for predictability. You know exactly what will happen because you authored every step.
14
Agents flip that, giving you open-ended flexibility at the cost of predictability
15
and debuggability. Here's what practitioners often miss. Most real tasks fall much closer to the
16
workflow end of that spectrum. The appeal of agents is real, but choosing them when a workflow would
17
work adds token cost and operational risk without adding meaningful capability. That's a trade-off
18
worth being deliberate about every single time you design a system. Let's put workflows and agents
19
side by side. Workflows have fixed control flow, predictable output, auditable steps, lower latency,
20
and lower token cost. Every step is individually testable, and failures localize cleanly to a
21
specific location. Agents give you dynamic control flow and adaptive behavior. The capability ceiling
22
is higher, but so is the cost, the latency, and the failure surface. Debugging an agentic reasoning
23
trace is significantly harder than reading a structured workflow execution log. For any given
24
task, ask yourself which set of properties you actually need, not which architecture sounds more
25
capable or technically impressive to your team. Before reaching for an agent, run through three
26
quick checks. First, is the input format predictable and well-defined at the time you build the system?
27
Second, is the output format known and testable against a clear specification? Third, can every
28
processing step be specified at design time without requiring runtime judgment from the model?
29
When all three are true, a workflow is the right answer. You're not leaving capability on the table
30
by choosing a workflow. You're making a deliberate engineering choice that gives you lower cost,
31
faster execution, and a system your team can actually reason about and fix confidently when
32
something inevitably goes wrong in production. Anthropx documentation defines five core workflow
33
patterns. Prompt chaining, sequential LLM calls where each output feeds the next step. Routing,
34
a classifier step directs input to the right specialized prompt or subagent. Parallelization,
35
multiple LLM calls run simultaneously with results collected at a fan-in step. Orchestrator
36
subagent, a controlling component decomposes a goal and delegates to specialized workers.
37
And evaluator optimizer, one call generates output, another evaluates it, looping until
38
quality criteria are met. Each pattern solves a distinct structural problem and each one remains
39
a workflow. None of them require the full autonomy and unpredictability that define a true agentic
40
system. Routing and parallelization are worth a closer look because they handle real complexity
41
without requiring agentic autonomy. A routing workflow uses a classifier, sometimes an LLM,
42
sometimes a simple rule to sort inputs before processing them. The topology is fixed even when
43
the router itself calls a language model. Parallelization fans work out across multiple
44
concurrent calls and collects results at a synchronization point before proceeding.
45
Both patterns scale to complex workloads and production traffic volumes. And because the
46
control flow is still authored at design time, both remain fully auditable. You get sophisticated
47
complexity handling without giving up the predictability that makes systems reliable
48
and maintainable. Three practical levers where workflows consistently win. Token cost, latency,
49
and reliability. On cost, workflows make fewer unplanned LLM calls. Each step is scoped so your
50
token usage is predictable and budgetable before you deploy. On latency, you control the sequencing
51
directly. Steps can be pipelined or deliberately parallelized where it makes sense. Agents introduce
52
variable latency from replanning loops you didn't anticipate and can't fully predict in advance.
53
On reliability, deterministic steps are individually testable in isolation.
54
When a workflow fails, the failure is localized to a specific identifiable step. In an agentic trace,
55
the root cause can be buried anywhere in a long and complex reasoning chain.
56
Let's be precise about when not to over-index on agents. Agent overhead, which includes the extra
57
token spend, the dynamic planning loops, and the harder-to-audit execution traces, is only justified
58
when the task genuinely can't be prescripted. Agents increase token spend on every single
59
task run, not just occasionally. Every autonomous decision the LLM makes is a potential failure
60
point you didn't explicitly design for or anticipate, and debugging agentic traces takes
61
significantly more engineering time than reading a structured workflow execution log. The principle?
62
Reach for agents only when workflows demonstrably fall short. That's not a limitation. That's
63
disciplined professional engineering practice. To close out this lesson, workflows outperform
64
agents whenever the task is predictable, the output is known, and the steps can be authored
65
at design time. The five patterns give you a full toolkit for handling routing, parallelization,
66
and sequential complexity without agent overhead. That covers when deterministic workflows beat
67
agentic systems. In the next lesson, we'll build a decision framework for choosing the
68
right architecture. I'll see you there.


=== D2: Tool Design & MCP - Building MCP Servers ===


--- Choosing the Right Architecture ---

1
Welcome back.
2
In this lesson, we're building a practical decision framework
3
for choosing between workflows and agentic systems.
4
The goal isn't to declare one architecture a winner.
5
It's to give you a repeatable process
6
for making the right call on any task you encounter
7
in professional practice.
8
Let's get into it.
9
A handful of terms anchor this framework.
10
Incremental complexity means starting
11
with the simplest architecture that works
12
and adding agent behavior only where workflows demonstrably
13
fail to meet the task requirements.
14
An orchestrator is the controlling component
15
that decomposes a goal, delegates sub-tasks
16
to sub-agents or tools, and synthesizes the final result.
17
And over-engineering is the trap of adding
18
agentic autonomy that costs more tokens,
19
introduces more operational risk,
20
and creates harder-to-audit execution paths,
21
all without delivering proportional capability benefit.
22
Knowing when you're over-engineering a solution
23
is just as important as knowing how to build
24
sophisticated agentic systems.
25
Both skills matter equally in professional practice.
26
The first rule of architectural choice
27
is to start with the least complex solution that
28
solves the problem.
29
A simple prompt chain beats a complex agent
30
for any scoped, well-defined task.
31
Simplicity reduces cost, reduces latency,
32
and reduces the number of ways things
33
can go wrong in production.
34
Here's the practical reality.
35
You can always add agent behavior later
36
if the workflow turns out to be insufficient for edge cases
37
or expanding requirements.
38
But starting with a complex agentic system
39
and then trying to simplify it is significantly harder.
40
Complexity is much easier to add than to remove
41
from a running production system.
42
How do you know when agentic complexity is actually warranted?
43
Watch for three clear signals.
44
First, the goal is open-ended and can't be fully
45
pre-specified at design time.
46
You can describe what success looks like,
47
but you can't enumerate every step required to get there.
48
Second, the tool sequence is unpredictable.
49
You don't know at design time which
50
tools the system will need to call or in what order.
51
Third, the task requires dynamic replanning mid-execution
52
because earlier steps produced unexpected results that
53
change the path forward.
54
If none of these three conditions apply to your task,
55
a workflow is almost certainly the better choice.
56
Let's put reliability and adaptability side by side
57
because this is the central trade-off in the framework.
58
Workflows give you deterministic steps, testable outputs,
59
localized failure, predictable token cost,
60
and systems that are straightforward to monitor
61
and maintain over time.
62
Agents give you dynamic behavior that
63
handles open-ended goals and a higher capability ceiling
64
for genuinely complex tasks.
65
But agents also mean variable cost,
66
harder to audit execution traces,
67
and a broader failure surface that's harder
68
to anticipate at design time.
69
The question is never which is better in the abstract.
70
It's always which set of properties
71
matches what your specific task actually requires.
72
There are three specific trade-off axes
73
worth evaluating for any architectural decision you
74
make, cost versus capability.
75
Agents spend more tokens per task run.
76
So you need to justify that spend with capability
77
you genuinely require right now,
78
not capability you might eventually want someday.
79
Auditability versus flexibility.
80
Workflows are fully auditable step-by-step,
81
while agents trade some auditability
82
for runtime flexibility that's sometimes necessary.
83
Reliability versus adaptability.
84
Workflows fail predictably at specific identifiable steps,
85
while agents can adapt dynamically
86
but introduce variable failure modes
87
that are harder to anticipate and reproduce in testing.
88
Work through all three axes explicitly
89
before committing to any architecture for a production system.
90
The right way to introduce agent behavior
91
is incrementally, not all at once.
92
Build and validate the workflow version of your system first.
93
Then run it against real inputs and identify
94
the specific step where the workflow breaks down or fails
95
to handle the required variation.
96
Maybe the routing logic fails on unusual edge cases.
97
Maybe a synthesis step needs more flexibility
98
than any fixed prompt can provide.
99
Replace only that specific step with agent behavior,
100
leaving the rest of the workflow intact and auditable.
101
This approach keeps the system as simple as the task allows,
102
and it creates a clear, reviewable record
103
of why each architectural decision was made.
104
Let's walk through three concrete scenarios
105
to make the framework tangible.
106
In a workflow scenario, you're extracting structured data
107
from a fixed format document.
108
Input is predictable, and output schema is known.
109
A prompt chain with validation handles
110
this cleanly and efficiently.
111
In an agent scenario, you're researching and synthesizing
112
a topic from open-ended web sources.
113
The goal is unbounded, and tool sequences
114
are unpredictable at design time.
115
An agent with planning is the right architectural choice here.
116
In a hybrid scenario, you're routing customer inquiries
117
to specialists, and then synthesizing their responses.
118
Fixed routing feeds into a dynamic synthesis step,
119
combining a routing workflow with a focused agent.
120
When you're evaluating a new task,
121
run through four diagnostic questions.
122
Can every step be authored at design time
123
without runtime judgment?
124
Is the output format known and testable
125
against a clear specification?
126
Does the task require the system to select tools dynamically
127
based on results it hasn't seen yet?
128
And does a failure at one step require dynamic recovery
129
rather than a simple fixed fallback?
130
If you answer no to two or more of those questions,
131
agentic complexity is likely warranted for that task.
132
If you answer yes to all four, you have a workflow.
133
The framework isn't about finding
134
the most powerful architecture available.
135
It's about finding the right one for the specific problem
136
you're actually solving.
137
The decision framework is straightforward.
138
Start with the simplest architecture
139
that solves the problem and add complexity
140
only where workflows fail.
141
Evaluate cost, auditability, reliability, and adaptability
142
against what your task requires.
143
The mark of a well-designed system
144
is using exactly as much complexity as the task demands and not extra.
145
In the next lesson, we'll start on task decomposition.
146
I'll see you there.


=== D2: Tool Design & MCP - MCP Production & Built-in Tools ===


--- Breaking Complex Goals Into Actionable Subtasks ---

1
Welcome. In this lesson, we'll talk about task decomposition, the skill of breaking complex
2
goals into structured subtasks. We'll cover why decomposition matters, how to analyze goals for
3
natural boundaries, what makes a subtask well-defined, and the real cost of over-decomposition.
4
This is one of the core architectural skills for anyone building agentic systems,
5
so let's get into it. Start with the vocabulary. Task decomposition is the process of breaking a
6
high-level goal into smaller subtasks that agents or tools can actually execute. A handoff point is
7
the boundary between two subtasks, where one step's output becomes the next step's input,
8
and it requires an explicit schema, not just a verbal description. And the orchestrator is the
9
controlling agent that holds the master plan, assigns subtasks, manages execution order,
10
and synthesizes everything into a final result. These three concepts work together in almost
11
every agentic workflow you'll encounter on the exam and in production, so get comfortable with
12
all of them. Why does decomposition matter so much? Because agents can't infer structure from
13
an underspecified goal. If you hand an agent a complex, monolithic task with no breakdown,
14
it has to guess at boundaries, guess at priorities, and guess at what done even looks like. When that
15
fails, you have to retry the whole thing from scratch. When you decompose the goal into subtasks,
16
each piece becomes independently retryable, delegatable, and testable. You can also target
17
error recovery precisely. If step 4 fails, you retry step 4, not steps 1 through 3 again.
18
That's the real reliability gain. Not smarter agents, but better structured work flowing
19
through the system. There's a spectrum here, and both ends cause problems. On one end, monolithic
20
tasks are simple to assign, but fragile. One failure takes down the whole workflow, and there's
21
no way to retry just the part that broke. On the other end, over-decomposed tasks have too many
22
steps, each one tiny, with coordination logic tying them together. You end up spending more
23
resources managing the workflow than doing the actual work. The latency of passing results through
24
10 handoffs can exceed the cost of just running a single well-scoped agent. The right approach
25
is somewhere in the middle. Subtasks that are meaningful, executable units of work, not single
26
function microsteps that should have been combined. Let's compare these two approaches
27
directly. A monolithic task is simple to hand off. You write one prompt, get one result. But
28
it's hard to debug because the whole thing is one opaque unit. It's impossible to parallelize,
29
and it fails completely if any part goes wrong, meaning you retry the entire task even if only
30
one step broke. A decomposed task has more upfront design work. You need to define subtasks, their
31
inputs, their outputs, and the handoff schemas between them. That's real investment. But each
32
piece is independently retryable, and you can route different subtasks to agents with different
33
specializations, such as a retrieval agent, an analysis agent, and a generation agent.
34
The overhead is real, but so is the resilience you get in return.
35
Before you decompose anything, you need to analyze the goal structure.
36
There are three questions worth asking. First, can any part of this goal run independently
37
without needing output from another part? If yes, you have a candidate for parallel decomposition.
38
Second, does one step's output feed directly into the next step's input?
39
That's a sequential dependency chain, and it has to stay sequential.
40
Third, does the goal require distinctly different capabilities, like retrieval, then analysis,
41
then generation? Each capability boundary is a natural decomposition point. These three
42
questions give you a structural map of the goal before you write a single line of orchestration
43
code. It's worth spending time on this upfront analysis because the wrong decomposition design
44
is expensive to fix after you've built the workflow. So what makes a subtask well-defined?
45
Four things. First, single responsibility. One thing it does, one output it produces.
46
Subtasks that try to do two things at once are harder to test and harder to retry cleanly.
47
Second, defined inputs. The subtask knows exactly what context it receives
48
and doesn't rely on ambient state from a prior conversation it wasn't part of.
49
Third, bounded scope. Small enough that retrying it on failure
50
doesn't force you to rerun the entire workflow from the beginning.
51
And fourth, clean structured output that the orchestrator can route directly without parsing
52
freeform text. If a subtask fails any of these four criteria, it'll cause reliability problems
53
downstream that are hard to trace back to the source. Handoff points are where most
54
agentic workflows break down in practice. A handoff is the boundary between two subtasks,
55
where one agent's output becomes another agent's input. The first key principle is to pass only
56
what the next step actually needs. Don't dump your entire context window into the handoff payload
57
because that creates noise and can cause the receiving agent to focus on irrelevant information.
58
Use structured schemas, not freeform text, so the receiving agent doesn't have to interpret
59
ambiguous output. And document what isn't passed. Subagents don't automatically have access to
60
everything the orchestrator knows. If you don't explicitly document those gaps, subagents make
61
false assumptions about what they have, and that causes silent errors that are very hard to debug
62
after the fact. Every boundary you add to a workflow has a cost. More subtasks means more
63
orchestration logic, more latency between steps, and more potential failure points. Each handoff
64
is a place where context can be lost, a schema mismatch can cause an error, or a partial failure
65
can leave your workflow in an inconsistent state. Deeply decomposed systems are also harder to trace
66
when something goes wrong. You're debugging a pipeline, not a single agent response. The trade-off
67
is reliability versus complexity. Decompose enough to get the reliability benefits, but stop before
68
you're spending more energy managing the workflow than executing it. Task decomposition is the
69
foundation of reliable agentic system design. Good subtasks have single responsibilities and
70
clean outputs. Handoffs need explicit schemas to prevent context loss, and every boundary you add
71
costs coordination overhead. So decompose thoughtfully. And that covers goal decomposition
72
as a core architectural skill. In the next lesson, we'll look at the three main decomposition patterns
73
hierarchical, sequential, and parallel. I'll see you there.


=== D3: Claude Code - Architecture & Setup ===


--- Decomposition Patterns — Hierarchical, Sequential, and Parallel ---

1
Welcome. This lesson covers the three decomposition patterns you'll see in almost every agentic
2
system. Hierarchical, sequential, and parallel. We'll look at how each one works, when to apply it,
3
how real systems combine all three, and which anti-patterns to watch out for. Let's get into it.
4
Let's get the language right first. Hierarchical decomposition is the strategy of recursively
5
splitting goals into sub-goals across multiple levels, creating a tree structure where each
6
level handles only its tier of complexity. Sequential execution is when tasks run in a
7
defined order, each one potentially depending on the output of the step before it, and that
8
dependency is what forces the sequence. And parallel execution is when independent tasks
9
run at the same time, cutting total wall clock time but introducing the need for synchronization
10
when the branches converge back into the main workflow. Keep these three patterns distinct in
11
your mind as we go through each one because real systems mix all three and you need to recognize
12
which pattern applies to which part of the workflow. Hierarchical decomposition builds a
13
goal tree. At the top you have the high level goal, below it sub-goals, below those tasks,
14
below those individual steps. The orchestrator at each level holds only its tier of complexity
15
and doesn't need to know the details of how lower levels execute. It delegates down and waits for
16
results to flow back up. This mirrors how real organizations delegate work. An executive sets
17
a strategic goal, a manager breaks it into projects, a team breaks those projects into tasks,
18
and individuals handle specific steps. The advantage of this structure is specialization
19
at each level. The risk is that coordination overhead grows with every layer you add,
20
and errors at the bottom have to travel all the way back up before they're handled.
21
One of the most important design decisions with hierarchical decomposition
22
is how deep to go. Going deeper adds specialization. You can route sub-goals to
23
agents that are purpose-built for that specific tier of complexity, and they don't have to deal
24
with the broader context above them. But each added level increases what's called the error
25
propagation surface. An error at the bottom of a five-level hierarchy has to travel back up
26
through four orchestration layers before it's caught. Each of those layers is a chance for the
27
error to be misinterpreted or swallowed. Flatten wherever you can. If two adjacent levels would
28
run the same type of agent with nearly the same prompt, there's no reason for the separation.
29
Depth should earn its cost through genuine specialization gains,
30
not just because it looks clean on a diagram. Sequential decomposition is the straightforward
31
pattern. Step 1 runs, produces output. Step 2 takes that output as input, produces its own output,
32
and so on down the chain. The key characteristic is that step order is enforced by data dependency.
33
You can't run step 3 before step 2 because step 3 needs what step 2 produces. Sequential patterns
34
are the simplest to reason about and debug. When something goes wrong, you know exactly
35
which step failed and what state the workflow was in. The trade-off is that sequential chains
36
can't exploit any parallelism, and total time equals the sum of all step durations.
37
When you're choosing between sequential and parallel, the deciding factor is always data
38
dependency. If step B needs step A's output to function, it's sequential by necessity. If steps
39
B and C both need step A's output but don't need anything from each other, they can run in parallel
40
after A completes. Using sequential execution when steps are actually independent is a missed
41
optimization. You're adding latency for no reliability benefit. But the reverse mistake
42
is worse. Treating dependent steps as if they're parallel causes data hazards. Step B starts before
43
it has the input it needs, uses stale or missing data, and produces wrong output. Dependency analysis
44
has to come first. Before you decide on execution pattern, map out what each step actually needs.
45
Parallel decomposition identifies subtasks that have no data dependencies on each other
46
and runs them concurrently. This is where you recover the latency that sequential patterns
47
sacrifice. If you have five independent research tasks that each take 10 seconds, sequential
48
execution takes 50 seconds. Parallel takes 10. The design requirements are strict. Each parallel
49
branch has to be truly independent with no shared mutable state. If two branches write to the same
50
resource without coordination, you get race conditions. When all branches complete, a fan-in
51
step collects results and continues the workflow. Parallel execution introduces a failure scenario.
52
Sequential patterns avoid. Partial failure, where some branches succeed and others don't.
53
Your orchestration logic has to handle that explicitly with a defined policy for what
54
happens when the fan-in collects incomplete results. Real systems combine all three patterns,
55
and seeing them together is more representative of actual architectures than studying each one
56
in isolation. A hierarchical orchestrator might delegate to two specialized subagents
57
running in parallel, where each subagent internally runs a sequential pipeline of steps.
58
Or a sequential pipeline might fan out to parallel branches for a batch operation,
59
then fan back in before the next stage. The decision at each point is local.
60
What does this specific segment of the workflow need? Sequential, where dependencies exist.
61
Parallel, where they don't. And hierarchical, where specialization justifies the layering.
62
The most reliable architectures pick the right pattern for each segment based on its structure,
63
not based on a preference for any one approach. Three anti-patterns show up repeatedly in agentic
64
systems, and all three are worth recognizing on site. Over-decomposition happens when you've added
65
so many steps and handoffs that the cost of coordinating them exceeds the cost of just
66
running the task as a single unit. The workflow spends more time passing context around than
67
doing work. Under-decomposition is the opposite. Tasks are so large that a single failure kills
68
the whole workflow, and retry means starting from scratch. And false parallelism is the most
69
dangerous, treating steps with data dependencies as if they're independent, running them in parallel,
70
and getting data hazards and silent incorrect outputs. When you see a failing system, diagnosing
71
which anti-pattern it has tells you exactly what kind of fix is needed. Hierarchical, sequential,
72
and parallel decomposition cover the full design space for agentic workflows. Hierarchical patterns
73
enable specialization. Sequential patterns handle data dependency chains, and parallel
74
patterns cut latency wherever subtasks are independent. Real systems combine all three,
75
and the skill is knowing which fits each part. In the next lesson, we'll look at sequential
76
pipeline design and its trade-offs. I'll see you there.


=== D3: Claude Code - CLAUDE.md Configuration ===


--- Sequential Pipelines — Design and Tradeoffs ---

1
In this lesson, we're covering sequential pipelines,
2
how they're structured, how to map dependencies between steps,
3
how to design clean handoffs, where sequential execution is
4
the right call, and where it quietly costs you
5
latency when steps don't actually need to be ordered.
6
Let's get into it.
7
There's a small vocabulary that frames every pipeline decision,
8
so let's lock it in first.
9
Sequential execution means tasks run one at a time
10
in a fixed order, where each step may
11
depend on the previous output.
12
It's simpler to reason about than parallel execution,
13
but it can't exploit task-level parallelism.
14
Prompt chaining is the specific pattern
15
where the output of one LLM call becomes the input to the next,
16
creating a sequential pipeline where each step has
17
a narrow, single responsibility.
18
And a dependency graph is the tool
19
you use to figure out which steps actually have
20
to come before which others.
21
It shows required ordering and also
22
reveals which steps don't actually
23
need to be sequential at all.
24
Know all three of these going into the rest of the lesson.
25
A sequential pipeline is a chain where each step's output
26
feeds forward into the next step's input.
27
The execution order is fixed and deterministic.
28
There's no branching by default. If step 2
29
depends on step 1's output, step 2
30
can't start until step 1 completes.
31
That's the defining constraint.
32
It also means the pipeline halts on failure.
33
If any step fails, every downstream step
34
is blocked because they have no input to work with.
35
This is the basic structure of prompt chaining,
36
which is a deterministic workflow technique rather
37
than an agentic pattern.
38
Understanding this structure is the starting point
39
for knowing when to use it and when to break out of it.
40
Dependency graphs are the practical tool
41
for designing sequential pipelines.
42
You draw an edge from step A to step B.
43
If B needs A's output to run, steps with no incoming edges
44
have no prerequisites and can potentially
45
run first or in parallel with other independent steps.
46
Steps with multiple incoming edges
47
are synchronization points.
48
They can't proceed until all predecessors complete.
49
Reading the graph tells you the minimum required
50
sequential ordering, which is often
51
less than what your initial pipeline design assumes.
52
Many pipelines end up more sequential
53
than they need to be because the dependency graph was never drawn.
54
Drawing it first is a fast way to find opportunities
55
to reduce latency without changing correctness.
56
Sequential execution is the right choice in four situations.
57
First, when there's a true data dependency where step B
58
literally needs step A's output as its input,
59
parallel execution won't work here.
60
Second, when steps accumulate state,
61
where each step builds on a growing context
62
that prior steps produced.
63
Third, when operations need to run in a specific order,
64
like parsing before validation, before enrichment.
65
And fourth, when simplicity and auditability
66
matter more than speed.
67
A sequential pipeline is easy to step through, log, and debug.
68
If you're building something where correctness
69
and traceability are the top priorities,
70
sequential is often the cleaner design,
71
even if parallel would technically be faster.
72
Sequential pipelines have a real trade-off.
73
Total latency equals the sum of every step's latency.
74
That's fine when steps genuinely depend on each other.
75
It's a problem when they don't.
76
On the other side, sequential pipelines
77
are genuinely easier to debug.
78
A failure in step three means you
79
look at step three's input and output,
80
and you know exactly what happened.
81
There's no synchronization logic to reason about,
82
no partial failures from concurrent branches.
83
The data flow is linear and transparent.
84
The key lesson here is that sequential
85
is the correct pattern when dependencies are real.
86
Choosing sequential over parallel
87
when steps are actually independent wastes latency
88
and is an exam trap worth knowing.
89
Every boundary between pipeline steps
90
needs a defined schema for what passes
91
from one step to the next.
92
The output schema of step n becomes the input contract
93
for step n plus 1.
94
Making these schemas explicit catches mismatches early
95
before they cause runtime failures.
96
Include required fields, types, and nullability
97
in every handoff spec.
98
Loose, informal handoffs are a common source
99
of silent data loss.
100
Step n plus 1 might receive a response
101
that's missing a field it expects,
102
silently default to a null or empty value,
103
and continue running with corrupted data
104
that only shows up as a wrong result several steps later.
105
Explicit schemas give you a clear failure mode
106
at the boundary rather than a mysterious output failure
107
at the end of the pipeline.
108
Error handling in sequential pipelines
109
deserves specific attention because failures cascade.
110
If step 2 fails, step 3 has no input and can't run.
111
Step 4 has nothing from step 3 and can't run either.
112
The failure propagates down the entire remaining pipeline.
113
The cascade risk increases with depth.
114
A five-step pipeline has more failure surface
115
than a two-step one.
116
Early validation at each step's entry point
117
reduces how far bad data travels before someone catches it.
118
Design explicit error handling at every step boundary,
119
not just at the pipeline's final output.
120
When a step gets invalid input, it
121
should fail loudly and immediately
122
rather than silently passing malformed data forward
123
to the next step.
124
Not every sequential pipeline has genuine data dependencies.
125
Some are sequential purely by convention.
126
A true dependency means step B reads a field
127
that step A produced.
128
The ordering is required by the data.
129
An artificial dependency means the steps
130
are run sequentially only because that's
131
how the code was written, not because B actually
132
needs A's output.
133
Removing artificial dependencies unlocks parallel execution opportunities.
134
Before committing to a fully sequential design,
135
audit each step boundary.
136
Ask whether each step genuinely needs its predecessor's output
137
or whether it could run concurrently.
138
That audit is often where the biggest latency
139
wins are found without any change
140
to correctness or reliability.
141
And that covers sequential pipeline design
142
and when ordering matters.
143
Sequential pipelines are the right call
144
when data dependencies are real, when state accumulates
145
across steps, and when debugability comes first.
146
Draw dependency graphs, design explicit handoff schemas,
147
and handle errors at each boundary
148
rather than only at the end.
149
In the next lesson, we'll shift to parallel execution,
150
covering fan out, fan in, and synchronization.
151
I'll see you there.


=== D3: Claude Code - Slash Commands, Skills & Subagents ===


--- Parallel Execution — Fan-Out, Fan-In, and Synchronization ---

1
Welcome back.
2
This lesson is about parallel execution patterns,
3
how fan-out works, how to verify that branches are actually
4
independent before dispatching them,
5
how fan-in aggregates results at a synchronization point,
6
what to do when some branches fail,
7
and how the cost versus latency trade-off plays out in practice.
8
Let's get into it.
9
Three terms drive everything in this lesson.
10
Parallel execution means multiple independent tasks
11
run concurrently, reducing total wall clock time.
12
It requires a synchronization point to collect results
13
and handle partial failures before the pipeline continues.
14
Fan-out is the dispatch step where a single task splits
15
into multiple concurrent subtasks all dispatched at once.
16
The key requirement is that all branches
17
are independent of each other.
18
Fan-in is the synchronization step on the other side.
19
It collects results from all parallel branches,
20
handles varying result counts, deals with partial failures,
21
and reassembles results before the pipeline moves forward.
22
These three patterns work together,
23
and you need to understand each one to design parallel
24
execution correctly.
25
Fan-out is the dispatch step in a parallel execution pattern.
26
One control point triggers multiple concurrent subtasks
27
all at the same time.
28
Every branch gets its own inputs and runs independently.
29
The branches don't have to be simple function calls.
30
They can be full subagents, tool invocations,
31
or nested workflows.
32
The key constraint is that every branch has to be independent.
33
Fan-out increases throughput by running work simultaneously
34
rather than one at a time.
35
But it only works correctly when the branches genuinely
36
don't depend on each other.
37
A fan-out with hidden interbranch dependencies
38
produces incorrect results because branches
39
might run in any order or read stale state written
40
by a sibling branch that ran concurrently.
41
Before committing to a fan-out design,
42
run the independence test on every branch pair.
43
There are three questions to ask.
44
First, can branch B start without waiting for any output from branch A?
45
If B needs A's output, they have a dependency and can't fan out.
46
Second, do the branches write to shared state
47
that could cause race conditions?
48
If two branches both update the same record,
49
the result depends on which one finishes last,
50
and that's not deterministic.
51
Third, would running the branches in any order
52
produce the same final result?
53
If order matters between branches,
54
they're not independent.
55
If any of these questions reveals a dependency,
56
fan-out is the wrong pattern for those branches.
57
Fix the dependency first or use a sequential design
58
where ordering is enforced.
59
Fan-in has four responsibilities at the synchronization point.
60
First, it collects outputs from all branches
61
that completed successfully.
62
Second, it handles failures from branches that erred.
63
Third, if the order of results matters for the next pipeline
64
step, it reassembles outputs in the correct sequence.
65
And fourth, it merges the collected results
66
and passes the aggregated output to whatever comes next.
67
Each of these has to be explicitly designed.
68
An ad hoc fan-in that just takes whatever results are ready
69
and throws away failures is a silent failure source.
70
The fan-in logic is where partial failures show up,
71
and handling them explicitly is a non-negotiable design
72
requirement for parallel pipelines.
73
Parallel and sequential execution
74
have a real trade-off worth understanding clearly.
75
Parallel execution reduces total wall clock time
76
when branches are genuinely independent.
77
Multiple branches run simultaneously.
78
But it costs more in token spend per unit time
79
and introduces partial failure scenarios
80
that sequential execution doesn't have.
81
Sequential execution is slower.
82
Total time equals the sum of all step latencies.
83
But it's cheaper per run, and there's no synchronization
84
logic to write and no partial failures
85
from concurrent branches.
86
The exam trap here is worth flagging.
87
Parallel execution introduces partial failure scenarios
88
that sequential execution doesn't.
89
Designing explicit handling for the case
90
where some branches succeed and others fail
91
is a required part of any parallel pipeline design.
92
When some parallel branches succeed and others fail,
93
the fan-in step has three options.
94
First, fail the whole pipeline.
95
Any branch failure aborts the operation entirely.
96
This is appropriate when all branches are required
97
for a correct result.
98
Second, proceed with partial results.
99
Continue with what succeeded and flag what failed.
100
This works when partial results are still useful
101
and the pipeline can degrade gracefully.
102
Third, retry failed branches individually.
103
Redispatch only the branches that errored
104
rather than restarting the entire fan-out.
105
Whichever strategy you choose, it has to be explicit.
106
Silently dropping failed branches
107
and continuing as if they succeeded
108
is always the wrong answer.
109
Silent failures introduce incorrect results
110
that are extremely hard to diagnose after the fact.
111
How fan-in merges results depends on the pipeline's purpose.
112
Three common strategies. Voting.
113
Multiple branches answer the same question
114
and the majority result wins.
115
This is useful when you want resilience
116
against individual branch errors or inconsistencies. Concatenation.
117
Branches produce independent outputs
118
that are joined into a single list.
119
Works when each branch handles
120
a distinct segment of the input.
121
Structured aggregation.
122
Each branch contributes a named field
123
to a shared output object.
124
Useful when branches each compute
125
a different attribute of the same entity.
126
The key design point is to choose the merge strategy
127
before designing the branches.
128
The strategy shapes what each branch is expected to return,
129
which shapes its entire design.
130
Parallel execution cuts latency but raises token spend.
131
When three branches run simultaneously,
132
you're consuming roughly three times the tokens per second
133
compared to running the same work sequentially.
134
The total token cost is the sum across all branches,
135
not just the longest one.
136
Rate limits and quotas can also constrain
137
how much concurrency is actually achievable in practice.
138
If your API tier limits you
139
to a certain number of concurrent requests,
140
you may not get the latency improvement you expect
141
from a large fan out.
142
Model both dimensions explicitly
143
before choosing parallel over sequential.
144
The latency win is real,
145
but so is the cost increase
146
and the added complexity of synchronization
147
and partial failure handling.
148
And that wraps up parallel execution patterns
149
and synchronization.
150
Fan out dispatches concurrent branches from a single point.
151
The independence test confirms those branches
152
are actually safe to run in parallel.
153
Fan in collects results,
154
handles partial failures explicitly,
155
and merges outputs before the pipeline continues.
156
The independence test confirms those branches
157
are actually safe to run in parallel.
158
Fan in collects results,
159
handles partial failures explicitly,
160
and merges outputs before the pipeline continues.
161
Parallel reduces latency, but raises cost.
162
In the next lesson, we'll look at dynamic planning
163
and how to update the plan when things change mid-execution.
164
I'll see you there.


=== D3: Claude Code - Hooks, SDK & CI/CD ===


--- Adaptive Planning — Updating the Plan Mid-Execution ---

1
In this lesson, we're covering adaptive planning
2
in agentic systems.
3
We'll look at the difference between static and dynamic
4
planning, what events trigger a replanning decision,
5
and how to bound those cycles so the agent stays anchored
6
to its original goal.
7
Let's get into it.
8
Let's get the language right first.
9
Static planning means every step is authored
10
before execution begins.
11
The agent follows a pre-written script,
12
and nothing surprises you at runtime.
13
Dynamic planning is the opposite.
14
The model generates or revises its plan during execution
15
based on what it's actually seeing.
16
That's what enables adaptability,
17
but it's also what introduces unpredictability.
18
And replanning is the specific act
19
of revising the current plan in response to something
20
that went wrong or changed.
21
These three concepts sit at the core
22
of understanding how agentic systems handle uncertainty.
23
They come up constantly when you're
24
evaluating whether an agent is doing the right thing.
25
The key contrast between static and dynamic planning
26
comes down to one question.
27
When are the steps decided?
28
With static planning, every action
29
is locked in at design time.
30
You know what the agent will do before it runs.
31
That's powerful for predictability
32
and auditability.
33
With dynamic planning, the model figures out the next step
34
at inference time based on what it's seen so far.
35
That's powerful for open-ended adaptive tasks,
36
but it means the sequence isn't pre-audited,
37
and it's harder to predict or verify in advance.
38
In practice, most production systems blend both.
39
They use fixed structure where they can,
40
and dynamic decisions only where the task genuinely requires it.
41
Static and dynamic planning have genuinely different risk
42
profiles, and that's worth being explicit about.
43
Static plans are predictable, auditable, and cost efficient.
44
Every step is individually testable.
45
When something fails, you can localize it precisely.
46
Dynamic planning gives you adaptability
47
for goals that can't be fully pre-specified.
48
But because next steps aren't pre-audited,
49
the system requires stronger monitoring and fallback
50
mechanisms to stay safe.
51
The exam language here is important.
52
Dynamic planning increases unpredictability,
53
and that's a known trade-off you're accepting.
54
The engineering response is to compensate
55
with monitoring, logging, and explicit fallback paths
56
rather than hoping the model stays on track.
57
Four categories of events force an agent
58
to reconsider its current plan.
59
First, a failed tool call, where the tool returns
60
an error, a timeout, or an empty result
61
the plan didn't account for.
62
Second, an unexpected result, where
63
a tool returns something technically valid
64
but contradicts what the next step was designed to handle.
65
Third, a changed state, where the environment itself
66
has shifted since the agent started,
67
making earlier plan steps invalid.
68
Fourth, a constraint violation, where a proposed next action
69
would breach a defined goal constraint or safety boundary.
70
Each of these is a real signal that the current plan has a problem.
71
The agent's job is to assess whether the deviation is
72
recoverable within the existing plan
73
or whether a full replanning cycle is warranted.
74
It helps to think about replanning triggers
75
in four concrete buckets.
76
Tool call failures are the most obvious.
77
The tool is unavailable or returns an error.
78
Unexpected output is subtler.
79
The tool worked, but what it returned
80
is incompatible with what comes next.
81
State changes are environmental.
82
Something outside the agent's control
83
shifted while it was running.
84
And constraint violations are perhaps the most critical.
85
A proposed action would cross a goal boundary or safety limit
86
the agent is supposed to respect.
87
Each of these demands a different response.
88
And recognizing which bucket you're in
89
is the first step to handling it correctly.
90
Not every surprise requires throwing out the current plan.
91
The decision to replan versus continue
92
is one of the most important judgment
93
calls in agentic design.
94
Replanning is warranted when a core assumption the plan was
95
built on has been invalidated.
96
It's not warranted when the deviation is minor
97
and the original path is still recoverable.
98
The right question to ask is whether the original goal
99
is still achievable via the current plan with adjustments.
100
Unnecessary replanning wastes tokens, adds latency,
101
and increases the chance that the agent wanders off
102
in a new direction that only partially satisfies
103
the original requirements.
104
Once you've decided replanning is necessary,
105
you need to bound it.
106
Two mechanisms matter here.
107
First, a maximum iteration limit,
108
which is a hard cap on how many replanning cycles
109
the agent is allowed before it escalates or stops.
110
Without this cap, an agent that keeps hitting the same obstacle
111
will loop forever.
112
Second, a goal constraint check, which
113
validates that the revised plan still
114
satisfies the original task requirements
115
before execution resumes.
116
The constraint check is what prevents the agent
117
from solving the immediate obstacle at the cost
118
of ignoring the actual goal.
119
Both mechanisms together give you
120
a replanning loop that's adaptive but not unbounded.
121
Goal drift is one of the most dangerous failure
122
modes in replanning systems.
123
And it's subtle enough that it often
124
goes undetected until significant damage has been done.
125
Drift happens when the agent revises
126
its plan to overcome an obstacle,
127
but the new plan no longer satisfies
128
the original requirements.
129
Every replanning cycle should explicitly
130
validate against original intent.
131
The practical approach is to preserve the original goal
132
specification as a persistent artifact
133
and compare each revised plan against it before proceeding.
134
Logging the original goal alongside each plan revision
135
also creates an audit trail that makes
136
drift detectable and diagnosable after the fact.
137
Dynamic planning gives agents the flexibility
138
to pursue open-ended goals even when the path isn't
139
clear from the start.
140
The key guardrails are bounded planning cycles
141
and replanning cycles, explicit iteration limits,
142
and continuous goal constraint checking to prevent drift.
143
When those guardrails are in place,
144
replanning becomes a strength rather than a liability.
145
And that covers adaptive planning and replanning triggers.
146
In the next lesson, we'll look at handling ambiguity,
147
what to do when the goal isn't fully specified.
148
I'll see you there.


=== D4: Prompt Engineering - Writing Effective Prompts ===


--- Handling Ambiguity and Incomplete Specifications ---

1
In this lesson, we're looking at how agentic systems handle
2
ambiguous or incomplete goal specification.
3
We'll cover the two core strategies
4
for dealing with unclear instructions,
5
how iterative refinement loops help close quality gaps,
6
and why documenting assumptions matters for human oversight
7
and auditability.
8
Let's get into it.
9
Start with the vocabulary.
10
An ambiguous goal is a task specification
11
that doesn't fully define the expected outcome.
12
It leaves multiple valid interpretations open,
13
and the agent has to pick one to proceed.
14
The clarify first strategy means pausing execution
15
to ask a human for more information before proceeding.
16
It adds latency, but it prevents mistakes
17
when the cost of a wrong assumption is high.
18
The assume and proceed strategy means making
19
a reasonable inference and continuing
20
without waiting for human input.
21
