takes your schema and compiles it into a grammar. That grammar then constrains the model's token
22
sampling as it writes the tool call. In plain terms, at every step, the model is only allowed
23
to produce tokens that keep the output valid against your schema. Anything that would violate
24
the schema simply can't be sampled. This technique has a name. It's called constrained decoding,
25
and the key thing to understand is that it happens live as the model generates, not as a
26
check afterward. Here's my favorite part, what you get to stop writing. Because the guarantee holds
27
by construction, you get no validation layer. That's the code that used to inspect every tool
28
call and confirm the types and fields were correct. Gone. You also get no retry loop, the logic that
29
reprompted the model whenever a call came back malformed. Also gone. What's left gives you type
30
safe calls. Your functions receive correctly typed arguments every single time, which means they
31
behave predictably even when you're running at real production scale. Less code, fewer failure
32
paths, more confidence. That's a genuinely good trade, and it's the practical reason teams turn
33
strict on for agent work. Let me ground this in a concrete example. Imagine a booking function,
34
and it needs a passengers field that has to be an integer. Without strict mode, the model might give
35
you the string two spelled out, or it might give you a two in quotes, which your code then has to
36
detect and coerce into a real number. Either way, you're writing defensive glue. Turn strict on,
37
and you always receive the integer two clean. The whole class of failure just disappears.
38
There's no coercion step, no guard clause, no retry. The downstream booking call simply works
39
because the input arrived in exactly the form you asked for. Multiply that across every tool in a
40
real system, and you can see how much fragile plumbing strict mode quietly retires. Now I want
41
to draw a clear line between two features that sound similar, because students mix them up
42
constantly. Strict tool use constrains the tool inputs. It makes sure the arguments going into
43
your functions conform to your input schema. It's about what flows into the tools. Structured
44
outputs, on the other hand, constrains the text response itself. It shapes the model's final answer
45
to a schema you provide. It's about the shape of what comes back to the user, or the calling code.
46
Same underlying idea, valid by construction, pointed at two different targets. One governs the
47
tool call inputs. The other governs the response. Keep that distinction sharp, and you'll always
48
know which one you actually need. Here's what ties them together under the surface. Strict tool use
49
compiles your schemas into grammars using the very same pipeline that structured outputs uses.
50
It's not a separate system bolted on. It's the same machinery aimed at tool inputs.
51
Because of that shared foundation, both features accept the same supported subset of JSON schema.
52
That matters in practice. A handful of the more advanced schema keywords aren't allowed,
53
so you keep your schemas within the supported subset. And there's a performance nicety.
54
Those compiled schemas get cached for up to 24 hours since their last use. So if you call the
55
same tool repeatedly, you skip the recompilation cost after the first time. Same pipeline,
56
same schema rules, same cache. One more thing worth calling out,
57
because it connects straight back to the previous lesson. Strict combines cleanly with the tool
58
choice settings we covered. Set tool choice to any, and you guarantee that some tool gets called.
59
Then set strict to true on that tool, and you guarantee the inputs to it are valid. Stack
60
those two together, and you've closed the loop. You're certain a call happens, and you're certain
61
the call is well formed. There's no gap left where the model could either skip the tool
62
or feed it garbage. For anyone building agent workflows that have to run unattended,
63
that combination is a genuinely strong foundation to build on. Reliable trigger, reliable payload.
64
Let's recap. Setting strict to true guarantees your tool inputs conform to your input schema.
65
The name is valid, the types match, and required fields are present. It works through constrained
66
decoding on the same pipeline and cache as structured outputs. The payoff is you delete
67
your validation and retry loops. That wraps up this lesson. I'll see you in the next one.

--- Returning Useful Errors from Tools ---

1
When a tool breaks, what it sends back matters as much as what it does when it works.
2
Claude uses that error response to decide its next move. Retry, ask the user, or try something else.
3
In this lesson, we'll cover how to design tool errors that give Claude the information it needs.
4
Let's get into it. Let's get the language right first. A tool error response is a structured
5
reply that says execution failed and explains why. The parts you'll hear most are the error code,
6
the message, and the suggested action. The error code is a short stable string that code and models
7
can act on without reading the message. The message is the human readable explanation that's
8
safe to surface in a reply. The suggested action tells Claude what to do next, whether that's
9
retry, ask the user, or escalate. Together, these pieces give Claude a complete picture of what went
10
wrong and how to respond. It helps to know what the tool result block actually looks like when
11
Claude sends it back because it's leaner than people expect. It carries just three fields.
12
The tool use ID matches the result back to the specific call Claude made. The content field
13
holds the result itself, and when the tool fails, that same field holds your error message.
14
And the is error flag set to true tells Claude the execution failed rather than succeeded. There's
15
no dedicated error code field, no separate context field, nothing else in the block. So any richer
16
error shape you design, a code, a reason, a suggested action, lives inside the content field as text.
17
Claude reads it there. Keep that in mind. The structure you invent is a convention inside
18
content, not extra fields on the block itself. One of the most useful distinctions in error
19
handling is whether the failure is a user error or a system error. User errors mean the caller
20
sent bad data, the wrong format, a missing required field, or a value that's out of range. For these,
21
retrying won't help. Claude should surface the problem to the user and ask for a correction.
22
System errors are different. A timeout, a service that's temporarily unavailable,
23
or an unexpected upstream response are all things the user didn't cause and can't fix.
24
For these, Claude can retry, wait, or try an alternative path without involving the user
25
at all. Your error code should make this distinction explicit. When you write error
26
messages, keep in mind that Claude is the primary consumer, not a developer reading logs. Codes need
27
to stay stable across versions, so any routing logic built on them doesn't break when you ship
28
an update. Include the input value that triggered the error in the context field, because the same
29
code can mean different things depending on which parameter failed. And avoid raw stack traces as
30
the main message. A stack trace is useful in a log file, but it's noise in a tool response.
31
Replace it with a clean message and put the technical detail somewhere else.
32
The goal is an error that Claude can act on, not just read.
33
These four fields form the skeleton of a well-designed error response.
34
The error code is the stable, machine-readable string that never changes across versions.
35
The message is the human-readable description you'd be comfortable surfacing directly in a
36
chat reply without editing. The context block carries the key value pairs that identify what
37
failed and why, so debugging doesn't require guessing. And the suggested action is the
38
explicit hint about what Claude should do next, which saves it from having to infer the right
39
path on its own. Each field serves a different consumer. The code serves routing logic. The
40
message serves users. The context serves debugging. The suggested action serves Claude's decision
41
engine. Design each one with its consumer in mind. The suggested action field is optional,
42
but valuable. Not every error needs one, but many benefit from explicit guidance.
43
A rate-limited error should say to retry after a short delay. An invalid input error should say to
44
ask the user to correct the value. A service-unavailable error might say to try an alternative
45
tool or escalate to a human. Without this field, Claude has to infer the right response from the
46
error code alone, which works for common codes but breaks down for domain-specific ones.
47
Adding a suggested action is low cost on the tool side and high value on the model side.
48
It's worth including whenever the right next step isn't obvious. Recovery and escalation
49
are the two outcomes an error response should steer Claude toward. Recovery means Claude handles
50
the problem on its own. Escalation means Claude brings a human into the loop because the problem
51
needs judgment or action that the model can't provide. Error routing uses the error code to
52
decide which path to take. A well-designed set of codes lets you define routing rules once and
53
apply them consistently across every tool in your system. The cleaner your codes, the easier it is
54
to build reliable routing logic on top of them. A few mistakes show up repeatedly in tool error
55
design. The first is returning a success status with an error buried somewhere in the response
56
body. Claude may not look for it and the model will proceed as if the call succeeded. The second
57
is using vague codes like failed or error with no subtype which forces Claude to parse the message
58
to understand what happened. The third is leaving out context when the same code can mean different
59
things depending on which input failed. Each of these mistakes adds friction to recovery and
60
increases the chance that Claude makes the wrong next move. Specific context-rich errors are worth
61
the extra effort. There's one more thing to internalize and it's a security point, not a
62
formatting one. A tool result often carries content from sources you don't control. A fetched web page,
63
an inbound email, a user upload, a third-party service response. You have to treat all of that
64
as untrusted input. An attacker who can influence it may bury instructions in the text that try to
65
redirect Claude, telling it to ignore its task or leak data. That's indirect prompt injection and it
66
turns your tool into a confused deputy acting on someone else's orders. The guardrail Anthropic
67
recommends is to keep that untrusted content inside tool result blocks. Never promote it into the
68
system prompt or a plain user message so the model always knows it came from a tool and treats it with
69
appropriate suspicion. The big picture, the key is combining a stable error code, a clear message,
70
useful context, and an explicit suggested action. That's tool error response design.
71
Next up, retry logic and how to handle partial success. That's the playbook for production.
72
These pieces fit together into a coherent design, so I'll see you there.

--- Tool Retry Logic and Partial Success Patterns ---

1
Welcome back.
2
Tool failures don't always mean the operation is impossible.
3
Sometimes a service is temporarily overloaded,
4
and a retry will succeed.
5
Sometimes a batch job finishes half its items
6
before hitting an error.
7
This lesson covers retry safe patterns, partial success
8
responses, and circuit breakers for unreliable downstream services.
9
Let's jump into it.
10
There's a small vocabulary that frames every retry decision.
11
So let's lock it in first.
12
Partial success describes the outcome
13
when a batch tool completes some operations
14
and fails on others.
15
The response has to say which items succeeded
16
and which didn't.
17
So Claude can reason about what to do next.
18
Item potency is the property that calling a tool twice
19
with the same input is safe.
20
It's the foundation of retry logic,
21
because without it, retrying a call
22
can create duplicate side effects.
23
And the circuit breaker is a pattern
24
that cuts off calls to a failing downstream service
25
after repeated failures.
26
Preventing the kind of cascading errors
27
that fill a context window with noise.
28
Before you retry, you need to know whether retrying actually makes sense.
29
Transient errors like timeouts and rate limit responses
30
are the clearest candidates.
31
The service was busy or temporarily unavailable.
32
And waiting a short moment before trying again
33
is the right call.
34
User errors are the opposite.
35
If the input was invalid, sending the exact same request
36
again won't fix anything.
37
Claude should surface the problem to the user
38
and ask for a correction instead.
39
System errors without a suggested action
40
fall somewhere in between and need judgment before retrying.
41
Your error codes should make the category explicit
42
so Claude doesn't have to infer it from message text alone.
43
Item potency is the key property that makes retry safe.
44
An item potent tool returns the same result,
45
whether you call it once or 10 times with the same input.
46
A lookup or a read is idempotent by nature.
47
A write that creates a new record
48
isn't unless you build idempotency in deliberately.
49
The standard pattern is for the caller
50
to generate a unique idempotency key
51
and include it with the request.
52
The tool stores the key, and if it sees the same key again,
53
returns the original response instead of executing again.
54
Payment APIs use this pattern extensively.
55
It's worth adopting in any tool that creates or modifies state.
56
Designing for safe retry starts at the tool interface level.
57
The most direct approach is accepting a caller-provided
58
idempotency key with every state-changing request.
59
The tool stores the key alongside the result
60
and returns the cached result for any duplicate call.
61
Conditional writes are another option.
62
The tool checks current state before applying a change
63
and skips the write if it's already been applied.
64
If either approach turns a non-idempotent operation into one,
65
Claude can safely retry on transient failures.
66
The cost is a bit of storage and a lookup on each call.
67
The benefit is that Claude never creates a duplicate charge,
68
email, or record because of a network hiccup
69
between the tool call and the response.
70
Three retry failure modes come up frequently enough
71
to call out by name.
72
Duplicate side effects happen when a non-idempotent tool
73
gets retried and creates two records, charges, or messages instead of one.
74
This is the most expensive failure mode
75
because real-world consequences, like double charges,
76
are hard to reverse.
77
Infinite retry loops happen when there's no back-off delay
78
or retry limit, so Claude keeps hammering a failing service
79
until the context window fills.
80
And stale state failures happen when
81
a retry reapplies steps that already completed
82
during a partial run, leaving the system
83
in an inconsistent state.
84
Each of these is avoidable with the right design choices.
85
Idempotency keys prevent duplicates,
86
back-off and limits prevent loops,
87
and partial success responses prevent
88
rerunning completed steps.
89
Partial success is the case where a batch tool
90
completes some items and fails on others.
91
The wrong response is to return a generic error that
92
hides what succeeded.
93
The right response uses a partial success status
94
and splits the result into two lists, a succeeded list
95
with the IDEs or keys that completed,
96
and a failed list with per-item error codes and context.
97
When Claude receives this structure,
98
it can decide what to retry, what to skip,
99
and what to surface to the user.
100
Without it, Claude either has to guess or rerun
101
the entire batch, which risks duplicating the operations
102
that already finished.
103
Surfacing partial success explicitly
104
is what the CCA exam tests in this area.
105
The circuit breaker pattern manages the relationship
106
between your tool and an unreliable downstream service.
107
It has three states.
108
In the closed state, calls pass through normally
109
and failures are counted.
110
Once failures exceed a threshold, the breaker opens
111
and calls are blocked immediately
112
without hitting the downstream service.
113
After a configured interval, the breaker moves to half open
114
and allows a single probe call through.
115
If the probe succeeds, the breaker closes
116
and normal traffic resumes.
117
If it fails, the breaker stays open and the interval resets.
118
This pattern prevents Claude from filling its context
119
with errors while waiting for a service that
120
won't recover on its own.
121
You should add a circuit breaker when a downstream tool has
122
intermittent outages that aren't predictable.
123
The configuration has three parameters
124
you need to set deliberately.
125
The failure threshold is how many consecutive failures
126
trigger the open state.
127
Set it too low and the breaker trips on normal variance.
128
Set it too high and you waste calls on a broken service.
129
The recovery probe interval is how long
130
to wait before testing the service again.
131
And the fallback defines what to return
132
while the circuit is open, a cached result,
133
a simplified response, or a clean error
134
that tells Claude to escalate.
135
Without a fallback, the circuit breaker
136
just converts tool failures into blocked calls.
137
One more time, idempotency keys allow safe retries
138
without duplicates.
139
Partial success responses give the detail
140
needed to retry on what failed.
141
Circuit breakers stop cascading calls to struggling services.
142
Together, they build the reliability layer
143
production-ready integration requires.
144
Coming up, MCP, what it is and why it exists.
145
I'll see you there.

--- Tool Scoping per Agent — Why Less Is More ---

1
Welcome back. In this lesson we're looking at tool distribution across agents, specifically
2
why giving an agent fewer tools almost always produces better results. We'll cover the cost
3
of tool bloat, how overlapping tools break routing, and the specialist sub-agent pattern
4
that keeps each agent's context clean and focused. Let's get into it. Let's anchor this lesson with
5
three terms. First, tool bloat. That's the anti-pattern where you give an agent more
6
tools than it needs. Every tool description loads into context on every turn, so extra tools cost
7
tokens even when they're never called. Second, specialist sub-agent. That's an agent configured
8
with a narrow palette matched exactly to its role. It's a design choice that makes behavior
9
more predictable and easier to debug. Third, context isolation. When each sub-agent only
10
sees the tools relevant to its job, the orchestrator's window stays clean. These three
11
ideas work together. Understanding them sets up everything else in this lesson.
12
Let's talk about what tool bloat actually costs. Every tool you register with an agent has a
13
description, and that description loads into the context window on every single turn, whether the
14
agent uses that tool or not. If you ship 30 tools to an agent that only ever calls three of them,
15
you're burning context budget on 27 irrelevant descriptions every time the agent runs. That's
16
not just wasteful, it's actively harmful, because those descriptions crowd out content the model
17
actually needs to do its job well. The practical fix is simple. Audit your tool list and remove
18
anything the agent doesn't call in normal operation. Lean context windows produce faster,
19
cheaper, and more reliable agents. I want you to hold two mental models side by side. First,
20
the bloated agent. It receives every tool in the catalog because it was easier to configure that
21
way. Its context fills up with tool descriptions for things it will never call. When two tools
22
overlap, the model picks between them inconsistently. Responses slow down, and the agent becomes harder
23
to trust in production. Now compare that to the specialist agent. It receives only the tools it
24
needs for its mission. The model always sees one obvious candidate for each request. Routing is
25
clean, behavior is consistent, and when something goes wrong, you know exactly which tool to look at.
26
Specialization is an investment that pays back in reliability. Tool overlap is a subtle reliability
27
risk that teams often underestimate. When two tools could plausibly handle the same request,
28
the model doesn't flip a coin. It picks based on description quality, and the pick may change
29
depending on small variations in the user's input. That inconsistency is a problem in production.
30
Users expect the same action to trigger the same tool every time. When the model alternates
31
between two similar tools, you get divergent outcomes that are hard to diagnose because
32
the input looks identical. The fix is to make sure each tool owns a clearly distinct scope.
33
If two tools overlap, either merge them into one or rewrite their descriptions so the distinction
34
is unambiguous. Precision in tool definitions is a form of reliability engineering.
35
Four principles for scoping tools to agents. First, one agent, one mission. Each agent should
36
have a defined role, and the tools it receives should map directly to that role. Second, tools
37
earn their slot. Don't add a tool speculatively. Add it when no existing tool covers the need,
38
and consider removing it if usage data shows it's rarely called. Third, audit the catalog.
39
Tools added during early prototyping often stick around long after they're useful.
40
A quarterly review of tool assignments catches this drift before it becomes a problem. Fourth,
41
name precision matters. If two tools have similar names or vague descriptions, the model can't
42
distinguish them cleanly. Invest time in description quality. It's the highest leverage
43
thing you can do for routing accuracy. The specialist sub-agent pattern is the most practical
44
way to apply these principles at scale. The idea is straightforward. You decompose a complex agent
45
workflow into distinct roles, and you configure each sub-agent with only the tools that role
46
requires. A retrieval sub-agent gets search and fetch tools. It doesn't need file write access.
47
A write sub-agent gets database and file tools. It doesn't need search capabilities. The
48
orchestrator coordinates between them without touching domain-specific tools directly.
49
Each sub-agent's tool palette is small enough that the model can always identify the right call.
50
Because the roles are isolated, you can test each sub-agent independently. You can also swap out one
51
sub-agent's implementation without touching the others. That's a significant operational advantage
52
as your system grows. Three vocabulary terms worth locking in before we move on. Tool routing is what
53
happens when the model reads the available tools and decides which one to call. The quality of that
54
decision depends almost entirely on description text. That's why tool descriptions are worth
55
taking seriously. A well-written description tells the model what the tool does, when it should be
56
used, and importantly, when it shouldn't be used. That last part is often skipped, and skipping it
57
causes most routing errors. Finally, cross-cutting tools. Some tools legitimately belong on multiple
58
agents. Logging is the clearest example. If you need every agent to emit structured logs, you'll
59
register the logging tool everywhere. That's not bloat, that's intentional architecture. The
60
distinction matters. We've spent this lesson arguing for lean tool palettes, so let's address
61
the exception. Some tools should appear on multiple agents, and that's fine. Logging is the obvious
62
case. If you want consistent observability across your system, every agent that produces output
63
should have a logging tool. Status reporting is similar. An orchestrator and its sub-agents
64
may all need to write to a shared status store, so you can track workflow progress from a single
65
view. The key distinction is intent. Cross-cutting tools are deliberately shared because their scope
66
is genuinely broader than one role. Tool bloat happens when tools end up on agents by accident,
67
because no one cleaned up after a prototype. Minimize by design, not by accident. Focused
68
tool palettes reduce context overhead, sharpen routing, and make your agents easier to test
69
and trust. The specialist sub-agent pattern puts this into practice by matching each agent's tools
70
to its role, keeping every part of the system lean and auditable. And that covers tool scoping.
71
In the next lesson, we'll dig into how Claude actually picks one tool over the other,
72
and where the selection breaks down. I'll see you there.

--- Selection Heuristics and Tool-Selection Failures ---

1
Welcome. This lesson is about how Claude actually picks a tool from the available palette,
2
and what happens when the pick goes wrong. We'll look at the two main failure modes and
3
description writing techniques that prevent them, and how to use production logging to catch routing
4
degradation before it becomes a problem. Let's get into it. Three terms define the failure
5
landscape for tool selection. First, no good match failure. This happens when none of the
6
available tool descriptions closely match the user's request. The model might fabricate a tool
7
name, refuse to act, or call the least wrong option, and silently produce bad output. Second,
8
two good matches failure. This happens when two tools both look like valid candidates.
9
The model alternates between them across turns, making behavior non-deterministic and hard to
10
debug in production. Third, disambiguation patterns. These are the description writing
11
techniques we use to ensure each tool is the unambiguous winner for its intended request type.
12
Understanding these three ideas gives you a diagnostic vocabulary for any routing problem
13
you encounter. It helps to think of tool selection as a matching problem. On every turn, the model
14
reads the incoming request and scores each available tool's description for relevance.
15
The highest scoring match wins, and the model calls that tool. Notice what's not in this process.
16
The model doesn't run the tool and check whether the output looks right. It commits to a selection
17
based purely on descriptions. That's why description quality is the primary lever for improving routing
18
accuracy. A tool that can do exactly what the user needs will still lose the routing competition
19
if its description is vague, generic, or uses vocabulary that doesn't align
20
with how users phrase their requests. Writing descriptions is a form of precision engineering,
21
not documentation. Let me walk through both failure modes in concrete terms.
22
The no good match failure happens when the model reads the request and finds no strong candidate.
23
What it does next depends on its configuration. In some cases, it fabricates a tool name that
24
doesn't exist. In others, it refuses to act and says it can't help. In the worst case, it calls
25
the least wrong option and produces output that looks plausible but is incorrect. Silent errors
26
are the hardest to catch. The two good matches failure is different. The model finds two strong
27
candidates and picks between them non-deterministically. You'll see it alternate between
28
the two tools on requests that look identical. That kind of inconsistency is hard to diagnose
29
because the inputs aren't obviously different. The most reliable fix for both failure modes is
30
writing descriptions that make each tool the unambiguous winner for its intended request type.
31
The two core techniques are distinct verbs and narrow scopes. Distinct verbs means each tool
32
description starts with a verb that doesn't apply to any other tool in the palette. Retrieves,
33
writes, deletes, summarizes, translates. These verbs signal intent clearly and rarely overlap.
34
Narrow scope means you tell the model not just what the tool does but what it doesn't do. Retrieves
35
records from the user database, doesn't write or update records. That negative boundary is often
36
more useful than the positive description because it's exactly what the model needs to break a tie
37
between two similar tools. Tool descriptions carry most of the routing weight but the system prompt
38
can reinforce them when descriptions alone aren't enough. One effective pattern is adding an
39
explicit routing section to the system prompt. This section names each tool and states when to use it
40
versus the alternatives. It's particularly useful when you have tools that are similar by nature
41
and can't be distinguished by description alone. Tie-breaking instructions are another technique.
42
When two tools are genuinely close in scope you can instruct the model to prefer one
43
based on a specific criterion. For example, prefer the cached retrieval tool when the data
44
is less than five minutes old. Negative examples round out the toolkit. Listing which tool not to
45
call for common edge cases gives the model a concrete guide for the ambiguous situations
46
your descriptions don't fully resolve. Routing quality doesn't stay constant. As you add tools,
47
rename them, or adjust descriptions, the selection behavior changes. Users change how they phrase
48
requests over time too. Logging is how you detect this drift before it becomes a reliability incident.
49
The basic implementation is straightforward. Log every tool call with the tool name and the request
50
that triggered it. Over time you'll be able to track selection frequency for each tool.
51
A tool that drops from 10 calls per hour to 2 calls per hour without a corresponding change in
52
user behavior is a signal that something in the routing has shifted. Maybe a newer tool is stealing
53
its requests. Maybe a description change introduced ambiguity. Either way, the log gives you the data
54
to investigate. Without logging, you find out about routing degradation from user complaints.
55
Three vocabulary terms I want you to carry forward. Tool description is the text passed to
56
Claude that explains what a tool does. It's the primary signal for routing, and it deserves more
57
attention than it typically gets in implementation timelines. Tool routing is the model's selection
58
process. It's not a black box. It's a matching algorithm operating on description text. Once you
59
think of it that way, improving routing becomes a concrete engineering problem, not a mystery.
60
Selection drift is the gradual degradation of routing accuracy as descriptions become
61
stale or inconsistent with how users phrase requests. Drift is invisible without logging,
62
which is why production observability is part of the architecture, not an afterthought. These
63
three concepts give you a coherent mental model for building reliable tool-enabled agents.
64
One of the most common mistakes teams make when a tool isn't routing correctly
65
is adding a new tool to cover the gap. That's almost always the wrong move.
66
Adding a tool grows the catalog, which increases context overhead and introduces new opportunities
67
for overlap. Before you add, ask whether the existing tool could cover the need if its
68
description were refined. In most cases, the answer is yes. A better description fixes routing
69
without any of the downsides. The rule I'd suggest is default to refinement and add a tool only when
70
the use case is genuinely outside the scope of anything you already have. When you do add,
71
write the new tool's description and the neighboring tool's descriptions at the same time
72
to make sure the boundaries are clean from the start. Tool selection is a description quality
73
problem. The two failure modes, no good match or two good matches, both have description level
74
fixes. Log selections in production and you can catch routing drift before users notice it.
75
Refine before you add and treat descriptions as first-class engineering artifacts.
76
That wraps up tool selection.

--- Controlling Tool Selection ---

1
Welcome back. When you give the model tools, it decides on its own whether to reach for one.
2
That default works most of the time, but every so often you need a firmer hand. This lesson
3
covers the one parameter that gives you that control and the four ways to set it.
4
The parameter is called tool choice and it takes four values. The first is auto. With auto,
5
the model looks at the request and decides for itself whether calling a tool helps. This is
6
the default whenever you supply tools. The second is any. With any, the model has to call one of
7
your tools, but it still picks which one fits. The third is tool. Here you name a specific tool
8
and the model calls that exact one every single time. We'll get to the fourth, none, a little
9
later. For now, hold on to the shape of it. Auto is a free choice. Any is a forced choice among
10
options. Tool is a fixed choice you make for the model. Let's start with auto because it's the one
11
you'll use most. With auto, the model reads what the user asked and judges whether a tool actually
12
helps. If someone asks a question, it can answer from its own knowledge. It just answers. No tool
13
call. But if the task genuinely needs a live lookup or an action, it calls the right tool.
14
And here's a nice touch. With auto, the model can narrate what it's about to do before it makes the
15
call. That running commentary feels natural to a user. For most assistant style applications, this
16
balance is exactly what you want, so reach for auto first and only tighten things when you have
17
a concrete reason. Now, what happens when you force a call? When you set any or tool, the model pre-fills
18
its own reply so that a tool call is guaranteed to come out. There's a consequence to that. The
19
model skips the friendly natural language preamble. It won't explain itself first. You get a tool call
20
as the very first block of content. So use forcing when a plain prose answer is never an acceptable
21
outcome for that turn. The trade is real. You gain certainty, but you lose that conversational
22
explanation. If you want both, there's a trick. Keep tool choice on auto and just add a clear
23
instruction in your user message asking the model to use the tool. That often gets you the call
24
and the explanation together. Let's put any and tool side by side because people mix them up.
25
Any guarantees that some tool runs, but it leaves the choice of which one to the model.
26
That's your go-to when you have a set of actions and any of them could be the right answer.
27
Think of routing a request to one of several valid handlers. Tool is stricter. You name one exact tool
28
and the model calls that one every time, full stop. That fits a fixed step in a pipeline, something
29
like a data extraction call that should always happen regardless of what the user said. So the
30
question to ask yourself is simple. Do I need a specific tool or just any valid tool? That answer
31
picks the setting for you. That brings us to the fourth value, none. None blocks the model from
32
calling any tool at all, even when you've attached tools to the request. Two things make it useful.
33
First, none is actually the default whenever you send no tools. That's the quiet baseline behavior.
34
Second, and more interesting, you can keep your tools attached and still set none to force a text.
35
Only turn in the middle of a longer conversation. Imagine a multi-step flow where at one point
36
you want the model to summarize what happened so far without touching a tool. You set none for that
37
turn, get your pros, then switch back. It's a clean way to pause tool activity without stripping the
38
tools out of your request. Let me show you a concrete routing pattern because this is where
39
any really shines. Say you've built a handful of database actions, look up a record, create one,
40
update one. You want the model to always pick one of those, never to wander off into a chatty non-answer.
41
You set tool choice to any. Now the model has to route the request into one of your actions.
42
It reads the intent and dispatches to the right one for you. You never get an off-topic pros reply
43
because pros isn't an option that turn. This effectively turns the model into a dependable
44
dispatcher sitting in front of your tools. For any workflow where the next step has to be an action,
45
that pattern is worth keeping in your pocket. Before we wrap, a few practical things to watch.
46
Start with caching. If you're using prompt caching, changing tool choice invalidates your cached
47
message blocks. The good news is your tool definitions and your system prompt stay cached.
48
Only the message content has to be reprocessed, so don't flip tool choice around casually if you're
49
leaning on the cache. Next, extended thinking. When the model is using extended thinking,
50
only auto and none are supported. If you try to use any or tool alongside thinking,
51
you'll get an error back. And remember, both any and tool pre-fill the assistant turn,
52
which is exactly why no explanation shows up before the call.
53
Keep those limits in mind and you'll avoid the common surprises.
54
So how do you actually choose? Match the setting to how much freedom the task should have.
55
For open-ended assistant work, where sometimes a tool helps and sometimes it doesn't,
56
stay with auto. When the model has to act but any of several actions is valid, use any. When
57
there's exactly one mandatory step that has to run, name it with tool. And when you need a text
58
only turn or you've sent no tools, none is your answer. Honestly, when you're unsure, auto paired
59
with a clear instruction in your message handles a surprising amount. Start loose. Tighten only
60
where the task demands it and you'll rarely fight the model. Here's the bottom line. The tool choice
61
parameter has four settings and together they decide whether the model calls a tool and which
62
one. Auto is the default. The model chooses for itself. Any means that it has to call a tool,
63
but it still gets to pick which one. Name a specific tool and it calls that one every single
64
time. And a setting of none turns tool calling off completely. The rule of thumb is use the
65
tightest setting that fits the task at hand. I'll see you in the next lesson.

--- Tool Search for Large Catalogs ---

1
Hello. Working with a handful of tools is easy, but something breaks when your catalog grows.
2
The model gets quietly worse at choosing among them, and all those definitions start eating your context window.
3
This lesson covers the pair that fixes both problems, the tool search tool, and a flag called defer
4
loading. Let's jump in.
5
Let's name the first problem plainly.
6
It's an accuracy cliff.
7
When you have just a handful of tools, the model's ability to pick the right one is solid, no issue.
8
But that ability starts to degrade once you push past roughly 30 to 50 available tools. Think about why.
9
Every extra tool you add is one more option the model has to consider, and
10
one more chance for it to reach for the wrong one. The choices start to blur together.
11
So the naive instinct, just hand the model every tool you've got and let it sort them out,
12
actually works against you as you scale.
13
Past that 30 to 50 range, more tools in context means less reliable selection, not more capability.
14
That's the cliff we have to design around.
15
There's a second cost, and it's about context.
16
By default, every tool definition you provide sits in the context window from the very first request,
17
whether or not that tool ever gets used. That adds up fast.
18
A typical setup that stitches together several servers can burn something like 55,000 tokens just in tool definitions
19
before the model does a single useful thing.
20
That's a lot of your budget spent on tools that mostly sit idle.
21
Here's the fix in one line. Tool search loads only the few tools a given request actually needs.
22
In practice, that trims the tokens spent on definitions by over 85%.
23
You stop paying to keep hundreds of unused tools resident in context on every call.
24
So how does tool search work?
25
You add a tool search tool to your tools list, and that tool becomes the model's way of finding others.
26
The mechanics matter, so follow closely. You still send every tool definition in your request.
27
Nothing is hidden from the API.
28
The difference is that the tools you mark as deferred stay out of the model's active context.
29
They're available to be found, but they're not loaded up front. When the model realizes it needs something
30
it doesn't currently have, it runs a search. The matches come back as lightweight references, and the API
31
expands each reference into the full tool definition on demand, right when it's needed.
32
So the model discovers tools just in time, rather than carrying them all from the start.
33
The mechanism that controls all this is a single flag called defer loading.
34
When you set defer loading to true on a tool,
35
you're saying, keep this one out of my active context until the model actually searches for it.
36
That's what shrinks the footprint. Two details trip people up, so let me be precise.
37
First, deferring a tool doesn't mean you leave it out of the request.
38
You still include its full definition every time, because the search needs it there to find it and expand it.
39
Defer loading controls what enters the context window, not what you send.
40
Second, you can't defer everything. At least one tool has to stay non-deferred, and normally
41
that's the search tool itself, since the model needs something loaded to start from.
42
Now let's connect this straight back to accuracy, because that's the real payoff.
43
The whole point of deferring tools is to keep the model's active toolset small and focused at any given moment.
44
Instead of staring at hundreds of options, the model typically works with just the three to five
45
relevant tools that a search
46
surfaced for the current request.
47
It's choosing from a short, sharp list rather than a sprawling crowd.
48
And here's the striking result. Because the active set stays small, selection accuracy stays high,
49
even when your total catalog runs into the thousands of tools.
50
You get the reach of an enormous library with the clarity of a tiny menu.
51
That combination is exactly what lets these systems scale without falling off the accuracy cliff we talked about.
52
Let's touch on how the search itself happens, because it comes in a couple of flavors.
53
There's a regex variant. Here, the model writes regular expression patterns to match against your
54
tool names and descriptions,
55
and those patterns cap out at 200 characters.
56
There's also a bm25 variant.
57
With that one, the model searches using natural language queries instead, and those queries can run
58
up to 500 characters.
59
Pick whichever fits how you think about matching, and it's worth knowing what actually gets searched.
60
Both variants look across tool names, descriptions, argument names, and argument descriptions.
61
So writing clear, keyword-rich descriptions genuinely helps the model find the right tool,
62
because every one of those fields is fair game for the search.
63
So when should you actually reach for tool search?
64
It earns its keep once your tool surface passes a few thresholds.
65
A good rule of thumb is 10 or more tools available.
66
Another signal is when your tool definitions start running past 10,000 tokens of context.
67
And it's almost a given when you aggregate several servers together, and suddenly you're sitting on hundreds of tools.
68
In any of those cases, tool search pays off.
69
But be honest about the flip side.
70
If you have fewer than 10 tools, or if every tool you've defined gets used on basically every turn,
71
then standard tool calling is simpler, and there's no reason to add the machinery.
72
Match the tool to the scale of the problem, not the other way around. Let's wrap up.
73
Selection accuracy degrades once you push past 30 to 50 tools,
74
and loading a full catalog up front bloats your context window.
75
Tool search fixes both by loading only the tools at request needs.
76
The defer loading flag keeps a tool out of context until the model finds it.
77
A small active set holds accuracy high across thousands of tools.
78
And that wraps up this lesson.
79
I'll see you in the next one.

--- What MCP Is and Why It Exists ---

1
When you're building AI applications, you'll constantly need to connect CLOD to outside systems,
2
including databases, APIs, and internal tools. Before MCP, every one of these connections was
3
a custom job. In this lesson, we're going to look at what the Model Context Protocol is,
4
the problem it was designed to solve, and how the client-server model works.
5
Let's start with the core vocabulary. The Model Context Protocol, or MCP, is an open standard.
6
That's important. It's not something Anthropic owns exclusively or controls.
7
Any AI model or platform that follows the spec can implement it. The server is the process that
8
hosts capabilities and exposes them over a defined transport layer. The client is whatever consumes
9
those capabilities. In most cases, that's your application or agent host, with CLOD doing the
10
reasoning over the results it gets back. These three terms form the foundation of everything
11
else in this module. Protocol, server, and client. Get them locked in before moving forward.
12
Here's the core problem MCP was built to solve. Before it existed, if you wanted CLOD to call
13
a database, you wrote custom glue code. If you wanted it to call a weather API,
14
you wrote different glue code. Every tool had its own contract, its own auth scheme,
15
its own error format. When any tool updated its API, your glue code broke. You were rebuilding
16
the same integration logic over and over for every new capability you added to your system.
17
MCP replaces all of that with one consistent interface that both sides agree to follow up
18
front before any code gets written. The before and after here is stark. Before MCP,
19
you've got a growing web of custom adapters where every new tool you add increases the complexity
20
and every change to a tool ripples through your code base in unpredictable ways.
21
After MCP, the server exposes capabilities through a standard interface and the client connects to it
22
the same way it connects to every other server. Swapping out the underlying tool no longer
23
requires touching your client code at all. That's where the compounding value comes from. Each new
24
capability you add gets cheaper to integrate, not more expensive, because you're reusing the same
25
contract every time. The architecture follows a classic client-server split, but the roles are
26
worth being precise about. The server is the provider. It hosts capabilities and waits for
27
requests. The client connects to one or more servers, negotiates capabilities during the
28
initialized handshake, then calls each server's list methods to see what's available, and routes
29
requests to the right server at the right time. One client can connect to multiple servers
30
simultaneously, so your application might have a database server, a web search server, and an
31
internal knowledge base server all running at the same time, and your client talks to all of them
32
through the same protocol. That separation keeps capability providers completely independent of the
33
consumers. One of the strongest design decisions in MCP is that it's fully model-agnostic. The
34
protocol doesn't care whether Claude, another LLM, or a local open source model is on the client
35
side. Servers don't know or care which model is consuming them. Clients don't know which vendor
36
built the server. That means you can build an MCP server for your internal tools today,
37
and it'll work with whatever AI model your team adopts next year without any rework. You're not
38
locked into Anthropic or any other vendor. That vendor-neutral composability is what makes MCP
39
worth investing in as foundational infrastructure for your entire AI application stack. When Claude
40
connects to an MCP server, it's acting as the MCP client. It opens a connection over the configured
41
transport, usually standard IO, for local servers, or streamable HTTP for remote ones, which can
42
optionally use server sent events to stream messages back. Client and server first negotiate
43
capabilities during the initialized handshake. Then Claude calls the server's list methods,
44
meaning tools list, resources list, and prompts list to learn what's available. The full set of
45
tools, resources, and prompts gets loaded into Claude's context. From there, Claude reasons over
46
those capabilities like any other information in its context window. It can decide to call a tool,
47
request a resource, or apply a prompt template based on what the user needs in that specific
48
conversation. There are a few supporting concepts worth naming here before we move on. The transport
49
layer is the communication channel, meaning the mechanism by which bytes actually move between the
50
client and server. Standard IO and streamable HTTP are the two standard transports. Server sent
51
events is an optional streaming mechanism within streamable HTTP, and the older standalone HTTP
52
plus SSE transport is now deprecated. Capability discovery is where the client calls the server's
53
list methods and receives a structured list of tools, resources, and prompts in response.
54
Capability negotiation is the initialized handshake step where client and server declare
55
which features they support, after which the server advertises what it offers. Having clean
56
definitions before you start building saves real time. In production, MCP lets teams work
57
independently without tight coupling between them. A database team ships an MCP server for their data
58
layer. A search team ships a completely separate server. The application team doesn't need to
59
understand the internals of either one. They just connect and route calls through the protocol. When
60
the database team changes their implementation or migrates to a new system, they update their server.
61
The client contract stays stable and nothing downstream breaks. This contract first approach
62
is how you scale a complex AI system across a large organization without the whole thing
63
becoming tightly coupled code that breaks every time any dependency changes. MCP replaces a mess
64
of custom adapters with a single open protocol. The server exposes capabilities, the client
65
discovers and consumes them, and neither side needs to know the details of the other.
66
One consistent contract, any compliant implementation.
67
That's the MCP architecture. That wraps up MCP discussion. I'll see you in the next one.

--- Tools, Resources, and Prompts — The Three MCP Primitives ---

1
MCP exposes capabilities through exactly three primitives, tools, resources, and prompts.
2
Once you understand each one and who controls it, you know how to model any capability on an MCP server.
3
In this lesson, we'll define each primitive, see what makes them different, and build intuition
4
for when to use each one.
5
Let's get into it.
6
Before we go further, let's pin down the language.
7
A tool is a calible function the model invokes.
8
A resource is a data object identified by URI, which the application injects into context.
9
A prompt is a reusable, parameterized template the user selects to start an interaction.
10
Notice the pattern.
11
Each primitive has a different owner.
12
That ownership distinction is the single most important thing to internalize in this lesson,
13
and it's a concept the CCA exam tests directly.
14
Model controls tools, application controls resources, user controls prompts.
15
Memorize that three-way split, and you've got the core of the entire primitives topic locked down.
16
Tools are the most active primitive.
17
When you expose a function as an MCP tool, you're giving the model the ability to do
18
something, such as run a database query, send a notification, call a calculation service,
19
or trigger any other side-affecting operation.
20
Each tool gets a JSON schema that describes its name, required inputs, types, and what it returns.
21
Claude reads that schema and uses it to understand how to invoke the tool correctly.
22
Then it reasons about whether calling the tool actually serves the user's goal in this conversation.
23
The keyword is decides.
24
The model is in control of when and whether the tool gets called.
25
That's the practical meaning of model-controlled.
26
Resources work differently from tools.
27
They're data sources that the server exposes for the model to read, files, database records,
28
configuration documents, and knowledge bases.
29
The application injects them into Claude's context before or during a conversation.
30
The server registers each resource with a URI so clients can address and request it.
31
But the decision about which resources to include in context isn't made by the model.
32
The host application makes that call based on its own logic about what data should be present.
33
So if you've got a knowledge base that should always be in context when a user asks a product
34
question, that's a resource.
35
The app loads it automatically, not because Claude asked for it.
36
The comparison between tools and resources comes down to control and dynamism.
37
Tools are model-controlled and action-oriented.
38
Claude uses them when the conversation warrants it, based on its own in-context reasoning.
39
Resources are application-controlled and data-oriented.
40
The host loads them based on its own business logic, completely independent of what the
41
model thinks it needs.
42
Here's a practical heuristic.
43
A weather lookup the model calls on demand because the user asked about the weather? That's a tool.
44
A company policy document the application always loads into context before any compliance-related conversation?
45
That's a resource.
46
When you're designing a server, just ask yourself, who makes the decision to use this, the model,
47
or the application?
48
The ownership model is worth sitting with a bit longer, because the three-way split
49
isn't arbitrary.
50
It's principled.
51
The model drives tools because it's the one reasoning about what action needs to happen
52
next in a conversation, and it has the most context for that decision.
53
The application drives resources because it has the broader system-level context to know
54
what data should be present for a given workflow or user state.
55
The user drives prompts because prompts are really about letting humans configure their
56
own starting point intentionally before an interaction begins.
57
MCP landed on this split deliberately.
58
Each ownership model maps to the party with the most relevant information to make that
59
specific decision correctly, safely, and without overstepping.
60
Prompts are the third primitive.
61
They're reusable, parameterized templates stored on the server that users can browse
62
and explicitly apply.
63
Think of them as pre-built workflows a user can launch by name.
64
The mental model, a user opens a chat interface, sees a list of prompts the connected server
65
makes available, picks one, fills in any required variables, and the interaction starts with
66
that template already applied and active.
67
The user is explicitly initiating this.
68
The model doesn't choose to apply a prompt on its own, and the application doesn't inject
69
it silently without the user knowing.
70
The human makes a deliberate, intentional selection.
71
That's the practical meaning of user-controlled for this particular primitive.
72
Each primitive has a technical backing worth knowing before you start building.
73
Tools use JSON schema.
74
That's how the model understands what inputs a tool takes, what types are required, and
75
what it returns, so it can call the tool correctly without hallucinating argument names or formats.
76
Resources use URI addressing.
77
Each data object on the server has a unique identifier the client uses to request exactly
78
the right thing.
79
Prompts use parameterized templates, named variables the user fills in at selection time
80
before the template gets applied.
81
Knowing the mechanics of each primitive helps you design cleaner servers, write better schemas,
82
and diagnose problems faster when behavior doesn't match expectations.
83
When you're designing an MCP server, the primitive choice is a real design decision, with real
84
consequences for how your server gets used and misused.
85
Use a tool for actions or computations the model should trigger during its reasoning,
86
such as search queries, calculations, and mutation operations that change state.
87
Use a resource for background data the application should load and inject before the model generates a response.
88
Use a prompt for reusable starting point workflows that make sense for users to select intentionally
89
before beginning a task.
90
The reliable shortcut is always to ask who drives this decision.
91
If it's the model, it's a tool.
92
If it's the application, it's a resource.
93
If it's the user, it's a prompt.
94
Tools, resources, and prompts give you a complete model for exposing capabilities through MCP.
95
Each has a clear owner and a clear use case.
96
Match the primitive to who drives the decision, and your servers will be intuitive to use.
97
So that's the MCP primitives framework.
98
Next, we're going to talk about building an actual MCP server in Python.
99
I'll see you there.

--- Building Your First MCP Server in Python ---

1
Before a model can call any tool through MCP, you need a server to expose that tool.
2
The MCP Python SDK gives you everything you need to build one, from the transport layer
3
to the request routing. In this lesson, we'll walk through installation, project structure,
4
server initialization, and the lifecycle hooks that keep everything running reliably.
5
Let's get the language right first. An MCP server is a process that exposes tools,
6
resources, and prompt templates to clients over a defined transport. It handles capability
7
registration, request routing, and response formatting. The MCP inspector is the official
8
MCP debugging tool that lets you explore what your server exposes and send test requests
9
without building a full client. And an MCP tool primitive is a callable function exposed by the
10
server that the model can invoke to perform actions or get computed results. Tools are
11
model controlled, meaning the AI decides when and how to call them, not the application code.
12
These concepts form the vocabulary we'll use throughout this lesson and the next.
13
Installing the MCP SDK is straightforward. A single pip install MCP command pulls in the
14
server runtime, transport adapters, and type stubs. Add it to a requirements file so your
15
server is reproducible across environments. Python 3.10 or higher is required because the SDK
16
uses async features introduced in that version. Working inside a virtual environment keeps your
17
MCP dependencies isolated from other projects on the same machine. That separation matters when
18
you're running multiple servers locally during development, each potentially needing different
19
versions of the same underlying libraries. Starting clean saves debugging time later
20
when dependency conflicts would otherwise be difficult to trace. The transport layer determines
21
how clients and servers communicate. The STDIO transport is the simplest option. The client
22
spawns the server as a sub process, writes JSON RPC messages to its stand-in, and reads responses
23
from its standout. It's perfect for local development and tools like Cloud Desktop
24
that manage server processes directly. The streamable HTTP transport runs the server as a
25
standalone process that listens on a URL client's connect over HTTP, and the server can optionally
26
use server sent events to stream messages back. Choose streamable HTTP when multiple clients need
27
to connect simultaneously or when the server runs remotely. The older standalone HTTP plus SSE
28
transport is now deprecated. The SDK supports both transports with the same tool registration API.
29
Every MCP server follows the same three-part structure. You create a server instance by
30
calling the server constructor with a name and a version string. You register tools, resources,
31
and prompts against that instance. Then you call run with the transport configuration and the
32
server blocks, processing requests until it shuts down or crashes. The SDK's run function
33
wraps a STDIO under the hood, so you don't need to manage the event loop yourself. Keep the entry
34
point file thin. Its job is to wire together the pieces and start the loop. Tool definitions and
35
handler logic belong in separate modules, so the code base stays organized as the server grows.
36
A recommended project layout separates concerns into four files. The server.pi entry point creates
37
the server instance and calls run. A tools.py module holds all tool definitions and handler
38
functions imported at startup. A requirements.txt pins MCP and any tool dependencies, so deployments
39
are reproducible across machines and environments. A readme documents the transport, available tools,
40
and any environment variables the server requires to start. This structure keeps the
41
entry point readable and makes it easy to add new tools without touching the startup logic.
42
As your server grows, you can split tools.pi into domain-specific modules without changing
43
how the server itself is initialized or started. FastMCP manages resources through a single
44
lifespan async context manager, not separate hooks. You write an async function decorated
45
with async context manager and pass it to the constructor. Everything before the yield statement
46
runs once at startup, so that's where you open database connections, load configuration, and
47
warm any caches your tools need. The yield hands control to the running server. When the server
48
shuts down, execution resumes after the yield, and a finally block is where you close connections,
49
flush buffers, and release file handles cleanly. Because it's async, you can await inside it.
50
Your handlers reach those shared resources through the lifespan context,
51
so setup and teardown live in one clear place instead of scattered callbacks.
52
When a client first connects, the server and client exchange capability lists.
53
The server declares which primitives it supports, tools, resources, or prompts. The client inspects
54
the list and decides which ones it will use. If a tool isn't registered before run is called,
55
it won't appear in the capability list and the client won't be able to call it.
56
This handshake is also where the server announces its name and version, which appears in client logs
57
and debugging tools. Understanding the negotiation sequence helps you diagnose connection issues
58
during development because a missing tool is almost always a registration ordering problem
59
rather than a deeper protocol issue. Three initialization mistakes come up repeatedly.
60
The first is registering tools after run is called. The capability handshake happens at
61
connection time, so any tool you add afterward is invisible to the client. The second is blocking
62
the event loop inside a tool handler with synchronous IO, like a file read or a database
63
query using a sync library. One blocking call stalls every other request the server is processing.
64
Use async libraries or wrap sync calls with run in executor to avoid this. The third is leaving
65
tool handlers without explicit error handling. FastMCP does catch unhandled exceptions and
66
surface them as error results, so the server keeps running. But you should still handle errors
67
yourself, raising tool error or masking details to return clean, actionable messages and avoid
68
leaking internals to the client. Here's a quick recap. Installing the SDK, choosing a transport,
69
structuring the project, and using a lifespan context manager for resource management. Those
70
are the building blocks. A well-structured server with clean startup and shutdown is the foundation
71
everything else builds on. And that wraps up this lesson. I'll see you in the next one.

--- Defining and Exposing Tools via MCP ---

1
Welcome back. Tools are how an MCP server gives a language model the ability to actually do things.
2
In this lesson, we'll go through every step of exposing a tool. Writing the decorator,
3
specifying the input schema, choosing the right return format, handling errors inside the handler,
4
and verifying the whole thing works with a server inspector before connecting to a real client.
5
Let's get into it. An MCP server is the process that handles registration and request routing.
6
An MCP tool primitive is a callable function the model can invoke. It's model controlled. The AI
7
decides when to call it and what arguments to pass based on the tool's name, description,
8
and input schema. And the MCP inspector is the debugging tool that connects to your server,
9
shows you exactly what's registered, and lets you fire test requests manually.
10
You'll use the inspector to verify that your tools are described correctly
11
and return the right output before any real client ever touches the server.
12
With FastMCP, you first create a server instance by calling FastMCP with a server name.
13
Then the at MCP tool decorator registers a Python function as an MCP tool. Place it directly above
14
the function definition. The function name becomes the tool name clients see in the capability list.
15
The doc string becomes the tool description that the model reads to decide whether and when to
16
call it. This makes the doc string one of the most important pieces of text you write for your
17
server. A vague description like gets data gives the model nothing to work with. A specific
18
description like queries the ticket system and returns open tickets filtered by assignee and
19
status tells the model exactly when the tool is appropriate. Invest time in descriptions.
20
They directly affect how reliably the model chooses and uses your tools.
21
The input schema is what tells the model which arguments to pass and in what format. The SDK
22
generates a JSON schema automatically from your Python type annotations, so you don't write raw
23
JSON yourself. Annotate parameters with standard Python types str, int, float, bool, list, and
24
dict all map cleanly. Mark optional parameters with optional and provide default values so the
25
model knows it doesn't have to include them. If you're using Pydantic, add field description
26
to each parameter to include per field documentation in the schema. The model
27
reads these descriptions when it constructs a tool call, so clear field descriptions
28
reduce the number of malformed calls you'll see during testing and in production.
29
Tools can return two kinds of output, plain text or structured data. Return a plain string
30
when the model needs to read the result and compose it into a reply. Lookup results,
31
generated content, and human readable summaries all fit the text format. Return a JSON serializable
32
dict when the output has multiple fields or when another tool in the same conversation will consume
33
it. Multi-field records, lists of objects, and paginated results all benefit from structured
34
returns. The SDK serializes dicts to JSON automatically before sending them to the
35
client. Pick the format based on how the result will be used, not on what's easier to write in
36
the handler. Three patterns cover the majority of MCP tools you'll build. The database query
37
pattern accepts filter parameters, runs a SQL query using an async database driver, and returns
38
rows as a structured list. The API wrapper pattern accepts request parameters, calls an external
39
HTTP endpoint using HTTPX or AIO HTTP, and returns the parsed response. The file system
40
access pattern accepts a path and an operation type, reads or writes using AIO files, and returns
41
the file content or a confirmation string. Each of these follows the same decorator plus handler
42
structure. What changes is the library you use for the async IO and the schema you define for the
43
inputs. Recognizing these patterns speeds up tool development considerably. To signal that a tool
44
failed, you raise an exception rather than return one. For a controlled client visible message,
45
raise tool error with the text you want the model to see. Any standard exception works too,
46
and fast MCP converts it into a tool result marked with is error set to true. This matters
47
because a returned dict becomes tool content. A successful result, not an error. So raising is how
48
you actually flag failure, and you don't have to fear an uncaught exception. Fast MCP automatically
49
catches unhandled exceptions in your handler and returns them as error results, so the server keeps
50
running and other clients stay connected. Use tool error or mask error details to control exactly what
51
the client sees when something goes wrong. The MCP inspector is the right tool for testing before
52
you connect a real client. Run it with npx at at model context protocol slash inspector and point
53
it at your server process. The inspector connects over whatever transport you configured and displays
54
the full capability list. Every tool resource and prompt your server registered. Click any tool to
55
see its schema, then fill in the input form and submit a test request. The inspector shows you the
56
raw JSON RPC request and response, so you can verify that the schema matches what you expected,
57
that the handler runs without errors, and that the return format is what the client will receive.
58
Catching schema mismatches and handler bugs here is much faster than debugging them through a model
59
interaction. How you write tool descriptions has a direct effect on how reliably the model uses your
60
server. Three rules guide good description writing. Be specific. Instead of gets tickets, say returns a
61
list of open tickets filtered by assignee and status sorted by creation date. State side effects.
62
If the tool writes data, deletes records, or sends an email, say so explicitly. The model uses this to
63
avoid triggering mutations when it's only trying to read. List limits, document rate limits, pagination
64
caps, and size constraints. The model won't guess that a result is capped at 100 rows unless you say
65
so. These three elements, specificity, side effects, and limits are what separate descriptions that work
66
from descriptions that lead to incorrect or unexpected tool calls. And that wraps it up.
67
The decorator registers the function, the type annotations generate the schema,
68
the return value format matches the use case, and the inspector verifies it all before a real
69
client connects. That wraps up the lesson. I'll see you in the next one.

--- Defining and Exposing Resources ---

1
MCP servers can expose more than tools. Resources are one of the three core MCP primitives,
2
and they give host applications a structured way to pull data from a server
3
and inject it into model context. In this lesson, we'll cover how resources are defined,
4
what URI schemes and MIME types do, and the difference between static and dynamic content.
5
Let's get into it. Let's anchor this lesson with a few definitions. An MCP resource is a URI.
6
Addressable data object that a server exposes so the host application can inject it into context.
7
The keyword there is application controlled. The host decides which resources get pulled in,
8
not the model. URI schemes are the protocol prefixes like file, db, or config that tell
9
clients how to interpret an address. And MIME types are the standard labels like text slash
10
plain or application slash json that declare the format of the content being returned.
11
Together, these three pieces form the contract between the server that owns the data and the
12
host application that decides when to use it. A resource definition has only two required fields.
13
The URI is the stable address a client uses to request that resource, and the name is a human
14
readable label that identifies it. Those two are all the spec demands. The MIME type is optional.
15
When you include it, it helps the host interpret the content it gets back, telling it whether to
16
treat the payload as plain text, json, or something binary. But the resource is still perfectly
17
retrievable without one. Behind the scenes, a content handler on the server produces or fetches
18
the data when a client calls resources slash read. So think of URI and name as the contract
19
and MIME type as helpful metadata that makes the returned content easier for the host to
20
handle correctly. The most important design decision for any resource is whether it's static
21
or dynamic. Static resources hold content that doesn't change between requests. Configuration
22
files, fixed reference tables, and policy documents are typical examples. Because they're stable,
23
they can be cached safely and don't require a live handler to run on every call. Dynamic resources
24
are the opposite. They're generated or fetched at request time, so the content can differ from one
25
call to the next. Database query results, live API responses, and user-specific data all fall
26
into this category. Caching a dynamic resource without an expiry policy is a common mistake that
27
causes models to work with stale context. Scheme design matters more than it might seem. A well-designed
28
scheme is self-describing and domain consistent. Using db colon slash slash for database records,
29
file colon slash slash for file system content, and config colon slash slash for configuration
30
snapshots makes the source of a resource obvious from the address alone. Stability is equally
31
important. If a URI changes between server versions, any client logic that cached the old address
32
breaks silently. You should also avoid embedding volatile state, like session tokens or timestamps,
33
directly into the URI path. Stable URIs let host applications build resource injection rules
34
once and rely on them across deployments without ongoing maintenance overhead. A consistent scheme
35
also makes it easier to write access control rules that apply across all resources of the same type.
36
Here's a practical problem. A single resource entry points at one fixed URI, but a server often
37
holds thousands of files or records. You can't list them all in advance. That's what resource
38
templates solve. A template exposes a parameterized URI with a named placeholder
39
written in the standard URI template syntax, so file colon slash slash slash open brace path
40
close brace stands in for any path. The client discovers these templates through a distinct call
41
resources slash templates slash list, which is separate from the plain resources slash list.
42
Once the host knows the template, it fills in the parameter, say the actual file path,
43
and then calls resources slash read on that concrete URI. So templates give you a compact
44
way to advertise a whole family of addressable resources without enumerating everyone up front.
45
There are three resource types you'll encounter most often in MCP server designs. File resources
46
use the file colon slash slash scheme and return plain text or binary content
47
with a content type of text slash plain or application slash octet stream. Database
48
records use a DB colon slash slash scheme and return query results as JSON with a content type
49
of application slash JSON. Config snapshots use config colon slash slash and return structured
50
key value content from a configuration store. Each type pairs a scheme with a predictable
51
MIME type, which makes it straightforward for host applications to route the content
52
to the right part of the model's context without inspecting the payload on every call.
53
MCP supports resource subscriptions, which solve a real problem with dynamic content.
54
Without subscriptions, the only way to keep a resource current is to pull,
55
fetch it on every request, and hope the version in context is fresh.
56
With subscriptions, a client registers interest in a specific URI after the first retrieval.
57
When the server detects that resources content has changed, it sends a notification to the
58
subscribed client. The client then re-fetches the updated content using the same URI. This pattern
59
keeps dynamic resources fresh without unnecessary polling and without flooding the server with
60
repeat read calls when nothing has changed. Exposing a resource from a server involves
61
three implementation steps. First, you declare the resource in the server's resource list at startup
62
so clients can discover it when they call resources slash list. Second, you implement
63
a read handler that accepts a URI and returns the resource content along with the appropriate MIME
64
type. Third, if the resource is dynamic and change notifications matter for your use case,
65
you implement a subscribe handler that manages client registrations. The host application
66
orchestrates all of this by calling resources slash list to see what's available and resources
67
slash read to retrieve specific content when it decides to inject it. The application-controlled
68
nature of resources is one of the things the CCA exam tests carefully. The host application decides
69
which resources to retrieve and when to inject them into context. The model doesn't invoke
70
resources the way it invokes tools. Once the host has injected resource content into the conversation,
71
the model reads it as it would any other context, but it didn't select it. This is the key
72
distinction between resources and tools. Tools are model-controlled. Resources are application-controlled.
73
The host selects. The model reads. Confusing these two control models is a common exam trap
74
and the most likely source of a wrong answer on this topic. Keep the three control points sharp.
75
User controls prompts, application controls resources, and model controls tool invocations.
76
Resources are application-controlled primitives. The host selects what gets injected and the model
77
reads what's provided. URI schemes, MIME types, and content handlers define each resource and
78
subscriptions keep dynamic content current. Coming up, MCP prompt templates and when they
79
add real value. I'll see you there.

--- MCP Prompt Templates — Design and Use Cases ---

1
Welcome back.
2
MCP prompt templates are the third
3
of the three MCP primitives alongside tools and resources.
4
They're reusable, parameterized prompt instructions
5
stored on a server that users can select
6
to pre-populate an interaction.
7
In this lesson, we'll cover how templates are defined,
8
how arguments work, and the scenarios
9
where server-side prompts add real value.
10
