Let's jump into it.
11
A handful of terms anchor this lesson.
12
An MCP prompt is one of the three MCP primitives.
13
It's a reusable, parameterized template
14
stored on a server that users select
15
to pre-populate an interaction.
16
The critical word is user-controlled.
17
A human explicitly chooses to apply a prompt template.
18
That distinguishes it from resources,
19
which are injected by the host, and tools,
20
which the model invokes.
21
Prompt arguments are the named, typed parameters
22
the client fills in before the template is rendered.
23
And prompt rendering is the server-side process
24
of substituting those argument values
25
into the template body and returning the completed prompt
26
to the requesting client.
27
Every MCP prompt template definition has three components.
28
The name is the stable identifier that clients use
29
to request the template when they call prompts slash get.
30
The arguments list declares the named parameters
31
the template expects, along with their types
32
and any default values.
33
And the template body is the actual prompt text
34
with placeholders for each argument
35
that gets substituted at render time.
36
When a client calls prompts slash get with the template name
37
and a set of filled argument values,
38
the server substitutes those values into the body
39
and returns the fully rendered prompt.
40
The client then injects that rendered prompt
41
into the conversation as the opening instruction context.
42
Understanding how prompts differ from the other two.
43
MCP primitives matters for the exam.
44
Prompts are user-controlled.
45
The human explicitly selects a template
46
from a list surfaced in the host UI
47
and the system pre-populates the interaction with it.
48
Resources are application-controlled.
49
The host decides which data objects to retrieve and inject.
50
The user doesn't necessarily know it's happening.
51
Tools are model-controlled.
52
The model decides when to invoke a tool
53
based on what it needs to accomplish.
54
Three primitives, three control points.
55
User, application, and model.
56
Keeping these distinctions clear
57
is exactly what the CCA exam tests in this area.
58
Server-side prompt templates are most valuable
59
when task instructions are complex, long,
60
or need to be consistent across many users.
61
Multi-step analysis workflows benefit enormously
62
from a structured template
63
that enforces a repeatable methodology
64
instead of relying on each user
65
to retype a long instruction set.
66
Compliance workflows are another strong use case.
67
Approved instruction sets can be stored as templates,
68
reviewed, and versioned,
69
which means users can't accidentally modify critical wording.
70
Onboarding flows for unfamiliar processes
71
are a third category.
72
A well-designed template walks a user
73
through a complex task without requiring them
74
to know the right prompting strategy ahead of time.
75
Versioning is one of the operational realities
76
of prompt template management.
77
Three strategies cover most scenarios.
78
The simplest is versioning in the name itself.
79
Append a version number like review v2.
80
So old and new templates coexist
81
as separate entries in the server's prompt list.
82
The more flexible approach is passing version
83
as a runtime argument and resolving the right template,
84
body server-side, based on the value.
85
This keeps the registration count low
86
while still supporting multiple versions simultaneously.
87
The third strategy is using a deprecation flag
88
in the server response to mark old templates as retired,
89
which allows clients to warn users
90
that the template will be removed
91
without breaking existing workflows immediately.
92
Client-side access to prompt templates
93
follows the same discovery pattern as resources.
94
A client starts by calling prompts slash list
95
to retrieve all available templates
96
along with their argument schemas.
97
The host UI surfaces these to the user as a selectable list.
98
When the user picks one,
99
the host collects the required argument values,
100
validates them against the declared types if possible,
101
and calls prompts slash get
102
with the template name and the filled argument values.
103
The server renders the final prompt
104
by substituting the values and returns the complete text.
105
The client then injects that rendered prompt
106
into the conversation.
107
The server handles all the rendering complexity.
108
The client only needs to handle
109
argument collection and injection.
110
Argument design is where prompt template quality is won or lost.
111
Templates with too many required arguments create friction.
112
Users have to fill in every field
113
before they can get started,
114
and the cognitive load reduces adoption.
115
The right approach is to keep required arguments minimal
116
and provide sensible defaults for everything optional.
117
Typed arguments are worth the extra definition effort
118
because they let clients validate input
119
before calling prompts slash get,
120
which catches errors before they reach the server.
121
Well-designed arguments make the same template applicable
122
across many different contexts and user skill levels
123
without making the template body so generic
124
that it loses its value as a standardized instruction.
125
One of the strongest arguments
126
for server-side prompt templates
127
is what they do for consistency and maintainability.
128
When every user who selects a template
129
gets the same rendered instruction set,
130
the variance in model outputs drops significantly.
131
Compare that to a workflow
132
where each user writes their own version of the same prompt.
133
You get different levels of detail,
134
different structures, and different results.
135
Maintenance is equally strong.
136
Update the template body on the server once,
137
and every client picks up the change
138
without any client-side code changes.
139
And for compliance-sensitive workflows,
140
server-side templates can be formally reviewed,
141

142
before they're ever surfaced to users.
143
MCP prompt templates give teams
144

145
and make them reusable across every user
146
who accesses the server.
147
Defined with a name, typed arguments,
148

149
A human selects them explicitly.
150
And versioning strategies keep evolving templates manageable.

And that's resource and prompts as MCP primitives.

--- Implementing MCP Clients and Connecting to Servers ---

1
Welcome back. The question this lesson answers, how does an AI application actually talk to MCP
2
tool servers? The answer is through an MCP client, the software layer that manages connections,
3
runs the capability handshake, invokes tools, and routes across multiple servers. Let's walk
4
through how each piece works in this lesson. An MCP client is the mediator between the
5
language model and every tool server in your system. When the model decides it needs a tool,
6
it doesn't reach out to the server directly. It asks the client. The client knows which servers
7
are connected, what tools each one exposes, and how to route the request. In practice,
8
the client is often embedded inside an agent framework or an application like Claw Desktop.
9
The key exam point is that the client is software, not the end user. It's responsible for the full
10
communication layer, which means opening connections, keeping them alive, and tearing
11
them down cleanly when work is done. So what does an MCP client actually do at runtime?
12
It manages the connection lifecycle from start to finish. It opens a transport channel to each
13
server, sends an initialize request to kick off the protocol handshake, then keeps the connection
14
alive while the agent is running. When the session ends, it closes the transport cleanly.
15
Between open and close, it also caches the list of tools and resources each server reported during
16
negotiation, so it can make fast routing decisions later without re-querying. That
17
mediation role is what separates MCP from raw API calls. The client handles all the coordination,
18
so your application code doesn't have to. In Python, the MCP SDK gives you a high-level
19
client class that works with both transport types. For a local server, you create stdio.
20
Server parameters, pointing at the server script, pass it to the client and call initialize.
21
The initialized call does the protocol handshake. It sends your client's supported protocol version,
22
receives the server's version and full capability list, and sends back an initialized notification
23
to confirm everything is aligned. After that single call, your client is fully ready. The
24
SDK handles all the low-level JSON framing and message serialization, so you're working with
25
clean Python objects rather than raw byte streams. That abstraction keeps your application logic
26
simple, even as the underlying protocol handles complex negotiation. Every connection goes through
27
three phases, connect, negotiate, and close. In the connect phase, the transport opens and the
28
client sends the initialized request with its protocol version. In the negotiate phase, the
29
server replies with its version and its full capability list, covering which tools it has,
30
which resources, and which prompts. The client confirms receipt with an initialized notification.
31
In the close phase, there's no protocol shutdown message. Termination happens at the transport
32
layer instead. With stdio, the client closes the server's standard input, which triggers a
33
shutdown signal to the child process. With streamable HTTP, you simply close the connection.
34
Getting this right matters because closing abruptly can leave server processes hanging,
35
especially with stdio transport, where the server runs as a child process.
36
Right after the handshake, the client runs a discovery pass. It calls list tools to get tool
37
names, input schemas, and descriptions. It can also call list resources for data resources and
38
list prompts for reusable prompt templates. These results get cached for the rest of the session.
39
Discovery is important because the model uses tool descriptions to decide which tool to call.
40
If the description is vague or missing, the model will make poor routing decisions.
41
It's also where you catch mismatches early. If the server doesn't expose the tool you expected,
42
you know immediately, rather than getting a runtime error later.
43
Discovery isn't always a one-and-done step. A server's set of tools and resources can change
44
while a session is still running, and if the client keeps working from its original cached list,
45
it'll miss new capabilities or keep calling ones that went away.
46
MCP handles this with list-changed notifications, but there's a catch worth knowing. It's capability
47
gated. During initialization, a server declares a list-changed capability. Only then does it
48
promise to send a notification whenever its tool or resource list changes. When the client receives
49
that notification, it simply reruns list tools or list resources to refresh what it has cached.
50
If a server never declares that capability, the client won't get any updates, and its catalog
51
can go stale without anyone noticing. So don't assume freshness. Confirm the capability is
52
actually there. The choice between STDIO and Streamable HTTP Transport comes down to deployment
53
context. STDIO spawns the server as a sub-process on the same machine and communicates over stand-in
54
and stand-out, with no network needed, very simple to set up, and a great fit for local
55
tools and developer workstations. Streamable HTTP runs the server as an HTTP service and supports
56
both standard request-response cycles and streaming via server-sent events. It's designed
57
for remote servers, shared infrastructure, and production deployments where multiple clients
58
connect simultaneously. The CCA exam regularly asks you to match a transport type to a described
59
scenario, so keep that local versus remote distinction sharp in your mind going into the test.
60
Invoking a tool is straightforward once you're connected. You call callTool with the tool's name
61
and a dictionary of arguments. The client serializes the arguments to JSON, sends the request to the
62
correct server, and returns a structured result object with content blocks, text, images, or
63
resource references. Now, error handling has two distinct paths, and mixing them up is a common bug.
64
When the tool itself runs but fails, the result comes back with an ISERROR flag set to true,
65
so you check that flag before forwarding the content to the model. But protocol-level problems,
66
like an unknown tool name, bad arguments, or a closed connection, raise an MCPERROR exception
67
instead. Those you catch with a try accept block, so handle both. Check ISERROR for tool failures
68
and catch exceptions for protocol errors. One of the most powerful MCP client features
69
is multi-server support. A single client instance can hold open connections to several servers
70
at once. Each server gets a unique name when you register it, and the client uses that name plus
71
the tool's name to route calls correctly. If two servers happen to expose a tool with the same name,
72
namespace prefixes prevent collisions. This is how large agent systems work in practice.
73
One server might handle file operations, another handles web search, another handles database
74
queries, and the client orchestrates all of them transparently. The model just picks a tool,
75
the client figures out which server to call. MCP clients are the connective tissue of any
76
tool-enabled agent system. Initialize, handshake, discover, invoke, route across multiple servers.
77
That's the full lifecycle. Next up, transport mechanisms, starting with STDIO for local
78
servers. These pieces fit together into a coherent design. I'll see you in the next lesson.

--- STDIO Transport — Local Servers and Process Communication ---

1
When you're choosing how an MCP client talks to a server,
2
STDIO is one of your two primary options.
3
It spawns the server as a subprocess and
4
exchanges messages through stand-in and stand-out.
5
Let me walk through how that works,
6
when it's the right choice,
7
and why Cloud Code uses STDIO natively.
8
Before the mechanics, let's get the language right.
9
STDIO transport is the MCP transport mechanism
10
where the client starts the server as a child process
11
and routes all messages through stand-in and stand-out.
12
A child process is a process
13
that a parent process spawns and manages.
14
In this model, the client is the parent
15
and the server is the child.
16
Standard streams are the default IO channels
17
that every process gets from the operating system.
18
Stand-in for input, stand-out for output,
19
and STDRR for errors.
20
STDIO transport uses stand-in and stand-out
21
as a bidirectional pipe.
22
These streams are already available to every process,
23
which is exactly why this transport
24
requires zero network configuration.
25
Here's how STDIO transport
26
actually works at the process level.
27
When the client starts, it reads its configuration
28
to find the command that launches each registered MCP server.
29
It then spawns those servers as child processes
30
using the operating system's
31
standard process creation facilities.
32
Once the child is running,
33
the client and server exchange
34
newline-delimited JSON messages.
35
Client writes to the child's stand-in.
36
Server reads from its own stand-in
37
and writes responses to stand-out.
38
Client reads from the child's stand-out.
39
That's the entire communication model.
40
There's no socket, no port, no HTTP layer.
41
The OS process table and pipe infrastructure
42
handle everything.
43
This is fast, simple, and completely self-contained
44
on a single machine.
45
The contrast between STDIO and network-based transports
46
is sharper than it looks.
47
STDIO uses OS-level pipes, not the network stack.
48
There's no port to bind, no firewall rule to configure,
49
no TLS certificate to manage.
50
It's inherently single-user
51
because the process is private to the client that spawned it.
52
Network transports do the opposite.
53
The server binds to a port, listens for connections,
54
and handles authentication and encryption
55
to safely serve multiple clients.
56
Network transport is more complex to deploy and operate,
57
but it's the only option when the client and server
58
aren't on the same machine.
59
STDIO is simple precisely because it gives up everything
60
that network transport offers.
61
STDIO fits best in three situations.
62
Local development is the obvious one.
63
When you're building and testing an MCP server,
64
running it as a sub-process on your dev machine
65
is the fastest iteration loop.
66
Desktop tool integrations are another.
67
When a desktop application bundles its own MCP server,
68
STDIO is the natural transport
69
because both components are always on the same machine.
70
And then there's Cloud Code.
71
Cloud Code supports STDIO for local MCP servers,
72
which makes it a clean illustration
73
of the local process transport model.
74
For remote servers, Cloud Code uses HTTP or SSE.
75
What STDIO can't do is equally important.
76
The moment a use case requires access from a different host
77
or more than one simultaneous user session,
78
STDIO is the wrong transport.
79
Three characteristics define STDIO deployments.
80
Zero configuration means there's nothing to set up
81
on the network side.
82
The OS handles the pipe.
83
Single user scope means one server process
84
per client instance.
85
There's no concept of sharing.
86
And sub-process lifecycle means the server starts
87
when the client spawns it and exits when the client exits.
88
These three properties work together.
89
Because each client owns its process
90
and that process lives only for the session,
91
there's no shared state between clients,
92
no port conflicts, and no cleanup problem
93
when a client disconnects.
94
The simplicity is a feature when you're operating
95
in the right context, but each of these properties
96
is also a constraint.
97
State management in STDIO is straightforward.
98
Because one server process is tied to one client session,
99
the server can keep state and memory
100
across all tool calls within that session.
101
You don't need to serialize state
102
to a database between calls
103
because the process stays alive.
104
A tool call made early in the session
105
can produce a result that a later call reads from memory.
106
When the client exits, the process exits,
107
and all in-memory state is released.
108
There's no risk of stale session state
109
leaking to another user
110
because each user gets their own process.
111
This model simplifies server implementation significantly,
112
but it means STDIO can't serve a shared cache
113
or shared resource across multiple clients.
114
Cloud Code's local server support
115
is worth looking at in detail
116
because it's a clean illustration
117
of the local process transport model.
118
Cloud Code supports STDIO for local MCP servers,
119
and it uses HTTP or SSE for remote ones.
120
For a local server,
121
you register it in Cloud Code's settings file
122
with a command field that specifies how to launch it.
123
When a session starts,
124
Cloud Code reads that configuration
125
and spawns each local server as a child process.
126
Tool calls from the language model route
127
through the stand-in slash stand-out pipe
128
for the relevant server process.
129
From the server's perspective,
130
it's just reading from stand-in and writing to stand-out.
131
This design means you can add a local MCP server
132
by providing a single command
133
with no network setup at all.
134
STDIO has three limitations
135
that are important for the exam.
136
No remote access.
137
Because the server is a sub-process,
138
it's physically inaccessible from any other host.
139
There's no address to connect to.
140
No multi-user sharing.
141
Each user gets their own process.
142
Sharing a single STDIO server across clients
143
isn't architecturally possible,
144
and STDIO isn't appropriate for production API deployments.
145
If you're building a server that multiple users
146
or multiple applications need to reach over a network,
147
STDIO isn't the right transport.
148
These aren't bugs.
149
They're design choices that make STDIO simpler,
150
but they do define a clear ceiling on where STDIO applies.
151
Pulling this together,
152
STDIO spawns the server as a child process
153
and routes all communication through stand-in and stand-out.
154
No network configuration needed.
155
State is held in memory per process
156
and stays consistent across the session.
157
Cloud Code uses it for local servers.
158
Right choice for local, single-user scenarios.
159
And that wraps up this lesson.
160
I'll catch you in the next one.

--- StreamableHTTP Transport — Remote Servers and Production Deployment ---

1
Streamable HTTP is what happens when STDIO falls short. It turns an MCP server into a network
2
accessible HTTP service, uses server sent events for streaming, and supports both stateless and
3
session-based state. Here's what you need to know to pick the right transport for any deployment
4
context. Start with the vocabulary. Streamable HTTP transport is the MCP transport that runs
5
the server as an HTTP service, supporting both standard request response patterns and streaming
6
via server sent events. It's the current standard for remote and production MCP deployments.
7
Server sent events, or SSE, is a unidirectional HTTP streaming protocol where the server pushes
8
a sequence of events to the client over a long-lived connection. Stateless session is the
9
HTTP interaction model where each request carries all the context it needs, so the server doesn't
10
need to store per client state between requests. Streamable HTTP supports both stateless and
11
session-based models, which we'll look at in detail. Streamable HTTP works by turning the MCP
12
server into a standard HTTP service. The server binds to a port and exposes a single endpoint.
13
To make a tool call, the client sends one HTTP post containing the MCP message. Here's the key
14
detail. The server decides how to respond. It can reply with a plain JSON body, or it can upgrade
15
that same response to a server sent events stream, pushing progress notifications first and then the
16
final result. So the server initiates streaming, not the client. Separately, the client also opens
17
a long-lived HTTP GET connection, which gives the server a channel to push its own notifications
18
and requests to the client. One endpoint, both directions covered. Server sent events is the
19
streaming mechanism inside streamable HTTP, and it's worth understanding how it works at the
20
protocol level. The client sends an HTTP request that stays open. Instead of sending a single
21
response and closing the connection, the server keeps writing event frames to the response stream
22
as they become available. Each event frame follows the SSE text format, a data field containing the
23
event payload terminated by a blank line. The client reads these frames as they arrive. This
24
is unidirectional. The server pushes, the client receives. The client can't send messages over the
25
same connection. For MCP, SSE is used to stream progress notifications back to the client while
26
a long-running tool operation is still executing on the server. Streamable HTTP lets you choose
27
between two state management approaches, and the choice has real scalability implications.
28
Stateless HTTP means each request is completely self-contained. The client sends all the context
29
it needs in the request body, and the server returns a result without storing anything between requests.
30
Because no per-client state exists on the server, any server instance in a load-balanced
31
pool can handle any request. This scales horizontally with no coordination overhead.
32
Session-based state means the client sends a session token, and the server maintains a context
33
store keyed to that token. This supports multi-turn interactions where the server needs to remember
34
what happened in earlier calls. It works, but it requires sticky sessions or a shared state backend
35
to function correctly behind a load balancer. Three scenarios point clearly to streamable HTTP
36
as the right choice. Remote clients. If the client and server are on different machines,
37
you need an HTTP-based transport. There's no alternative because STDIO can't cross a network.
38
Multi-user deployments. A single streamable HTTP server can handle concurrent connections from
39
multiple clients. There's no need to spawn a dedicated process per user. Production APIs.
40
HTTP gives you the entire production infrastructure stack. TLS for encryption, bearer tokens or API
41
keys for authentication, and load balancers for traffic distribution. These are things you'd have
42
to build a completely separate layer for if you were using STDIO, and none of them would work
43
remotely anyway. There's a naming point worth flagging here. In the older 2024-11-05 version of
44
the MCP specification, there was a separate transport called HTTP plus SSE. That has changed.
45
Current MCP consolidates streaming under the streamable HTTP transport, and the old HTTP plus
46
SSE transport is deprecated. So SSE today is the streaming delivery mechanism inside streamable
47
HTTP, not a standalone peer-to-it. If you see the deprecated HTTP plus SSE name presented as a
48
current separate option, the correct framing is that it's been folded into streamable HTTP.
49
The transport that supports streaming in current MCP is streamable HTTP full stop.
50
When you deploy an MCP server over streamable HTTP in production, you take on standard HTTP
51
service responsibilities that don't exist for STDIO. TLS is the first requirement. If the server
52
is reachable over any untrusted network, all traffic should be encrypted. Authentication is
53
the second. Every client request should carry a credential, either a bearer token, an API key,
54
or an OAuth token that the server validates before processing the MCP message.
55
Load balancing is the third. A production server handling concurrent clients should be able to
56
distribute load across multiple instances. All of this is standard infrastructure for any HTTP
57
service, but it's a meaningful operational step up from the zero configuration model that STDIO
58
provides. Let's make the transport selection decision concrete. Use STDIO when the server
59
and client are on the same machine, when you're serving exactly one user at a time, and when you
60
want zero network configuration. Local development tools, desktop applications that bundle their own
61
MCP server, and cloud code integrations all fit this pattern. Use streamable HTTP when the client
62
needs to reach the server over a network, when multiple users or multiple client applications
63
need to share the same server, or when you need production infrastructure like TLS and authentication.
64
The decision rule is simple. If the deployment crosses a network boundary or serves more than
65
one user, STDIO is eliminated from consideration. Streamable HTTP runs an MCP server as an HTTP
66
service. Request responsible plus SSE streaming is one transport. Stateless or session-based,
67
your choice. SSE is a component inside streamable HTTP, not a separate transport. Transport choice
68
reduces to one question. Is this local and single user, or is it not? That's transport selection,
69
and that wraps up this lesson. I'll see you in the next one.

--- Letting Servers Make LLM Calls Through the Client ---

1
Welcome back.
2
Here's what we're tackling in this one.
3
Sampling, one of MCP's most advanced features.
4
It lets the server ask the client
5
to make an LLM inference call and send the result back.
6
We'll cover why this design choice keeps control
7
where it belongs and how to implement a sampling handler
8
in your own MCP client.
9
Let's get into it.
10
Before we dig into the mechanics,
11
let's get the key terms straight.
12
Sampling in MCP is the pattern where a server
13
sends a create message request to the client.
14
The client calls an LLM on its behalf,
15
and the result comes back through the MCP channel.
16
A sampling handler is the callback
17
you register in the client to make that happen.
18
It receives the request, calls your LLM API,
19
and returns the model's response.
20
The human-in-the-loop option adds an approval gate
21
so a user can review and accept or reject the sampling request
22
before the model is ever called.
23
These three concepts work together
24
to give servers intelligence without giving them
25
direct model access.
26
Why does sampling exist at all?
27
The answer is about separating concerns cleanly.
28
A well-designed MCP server is stateless and focused
29
on its domain, covering file operations, database queries, and web search.
30
It shouldn't need to hold its own LLM API credentials
31
or worry about which model to use.
32
The client already has that relationship.
33
It has credentials, it knows the user's preferred model,
34
and it enforces policy about what data can go to the model.
35
Sampling reuses all of that existing infrastructure.
36
Instead of every server independently managing
37
model access, which multiplies credential sprawl and policy
38
gaps, sampling centralizes that control.
39
The client becomes the single control point
40
for all model calls in the system.
41
That's cleaner architecturally and much easier to audit.
42
Let's walk through the sampling flow step by step
43
so it's clear what happens at each stage.
44
First, the server decides it needs LLM assistance
45
and sends a create message request
46
to the client over the standard MCP channel.
47
That request includes the messages it wants processed,
48
an optional system prompt, and any model preference hints.
49
Second, the client receives the request.
50
If human in the loop is configured,
51
it pauses and shows the user the pending request for approval.
52
Third, the client calls the API once approved or immediately
53
if no approval gate is set.
54
Fourth, the model's response comes back
55
to the client, which packages it as a standard MCP result
56
and returns it to the server.
57
The server then uses that result just
58
like any other tool response.
59
One of the most important things to understand about sampling
60
is the distinction between what the server controls
61
and what the client controls.
62
The server owns the message content.
63
It decides what question to ask, what context to include,
64
and what system prompt to use.
65
It can also express model preferences.
66
But the client owns the actual model selection,
67
the credentials, and the data policies.
68
This division is intentional.
69
It means a server operator can't unilaterally
70
choose an expensive model or route sensitive data
71
to an unapproved endpoint.
72
The client owner, meaning the person deploying the agent,
73
always has the final say.
74
That separation is what makes sampling safe to enable
75
in production systems.
76
Implementing a sampling handler in Python
77
is straightforward with the MCP SDK.
78
You define an async function that accepts a create message
79
request object, which contains the messages list,
80
the optional system prompt, and the model preferences.
81
Inside that function, you call your LLM API.
82
In most cases, that means the Anthropic API.
83
Using the message content from the sampling request,
84
you then wrap the model's text response
85
in a create message result and return it.
86
You register this function on the client
87
before connecting to any servers.
88
And the SDK automatically routes incoming create message requests to it.
89
The handler is the only code you need to write.
90
The SDK handles all the serialization and protocol
91
routing around it.
92
To make the difference concrete, compare sampling
93
to giving a server its own API key.
94
With direct access, the server calls the LLM independently,
95
using its own credentials.
96
You lose visibility into those calls.
97
You can't enforce model selection policy.
98
And credentials multiply across every server you deploy.
99
With sampling, all of that disappears.
100
The server never touches an API key.
101
Every inference request flows through the client,
102
which means every call is visible, logged,
103
and subject to the same policies.
104
If you want to swap models, you change it in one place.
105
If you want to audit what's being sent to the LLM,
106
you have a single choke point.
107
Sampling is simply the more secure and maintainable design
108
for any non-trivial deployment.
109
When a server sends a create message request,
110
it can include model preference hints.
111
These aren't hard requirements.
112
They're suggestions.
113
The intelligence level hint indicates
114
how capable the model needs to be.
115
Low for simple extraction, high for complex reasoning.
116
The speed level hint signals urgency.
117
The server can also include a prioritized list
118
of model names it would prefer.
119
The key constraint is that the client is always
120
free to honor, substitute, or ignore these hints entirely.
121
If the user's policy says only one model is approved,
122
that's what gets used regardless of server preferences.
123
Hints are a way for servers to communicate requirements
124
without demanding control, which keeps
125
the client firmly in charge.
126
Human-in-the-loop sampling is a practical safety feature,
127
especially during development or when
128
servers are requesting access to sensitive content.
129
When it's enabled, the client doesn't immediately
130
forward the sampling request to the LLM.
131
Instead, it surfaces the full request
132
so the user can review and approve it,
133
including the messages the server wants
134
to send to the model.
135
The user reviews it and either approves, edits, or rejects it.
136
Only approved requests move forward.
137
This means a misconfigured or malicious server
138
can't silently pump sensitive data into a model call
139
without the user noticing.
140
For production systems handling regulated data,
141
human-in-the-loop can also serve as a compliance checkpoint,
142
giving you an auditable record of every approved model interaction.
143
Sampling is a clean solution to a real architectural problem.
144
How do you give servers intelligence without giving
145
them direct model access?
146
You don't route model access to servers directly.
147
You route everything through the client,
148
which already has model access and keeps the policy controls.
149
So that's sampling and server-side intelligence.
150
Coming up, notifications, routes, and progress reporting,
151
three more advanced MCP features that
152
help servers communicate state and access files safely.
153
I'll see you there.

--- Notifications, Roots, and Progress Reporting ---

1
If you're building MCP servers that run in production, log notifications, progress notifications,
2
and routes are the three features that matter most. They let servers communicate status during
3
long-running operations and give servers controlled, scoped access to the client file system
4
without opening up a security loophole. Let's go through each one in this lesson.
5
Let's get the vocabulary straight before we go further. A progress notification is an
6
MCP notification a server sends during a long-running tool call to report incremental status.
7
It carries a numeric progress value and an optional total, so the client can compute a
8
percentage for the user. A log notification carries diagnostic text with a severity level
9
such as info, warning, or error, and an optional logger name during execution. And routes are the
10
list of file system paths or URIs that the client advises a server to work within.
11
Routes communicate the intended file system scope, but they are advisory. The protocol doesn't
12
enforce them. All three features extend the core request response pattern to support richer,
13
safer server behavior in real deployments. The standard MCP tool call model is synchronous.
14
The client sends a request and waits for the result. For tools that finish in milliseconds,
15
that's completely fine. But for tools that process large files, run database queries,
16
or make external API calls, the client can be waiting for seconds or even minutes with no
17
feedback. That silence creates a bad user experience and makes it hard to diagnose
18
problems when something goes wrong. Notifications break that silence. The server sends status
19
updates as the operation progresses, and the client can display them, log them, or surface
20
them to the user in real time. Think of notifications as the MCP equivalent of a
21
progress bar. They transform an opaque weight into an observable process. Log notifications
22
are the simpler of the two notification types. They carry a severity level, a logger name,
23
and a message string. These are the eight standard syslog severity levels. Debug, info, notice,
24
warning, error, critical, alert, and emergency, an increasing order of urgency. The server sends
25
these whenever it wants to surface diagnostic information to the client. The client can
26
display them in a log pane, filter by severity threshold, store them for later review, or ignore
27
them entirely. Log notifications are fire and forget. They don't expect a response, so a server
28
can emit them during any operation without blocking. You call a single context method,
29
and the MCP framework handles the rest, especially valuable during development
30
when you want step-by-step visibility into what a tool is doing. Progress notifications have a
31
slightly more structured format because they're designed to drive UI elements, like progress bars.
32
Every progress notification includes a progress token, an opaque identifier from the original
33
tool call request. That token ties the update to its parent operation, so the client knows
34
which tool call is reporting. The progress field is a numeric value, indicating how far along the
35
operation is. The total field is optional. Include it, and clients can divide progress by total to
36
get a percentage. Leave it out, and the client shows an indeterminate indicator. The convention
37
is to call report progress at meaningful checkpoints after each major step completes,
38
not on every minor iteration. That way, the client always has a current view without being
39
flooded with updates. Wiring progress notifications into a tool is straightforward. When the client
40
sends a tool call request, it can include a progress token in the meta field. Inside your
41
handler, you check for that token. If it's present, you call ctx.report progress at each meaningful
42
step. The MCP server framework serializes the notification and sends it to the client
43
automatically. You don't manage the transport yourself. One important detail, if the client
44
didn't include a progress token, it means the client doesn't want progress updates for this call.
45
Skip the report progress calls. Clients that don't support progress notifications won't send tokens,
46
and your tool degrades gracefully with no special handling required. It's worth comparing log and
47
progress notifications directly, because developers sometimes reach for the wrong one. Log notifications
48
are for diagnostic text, meaning messages that tell you what the server is doing and whether
49
it's healthy. They're tagged with severity so they can be filtered and stored. Progress notifications
50
are for numeric status tied to a specific operation. They're designed to drive a progress
51
indicator in the user interface. You wouldn't use a log notification to update a progress bar,
52
and you wouldn't use a progress notification to surface an error message. In a production server,
53
you'll typically use both. Log notifications for observability and debugging. Progress notifications
54
for user-facing feedback during long operations. Roots are how MCP communicates file system scope.
55
Here's the part people get backwards. It's the client, not the server, that declares the roots
56
capability during initialization. Once it has, the server can request the list of roots by calling
57
roots slash list, and each root is a URI pointing to a specific directory or file. Those roots are
58
advisory. They tell a well-behaved server which paths it should confine its operations to,
59
but the protocol itself doesn't stop a server from reaching outside them. So roots communicate intent,
60
they don't enforce it. Real enforcement has to come from somewhere else, like operating system
61
file permissions, a sandbox around the server, or the client validating paths before it acts on a
62
result. So think of the roots model as scope communication, not a security wall. The client
63
declares the roots, and the client owner, the person deploying the agent, decides what scope
64
to advise. That matters most in multi-server deployments where different servers come from
65
different vendors. You don't want a third-party tool server quietly reading files outside its
66
expected scope, but roots alone won't stop it. Because roots are advisory, the actual protection
67
has to be layered underneath. That means running the server with restricted operating system
68
permissions, isolating it in a sandbox or container, or having the client validate every
69
path a server touches against the declared roots before acting. Roots make the intended scope
70
explicit and auditable. The enforcement is your job. Notifications and roots round out the advanced
71
MCP feature set alongside sampling. Log notifications give you observability. Progress
72
notifications give users feedback during long operations. And roots give servers exactly the
73
file system access they need and nothing more. Together with sampling, these features form the
74
production-ready layer of MCP design. That wraps up the discussion of MCP design here.
75
I'll see you in the next lesson.

--- Tool Boundaries, Access Scoping, and Least Privilege ---

1
Every tool you define on an MCP server is a trust boundary, meaning a set of permissions the model
2
can exercise on behalf of the user. That framing changes how you approach tool design. Let me walk
3
you through the least privilege principle, access scoping mechanisms, and input sanitization at the
4
server boundary. Access scoping means defining explicit boundaries on what data, systems,
5
and operations each MCP server and its tools may reach. It's enforced through schema constraints,
6
server-side authorization, and operating system permissions. Roots are a softer related signal.
7
A client can advertise which directories it wants a server to focus on, but a root is advisory,
8
not a security boundary. So real enforcement still lives on the server and the host.
9
Least privilege means each tool gets only the permissions its function requires and nothing
10
more. And input sanitization means validating and cleaning every argument that arrives at the
11
server boundary before it reaches any downstream system. Together these practices form the
12
foundation of a secure MCP tool design. Why should you think of a tool definition as a trust boundary?
13
Because the schema and handler together define the full scope of what the model can do
14
when it invokes that tool. The schema sets the expected argument types. The handler determines
15
which systems the tool connects to and what operations it can perform. If you define a tool
16
loosely, accepting freeform inputs and connecting to everything, you create a large and poorly
17
controlled attack surface. You've effectively handed the model, and anyone who can influence
18
its outputs, a very broad set of permissions. Treating every tool as a boundary forces you to
19
ask during design exactly what should this tool be allowed to do and what should it explicitly not do.
20
That question, answered honestly for every tool, is how you build an MCP.
21
Applying least privilege to MCP tools starts with a simple rule. One tool per logical operation.
22
A read-only search tool should have no write permissions at all. A tool scoped to one database
23
should have credentials that only reach that database. A file tool should be bound to specific
24
directory paths rather than having access to the full file system. When you give each tool the
25
minimum access it needs for its purpose, you shrink the blast radius of any misuse. If the
26
model is manipulated into calling a tool in an unexpected way, or if the MCP server itself is
27
compromised, narrow scope limits how much damage can result. Least privilege isn't just a security
28
checkbox. It's a design discipline that makes the system easier to audit and reason about over time.
29
MCP gives you three concrete mechanisms to enforce access scoping. Schema constraints are the first
30
layer. Declare a strict enum for an argument and the server rejects inputs outside that set before
31
your handler logic ever runs. Server-side authorization is the second. Even after the
32
schema validates the input, your handler checks the caller's identity and permissions before
33
touching any downstream resource. Operating system permissions are the third. File system
34
and process limits on the host cap what the server can actually reach. It's worth clearing up roots
35
here because people mistake them for enforcement. Roots are an advisory signal. A client advertises
36
the directories it wants a server to focus on, and a well-behaved server should honor them,
37
but nothing forces it to. Real enforcement lives in those three layers. The contrast between broad
38
and narrow tool scope illustrates why this matters. A tool that accepts any SQL string
39
and connects to any database gives the model complete data access. An attacker who can
40
influence the model's outputs through prompt injection, for example, can potentially redirect
41
tool calls to extract, modify, or delete arbitrary data through that one tool. A narrow design
42
replaces that single permissive tool with multiple specialized ones. One for reading a specific table
43
with schema-constrained filter arguments, another for a different table with its own constraints.
44
Each tool connects with credentials scoped to its purpose. If any single tool is abused,
45
the damage is contained to what that tool can reach, which by design is very little.
46
Input sanitization happens at the server boundary, and it should happen before any
47
handler logic runs. Every argument arriving at an MCP server should be treated as untrusted input,
48
regardless of where the client claims it came from. The first step is type and format validation
49
against the declared schema. If the schema says an argument is an integer, reject anything that isn't.
50
The next step is sanitizing or escaping characters that have special meaning in the downstream
51
system. SQL wildcards, shell metacharacters, path traversal sequences. Length limits matter too.
52
An unbounded string argument can cause resource exhaustion or expose buffer overflow risks in
53
downstream code. Server-side sanitization is the last defense before your tool handler reaches
54
production systems. Designing tools with least privilege means making access decisions before
55
you write a single line of handler code. Define one tool per logical operation, not one tool per domain.
56
Use enum constraints wherever possible so the model can only select from a valid, pre-approved
57
set of values. Filter the results your tool returns to include only what the caller is
58
actually authorized to see. Don't return raw database rows and hope the host application
59
filters them. Tools designed this way are narrow, auditable, and safe by default. Tools designed
60
without this discipline may work correctly in normal use, but they're brittle under adversarial
61
conditions and hard to secure after the fact. A few scoping mistakes come up repeatedly in
62
MCP server designs. The first is overly permissive schemas. Using freeform string
63
arguments where an enum would work allows the model to craft arbitrary downstream queries.
64
The second is shared credentials. When all tools on a server share one set of backend credentials,
65
any tool's compromise exposes every system those credentials can reach.
66
The third is missing output filtering. Returning raw query results without filtering can expose
67
records the calling user has no right to see. Each of these is a design decision that feels
68
harmless in a development environment, but creates significant exposure in production.
69
Fixing them early during tool design is much cheaper than auditing and remediating them later.
70
Let's recap the lesson. Least privilege and access scoping make MCP tools safe to deploy
71
alongside real data. Schema constraints, server-side authorization, and operating
72
system permission each enforce access, while client roots stay advisory. Sanitize inputs
73
at the server boundary as well. And that wraps up this lesson. I'll see you in the next one.

--- MCP in Production — Authentication, Versioning, and Reliability ---

1
Welcome back. When you're deploying an MCP server to production, development assumptions break fast.
2
Development servers trust the local process. Production servers need answers to harder
3
questions. Who is calling? Are they authorized? How do you handle multiple client versions?
4
And what happens under load? We'll work through authentication, versioning, rate limiting,
5
and monitoring. Let's jump into it. A handful of terms anchor this lesson.
6
MCP authentication is the mechanism by which a client proves its identity to a remote MCP server
7
before accessing tools and resources. For remote streamable HTTP servers, MCP defines an optional
8
OAuth 2.1 based authorization flow as the recommended mechanism. Simpler options like
9
API keys still work, but OAuth is the standardized path the spec points you toward. Semantic
10
versioning uses major, minor, and patch numbers to signal how much a change breaks existing
11
consumers. A major bump means breaking changes. Minor means new features that are backward
12
compatible. Patch means bug fixes. And rate limiting is capping how many requests a client
13
or user can send per unit time, protecting shared infrastructure from overload and preventing one
14
client from crowding out all the others. A local MCP server running on a developer's machine can
15
trust the process that called it. There's no network boundary to cross. A remote production
16
server is completely different. Any client that can reach the endpoint over the network
17
could try to invoke its tools. Without authentication, there's no way to know who is
18
calling, no stable identity to attach to audit logs, and no foundation for authorization decisions.
19
You can't limit which tools a client can invoke if you don't know who the client is.
20
Authentication is the prerequisite for everything else in a secure production deployment.
21
It enables authorization, meaningful logging, and per-client rate limiting. A production MCP
22
server without authentication is essentially a public API for every tool it exposes.
23
MCP's specification defines an optional OAuth 2.1 authorization flow as the recommended mechanism
24
for remote streamable HTTP servers. The flow works as you'd expect. The client goes through
25
an OAuth authorization flow with an identity provider to obtain an access token. That token
26
is included with each request to the MCP server. The server validates the token and reads the
27
scopes it carries. Crucially, those scopes can map directly to which tools the client is allowed
28
to invoke. A token with a read-only scope should only be able to call read tools. This design keeps
29
the MCP server stateless. It doesn't manage sessions or store credentials itself. The identity
30
provider handles that complexity, and the MCP server just validates tokens on every request.
31
OAuth 2.1 is the answer to reach for when the question involves authenticating clients to a
32
remote streamable HTTP MCP server, but it's useful to understand the alternatives and why they fall
33
short for this use case. API keys are simple shared secrets passed in request headers. They're
34
easy to implement, but provide no per-user identity, no scope granularity, and no standard
35
revocation flow. A shared key can't tell you which user made a call. Mutual TLS or MTLS authenticates
36
both client and server using certificates. It's strong for service-to-service scenarios, but adds
37
certificate management overhead that most MCP deployments don't need. OAuth handles the human-in-
38
the-loop case where an actual user needs to authorize a client to access tools on their behalf.
39
Before we leave authentication, there's a set of transport-level security rules.
40
The MCP specification is unusually firm about for streamable HTTP servers, and they're easy to miss.
41
First, the server is required to validate the origin header on every incoming connection.
42
This is what stops a DNS rebinding attack, where a malicious web page tricks a browser into talking
43
to your server. Second, when the server runs locally, it should bind only to the loopback
44
address 127.0.0.1, rather than to all network interfaces. Binding to everything exposes a
45
local server to the whole network. Third, the server should authenticate its connections.
46
Notice the strength here. Validating origin is mandatory in the spec. The other two are strong
47
recommendations. Skip them, and a remote site could reach in and drive a server meant to stay local.
48
Versioning MCP servers is about managing change without breaking clients that depend on tools
49
that already exist. The rule is straightforward. Backward-compatible changes don't require a
50
version bump that breaks clients. Adding a new optional tool, adding optional fields to an
51
existing schema, or fixing a bug in a handler, while clients that haven't updated continue working
52
exactly as before. Breaking changes are different. Removing a tool, renaming it, or changing required
53
argument names or types will break any client that calls that tool. These need a major version
54
bump, a migration window where both old and new versions are served simultaneously, and explicit
55
deprecation notices before the old version is retired. The pattern is the same as any other API.
56
Communicate changes early and give clients time to adapt. Rate limiting is how a shared MCP server
57
stays healthy when multiple clients are hitting it simultaneously. Without limits, a single client
58
running a heavy batch job can saturate the server's resources and make it unresponsive for everyone
59
else. The baseline practice is a per client cap on requests per time window. Beyond that, expensive
60
operations need their own tighter limits. A tool that makes external API calls or runs database
61
queries has a different cost profile than a tool that does a simple lookup. When a client hits a
62
limit, the server should return an HTTP 429 response with a retry after header so the client
63
knows when it can try again. Rate limits make infrastructure costs predictable and protect all
64
clients equally. Monitoring a production MCP server means treating it like any other API service in
65
your stack. Every tool invocation should generate a log entry that captures the caller's identity,
66
a sanitized summary of the arguments, the result status, and the end-to-end latency. With that data,
67
you can track error rates by tool and detect when a downstream dependency starts degrading.
68
You can set alerts when error rates or P99 latency breach thresholds you define. You can review access
69
patterns to spot unexpected usage or potential misuse. Visibility into which tools are being
70
called, by whom, and at what frequency is also directly useful for capacity planning.
71
Without this observability, you're operating a production service blind.
72
Three reliability practices every production MCP server needs. First, a dedicated health endpoint
73
that load balancers and uptime monitors can poll without invoking real tools. This lets
74
infrastructure verify the server is responsive without creating side effects in the tools
75
themselves. Second, graceful degradation when a downstream dependency is unavailable. Return a
76
structured error result instead of crashing or hanging so clients can retry or fall back without
77
a hard failure. Third, hard timeouts on tool handler execution so a slow downstream system
78
can't hold a connection open indefinitely and exhaust the server's connection pool. These
79
three practices cover the most common production failure modes, health checks, graceful errors,
80
and timeouts. Deploying MCP to production means applying the same discipline you'd bring to any
81
API service. An OAuth 2.1 flow handles authentication. Semantic versioning with
82
migration windows protects existing clients. Rate limiting keeps shared servers stable under load.
83
Logging and alerting give you the visibility to catch problems before a user does.
84
And that's MCP security and production operations. I'll see you in the next lesson.

--- The Built-in Toolset — What Ships with Claude ---

1
In this lesson, we're going through the built-in tools that ship with the Cloud API.
2
We'll cover what each tool does, where its boundaries are, and how the cost and latency
3
profile compares to rolling your own custom tooling. If you're designing a Cloud-powered
4
system, this is the foundation you need. Let's dive in. Let's anchor three terms before we go
5
further. A built-in tool is one that Anthropic has natively wired into the Cloud API. You enable it
6
through a typed parameter rather than writing a schema yourself. That's the key distinction.
7
Web Search is the first built-in most architects reach for. It runs a constrained query,
8
surfaces ranked snippets, and attaches source citations as structured metadata you can use
9
downstream. Computer Use is the other big one, and it's an Anthropic schema client tool.
10
It gives Cloud screenshot capability, mouse control, and keyboard input, but your own
11
application controls the agent loop and runs it against a desktop environment you supply.
12
There's no Anthropic-enforced sandbox here. What it can reach is bounded by whatever
13
environment you connect it to, so isolating it in a VM or container is a developer responsibility.
14
The built-in catalog splits into two groups, and the split matters more than any tool count.
15
Server tools like Web Search and Code Execution run on Anthropic's own infrastructure. You get
16
the results back directly with no handler code to write. Client tools like Computer Use, Bash,
17
and Text Editor use an Anthropic-published schema, but your application actually executes them.
18
Cloud returns a tool use block, and your code runs it. What both groups share is that you
19
skip schema authoring. Anthropic has already done the prompt engineering and routing optimization,
20
but client tools still need your handler code, and they run wherever your application runs.
21
That distinction is the whole game when we get to security boundaries.
22
For now, just hold on to the idea that where a tool runs decides what it can reach.
23
The built-in versus custom distinction comes up constantly in API design conversations,
24
so let's be precise about it. A built-in tool is defined and maintained by Anthropic.
25
You get vetted prompts, optimized routing, and no schema work. A custom tool is something you
26
define yourself through the standard tool use schema, typically backed by an MCP server or an
27
in-process function. The trade-off is straightforward. Built-ins are fast to
28
adopt and reliable for common tasks, but they have fixed scope. Custom tools give you full
29
control over behavior, schema, and integration, but you carry the maintenance burden. The capability
30
gap concept captures this tension. When what a built-in can do falls short of what your workflow
31
actually needs, that gap is your signal to go custom. Web search has a few behavior details
32
that trip people up. The model constructs the query internally based on the user's request
33
and context. You don't pass a query string directly. The number of searches per request
34
is capped by an optional max uses parameter, and each search counts as one use no matter how many
35
results return. That's your lever for bounding cost. Rate limits are a separate concern. If
36
Claude exceeds them, the response still comes back successfully, but it carries a web search
37
tool result error object with an error code of too many requests rather than silently returning
38
fewer results. Every result comes with source citation metadata, so web search isn't a raw
39
search wrapper. It's an opinionated tool with its own query logic. If you need precise query control,
40
build a custom tool around the search service you want. Computer use is the most powerful built-in
41
and also the one most misunderstood. It's a client tool, which means your application controls the
42
agent loop and runs it against a desktop environment you supply. Screenshots capture
43
visible screen state in that environment. Mouse control handles clicks, drags, and scrolls there.
44
Keyboard input sends text and keystrokes to that same session. Here's the key point architects get
45
wrong. There is no Anthropic-enforced sandbox. What computer use can reach is bounded by whatever
46
environment you connect it to and nothing more. If you point it at a machine with access to your
47
host operating system or your private network, it can reach those. So isolation is your responsibility.
48
Anthropic recommends running it inside a VM or container and giving it only the access the task
49
genuinely needs. Code execution gives Cloud a place to run code and the key detail is that the
50
container persists. It lives for up to 30 days after creation and you can reuse it across requests
51
by passing its container ID back in. That means files and state you create can carry forward,
52
not vanish between calls. The environment ships a fixed set of pre-installed libraries like pandas,
53
numpy, and matplotlib. You work with what's there because the container has no internet access.
54
Outbound network requests are fully disabled for security so you can't pull down new packages at
55
runtime. Newer tool versions add in-session state persistence on top of the file persistence.
56
For system design, the takeaway is that code execution can hold state when you reuse the
57
container but plan around its fixed library set and its complete lack of network access.
58
File operations are worth clearing up because there's a common misconception here.
59
The Cloud API doesn't ship a built-in glob or grep file tool. What it gives you is the text
60
editor client tool which handles viewing, creating, and doing string replacements in files,
61
and the bash client tool for running shell commands. Both are anthropic schema client tools
62
so your own code executes them and file discovery like listing or searching happens through bash.
63
Because you run them, the boundary is whatever your environment exposes.
64
The important comparison is to a custom file MCP server. A custom server gives you full control
65
over access rules, logging, and error handling. The client tools are faster to enable but give
66
you less control. If you need fine-grained audit trails on every file operation,
67
build the custom server. The capability gap concept applies directly here.
68
Cost and latency are real factors in built-in tool selection, and architects often underestimate how
69
much they vary. Web search adds a full round trip for each query. If your workflow issues multiple
70
searches per user request, that latency compounds. Computer use is computationally expensive per
71
screenshot cycle because every capture requires rendering, encoding, and transmission. Code
72
execution carries a cold start overhead every time the container spins up, which matters in
73
latency-sensitive applications. Custom tools backed by fast internal services can actually
74
outperform built-ins on latency when the internal service is well optimized. The right design choice
75
depends on your latency budget. For a background research task, web search latency is probably
76
acceptable. For a real-time user interaction, you should measure before committing to a built-in.
77
Cloud's built-in tools give you real capability with zero schema work, but they split into server
78
tools that run on Anthropx infrastructure and client tools that run in yours. Each carries
79
its own cost and latency profile that you need to understand. That covers the built-in tool set,
80
and wraps up this lesson. I'll see you in the next one.

--- Choosing Built-in vs Custom — Boundaries and Tradeoffs ---

1
Welcome back. This lesson is about making the right choice between built-in and custom tools.
2
We'll go through the conditions that favor each approach, how a hybrid pattern fits them together,
3
the security boundaries you need to understand, and two specific pitfalls that trip up architects
4
that are new to the cloud tool ecosystem. Three terms anchor this lesson's decision framework.
5
Capability gap is the most important. It describes the distance between what a built-in tool can do
6
and what your workflow actually needs. When that gap is small, reach for the built-in. When it's
7
large, build custom. The hybrid pattern is what most production systems end up using.
8
Built-ins handle general purpose work like public research and code transformation.
9
A custom MCP server handles proprietary data, internal APIs, and regulated records. The third
10
is a naming best practice worth adopting early. Give your custom tools distinct, service-prefixed
11
names so selection stays unambiguous. Duplicate or invalid tool definitions are generally rejected
12
when you make the request, so distinct names keep things clean even when the functionality overlaps
13
a built-in. Three conditions favor built-in tools, and when all three hold, you should default to
14
them. First, quick prototyping. Built-ins require no schema work, so you can get a working prototype
15
in front of stakeholders without a full tool definition. Second, well-fitted scope. If web
16
search does exactly what your research workflow needs, building a custom search wrapper adds
17
complexity with no benefit. Third, no internal or regulated data flowing through the tool.
18
Built-ins run inside Anthropx infrastructure. If your workflow handles data that shouldn't leave
19
your controlled environment, a built-in isn't the right path. But when the task is public-facing,
20
the scope fits, and speed matters, built-ins are the faster and more maintainable choice.
21
Four conditions signal that you need a custom tool rather than a built-in. Domain-specific
22
schemas are the first signal. Built-ins use Anthropx's fixed schema. If your workflow
23
needs custom field names, specific types, or domain validation, you need to define the schema yourself.
24
Internal APIs are the second signal. Built-in tools can't reach services behind your private
25
network boundary. If Claude needs to query your internal database or call a service that isn't
26
publicly accessible, a custom MCP server is the only path. Regulated data is the third signal.
27
Data that has to stay in your controlled environment shouldn't flow through Anthropx
28
infrastructure. Audit trail requirements are the fourth. Built-ins don't expose per-call logs.
29
If you need to record every tool invocation for compliance, custom tools give you that control.
30
Let's make those custom tool requirements concrete with four specific scenarios.
31
If your workflow involves healthcare records, financial transactions, or any data covered by
32
a regulatory framework, those records shouldn't go through a tool you don't control. That rules
33
out built-ins for that portion of the workflow. If your company has internal APIs that serve product
34
catalog data, customer records, or operational metrics, Claude needs a custom tool to reach them
35
because built-ins are sandboxed away from your private network. If your security team
36
needs a log of every tool call, the parameters passed, and the response returned, built-ins
37
don't give you that. A custom MCP server does. And if your domain has a schema that doesn't map
38
cleanly to what a built-in expects, define your own. The hybrid pattern is the architecture most
39
mature Claude deployments converge on. The idea is to let built-in tools handle the general purpose
40
work, and let custom MCP servers handle the proprietary or regulated data layer.
41
Web search handles public research. Code execution handles data transformation on inputs.
42
Claude already has. A custom MCP server handles the internal database queries,
43
the regulated record lookups, and the domain-specific API calls. The two layers don't
44
interfere with each other as long as you use distinct tool names. The main design consideration
45
is routing. You need your system prompt to be clear about which tool Claude should reach for
46
in each situation. Ambiguous instructions lead to the model guessing, and guessing means
47
inconsistent behavior in production. The security boundary depends on whether a tool is a server
48
tool or a client tool, and this is where people get it backwards. Server tools like web search
49
and code execution run on Anthropx infrastructure. That environment has no path to your private
50
network and no access to secrets stored in your systems. Their blast radius is bounded by design.
51
Client tools are the opposite story. Bash, text editor, and computer run in your own environment
52
because your application executes them. They can and do reach whatever private resources you expose
53
to them, so the rule isn't that built-ins are always sandboxed. Match each tool to the trust
54
boundary you intend. Server tools stay outside your walls. Client tools run inside them, which is
55
why you scope what they can touch and never hand them more access than the task needs. Naming is a
56
small decision that pays off across a whole tool set, so it's worth being deliberate about. The best
57
practice is to give every custom tool a distinct service-prefixed name. Something like crm
58
underscore lookup or billing underscore refund tells you in the model which service owns the
59
tool at a glance. This keeps selection unambiguous even when a custom tool's functionality overlaps
60
a built-in. You don't have to worry about a silent collision routing to the wrong implementation
61
because duplicate or invalid tool definitions are generally rejected when you make the request,
62
not something that fails quietly at runtime. So the guidance is straightforward. Name for clarity,
63
prefix by service, and let distinct names do the disambiguation work up front.
64
It's one of the cheapest ways to keep a growing tool set predictable. Built-in tools evolve.
65
Anthropic can change query construction logic, update result formats, adjust rate limits,
66
or modify the behavior of any built-in between model releases. You shouldn't assume that because
67
a tool name is constant, the behavior is constant. The way to get stable behavior is to pin it through
68
your system prompt instructions. Instead of relying on web search to always format citations
69
a certain way, instruct the model explicitly on what format you expect and how to handle deviations.
70
Test built-in tool output in a staging environment after each model update.
71
Treat built-in tools the way you'd treat any third-party dependency,
72
version aware, tested on updates, and not assumed to be stable without verification.
73
That mindset prevents subtle regressions from appearing in production after a model rollout.
74
The built-in versus custom decision comes down to four factors. Capability gaps,
75
data sensitivity, latency budget, and audit requirements. When built-ins fit the scope
76
and the data is public, reach for them. When they don't fit, build a custom tool or adopt
77
the hybrid pattern. And always use distinct tool names to avoid the override pitfall.
78
That framework covers the decisions you'll face designing cloud-powered systems.

--- What Claude Code Is and How It Works ---

1
It might seem late in the course for this lesson,
2
but now you have the foundation to really talk deeply about
3
Cloud Code and that is the focus of this one.
4
Specifically, what kind of tool it actually is,
5
how it differs from IDE plugins,
6
how the planning loop runs,
7
how the context window fills up,
8
and what happens when it does,
9
as well as what the permissions model controls.
10
Understanding the architecture is what makes
11
the rest of the course really make sense.
12
Before we go further, let's pin down the language.
13
Cloud Code is Anthropx Agentix CLI.
14
It runs in your terminal and executes
15
multi-step tasks autonomously using a loop of planning,
16
tool selection, execution, and observation.
17
The planning loop is that core cycle.
18
Cloud reasons about what to do next,
19
picks a tool, runs it,
20
reads the result, then decides what to do next.
21
It keeps looping until the task is complete.
22
And the permission model is the layered control system
23
that determines which tools and actions
24
Cloud Code is actually allowed to perform.
25
It draws on user, project, local, and managed settings,
26
with managed settings taking precedence
27
and deny rules overriding allow rules.
28
Permissions are enforced before any tool executes.
29
These three concepts are the foundation
30
everything else builds on.
31
The most important framing to lock in early.
32
Cloud Code isn't a chat interface
33
and it's not an autocomplete tool.
34
It's an autonomous agent.
35
When you give it a task, it doesn't respond
36
with a suggestion for you to apply.
37
It executes, it reads files, runs shell commands,
38
edits code, and searches the web.
39
A single instruction can trigger dozens of tool calls
40
before it's done.
41
The practical implication is that Cloud Code
42
can do a lot of damage if pointed at the wrong thing
43
with the wrong permissions.
44
It can also complete a lot of real work
45
with no handholding.
46
The autonomous execution model is what makes it powerful
47
and what makes the permission model
48
worth understanding carefully.
49
IDE plugins like Copilot work
50
by watching your keystrokes
51
and offering inline suggestions
52
that you can accept or reject.
53
Cloud Code doesn't do that.
54
It receives a goal,
55
reasons about what steps are needed to achieve it,
56
and takes action autonomously across the entire code base.
57
Plugins have no shell access.
58
Cloud Code can run arbitrary bash commands,
59
read any file it has permission to read,
60
and make changes across multiple files in a single task.
61
The mental model is plugins assist one action at a time,
62
Cloud Code plans and executes multi-step work independently.
63
That distinction matters because it changes
64
how you think about what to ask for
65
and what guardrails to put in place.
66
The planning loop runs in four phases.
67
First, Cloud Plans.
68
It reasons about the current state of the task
69
and decides what the next action should be.
70
Second, it makes a tool call,
71
invoking the specific tool
72
with the parameters it determined.
73
Third, it observes the result from that tool call
74
and updates its understanding of the current state.
75
Fourth, it loops back to planning
76
and decides the next step.
77
This cycle repeats until the task is complete
78
or Cloud hits a decision point
79
where it needs clarification.
80
The loop is what makes Cloud Code agentic.
81
It's not waiting for instructions between steps.
82
It's continuously planning and acting
83
until the goal is reached.
84
It helps to contrast the agentic loop
85
with a standard single-shot LLM call.
86
A single-shot call is you send input,
87
you get a response, you're done.
88
No follow-up actions,
89
no ability to observe results and adjust.
90
That model works fine for generating text
91
or answering a question.
92
It breaks down the moment a task requires intermediate steps
93
where each step's output shapes what comes next.
94
The agentic loop handles that.
95
Cloud plans the first step, executes it,
96
reads what happened, and adjusts.
97
If a tool call produces an unexpected result,
98
the loop lets Cloud self-correct before proceeding.
99
That's the core difference in capability
100
between a chat interface
101
and an agentic execution environment.
102
The context window is the finite amount of text
103
Cloud Code can hold in a session.
104
Everything competes for that space,
105
the conversation history,
106
the results from every tool call
107
and any Cloud.md files that were loaded.
108
Verbose Cloud.md files directly reduce
109
how much space is left for code and tool output.
110
When the window fills up,
111
earlier content gets compacted or dropped.
112
The practical design implication
113
is to keep Cloud.md files focused and concise.
114
Every word you write there is a word
115
that can't be used for code context or tool results.
116
This is a real constraint, not a theoretical one,
117
and it shapes how you should structure
118
project configuration.
119
Cloud Code doesn't automatically carry
120
your full conversation history into a new session.
121
What does carry forward is anything written down.
122
Cloud.md files load automatically
123
and bring project context with them
124
because Cloud Code reads them from disk
125
at the start of every session,
126
not because they live in session memory.
127
Where auto-memory is enabled,
128
saved notes carry forward too.
129
And any explicit summary you write out
130
to a file before ending the session
131
is there when you come back.
132
Without those, a new session has no automatic recall
133
of the prior chat.
134
The design implication is simple.
135
Don't rely on Cloud Code to remember
136
loose conversation from a prior session.
137
Write state explicitly, commit configuration,
138
and structure your Cloud.md to carry what matters forward.
139
The permission model controls what Cloud Code
140
is allowed to do.
141
Permissions come from settings at several levels,
142
user, project, local, and managed enterprise settings.
143
When those levels conflict, managed settings win,
144
and a deny rule always overrides an allow rule.
145
By default, certain actions require explicit approval
146
before they run.
147
Now, hooks and permissions are two separate gates,
148
and the order matters.
149
A pre-tool use hook runs first
150
before the permission system is consulted.
151
So a hook gets a chance to deny an action
152
before permissions are even checked.
153
Both are pre-execution gates,
154
sitting in front of the tool call rather than after it.
155
That's the mental model to carry.
156
Pre-tool use hook, then the permission request,
157
then the tool runs.
158
Getting both right is what makes Cloud Code safe
159
to run in automated workflows,
160
where it won't be supervised step by step.
161
Let's recap this one.
162
It's an agentic CLI that plans, acts,
163
observes, and loops autonomously.
164
The context window is finite,
165
and everything in a session consumes it.
166
New sessions don't auto-recall prior chats.
167
Permissions control what's allowed before any tool can run.
