168
That wraps up this lesson.
169
I'll see you in the next one.

--- The Tool System — Reads, Bash, Edits, and Web Search ---

1
This lesson covers the Claude Code tool system, the core built-in tools you'll use the most,
2
what each one does, when Claude picks one tool over another, and why Bash is the most powerful
3
and most dangerous tool in the entire set. The tool system is how Claude Code actually
4
does things, so understanding these tools is foundational for everything that follows.
5
There's a small vocabulary that frames how the tool system works, so let's lock it in.
6
The tool system is the set of built-in capabilities Claude Code can invoke,
7
and it includes Read, Write, Edit, Bash, Glob, Grep, WebSearch, WebFetch, and Agent.
8
Each tool call is a discrete action that Claude selects based on what the current step requires.
9
Edit vs. Write is a critical distinction. Edit makes targeted find and replace changes to an
10
existing file, while Write overwrites the entire file. Edit is preferred for most code changes
11
because it's scoped and safe. Write is for new files or full rewrites, and the Agent tool
12
delegates a subtask to a sub-agent with its own separate context window, used when the work is
13
too large or too isolated to run inside the current session. Know these three cold before the exam.
14
The core tools split into natural groups. Read, Write, and Edit handle file operations.
15
Read lets Claude view a file's contents without touching anything. Write creates or completely
16
overwrites a file. Edit makes targeted changes inside an existing file. Bash executes shell
17
commands. Glob and Grep search for files and text. WebSearch and WebFetch reach the web.
18
Agent spawns a sub-agent with its own context window. Together, these tools are what make
19
Claude code capable of multi-step, autonomous work. Every action Claude takes in a session
20
flows through one of these tools. They're the building blocks of every workflow. Understanding
21
what each one can and can't do is what lets you design workflows that are both capable
22
and appropriately safe. Read is the safest tool in the set. It views the contents of a file without
23
making any modifications. One clarification worth locking in. Read operates on files,
24
not directories. If Claude needs a directory listing, it uses bash with a command like ls.
25
If it needs to find files by name or by content, it uses glob or grep. Read itself returns a file's
26
contents, including rendered images, pdfs, and notebooks. Claude typically runs read before
27
edit to confirm the current state of a file before changing it. That read before edit pattern
28
prevents a common class of errors where Claude acts on stale assumptions about what a file
29
contains. It has no modification risk by design, so reading the relevant files before modifying
30
them is standard practice. Write and edit are both file modification tools, but they behave
31
very differently. Write overwrites the entire file. Every byte of existing content is replaced
32
with the new content. If you use write on an existing file and leave out 10 lines, those 10
33
lines are gone. There's no partial update. Edit works differently. It takes a target string and
34
a replacement string and performs a scoped find and replace on the file. Only the matched section
35
changes. Everything else stays intact. For most code changes, edit is the right tool. Use write
36
for creating new files or for intentional full rewrites where you want to replace everything.
37
Conflating them is an exam trap worth knowing. Bash is the most capable tool and the most
38
dangerous one. It executes any shell command, running scripts, installing packages, moving or
39
deleting files, starting processes, querying databases, calling APIs. There's no built-in
40
constraint on what it can affect. A poorly scoped bash command can delete files, corrupt state,
41
or make irreversible changes to the system with no undo path. The permission model and hooks are
42
the primary controls. Without them, bash has wide reach across the system. Understanding this is
43
essential for designing Cloud Code workflows that run in automated or production environments
44
where a bad bash call could cause serious damage. Web search lets Cloud query the web for information
45
that goes beyond what it knows from training. Current documentation, recent API changes,
46
error messages from new library versions, release notes. These are all things that web search can
47
retrieve that Cloud's training data might not cover. Results come back as text that Cloud reads
48
and reasons about before acting. Cloud selects web search when it determines that the task
49
requires information it can't reliably know from training. It's not always invoked, only when the
50
current task has a knowledge gap that a web query can fill. Web search extends Cloud Code from a
51
local code-based tool to one that can pull in current external information. The agent tool
52
spawns a sub-agent with its own separate context window. The parent session delegates a sub-task
53
to the sub-agent, which runs independently and returns a result. The main use cases are tasks
54
too large to fit in the current session's context window, and tasks that are isolated enough to run
55
in parallel with other work. Agent is the tool that turns Cloud Code into a multi-agent orchestrator.
56
The parent coordinates the sub-agents do the isolated work. Because each sub-agent has its own
57
context window, you can handle much larger tasks by decomposing them into parallel sub-tasks
58
and delegating each one to a separate agent. Tool selection in Cloud Code is task-driven.
59
Cloud doesn't pick tools based on capability alone. It picks the tool that matches what the
60
current step requires. Inspecting existing state before acting. Read. Changing specific lines in a
61
known file. Edit. Creating a new file or completely rewriting one. Write. Running a command, script, or
62
system operation. Bash. Looking up current information that training data won't have.
63
Web search. Delegating an isolated sub-task to its own context. Agent. The planning loop runs this
64
selection decision at every step. Getting tool selection right is what makes a multi-step
65
workflow execute coherently, rather than reaching for the wrong capability at the wrong time.
66
That wraps it up for the tool system. Read inspects safely. Edit targets specific changes.
67
Write replaces entire files. And bash is powerful and dangerous. Web search reaches the current web.
68
Agent delegates to sub-agents with their own context windows. Tool selection is task-driven
69
at each step of the planning loop. That wraps up this lesson. I'll see you in the next one.

--- Starting a Project — Setup, Onboarding Claude to a Codebase ---

1
When you're setting up Cloud Code on a real project, how you onboard it matters.
2
We'll look at how it discovers project structure, what the slash init command generates,
3
why the output needs review before team adoption,
4
and what to verify before running Cloud Code on real production code. Let's jump in.
5
Cloud Code is Anthropx's official agentic CLI. It's not a chat interface, and it's not
6
autocomplete. It operates as a loop, planning, tool use, and response, until the task is complete.
7
That distinction matters on the CCAF exam. The slash init command is a built-in slash
8
command that analyzes the current repo, and auto-generates a starter, clod.md.
9
Important framing, it generates a starting point, not a final configuration. And clod.md is the
10
markdown file that provides persistent instructions for a project. It's loaded at session start,
11
and it counts against the context window. Brevity and precision matter because verbose.
12
clod.md files consume space that would otherwise go to code and tool output.
13
Starting Cloud Code is straightforward. You run the clod command from the project root directory.
14
The root matters because it determines the default scope of what Cloud Code can see.
15
In a new project, it starts with minimal context and reads whatever files already exist.
16
In an existing project, it picks up any clod.md files at startup,
17
then reads the file tree and other files on demand as tasks require. There's no automatic
18
full directory scan at startup, and you don't need to manually list files or describe the structure
19
before you begin. But the quality of what Cloud Code finds depends entirely on what's in the
20
project. A well-structured repo with a clear readme and accurate configs gives Cloud Code
21
much more to work with than a repo that has none of those things. The starting directory is the
22
boundary. Cloud Code actively reads the project to orient itself rather than waiting for you to
23
describe it. It reads file trees to understand directory layout and how modules are organized.
24
It reads readme files for project purpose, setup instructions, and usage conventions.
25
It reads config files like package.json for Node projects, pyproject.toml for Python,
26
and .env examples for expected environment variables. This automatic discovery is useful,
27
but it has limits. If the project has an outdated readme, incorrect configs, or no documentation at
28
all, Cloud Code will still try to orient based on what's there, but it gets a less accurate
29
picture. The value of good project documentation compounds when Cloud Code is in the loop.
30
The slash init command is the fastest way to give Cloud Code standing context on an unfamiliar code
31
base. You run it once inside the project, and it analyzes the repo, file structure,
32
programming languages, frameworks, and likely entry points. From that analysis,
33
it auto-generates a starter, clod.md. The generated file includes the project name,
34
key files, and directories, the detected technology stack, and suggested behavioral conventions based
35
on patterns it found in the code. The core benefit is that you're not starting from a blank
36
clod.md and trying to remember everything worth documenting. The slash init draft captures the
37
structure automatically and gives you something concrete to edit rather than something to create
38
from scratch. Let's look at the four things slash init generates. First, the project name,
39
detected from the repo name, package config, or the readme header. Second, key files, entry points,
40
main config files, and primary source directories that Cloud Code should know about. Third,
41
detected languages and frameworks based on file extensions and dependency manifests.
42
If your project uses Python and FastAPI, slash init will typically detect both. Fourth, suggested
43
rules, initial coding conventions, and behavioral guidelines inferred from what it found.
44
These might include test patterns, import conventions, or formatting preferences.
45
All four of these are starting points derived from automated analysis. They're useful scaffolding,
46
but the team needs to verify each section before the file is used in practice.
47
The gap between slash init output and a finished clod.md is worth being explicit about. The slash
48
init draft is broad and generic. It captures what's detectable from file analysis, but it can't infer
49
team conventions that aren't written in the code, security constraints specific to the environment,
50
or project rules that live only in team knowledge. The reviewed version adds those things and removes
51
anything the autoscan got wrong or flagged as important when it isn't. The other critical
52
difference is precision. Every line in clod.md costs context window space. A verbose draft that
53
includes filler reduces the space available for actual code and tool output during sessions.
54
The goal of refinement is a file that's accurate, concise, and genuinely useful rather than
55
comprehensive for its own sake. Refining slash init output is a required step, not optional
56
polish. Verify that the detected frameworks and languages match the actual stack. Auto-detection
57
can misread dependency files, especially in polyglot repos. Add explicit rules that the scan
58
couldn't infer. Your branching policy, test coverage requirements, security sensitive file
59
paths. Remove noise slash init. Sometimes flags files that aren't actually important to day-to-day
60
work or includes generic boilerplate that adds length without adding clarity. The review step
61
is also where you add anything domain specific that the codebase doesn't make obvious. A clod.md
62
that ships as the raw slash init output is a liability because it may contain inaccuracies
63
and will almost certainly miss critical context. Before running clod code on a real codebase,
64
three checks prevent the most common failures. First, clod.md exists and is accurate, meaning
65
not the raw slash init draft but a reviewed version that reflects the actual project.
66
Second, permissions deny rules are configured in your settings dot json file. A deny rule on read
67
of your dot and v files, secrets, and large binaries stops clod code from ever reading
68
them into context so sensitive paths aren't exposed and the window isn't wasted. Third,
69
entry points, test commands, and build steps are documented in clod.md so clod code knows how to
70
interact with the project programmatically. These aren't bureaucratic check boxes. Each one directly
71
prevents a real failure mode, bad context, exposed credentials, or an agent that can't run the project
72
it's working on. Let's pull this together. Run clod from the project root. Let it discover
73
structure automatically. Use slash init to scaffold a starter clod.md file and always review and
74
refine that output before team adoption. Complete the checklist before first use.
75
That wraps up this lesson. I'll see you in the next one.

--- Adding and Managing Context Effectively ---

1
Context quality is what determines whether Cloud Code's output is useful or generic.
2
We'll look at the ways context gets into a session, what to include and what to exclude,
3
how deny rules in your settings file exclude files, why token efficiency matters,
4
and how Cloud MD functions as standing context rather than a one-time briefing. Let's jump in.
5
A handful of terms shape every context decision in this lesson.
6
Permissions deny rules are entries in your settings .json file
7
that block Cloud Code from reading specified files and directories.
8
You write them as read of a path, like read of your .env file,
9
and they serve two distinct purposes,
10
keeping credentials out of context as a security control
11
and preventing large or irrelevant files from consuming context window space as a performance control.
12
Know both dimensions.
13
The context window is the finite text space available in a Cloud Code session.
14
Cloud.md content, tool call results, and code all count against it.
15
When it fills, earlier content is compacted or dropped.
16
And session continuity.
17
The context window starts fresh each session,
18
but knowledge does persist across them through Cloud.md,
19
which loads automatically, and through auto-memory.
20
Context can enter a Cloud Code session through four main mechanisms.
21
File mentions by path are direct.
22
You reference a file, Cloud Code reads it, and it becomes part of the active context.
23
At sign references let you point to specific files, directories, or MCP resources inline in a prompt.
24
Pasting URLs causes Cloud Code to fetch and read that content as part of the session.
25
And inline instructions are plain text guidance.
26
You type directly in the prompt, which is the lightest weight method.
27
Each approach serves a different use case, but they all share one property.
28
They add to the session's token budget.
29
The more context you load, the less room remains for the actual work, code generation, tool calls,
30
and response output.
31
Useful context changes what Cloud Code would do if it didn't have it.
32
Architecture decisions are a clear example.
33
If the code base uses a specific pattern for dependency injection or async handling,
34
Cloud Code needs to know that to produce consistent code rather than guessing from file structure alone.
35
Coding conventions shape output quality directly.
36
Naming rules, formatting requirements, and patterns, the team enforces,
37
prevent Cloud Code from generating technically correct code that fails review.
38
Test requirements set expectations for coverage and framework usage.
39
Known constraints belong in Cloud.md, so the model never has to rediscover them.
40
Rate limits, compatibility floors, and security-sensitive paths.
41
Include these because their absence leads to work that has to be redone.
42
Four categories should be excluded with deny rules in your settings file.
43
Auto-generated files like build artifacts and compiled outputs add bulk
44
without contributing anything useful to Cloud Code's understanding.
45
Large binaries consume context window space in ways that are especially costly relative to what they contribute.
46
Images, compiled assets, and serialized data files.
47
Secrets and credentials are a security issue, not just an efficiency issue.
48
Loading a .env file or a private key config into Cloud Code context
49
means that content appears in the session history and potentially in tool call outputs.
50
Vendored libraries, unrelated packages, and documentation builds for external tools
51
should also stay out because they dilute the signal in the context window
52
without helping Cloud Code make better decisions.
53
Deny rules live in your settings..json file.
54
You write each one as read of a path, and glob patterns and wildcards work the way you'd expect,
55
so there's minimal learning curve.
56
Your deny list should cover three categories at minimum.
57
Secrets and credentials.
58
Read of your .env files.
59
Private key directories.
60
Any auth config that should never appear in session context. Large binaries.
61
Image directories.
62
Compiled output folders.
63
Vendor directories with third-party code.
64
And irrelevant paths.
65
CI cache directories.
66
Test fixture archives.
67
Documentation build outputs.
68
The dual framing to remember is that a deny rule is both a security control and a context efficiency tool.
69
A path left off the deny list that shouldn't be readable creates risk on one dimension,
70
or wastes budget on the other.
71
The comparison between over-inclusion and targeted inclusion matters
72
because more context doesn't automatically mean better results.
73
Over-inclusion means mentioning large numbers of files that are loosely relevant to the task.
74
Every file added takes tokens.
75
When the context window fills, earlier content gets compacted or dropped,
76
including things that actually mattered.
77
The result is that you've paid a token cost for files that didn't improve the output,
78
while pushing out content that would have.
79
Targeted inclusion means loading only the files and rules directly relevant to the current task.
80
A smaller, accurate context window consistently outperforms a large, noisy one
81
because clawed code has more working space for the actual task
82
and less irrelevant material competing for attention in the context.
83
Token budget thinking is a practical discipline for clawed code work.
84
Clawed.md content is loaded at session start, before any task begins.
85
A verbose clawed.md file that tries to document everything about the project
86
exhaustively reduces the working space available before you've typed a single prompt.
87
Tool call results compound this.
88
When clawed code reads a large file or runs a command with extensive output,
89
those results fill the context window further.
90
And the window has a hard limit.
91
When it fills, earlier content is compacted or dropped by the model.
92
Content that was important earlier in the session may no longer be available later.
93
Being intentional about what enters context is a performance decision
94
with direct consequences for the quality and reliability of clawed code's work.
95
Here's what happens when a session runs long enough to fill the context window.
96
Rather than failing, clawed code compacts.
97
It summarizes the older parts of the conversation into a condensed form
98
and keeps working, trading verbatim history, for a summary that preserves the gist.
99
There are two ways this gets triggered.
100
Auto-compaction fires on its own as the context window approaches its limit,
101
so a long session doesn't just stop, it condenses and keeps running.
102
And you can run the slash compact command yourself, on demand,
103
to summarize before you hit that ceiling.
104
There's one more lever worth knowing.
105
You can put a compact instruction in your Clawed.md
106
that tells the summarizer what to always keep.
107
If a task ID or a file path should survive compaction, name it there,
108
and it persists into the summary every time the window compacts.
109
Clawed.md is the primary solution to the session continuity problem.
110
The context window starts fresh each session,
111
so the running conversation doesn't carry over on its own.
112
What does carry over is knowledge you've written down through Clawed.md
113
and through auto-memory.
114
Clawed.md handles the standing instructions by loading automatically at every session start.
115
Rules, conventions, constraints, and architectural context in Clawed.md
116
are available from the first message of every session without any manual setup.
117
The practical distinction is that Clawed.md should contain standing rules,
118
things that are always true about the project,
119
and always relevant to Clawed code's behavior.
120
It's not the right place for one-time instructions or session-specific notes.
121
Those belong in the prompt.
122
Clawed.md is the persistent instruction set, not a conversation log.
123
The bottom line?
124
Every file you mention costs tokens from a finite budget,
125
so be intentional about what enters a session.
126
Include architecture decisions, conventions, and constraints.
127
Exclude secrets, binaries, auto-generated files, and irrelevant dependencies
128
using deny rules in your settings file.
129
And rely on Clawed.md as standing context across sessions
130
rather than re-briefing Clawed code from scratch every single time.
131
That wraps up this lesson.
132
I'll see you in the next one.

--- Claude Code Permission Modes ---

1
Hello! Every time Cloud Code wants to edit a file or run a command, it can stop and ask you first.
2
How often it stops is up to you, and that is what permission modes control. This lesson
3
covers the modes, how you switch between them, and which one fits which situation.
4
Let's start with three ideas that hold the whole system together.
5
A permission mode is a session-wide setting. It decides which actions run without asking,
6
and which ones still stop to prompt you. The second idea is the working directory.
7
That's the folder Cloud launched in, plus any extra directories you choose to add.
8
Auto-approval only reaches inside that scope. Nothing outside it gets a free pass.
9
The third idea is a protected path. These are sensitive files, like your git folder or Cloud's
10
own configuration folder, that never get auto-approved unless you're in the one mode
11
built to skip every check. Keep these three in mind, because the modes we cover next all lean on
12
them. Three modes cover most day-to-day work, and you'll move between them constantly. The first is
13
default. In default mode, reads run freely, but edits and commands stop to ask you every single
14
time. It's the safest starting point, and it's what I reach for on sensitive work. The second
15
is accept edits. Here, Cloud can create and change files in your working directory without stopping.
16
You review everything afterward, instead of one edit at a time. The third is plan. Plan mode reads
17
and explores your project, then proposes changes without ever touching your source. I love it for
18
getting the lay of the land before I let Cloud start editing. Three modes, three very different
19
levels of oversight. So how do you actually switch? The important thing to know is that
20
you set the mode through controls, not by asking Cloud in the chat. The fastest way is the keyboard.
21
Press shift tab in the middle of a session, and you'll cycle through default, then accept edits,
22
then plan. The current mode always shows in the status bar, so you can see at a glance where you
23
are. You can also pass the permission mode as a flag when you first start a session, which is
24
handy when you already know how you want to work. And if you have a preference you want every time,
25
you set default mode in your settings. That picks your persistent starting mode,
26
so you don't have to think about it. Let's look a little closer at accept edits, because it's the
27
one most people live in. When it's on, Cloud can create and edit files without prompting you each
28
time. It also auto approves a handful of common file commands, things like making a directory,
29
creating a file, moving and copying. Here's the guardrail that keeps it sane. That approval only
30
applies to paths inside your working directory. Anything reaching outside that scope still stops
31
and asks you. The idea is that you let the edits flow, then you review them together afterward,
32
usually with a git diff, rather than approving every little change in line. It's a great fit
33
when you're actively watching the code as it comes together. Plan mode is the one I reach for when I'm
34
exploring. It tells Cloud to research and propose changes without actually making them. Cloud reads
35
your files and runs read-only commands to understand the shape of the project, then it writes
36
up a plan. What it won't do is edit your source. When the plan is ready, Cloud presents it and asks
37
how you want to proceed. You can approve it, you can refine it with feedback, or you can keep planning.
38
And here's a nice detail. When you approve a plan, the session exits plan mode and switches into
39
whichever mode you picked to carry out the work. So planning and doing flow together in one smooth
40
handoff. Now let's look at two modes that pull their hands off the wheel in opposite ways.
41
The first is don't ask. In this mode, Cloud auto denies anything you haven't already pre-approved.
42
Only the tools on your allow list and read-only commands can run. That makes it fully non-interactive,
43
which is exactly what you want for a locked down pipeline where you've spelled out ahead of time
44
what's allowed. The second is bypass permissions. This one skips the prompts entirely, so tool calls
45
just run. It's powerful, but that power cuts both ways. You should only use it inside an isolated
46
container or virtual machine where nothing important can be damaged. Never point it at a
47
machine you care about. There's one more mode worth knowing, and that's auto mode. Auto mode lets Cloud
48
keep working without the routine prompts, but it isn't a free-for-all. A separate classifier reviews
49
each action before it runs. If something tries to escalate beyond what you asked for, the classifier
50
steps in and blocks it, and any explicit ask rule you've set still forces a prompt no matter what.
51
The point of auto mode is to cut down on interruptions during long tasks, so you're not
52
clicking approve a hundred times. Just keep in mind it reduces the prompts. It doesn't remove the need
53
for your judgment. On truly sensitive operations, you still want to review what's happening.
54
Let's talk about a safety net that runs underneath all of this. Certain files are treated as protected
55
paths, and rights to them are never auto approved. That includes things like your git folder and
56
Cloud's own configuration folder. How that plays out depends on the mode. In default, accept edits
57
and plan, and edit to one of those files stops and asks you first. In auto mode, the right gets
58
routed to the safety classifier instead. In don't ask, it's simply denied, and only bypass skips the
59
check entirely. Why does this matter? Because these files hold your repository state and Cloud's
60
own setup. If they got changed by accident during a fast hands-off session, you could quietly corrupt
61
your project or your configuration. Protecting them lets you move quickly on your actual code,
62
while the truly sensitive stuff stays behind a checkpoint. So how do you pick? The honest answer
63
is that it depends on how much oversight the work in front of you needs. When you're new to a project
64
or you just want to understand it before changing anything, plan mode is a great place to start.
65
When you're actively building and watching the code land, accept edits keeps you moving without
66
losing sight of what changed. And when you're setting up automation, where you've already
67
decided exactly which tools are allowed, don't ask locks it down cleanly. The rule of thumb is simple.
68
Choose more oversight for risky work, and choose fewer interruptions when you trust the direction
69
things are heading. Let's recap. Permission modes set how often Cloud code pauses before it acts.
70
It can get pretty annoying when you're working with Cloud if you constantly have to re-engage
71
to allow it to do things. And a lot of people favor just turning off these checks, but I would
72
urge you to be careful and intentional about what you turn off and what you keep on, so that you
73
remain safe while also not having to engage all the time. Just remember protected paths stay
74
guarded everywhere except on full bypass. That wraps up this lesson. I'll see you in the next one.

--- The Three-Level CLAUDE.md Hierarchy ---

1
Cloud.md, or the CloudMD file, is how Cloud Code gets persistent instructions,
2
and it's organized as a hierarchy of four scopes. We're going to cover those scopes now,
3
what each one is designed for, how Cloud Code loads them all at session start, and why separating
4
personal preferences from shared project standards is the core design principle behind the whole
5
system. Let's pin down the broadest three scopes first. Managed Policy is an org-wide Cloud.md.
6
at a system path. It loads first, it's the broadest layer, and it can't be excluded.
7
That's how an organization enforces standards for everyone. User Scope is .cloud slash Cloud.md,
8
your personal preferences across every session. Project Scope is Cloud.md at the repository root,
9
the shared instructions for all contributors. Version Controlled, so everyone gets the same
10
setup. There's a fourth and narrowest scope, Local, which is Cloud.local.md and gitignored,
11
and we'll come back to it. The key idea is load order. Cloud Code reads these broadest
12
to most specific and concatenates them into one set of instructions. Nothing gets erased,
13
they all apply together, and instructions closer to your working directory carry more weight when
14
guidance overlaps. Managed Policy is the broadest layer, and it loads first. It's an
15
org-wide Cloud.md placed at a system path by the organization, and its defining property is that
16
it can't be excluded. Every user in every project inherits it before any personal or project config
17
loads. That makes it the mechanism for enforcing standards across a whole company. Security
18
requirements, compliance language, house style that has to hold everywhere. This is the key
19
correction to internalize. Organization-wide enforcement is managed policy, not a shared
20
project file and not a team-level file type. If a rule truly has to apply to everyone and can't be
21
opted out of, managed policy is the only scope that guarantees it. It sets the floor the rest
22
of the hierarchy builds on. These two scopes sit between managed policy and local. User scope
23
is .cloud slash cloud.md, your personal defaults across every project you touch. Project scope is
24
the cloud.md at the repository root, and it's the workhorse of the hierarchy. It applies to everyone
25
who works on that project. It's the right place for coding conventions the team has agreed on.
26
Test requirements, architecture guidelines, and anti-patterns you want Cloud Code to avoid.
27
The most important detail for project scope is version control. The project cloud.md should be
28
committed to source control alongside the code, so every contributor automatically gets the same
29
instructions with no manual setup. Leaving it out of source control is a concrete anti-pattern.
30
A cloud.md that lives on one developer's machine but isn't in the repo produces inconsistent
31
results and defeats the point of a shared config. The four boxes here are the practical guide for
32
where each type of instruction belongs. Org-wide requirements go in managed policy, company
33
policies, security rules, and standards that need to apply across every project and can't be opted
34
out of. Personal style goes at user scope, your preferred tone, verbosity, and individual
35
shortcuts. Project standards go at project scope, coding conventions, test rules, architecture
36
guidelines, and anti-patterns. And local scope, your git-ignored cloud.local.md holds personal
37
tweaks for one project. The common trap is putting org-wide rules in a project file and assuming
38
they're enforced everywhere. They aren't. If a rule has to hold across the whole organization,
39
it belongs in managed policy where nobody can exclude it. The user level and project level
40
configs serve fundamentally different scopes. User level is personal scope. One person, all their
41
projects. Right for preferred tone, output format, and personal shortcuts. Wrong for anything the
42
team needs to be consistent on. Project level is team scope. Everyone on the repository, one project.
43
Right for code style, test requirements, and security rules. Wrong for anything that varies
44
by individual preference. The key design principle is that the level you choose should match the
45
audience for that instruction. Personal instructions go at personal scope. Team instructions go at team
46
scope. Mismatching the two is where config hierarchy problems start. Let's close out the
47
scopes. Start with local scope. That's clod local.md, the narrowest layer, and it's git-ignored
48
so it never leaves your machine. Use it for personal tweaks that apply to one project but
49
shouldn't be committed for the whole team. Second, and this is the important correction, org-wide
50
enforcement is managed policy, not a team file type. You can absolutely share config by importing
51
or symlinking a clod.md across projects, and that's a real technique, but imports and symlinks
52
are just sharing. They don't enforce anything. If a standard genuinely has to apply across the
53
whole organization and can't be opted out of, it belongs in managed policy, which loads first
54
and can't be excluded. When you open a session, clod code finds and loads all applicable clod.md
55
files at the same time. Every scope applies simultaneously, and the load order runs managed
56
policy first, then user, then project, then local. If a user scope instruction and a project scope
57
instruction don't conflict, both simply apply. The model follows both. Only when guidance overlaps
58
does the more specific file matter, and we'll cover that in the next lesson. The other key detail here
59
is context window cost. Every clod.md file that gets loaded counts against the session context
60
window. A verbose project, clod.md with hundreds of lines of detail shrinks the working space
61
available for code. Keep config files concise and specific. Clod.md is a standing instruction file
62
that you author by hand, not an auto-updating memory store. It's loaded fresh at every session
63
start and counts against the context window from the start. You manage it like code. It doesn't
64
rewrite itself as you work. That's an important distinction, because clod code does have a
65
separate auto-memory system. It lives in your home directory, keyed to the git repo you're in,
66
and stays on your machine rather than being committed to the repo. It persists notes across
67
sessions on its own, so the two serve different jobs. Clod.md is where you put standing instructions
68
and keep them lean. The memory system is where evolving project notes accumulate over time.
69
Treat clod.md like a configuration file, not a journal, and let the memory system carry the
70
running notes. Quick recap. Managed policy is broadest, loads first, and can't be excluded,
71
so it's how organization-wide standards are enforced. User scope sets your personal defaults
72
across all sessions. Project scope defines standards for the whole team and should be
73
version-controlled. Local scope is your gitignored file for one project. All of them
74
load simultaneously, and all content counts against the context window.
75
That wraps up this lesson. I'll see you in the next one.

--- Precedence Rules and Conflict Resolution ---

1
This lesson is about load order in the Clod MD hierarchy. What happens when instructions at
2
different levels overlap or conflict? How Clod code concatenates them and leans on the closer
3
file? How to write configs that minimize conflict in the first place and the anti-patterns that
4
quietly break team consistency without an error message? A handful of terms anchor this lesson.
5
Load order is the first one. Clod code concatenates your clod.md files broadest
6
to most specific. When two instructions genuinely conflict, there is no reliable precedence rule.
7
Clod may follow either one, so don't count on the closer file winning. That's why avoiding
8
contradictions matters. Complementary instructions are the far more common case.
9
Instructions at different levels that simply don't conflict at all. When instructions are
10
complementary, both apply simultaneously without any resolution step needed. The model follows
11
both. The config anti-pattern is placing team standards in user-level config rather than
12
project level. Other contributors don't see user-level config, so placing team rules there
13
breaks consistency silently with no warning or error. Know all three going into the rest of the
14
lesson. Load order, complementary behavior, and the placement anti-pattern. Here's how overlap
15
actually works. Clod code doesn't pick one file and throw the others away. It concatenates all
16
your clod.md files into one combined set of instructions, read broadest to most specific.
17
So most of the time, user-level and project-level guidance simply stacks and both apply. When two
18
instructions genuinely conflict on the same point, there is no reliable precedence. Clod may follow
19
either one. Think of a project rule about formatting sitting alongside a contradictory
20
personal style note. You can't count on the project rule shaping the result. There's no
21
rule engine that deterministically declares a winner. The practical takeaway is to avoid writing
22
directly contradictory instructions across levels. Design them to complement each other and you never
23
have to guess which one won. It's worth being clear about why you can't just lean on the project
24
file to win. Project-level config represents the team's agreed standards. It's version-controlled,
25
committed to the repo, and visible to every contributor. But when a personal instruction
26
genuinely contradicts it, there's no rule that reliably resolves toward the project file.
27
User-level config, by contrast, is invisible to teammates. It lives on one person's machine and
28
applies only to their sessions. That's exactly the right home for individual preference.
29
The thing to internalize is that concatenation is additive by nature. Both files stay in context.
30
So the way to keep shared standards reliable isn't to lean on a hard override rule.
31
It's to keep personal and team instructions from contradicting each other in the first place.
32
Then there's never a question about which one applied.
33
Resolution only matters when instructions actually overlap. An overlap means two instructions
34
address the same behavior with opposing directions. User says tabs, project says spaces.
35
Their cloud may pick either one, so it's cleaner to just not write the conflict.
36
When instructions are complementary and address different behaviors,
37
both simply apply. User says be concise in responses. Project says always add type hints.
38
These two address completely different things. There's no overlap, so both apply.
39
Clod code is concise and adds type hints. Understanding the complementary case is
40
important because most clod.md combinations don't overlap at all. Concatenation is additive
41
by default. Overlap resolution is the exception, not the standard operating mode.
42
The best way to avoid conflicts is to be intentional about scope at the design stage.
43
User-level config should only contain instructions that genuinely don't belong to any specific
44
project. Personal tone preferences, individual workflow shortcuts, output format defaults.
45
These are good user-level content. Avoid putting formatting rules or coding conventions at user
46
level if any project you work on has a project-level clod.md that covers the same topic.
47
At the project level, write all project-specific coding standards explicitly, even for topics your
48
user-level config also addresses. Since a genuine conflict resolves unpredictably,
49
making the intended rule explicit avoids confusion about which instruction is in effect
50
and makes the project config self-contained for new contributors.
51
The most dangerous config mistake is putting team standards in user-level config. It's dangerous
52
because it fails silently. There's no error. Clod code doesn't warn you that a team standard
53
wasn't loaded for your colleagues. Your sessions look fine because your user-level config is loaded,
54
but every other contributor's sessions run without those instructions. The team loses
55
consistency in a way that's hard to diagnose. A developer wondering why their pair programming
56
partner is getting different clod code behavior won't immediately think to check each other's
57
user-level configs. The fix is simple. Any instruction that should apply to the whole team
58
belongs in the project-level clod.md committed to source control.
59
If you want to see which instructions clod code is actually following in a session,
60
you have a few practical options. The most direct is the slash memory command,
61
which lists the loaded memory files, and the slash context command, which lets you inspect
62
what's currently loaded into context. There's no automatically printed list of files at startup,
63
so don't go looking for one. If you want that logged, an instructions loaded hook can capture
64
it, and you can test overlapping instructions explicitly. Set a user scope instruction,
65
set an opposing project scope instruction, and see which guidance actually takes effect.
66
This kind of verification is particularly useful when you're onboarding to a new project
67
and want to confirm the project's clod.md is loaded and active, or when you're debugging
68
inconsistent behavior between your sessions and a teammate's sessions. Let's make this concrete
69
with a real example. A team adds a no semicolons rule to their project-level clod.md. Developer A
70
has always used semicolons in their user-level config. Developer B has no semicolon rule in
71
their user-level config at all. Both developers open clod code on this project. For developer B,
72
there's no conflict, so the project rule just applies, and there are no semicolons.
73
Developer A is the interesting case, because now two instructions directly contradict.
74
And here's the honest answer. That outcome is non-deterministic.
75
Clod may follow the project rule, or it may follow the user rule. You can't count on the
76
more specific file winning. The right move is to avoid the conflict entirely, and if two scopes
77
might overlap, state the precedence you want explicitly in the more specific file, so the
78
intended rule is unambiguous. Let's frame the big picture. Clod code concatenates your clod.md files
79
rather than picking a single one, so team and personal instructions coexist. When two genuinely
80
conflict, there's no reliable precedence, so avoid contradictions. That wraps up this lesson.
81
I'll see you in the next.

--- Subdirectory CLAUDE.md Files and Scoped Instructions ---

1
When you're managing a codebase with multiple distinct layers,
2
directory scoped Cloud MD files are how you scope rules precisely to each layer.
3
We'll look at how these files lazy load when Cloud reads inside a folder,
4
how they interact with the parent configuration,
5
and practical use cases for directories like front-end, tests, and infrastructure.
6
There's a small vocabulary that frames every scoping decision,
7
so let's lock it in first.
8
A subdirectory config is a Cloud.md file
9
placed inside a specific subfolder rather than the project root.
10
Cloud code lazy loads it when it reads a file inside that directory.
11
No registration step, no manifest entry.
12
That's what we mean by a directory scoped Cloud.md.
13
Instructions scoped to a directory by virtue of where the file lives.
14
The file location is the scope boundary,
15
and the Cloud.md hierarchy describes how project level and subdirectory configs interact.
16
Subdirectory files add rules.
17
They don't erase or replace the parent instructions.
18
For the CCAF exam,
19
the key point is this,
20
a subdirectory Cloud.md augments rather than replaces the root config.
21
That distinction shows up in questions about
22
how scoped rules interact with global configuration.
23
Placement sets the scope,
24
but the trigger is a read.
25
Drop a Cloud.md file inside any subfolder,
26
and Cloud code lazy loads it the moment it reads a file inside that directory.
27
This is the subtle part worth getting right.
28
Simply being in the folder or having it in your path isn't what activates the rules. The read is.
29
Until Cloud actually reads a file in that subdirectory,
30
the subdirectory Cloud.md isn't loaded.
31
If you read a file in the front-end folder,
32
the front-end Cloud.md loads on demand.
33
If you then read a file in the tests folder,
34
the tests Cloud.md loads.
35
There's no registration step and no manifest.
36
This makes scoping intuitive and easy to audit.
37
You know which rules can apply by looking at where the Cloud.md files live,
38
and they come into effect as Cloud reads into those directories.
39
The relationship between a subdirectory config and
40
the parent config is additive, not replacement.
41
When Cloud code is working inside a subdirectory,
42
the parent instructions are still active.
43
The subdirectory file adds folder-specific rules on top of that baseline.
44
Parent rules remain in effect,
45
and since the files are concatenated root to leaf,
46
the subdirectory file is simply read last.
47
There's no guaranteed override winner,
48
so it's best not to write direct contradictions at all.
49
Think of it as layering.
50
Each level of the directory tree adds rules to the stack.
51
The root config provides the baseline everyone inherits.
52
Subdirectory configs narrow and extend that baseline for specific contexts.
53
An exam question might describe a subdirectory Cloud.md
54
and ask whether it cancels the parent config.
55
It doesn't. It adds to it.
56
Four directory patterns show how scoped config works in practice.
57
A slash front-end Cloud.md holds React hooks rules,
58
component naming conventions, and import ordering preferences
59
that only matter inside that framework.
60
A slash tests Cloud.md holds PyTest fixture patterns,
61
test naming conventions, and coverage thresholds specific to the test suite.
62
A slash infrastructure Cloud.md carries Terraform module structure rules
63
and prominent never delete constraints for live resources.
64
And a slash generated Cloud.md contains a single directive.
65
Never modify these files.
66
They're auto-generated and any edits will be overwritten on the next run.
67
Each of these examples is clean because the rules only apply where they're relevant.
68
Framework specifics don't leak into the infrastructure folder.
69
Infrastructure constraints don't clutter the front-end config.
70
The front-end directory pattern is a good concrete example.
71
React hooks have specific rules about where and how they can be called.
72
Custom hooks should follow the use naming prefix convention.
73
Component files should be named in Pascal case.
74
Test files and style modules are often co-located with the components they belong to.
75
None of these conventions are meaningful outside the front-end directory.
76
Putting them in the root Cloud.md would add noise
77
for anyone working on infrastructure or back-end code.
78
Putting them in a front-end Cloud.md scopes them precisely.
79
They're active when you're working on React components.
80
Invisible when you're not.
81
That's the design goal of subdirectory configs.
82
Precision without pollution.
83
The infrastructure directory pattern carries higher stakes than most.
84
Terraform manages live Cloud resources.
85
A wrong edit can delete things that are hard to recover.
86
The infrastructure Cloud.md is the right place for explicit
87
never-delete rules about specific resource types.
88
Naming conventions that are required to be followed to keep automation working.
89
And state file handling instructions that differ from normal code.
90
Placing these rules close to the code they protect has a practical benefit.
91
Anyone reviewing the infrastructure directory sees the constraints immediately.
92
They don't have to remember that the root config has special rules for this folder.
93
The constraints live where the risk lives.
94
Root and subdirectory configs serve different purposes.
95
The root Cloud.md applies across the entire project.
96
It holds baseline style rules, global security constraints,
97
and cross-cutting conventions that every directory inherits.
98
A subdirectory Cloud.md applies only within that folder.
99
It holds framework-specific rules, local conventions,
100
and constraints tied to that code's specific purpose.
101
The layering means both are active simultaneously when you're inside a subdirectory.
102
Root rules set the floor.
103
Subdirectory rules add specificity.
104
If you need a rule to apply everywhere, it belongs in the root config.
105
If a rule only makes sense in a specific context, it belongs in that directory's config.
106
Verifying that scoped rules fire correctly is a real part of setting up subdirectory configuration.
107
You want to confirm that working inside each subdirectory
108
actually activates the folder-specific rules you placed there.
109
You also want to confirm that parent rules are still active, not silently dropped.
110
And if you've written any explicit contradictions between a subdirectory config
111
and the parent config, you want to verify they resolve the way you intended.
112
Scoped configuration is only reliable if it's been validated.
113
A Cloud.md you've never tested in context might not be loading at all,
114
or might be overriding something you didn't mean to override.
115
Test it explicitly. Let's recap.
116
Placement sets the scope, and the file lazy loads when Cloud reads inside that folder.
117
Subdirectory configurations augment the parent config.
118
They don't replace it.
119
Test your scoped rules before relying on them.
120
And that wraps up this lesson.
121
I'll catch you in the next one.

--- Excluding Files and Content ---

1
Let's get into a question that every serious clog user will hit eventually.
2
Some files should never reach clog code. Your .env, your private keys, a folder of customer
3
secrets. Get this wrong and a credential can slip into context. This lesson covers the real
4
exclusion mechanism, how settings precedence decides what wins, and two myths worth retiring.
5
Let's get into it. Before we go further, let's pin down three terms.
6
A deny rule is a read entry in the permissions block of settings.json. It tells clog code not
7
to read a matching path, and it is the real mechanism for excluding files. The settings
8
hierarchy is how your settings files layer together. Managed, local, project, and user
9
settings each contribute rules, and higher precedence files win. And a glob pattern is a
10
path expression with wildcards. One star matches a single segment of a path. Two stars match across
11
directories, so you can name one file or an entire tree. Hold on to that distinction between deny,
12
the layered hierarchy, and the glob syntax. Every rule you write combines all three,
13
and the way they interact decides exactly which files clog code can and can't open.
14
So here is the actual mechanism. File exclusion lives in the permissions block of settings.json
15
as read deny entries. You write an entry like read of .env, and clog code will refuse to read
16
that path. The patterns use glob syntax. One star matches a single path segment. Two stars cross
17
directory boundaries, so you can match a whole nested tree. Anchors set the base for a pattern.
18
A leading slash anchors to the directory of the settings file that holds the rule,
19
and a double slash gives you a truly absolute path. A bare path resolves relative to the
20
working directory, so you list the paths you want off limits, choose the right anchor, and wildcards,
21
and clog code treats those files as unreadable. That is the whole idea. There is no separate
22
ignore file involved, just entries inside settings.json. Credential protection is the highest
23
value reason to write deny rules, so start there. For environment files, deny read of .env and deny
24
read of .env.* to catch variants like .env.local. For secret directories, deny read of a secrets
25
folder followed by a two star wildcard, which blocks everything nested inside it. For home
26
credentials, deny read of your ssh folder and your cloud credential folders using a tilde to anchor
27
at home. These files hold API keys, database passwords, and private keys. If they never enter
28
context, they can never leak into generated code or a suggestion. Writing a handful of deny rules
29
for these paths is the single highest impact configuration step for keeping secrets out of
30
clod code. Now, where do these rules live? Settings come from four layers, and it helps to know them
31
highest first. Managed settings are organization enforced policy. They sit at the top and can't be
32
overridden locally. Local settings are your get ignored local file for personal overrides that
33
stay off the repository. Project settings are the committed file that ships with the repo,
34
so they are shared team rules. And user settings live in your home directory as personal defaults
35
that apply across every project. The same deny rule can appear in any of these. For ordinary settings,
36
a higher layer overrides a lower one. But permission rules work differently. They merge
37
across all four layers, and we'll see next why a deny in any layer wins over and allow in any other.
38
Precedence works differently for permission rules than for ordinary settings, and it is worth being
39
precise. For ordinary non-permission settings, a higher layer simply overrides a lower one.
40
So managed beats, local beats, project beats user. But permission rules, the allow, ask, and deny
41
entries don't work that way. They merge across all four layers. Every deny from every layer is
42
collected and deny is evaluated before allow. So a deny in any layer, even your user file, blocks
43
and allow in a higher layer like managed. If a path matches any deny rule anywhere, it is blocked
44
and no allow rule can reopen it. That is the key idea. Deny beats allow across layers. That tells
45
you exactly why a given file is blocked or open. A quick note on the syntax itself, because it looks
46
familiar and that causes confusion. Deny rules use gitignore style path syntax. A bare path,
47
or one starting with dot slash, resolves relative to the working directory. A leading slash anchors
48
to the directory of the settings file that contains the rule. So it's the project root only
49
for a project settings file. And user settings resolve under your home clod directory instead.
50
For a genuinely absolute path, use a double slash. For wildcards, one star matches a single segment
51
and two stars match across nested directories. So the grammar is the one you already know from
52
dot gitignore. But here's the important part. Only the syntax is borrowed. These rules live inside
53
settings dot json. There is no gitignore style file that clod code reads for exclusions.
54
Deny rules are powerful, but you should know their boundary. Deny blocks clod code's built-in tools.
55
That means read, edit, and shell reads like cat or head are all stopped for a denied path. It also
56
blocks indirect access. So an at mention of the file and an editor selection are covered too.
57
What deny doesn't block is an arbitrary subprocess. If clod code runs a Python script, that script can
58
still open a denied file because deny governs the tools clod code drives, not the operating
59
system underneath. That is a real gap to understand. When you need the file to be unreadable,
60
no matter what runs, deny rules alone aren't enough. You reach for sandboxing, which enforces
61
access at the file system level and stops the shell itself from succeeding.
62
Let's retire two myths because both come up constantly and both are wrong. The first is the
63
dot clodignore file. There is no such file. It has never existed as a feature. If you see advice
64
telling you to create one, that advice is fabricated. The real answer is a deny rule.
65
The second myth is that clod code respects dot gitignore. It doesn't. A file being ignored by
66
git says nothing about whether clod code can read it. Ignored files are still fully readable
67
unless you deny them. There is also no magic ignore environment variable that hides files.
68
If you want a path excluded, there is exactly one supported path to it. You write a read deny rule
69
in settings dot json. Everything else is a misconception. Here's the bottom line. File
70
exclusion is done with read deny rules in settings dot json. They use glob syntax and
71
permission rules merge across layers with deny beating allow every time. Deny stops built-in
72
tools and at mentions, but not subprocesses, so pair it with sandboxing for real enforcement.
73
And remember there's no dot clodignore and dot gitignore is not always respected.
74
That wraps up this lesson. I'll see you in the next.

--- Instruction Design — What Claude Code Actually Reads and Follows ---

1
Here's what we're tackling in this one.
2
What separates a Cloud.md directive
3
that Cloud Code actually follows from one
4
that it reads and then ignores?
5
We'll look at how to write effective instructions, what
6
to encode, what to leave out, and how
7
to structure and verify a Cloud.md that does real work.
8
Let's jump into it.
9
A handful of terms anchor this lesson.
10
An effective instruction is specific, actionable,
11
and unambiguous.
12
Cloud Code can follow it without inferring intent
13
or filling gaps.
14
A vague directive may be read, but not reliably acted on.
15
Context window cost is the trade-off
16
at the center of Cloud.md design.
17
Every token you put in the file counts
18
against available context space during the session.
19
Verbose files leave less room for code and tool output.
20
And instruction encoding is the decision
21
about what belongs in Cloud.md versus what Cloud Code already
22
knows from training.
23
General programming knowledge doesn't belong in Cloud.md.
24
Repo-specific decisions do.
25
That distinction drives most of the design choices
26
we'll cover in this lesson.
27
Three traits define an effective Cloud.md directive.
28
Specific means it names the exact language
29
version, the precise file path, the actual naming convention.
30
Not use consistent naming, but all module files use snake case.
31
Actionable means Cloud Code can follow the instruction
32
without interpretation.
33
If following it requires a judgment call,
34
the instruction isn't actionable enough.
35
Unambiguous means one clear meaning.
36
No sentence that could reasonably be read two ways.
37
Vague instructions produce inconsistent behavior.
38
Cloud Code may read them, but the behavior
39
you see in practice will vary across sessions.
40
The standard is precision.
41
If you can't verify whether the instruction was followed,
42
rewrite it until you can.
43
Cloud Code reads both concise bullet directives
44
and verbose documentation, but concise directives
45
are more reliably followed in practice.
46
A short bullet point stating a specific rule
47
is harder to miss than the same rule buried three paragraphs
48
into a design rationale.
49
Verbose documentation also consumes context space.
50
Every sentence of narrative that doesn't add a new directive
51
is a sentence that could have been code or conversation.
52
The right mental model for writing Cloud.md
53
is a style guide checklist, not a design document.
54
Short bullets, one rule per line,
55
no explanatory paragraphs unless the instruction can't
56
be understood without context.
57
When in doubt, cut.
58
Four categories belong in almost every production Cloud.md.
59
Language and runtime versions, Python 3.11, Node 20 LTS,
60
whatever exact versions this project requires.
61
Cloud Code won't assume a specific version
62
without being told.
63
Style conventions, the naming patterns, import order,
64
and file organization decisions your team
65
has made for this repo specifically.
66
Test requirements, coverage thresholds,
67
test file naming conventions, fixture organization,
68
and any policies around mocking.
69
And commit format, the message structure, branch naming
70
pattern, and PR description template your team uses.
71
These are all repo-specific decisions.
72
They're not universal.
73
Cloud Code can't know them from training data.
74
That's exactly why they belong in Cloud.md.
75
Beyond conventions, Cloud.md is where
76
you document team process and known anti-patterns.
77
PR process rules should be explicit.
78
Who needs to approve, what the merge strategy is,
79
whether commits are squashed.
80
Coding anti-patterns specific to this code base
81
are worth encoding.
82
Patterns that have caused problems before,
83
architectural decisions to avoid,
84
common mistakes that show up in review.
85
And security rules belong here, too.
86
No hard-coded credentials, which files
87
are never to be modified, how secrets are handled.
88
These are decisions your team has already
89
made through experience and deliberation.
90
Encoding them in Cloud.md means Cloud Code
91
won't re-suggest the patterns you've already ruled out.
92
What not to encode is as important as what to encode.
93
Cloud.md is for repo-specific knowledge, not
94
general programming education.
95
Don't explain what Python is or document
96
how list comprehensions work.
97
Don't restate standard library behavior
98
that Cloud Code knows thoroughly from training.
99
Don't duplicate rules that your linter or formatter already enforces.
100
If black handles your formatting,
101
you don't need formatting rules in Cloud.md.
102
If ESLint catches a pattern, don't also
103
encode it as a directive.
104
Every line of general knowledge wastes context tokens
105
that could hold actual code output.
106
Be ruthless about cutting anything
107
that isn't specific to your repo and your team's decisions.
108
The encode versus skip decision comes down to one question.
109
Is this something Cloud Code can only know if you tell it?
110
Runtime version pins are a clear yes.
111
Cloud Code doesn't know which Python version your team
112
is targeting without being told.
113
Your PR naming convention is a clear yes.
114
Your team's deployment process is a clear yes.
115
General Python syntax is a clear no.
116
Cloud Code knows how Python works.
117
Standard design patterns are a clear no.
118
How git branching works is a clear no.
119
The practical test is, if I remove this line,
120
would Cloud Code behave differently
121
in a way that matters?
122
If yes, keep it. If no, cut it.
123
That filter keeps Cloud.md focused and context efficient.
124
Structure matters as much as content.
125
A Cloud.md with clear header sections
126
is faster for Cloud Code to process
127
and easier for you to verify and maintain.
128
Group related rules together.
129
A language section, a style section, a testing section,
130
a git section, a security section.
131
Use bullet points over paragraphs,
132
one directive per line.
133
Related rules in the same section
134
so context pressure hits a whole category at once
135
rather than a mixed list.
136
Good structure also makes testing easier.
137
When you sit down to verify that your Cloud.md works,
138
you can work through it section by section
139
instead of hunting for scattered rules
140
across a long document.
141
Headers and bullets are both a readability choice
142
and a reliability choice.
143
Effective Cloud.md instructions are specific, actionable,
144
and unambiguous.
145
In code, what's repo specific?
146
Skip what Cloud Code already knows.
147
Keep it concise and structured with headers and bullets.
148
After writing it, sit down in a real session
149
and verify Cloud Code actually follows each directive.
150
That's the only real test that matters.
151
Pulling this together, precision beats volume every time.
152
Next up, we'll dig into real world Cloud.md patterns
153
that teams actually rely on.
154
I'll see you there.

--- Common CLAUDE.md Patterns for Real Teams ---

1
Let me walk you through the clod.md patterns
2
that teams actually reach for across front-end, back-end,
3
and full-stack projects.
4
We'll cover what goes into each pattern, a security pattern,
5
a test pattern, and two anti-patterns worth knowing
6
so you can recognize and avoid them.
7
These ideas frame everything we'll do from here on. Let's jump in.
8
Start with the vocabulary.
9
A clod.md pattern is a reusable set
10
of directives suited to a specific team context.
11
Front-end patterns encode component naming
12
and state management rules.
13
Back-end patterns encode API design
14
and database conventions.
15
Each pattern is different because each context is different.
16
The directive versus narrative distinction
17
matters here too.
18
A directive tells clod code what to do.
19
A narrative explains background and rationale.
20
Clod.md should be directives, not narrative.
21
And tool duplication is the anti-pattern
22
of encoding rules that a linter or formatter already enforces.
23
It wastes context tokens and creates
24
conflicting sources of truth.
25
Recognizing all three of these concepts
26
is useful for the CCAF exam.
27
The front-end pattern covers four rule categories.
28
Component naming, Pascal case for component definitions,
29
the casing convention for file names,
30
and co-location rules for tests and styles.
31
CSS methodology, whether the project uses CSS modules,
32
tailwind utility classes, or styled components.
33
The choice is meaningful because it
34
affects how clod code should generate
35
any component with styles.
36
State management, which library is approved,
37
whether global state is permitted in certain layers,
38
what patterns are off limits.
39
And accessibility standards, required ARIA attributes,
40
keyboard navigation requirements for interactive elements.
41
None of these apply to back-end code.
42
That's exactly why a subdirectory clod.md
43
in the front-end folder is the right home for them.
44
The back-end pattern covers the API and data layers.
45
API design rules encode this project's
46
specific conventions, the rest design choices,
47
how the team version's endpoints,
48
what the standard error response format looks like.
49
Database query rules encode the ORM versus raw SQL policy.
50
Any query optimization requirements
51
and whether direct database access
52
is permitted outside certain layers.
53
Error handling rules specify which
54
error types are expected to surface, how they're logged,
55
and what format error responses take in the API.
56
These are all decisions specific to this service.
57
Clod code can't infer them from general back-end knowledge.
58
They have to be encoded.
59
Fullstack and monorepo projects need cross-cutting rules
60
that span multiple packages.
61
The fullstack pattern typically includes four categories.
62
Shared type conventions, where TypeScript interfaces
63
shared across front-end and back-end live,
64
and the import rules for consuming them.
65
Monorepo structure rules, which packages own, which concerns,
66
and what the import restrictions are across package boundaries.
67
Service boundary rules, what each service is responsible
68
for, what data crosses service lines,
69
and what's explicitly forbidden to cross.
70
And shared naming conventions that apply consistently
71
everywhere in the monorepo.
72
These rules prevent the drift that develops naturally
73
when multiple packages evolve independently
74
without shared guardrails in place.
75
Security directives are among the highest value
76
lines in any clod.md.
77
Four rules appear in nearly every security pattern.
78
No hard-coded credentials.
79
All secrets are passed via environment variables
80
or a secrets manager, never written in line and code.
81
.env usage policy, which env files exist in this project,
82
which ones are committed to version control,
83
and which are never committed.
84
Secrets management approach, which vault or service
85
handles secrets, and how clod code should reference them
86
in generated code.
87
And authentication patterns, approved libraries,
88
forbidden patterns, and session handling rules.
89
Security directives should be explicit, prominent,
90
and placed early in the file so they're hard to miss.
91
The test pattern addresses four areas
92
where inconsistency causes real problems.
93
Test file naming, where test files live relative to source,
94
whether they use a .test or .spec suffix,
95
and any module co-location requirements.
96
Coverage thresholds, the minimum percentages enforced in CI,
97
and any per-module targets the team has agreed to.
98
Fixture organization, where shared fixtures
99
live in the project and how they're
100
imported across different test files.
101
And mock policies, which external dependencies are
102
always mocked, which should be tested
103
with real implementations, and any rules
104
around third-party service mocks.
105
Test configuration rules prevent a test suite
106
from growing inconsistently as the code base scales.
107
The most common clod.md failure mode
108
is writing it as a narrative document instead
109
of a set of directives.
110
A narrative clod.md has long paragraphs explaining
111
context and rationale.
112
Rules are buried inside prose.
113
It reads like a design doc or a wiki page.
114
Under context pressure, clod code
115
is more likely to miss rules that aren't clearly delineated.
116
The directive pattern is the opposite.
117
Bullet points, one rule per line, clear headers,
118
scannable in seconds.
119
You can verify it by reading through in under a minute
120
and confirming each directive is present and explicit.
121
When you sit down to write clod.md, start with bullets.
122
Add prose only when an instruction genuinely
123
can't be understood without background context.
124
The tool duplication anti-pattern
125
is subtler but just as costly.
126
If black is configured to handle Python formatting,
127
encoding formatting rules in clod.md doesn't add safety.
128
It adds a second source of truth that may eventually
129
diverge from the formatter's configuration.
130
The same applies to eslint.
131
If the lint config catches an import pattern,
132
writing the same rule in clod.md is redundant.
133
If a type checker enforces annotations,
134
clod.md doesn't need to repeat it.
135
The right mental model is let tools
136
enforce what tools can enforce.
137
clod.md fills the gaps that automated tools can't cover.
138
Repo-specific decisions, team process,
139
anti-patterns that aren't lintable.
140
Those are where clod.md adds value,
141
not where it duplicates tools.
142
Real teams converge on patterns because they work.
143
A front-end pattern, a back-end pattern, a security pattern,
144
and a test pattern each encode the decisions that
145
matter most for that context.
146
Security directives belong in every project.
147
The narrative anti-pattern and the tool duplicate anti-pattern
148
are the two failure modes most worth avoiding.
149
And that wraps up clod.md patterns for real teams.
150
We've covered instruction design and practical patterns.
151
Apply them on your own projects going forward.
152
In the next lesson, we'll talk about creating custom slash commands.
153
I'll see you there.

--- Creating and Structuring Custom Slash Commands ---

1
If you're tired of retyping the same instructions session after session, custom slash commands are the solution.
2
We'll cover what they are, how to create one, where the files live, how file naming controls the keyword,
3
and how the arguments placeholder lets you handle variable input.
4
By the end, you'll be able to build commands your whole team can use.
5
Let's get the language right first.
6
A slash command is a user-defined shortcut.
7
A slash keyword that triggers an instruction template you wrote, stored as a markdown file under
8
.clod slash commands.
9
It's not a built-in capability. You create it.
10
One thing worth knowing up front, custom commands have since merged into skills.
11
Command files still work exactly as they always did, but the current recommended form is a skill.md
12
file under .clod slash skills.
13
We'll focus on commands here because the mechanics carry straight over.
14
And args is the placeholder you drop into a command file where you want variable input to land.
15
When someone runs the command and types text after the keyword, that text replaces args in your template.
16
The most important conceptual point about slash commands is what they actually are.
17
They're shortcuts. They map a keyword to a markdown file you wrote.
18
When you invoke slash deploy review, Cloud Code reads that file, treats its contents as a prompt,
19
and runs it against your current session.
20
Now, a lot of things ship with Cloud Code out of the box.
21
Built-in commands like slash init and slash help, plus bundled skills like slash code review and slash loop.
22
Those aren't yours. They come with the tool.
23
Everything else, anything named for your own team's workflows, like slash deploy review or slash run
24
tests, those are custom commands.
25
You created them.
26
The distinction between what ships with the tool and what you create matters, so stay sharp on it.
27
Slash commands come in two scopes, and the scope is determined entirely by where the file lives.
28
Project-level commands live in a .clod slash commands directory inside your project.
29
Because that directory gets committed to version control, the commands are automatically available
30
to every developer who opens the repo.
31
There's no manual sharing step, no extra configuration.
32
User-level commands live in .clod slash commands in your home directory.
33
Those are personal. They're not committed anywhere, and they follow you across every project you
34
open on your machine.
35
The practical rule is, if the workflow belongs to the team, use project-level.
36
If it's a personal shortcut, use user-level.
37
File naming is how you set the command keyword, and it's beautifully simple.
38
Drop a file called deployreview.md into .clod slash commands, and slash deploy review is now a valid command.
39
Rename it to reviewpr.md, and the command becomes slash reviewpr.
40
There's no configuration file to update, no registration step, nothing else to do.
41
The file name is the keyword, fullstop.
42
That simplicity is also a design constraint. Choose names that are descriptive enough to be self-documenting.
43
The keyword should signal the action without requiring anyone to open the file first.
44
If someone runs slash do it and gets unexpected output, the name didn't do its job.
45
We'll dig into naming conventions in the next deck.
46
The contents of a command file are the instruction template.
47
Think of it as a system prompt that runs every time the command is invoked.
48
A good command file should open with a role statement that gives clod context, describe the task clearly,
49
specify the expected output format, and add any constraints on scope or tone.
50
For a code review command, that might look like,
51
you are reviewing a pull request, check for logic errors, security issues, and style violations.
52
Return findings as a bulleted list grouped by severity.
53
The more specific the instructions, the more consistent the output.
54
A vague command file is a vague prompt, and you'll get vague results.
55
Let's look at scope from a practical angle.
56
Project-level commands in .clod slash commands are committed to version control.
57
The moment you push that directory, every team member who pulls the repo has access to the same
58
commands automatically.
59
Nothing extra required.
60
User-level commands in .clod slash commands never leave your machine unless you deliberately copy
61
them somewhere else.
62
They're yours alone, spanning every project you open.
63
For the exam, the file path is the scope signal.
64
Project-level means shared via version control.
65
User-level means personal.
66
Both use the same markdown file format and the same file name-to-keyword mapping.
67
The only difference is where the file lives and who can access it.
68
The args placeholder is what turns a static template into a flexible tool.
69
Say you have a reviewpr.md file that reviews pull requests.
70
Without args, it would review whatever context clod already has.
71
With args, you write something like, review pull request args for security issues.
72
Now when someone types slash reviewpr 4521, the number 4521 replaces args in the template before clod sees it.
73
You can reference args multiple times if needed.
74
Freeform input works well when a single piece of text is enough.
75
If your command needs structured fields with multiple values, build a more explicit template that
76
guides the user toward the right format.
77
A quick but important note on built-in commands.
78
Clod code ships with a whole set of them, many of them bundled skills.
79
Slash init generates a starter clod.md file for a new project.
80
Slash help shows you the available commands.
81
And there are others like slash code review and slash loop that come with the tool too.
82
The fastest way to see the full set is to type a single slash and let the list appear.
83
The practical advice is simple.
84
Check that list before you name a custom command.
85
If you reuse a built-in name, your own file gets shadowed, and that's confusing to debug.
86
For any unused name you choose, the file named to keyword mapping is fully yours to control.
87
Here's a quick recap.
88
Slash commands are markdown files or md files that map a file name to a reusable prompt.
89
Project level lives in .clod slash commands and is version controlled and shared.
90
User level lives in slash .clod slash commands and stays personal.
91
The file name is the keyword with no setup required.
92
And arguments handles dynamic input.
93
