That wraps up this lesson. I'll see you in the next one.

--- Command Design Patterns and Team Distribution ---

1
Welcome back. This lesson is about how to design slash commands that actually work well in practice,
2
not just technically. We'll cover the core design principles of narrow scope,
3
how to write an effective command prompt, when to use freeform args versus a structured template,
4
and how teams and enterprises distribute commands so everyone has access. Let's jump into it.
5
There's a small vocabulary that frames every command design decision,
6
so let's lock it in first. Narrow scope command means a command designed to do exactly one thing,
7
the single responsibility principle applied to slash commands. Verb-noun convention is the
8
naming pattern you'll see in the best command libraries, slash review PR, slash run tests,
9
slash explain file. The verb states the action and the noun states the target. Someone new to
10
the team can read the keyword and know what the command does before they ever open the file.
11
Managed settings is the enterprise level mechanism. Admins push commands to all users in an
12
organization without requiring each person to set anything up. Each of these connects to a concrete
13
decision you'll make when building a command library. The number one design rule for slash
14
commands is narrow scope. A command that does one thing is predictable, easy to maintain, and
15
composes well with other commands. A command that does many things, say it reviews the pull request,
16
runs the test suite, and suggests a deployment strategy all in one go is a problem waiting to
17
happen. The instruction template becomes a multi-paragraph tangle. Clod has conflicting
18
signals about what to prioritize. Users don't know what output to expect, and when something goes
19
wrong it's hard to tell which part of the template caused it. Build small focused commands. Compose
20
them when you need to cover more ground. Every command file is a system prompt. Four elements
21
make a system prompt effective when it's used as a reusable command. First, role. Tell Clod what
22
perspective it's operating from. You are a senior engineer reviewing for security issues. Second,
23
task. Describe exactly what to do. Review the diff passed as args. Third, output format. Specify how
24
the response should be structured. Return findings as a bulleted list grouped by severity. Critical,
25
high, low. Fourth, constraints. What to exclude, what tone to use, any scope limits. Missing any of
26
these leads to output that varies unpredictably across runs, which defeats the entire point of
27
having a reusable command. The choice between Freeform args and a structured template comes
28
down to how much shape the input needs. Freeform works perfectly when a single piece of text is all
29
you need. Slash explain file app dot pi passes one file name. Clean, low friction, no setup. A
30
structured template is better when input has multiple fields or when getting the shape wrong
31
would cause errors. In that case, write the template to explicitly prompt for each piece.
32
Provide your PR number, reviewer role, and primary focus area. Users follow the template rather than
33
guessing. The overhead of structure pays off when the command is complex enough that incorrect input
34
would produce meaningless or misleading output. Naming conventions matter more than they seem.
35
The verb noun pattern slash review PR slash run tests slash explain file slash generate change log
36
is the standard because it's self-documenting. Anyone reading the command list can infer
37
what each command does without opening a single file. Contrast that with slash PR slash code or
38
something worse like slash do it. Those names tell you nothing. You have to read the file to understand
39
what the command does and even then you might not be sure. A consistent naming convention across a
40
team's command library creates a shared vocabulary. New teammates can guess the right keyword before
41
they've even read the documentation and that's exactly the outcome you want. Team distribution
42
for project scoped commands is essentially free. You commit dot clod slash commands slash to version
43
control as part of the project and from that point forward every developer who clones or pulls the
44
repo has the full command library. Updates work the same way. Improve a command, commit, and the update
45
propagates via normal pull and merge. There's no separate sharing mechanism, no configuration
46
management layer, no documentation to update about where the files live. The commands travel with the
47
code base. This is one of the most underappreciated properties of the project level scope. The
48
distribution channel already exists. You're just using it. For organizations running clod code at
49
scale, managed settings provide a centralized distribution mechanism. Administrators configure
50
commands at the organization level and those commands are pushed automatically to every user
51
in the organization. Users don't need to do anything. They can't accidentally delete org
52
level commands or override them with personal files. This matters most for commands that
53
enforce compliance workflows, approved security checks, or organization-wide automation standards.
54
When you want to guarantee that every developer in a thousand person org has access to the same
55
slash review for compliance command, managed settings is how you do it without relying on
56
every team to maintain their own copy. Two anti-patterns come up constantly when teams start
57
building command libraries. The first is the overly broad command. Someone builds slash do
58
everything that reviews, refactors, tests, and deploys in one file. It works occasionally and
59
fails confusingly. Narrow the scope. The second anti-pattern is duplicating clod.md instructions
60
inside command files. clod.md already provides standing context at the session level.
61
If your command file copies those instructions, you've created maintenance debt. Update clod.md
62
later and forget to update the command and now they're out of sync. Commands should extend and
63
build on what clod.md establishes, not repeat it. These two patterns, over broad scope and
64
instruction duplication, are the most common ways command libraries become unreliable.
65
One more time, effective slash commands follow a narrow scope. Use verb noun naming and distribute
66
through version control or managed settings. Freeform orgs work for simple input and structured
67
templates work when input shape matters. Avoid broad commands and avoid duplicating clod.md.
68
That's custom commands and distribution covered. Next up, we talk about skills. The trigger
69
activated layer that fires automatically so your team doesn't have to remember to invoke anything.
70
I'll see you there.

--- What Skills Are and When to Build Them ---

1
Skills are what you build when you want a procedure Claude pulls in automatically whenever it fits a request,
2
without someone having to remember to type a command.
3
We'll look at how skills differ from slash commands and subagents,
4
and the decision framework for choosing to build one.
5
Also, we'll talk about where skills sit in the broader instruction stack.
6
A skill is a reusable instruction package.
7
A skill.md file, plus any optional supporting files.
8
Claude loads it automatically when its description matches what you're asking for,
9
or you invoke it directly by name.
10
A slash command is really a subset of skills now,
11
created in the .claude commands directory, and invoked with a slash name.
12
The difference is a loading pattern.
13
A manual-only command is one you type explicitly,
14
while a skill can also load itself when its description matches.
15
A subagent is a delegated autonomous worker, spun up to complete a task independently.
16
It's an active process, not a passive template, with its own context and tools.
17
So keep the three straight.
18
Skills are description-matched packages,
19
manual-only commands are skills you invoke explicitly,
20
and subagents are delegated workers.
21
Skills occupy a specific position in the instruction stack.
22
At the bottom, claude.md holds always-applied facts and rules that Claude sees on every interaction,
23
no matching or invocation involved.
24
In the middle, skills are description-gated procedures.
25
Claude reads each skill's description and pulls the skill in only when it's relevant to what you asked,
26
loading the body at that point.
27
At the top, subagents are autonomous workers you spin up when you need a task completed independently.
28
Skills are the selective layer.
29
They're more targeted than claude.md, which is always in context,
30
and more passive than subagents, which actively execute work.
31
If you have a procedure that should load only when it fits the request,
32
rather than sitting in context permanently, a skill is the right layer.
33
Here's a nuance worth getting right.
34
Custom commands are actually a subset of skills now.
35
Both are invoked with a slash name, so this isn't two separate mechanisms.
36
It's one mechanism with two loading patterns.
37
A manual-only command is one you type explicitly,
38
and it sets a disable-model-invocation flag so Claude won't fire it on its own.
39
That fits shortcuts you want to trigger by hand, like a common code review pattern,
40
or a standard documentation format.
41
A skill can also load itself when its description matches what you asked for,
42
with no need to remember to invoke it.
43
So the practical distinction is manual-only invocation versus automatic description-based loading.
44
Four patterns tell you a skill is the right choice.
45
Recurring multi-step workflows that you run across many sessions, or across a team,
46
benefit from being captured once in skill.md, rather than redescribed each time.
47
Teamwide conventions that should apply consistently for every contributor belong in a shared skill,
48
not in each person's individual Claude.md.
49
Procedures that should load automatically when relevant, not on a typed command,
50
fit the way skills get matched by their description.
51
And reusable instruction packages that should run the same way every time
52
benefit from the structure a skill.md gives you.
53
If your workflow fits two or more of these patterns, it belongs in a skill.
54
Knowing when not to build a skill is just as important.
55
One-time tasks don't justify the overhead of writing a formal skill.md.
56
Skills exist for reuse.
57
A task you'll run once is better handled in-line.
58
Simple lookups don't need a separate skill at all.
59
A Claude.md note, or a quick slash command, is less friction for straightforward requests.
60
And some patterns are better handled by a Claude.md rule that's always on
61
rather than a skill that only loads when it matches a request.
62
If the instruction should apply everywhere unconditionally, Claude.md is the right place.
63
Skills add complexity.
64
Only build one when the complexity is justified by real reuse value.
65
The build decision framework comes down to three factors.
66
Frequency, complexity, and reusability.
67
Frequency asks how often this workflow appears across sessions or team members.
68
If it's rare, a formal skill may not be worth the investment.
69
Complexity asks whether the workflow is multi-step enough
70
that a reusable template provides meaningful value over describing it in-line.
71
Simple one-liners don't justify the overhead.
72
Reusability asks whether more than one person, session, or team context
73
will benefit from the skill running consistently.
74
A workflow that scores high on all three is a strong skill candidate.
75
One that scores low on even one factor should make you consider whether a slash command
76
or a Claude.md rule handles it better with less friction.
77
Skills and sub-agents are both powerful, but they solve different problems.
78
A skill is a passive instruction package stored in skill.md.
79
When its description matches the request, Claude Code loads it
80
and follows the instructions itself.
81
The skill is the recipe. Claude Code is the cook.
82
A sub-agent is an active autonomous worker you delegate a task to.
83
It has its own context window, tools, and scope.
84
It runs independently and reports back when done.
85
You'll run into scenarios where you have to choose between these two.
86
The key question is whether you need an instruction package that loads
87
when it matches a request, or whether you need a worker
88
to handle something autonomously in parallel.
89
Packages vs. Workers. Skills vs. Sub-agents.
90
Reading the full instruction stack helps you place new patterns correctly.
91
Claude.md rules are always active and apply everywhere, always in context.
92
Skills stay out of context until their description matches the request,
93
and then Claude loads the instructions defined in their skill.md.
94
A manual-only command is just a skill you invoke explicitly with a slash name,
95
since commands are a subset of skills now.
96
Sub-agents are spawned for a specific task, run autonomously,
97
and aren't templates at all.
98
They're active processes. Choosing the right layer prevents over-engineering.
99
Putting a simple, always-on rule in a skill adds unnecessary complexity.
100
Marking a frequently-needed team pattern manual
101
only means every team member has to remember to invoke it by hand.
102
Match the mechanism to how it should load.
103
Let's recap. Skills are the description-gated middle layer
104
in the Claude code instruction stack.
105
They're more selective than Claude.md rules, and more passive than agents.
106
Use the Frequency, Complexity, and Reusability framework
107
to decide when one is worth building.
108
That wraps up this lesson. I'll see you in the next.

--- Authoring Agent Skills ---

1
Let's get into how you actually author a skill.
2
A skill is really just a folder with a skill.md file inside it, and Claude finds it on its own.
3
We'll walk through where skills live, what the front matter holds,
4
and why the description is the one part you want to get precisely right. Let's dig in.
5
Three pieces make up a skill, so let's name them first.
6
The skill directory is a named folder holding a skill.md file.
7
That folder name is the skill's name, and it's how you invoke it.
8
It gets discovered automatically, with no registration step anywhere.
9
The skill.md body is the markdown that sits below the front matter.
10
It's the actual set of instructions Claude follows,
11
and it always loads, even when there's no front matter at all.
12
The front matter itself is an optional yaml block at the very top.
13
Every field in it is optional.
14
The one field genuinely worth adding is the description,
15
because that's what Claude reads to decide whether the skill fits what you asked for.
16
Directory, body, front matter.
17
The first thing to internalize is that a skill is a directory, not a loose file.
18
The layout is a .claude folder, a skills folder inside it,
19
and then a named folder for your skill that holds the skill.md.
20
That folder name matters more than you'd expect.
21
It becomes the skill's name and the handle you use to invoke it.
22
Claude discovers every skill folder that's present automatically.
23
There's no registry to update, no manifest to edit,
24
and nothing to declare somewhere else to make the skill visible.
25
Drop the folder in the right place, and it's found.
26
When you want to run a specific skill directly, you call it by that folder name.
27
The directory name really is the entire addressing scheme here.
28
Here's the part the old material got backwards, so slow down on it.
29
The front matter is optional.
30
A skill.md file with no front matter block at all is still a perfectly working skill.
31
The markdown body loads, and Claude runs it.
32
Invoking the skill by its folder name still works with zero fields declared.
33
There is no silent failure lurking behind a missing front matter block.
34
That idea that leaving out front matter quietly breaks the skill is simply wrong.
35
The body is the substance of the skill, and it loads either way.
36
Front matter is there to tune how the skill behaves, which model it uses,
37
which tools it can touch, when it should fire.
38
But the skill exists and runs the moment the body exists.
39
So what can the front matter actually hold?
40
Quite a lot, and all of it optional.
41
Name sets the display label, and description, more importantly,
42
tells Claude when the skill fits a request.
43
A when to use field gives a plain language cue about the situations it should handle.
44
Allowed tools and disallowed tools aren't symmetric.
45
Allowed tools pre-approves the tools you list, so they run without a permission prompt,
46
but every other tool still stays available.
47
Disallowed tools is the one that actually removes tools from the pool.
48
Model and effort let you pick a model and set reasoning effort.
49
Context is the interesting one.
50
Set it to fork, and the skill runs in an isolated forked sub-agent,
51
paired with the agent field, to choose which sub-agent handles it.
52
None of these are mandatory.
53
You add the ones you need and leave the rest out.
54
Of all these fields, the description is the one that carries real weight,
55
so it's worth writing carefully.
56
Claude reads the description to decide whether a given skill fits the request in front of it.
57
That's the whole matching mechanism.
58
If the description is vague, the skill gets passed over in situations where it should have fired.
59
If it's sharp and specific, auto-invocation becomes reliable,
60
and the skill fires exactly when it should.
61
Everything else in the front matter tunes how the skill behaves once it's chosen.
62
The description is what gets it chosen in the first place.
63
So spend your effort there.
64
Say plainly what the skill does and the kinds of requests it's meant to handle.
65
A few more real fields are worth knowing, and they stay optional too.
66
An agent field binds the skill to a specific agent,
67
and a hooks field lets you wire in lifecycle hook behavior.
68
A paths field restricts the skill to certain file paths,
69
so it only applies when you're working in the right places.
70
An argument hint field signals the arguments the skill expects
71
when a user invokes it directly, which makes direct calls cleaner.
72
There are a couple more in the same spirit,
73
like fields that control whether the model can invoke the skill on its own,
74
or whether a user can.
75
The pattern holds throughout.
76
These are optional refinements, not required scaffolding.
77
Add what earns its place and skip the rest.
78
Now for a quick myth-clearing pass,
79
because a few fields get quoted that simply don't exist.
80
There is no trigger field.
81
Matching runs off the description, not off a separate trigger declaration.
82
If you've seen a skill written with a trigger field, that field does nothing.
83
There is no version field baked into the format,
84
and there is no author tags field either.
85
And to say it one more time, because it's the stickiest myth,
86
a missing front matter block doesn't cause a silent failure.
87
The body loads, and the skill runs.
88
Reach for the real fields, the description, the tool scoping, the paths.
89
Invented fields just sit there inert,
90
and mislead the next person who opens the file expecting them to matter.
91
Let's tie invocation together, because there are two clean ways it happens.
92
The default is auto-invocation.
93
Claude reads each skill's description,
94
matches it against what you asked for,
95
and runs the fitting skill on its own without you naming it.
96
This is why the description does so much work.
97
The other way is direct invocation.
98
You call the skill yourself by its folder name,
99
which is handy when you already know exactly which skill you want.
100
Same skill, same folder, two paths to reach it.
101
Auto-invocation leans entirely on a good description.
102
Direct invocation leans on you knowing the folder name.
103
Between them, a well-named folder and a well-written description
104
cover almost everything you'll want to do.
105
Here's what to take away.
106
The skill is a named directory holding a skill MD,
107
discovered automatically, and invoked by its folder name.
108
The front matter is fully optional, and the body always loads.
109
The real fields are things like description, tool scoping, and paths,
110
and the description is what drives matching,
111
so make sure you write it well.
112
Just remember, there's no trigger, version, or author-tagged fields.
113
That wraps up this lesson.
114
I'll see you next time.
115
See you in the next.

--- Creating and Configuring Custom Subagents ---

1
Here's what we're going to tackle in this one. How to create and configure sub-agents that
2
actually do useful, isolated work in Cloud Code. We're going to look at what sub-agents actually
3
are, how you spawn them, how context isolation works, and how to configure scope and permissions.
4
We'll finish with a practical look at when spawning is the right call. A sub-agent is a
5
separate agent instance spawned by a parent using the agent tool within the same Cloud Code session
6
to handle a delegated sub-task. Sub-agents run in their own isolated context window and
7
report results back. Context isolation means each sub-agent starts fresh with an empty context
8
window. Nothing passes from the parent automatically. Every piece of information
9
the sub-agent needs should be explicitly included in the handoff. And the orchestrator
10
is the parent agent that decomposes the goal, delegates work to sub-agents, and synthesizes
11
the results. The orchestrator doesn't share its context window with the sub-agents it spawns.
12
These three concepts form the foundation for everything else in this lesson.
13
Sub-agent, context isolation, and orchestrator. Cloud Code sub-agents are different from
14
multi-agent patterns you'd build directly with the API. In the API context, a multi-agent system
15
is usually a set of API completions you're orchestrating yourself in application code.
16
Cloud Code sub-agents are separate agent instances inside one session. They're not API calls
17
you're managing. They're spawned via the agent tool within the same session, each running with
18
its own isolated context window and its own configured tools and permissions. This distinction
19
matters. Sub-agents in Cloud Code aren't the same thing as agent orchestration using the API.
20
They operate at a different level and have different characteristics around context,
21
isolation, and tool access. Two paths exist for creating a sub-agent. The first is a markdown
22
definition file. Custom sub-agents are defined as markdown files, either in a .cloudagents folder
23
in your project or a .cloudagents folder in your home directory. You create one by asking
24
Cloud to write the file for you or by editing it directly to define the sub-agent's identity,
25
tools, and scope. The agent tool is the programmatic path. When a parent agent is running an
26
agentic workflow, it can invoke the agent tool to spawn a sub-agent dynamically as part of that
27
workflow. This is how delegation happens in code. The markdown file gives you a named reusable
28
sub-agent definition. The agent tool is how sub-agents get spawned during execution of a
29
larger workflow. Four things get configured on a per sub-agent basis. Tool access controls which
30
tools the sub-agent can invoke. Bash, file reads, file writes, web access, or others. File system
31
scope is shaped through permission rules and for stronger isolation you can run the sub-agent in a
32
work tree giving it its own copy of the repository to work in. Permissions control what the sub-agent
33
is allowed to do within its configured scope. Sub-agents can have narrower permissions than the
34
parent and the task description is the explicit set of instructions defining what the sub-agent
35
should do. All four of these are intentional design choices. You're not just spawning a session,
36
you're defining a scoped permissioned agent with a specific job. Context isolation is one of the
37
most important things to internalize about sub-agents. When a sub-agent is spawned it starts
38
fresh. None of the parent's conversation history, already read files, or prior work carries over.
39
If the sub-agent needs information from the parent's session the parent has to explicitly
40
include it in the handoff instructions. The sub-agent's results are returned to the parent
41
which then synthesizes them. Isolation is actually a feature here. It prevents the sub-agent from
42
being influenced by context that isn't relevant to its task. It keeps the sub-agent focused but
43
it means the handoff has to be deliberate and complete. By default a sub-agent inherits the
44
parent's tools and permission context but you can configure it with a narrower set than the parent
45
holds. A sub-agent that's inspecting files can be configured as read only meaning it can look
46
but not write. A sub-agent working on a specific service can be scoped to that directory only
47
with no access to adjacent paths. Restricting tool access reduces the blast radius if the
48
sub-agent does something unexpected. This is the principle of least privilege applied at
49
the sub-agent level. Give the sub-agent exactly the access it needs for its task
50
and nothing beyond that. Restricting below the parent is the intentional opt-in step.
51
Knowing when to spawn a sub-agent and when to stay in line is a practical design decision.
52
Sub-agents make sense when tasks are truly independent and can run in parallel,
53
when a task needs a different tool set than the parent is using, or when scope isolation
54
prevents context contamination between separate work streams. Staying in line makes sense when
55
the task needs full parent context to complete, when the work is sequential and depends on prior
56
steps in the same session, or when the task is simple enough that delegation overhead exceeds
57
the value. The overhead of spawning a sub-agent is real. Don't spawn one unless the isolation or
58
parallelism actually buys you something. The handoff is a design artifact, not an
59
afterthought. When you spawn a sub-agent, you're writing the only instructions it will ever have.
60
There's no shared context. There's no ability to ask a follow-up question from the parent
61
mid-task. The handoff needs to include a precise task description defining what done looks like,
62
the necessary context, only what the sub-agent actually needs, and the output format, exactly
63
what structure you expect the sub-agent to return. If any of these three are under-specified,
64
the results will be ambiguous or unusable. Designing the handoff is the most important
65
part of deploying a sub-agent effectively. Let's recap this one. Sub-agents run in
66
isolated context within one session, so the parent's conversation doesn't transfer,
67
while tool and permission scope inherits by default and can be narrowed. Configure each
68
one explicitly. Spawn when the work is independent and stay in line when you need parent context.
69
That wraps up this lesson on configuring sub-agents. I'll see you in the next one.

--- Designing Subagents for Delegation ---

1
Welcome back. If you're spawning subagents and getting back results you can't use,
2
the problem is almost always in the design, not the execution. We'll look at the three design
3
questions every subagent needs. Context window allocation, structured output design, error
4
reporting, and the common anti-patterns that make delegation counterproductive. Let's jump into it.
5
There's a small vocabulary that frames every subagent design decision.
6
The three design questions are what task, what context, and what output format. Every effective
7
subagent is built by answering all three before anything gets spawned. Structured output means
8
subagents should return parsable, well-formed results rather than natural language summaries.
9
The parent agent needs to process the results, not interpret them. And delegation overhead is
10
the real cost of spawning and managing a subagent. Context loading, coordination, latency, and cost
11
are all part of that overhead. For simple tasks the overhead can exceed the benefit of delegating
12
in the first place. Understanding when delegation adds value and when it just adds friction
13
is one of the key design skills this lesson covers. Before spawning any subagent answer
14
three questions. What task? Precisely what should this subagent do? And what does done actually look
15
like? What context? Exactly what information does the subagent need to complete that task?
16
Not what might be useful. Not everything the parent knows. Exactly what this specific task requires.
17
And what output format? How should the results be structured so the parent can actually use them?
18
If you skip any of the three you've built an underspecified subagent. It might run. It'll
19
return something. But the results will be inconsistent, hard to process, or simply wrong
20
because the design was incomplete to begin with. These three questions are the minimum viable
21
design checklist. Context window space in a subagent is a finite resource the same as anywhere
22
else. The difference is that the subagent's context is entirely provided by the parent.
23
There's no background knowledge being drawn from a shared session. What you pass is what the
24
subagent has. More context isn't better if it's not relevant to the task. Overstuffing the context
25
window with everything the parent knows crowds out the task relevant content and makes the
26
subagent less focused. The parent holds the full context. The subagent needs a focused, purposeful
27
slice of it. Disciplined context allocation keeps the subagent efficient, keeps costs lower,
28
and produces better results because the subagent isn't filtering through irrelevant noise.
29
Before you hand build every subagent, know that Cloud Code ships with a few ready-made ones.
30
Explore is built to investigate. It reads through the code base and reports back what it found
31
without making edits, which is exactly what you want during the research phase.
32
Plan is a read-only research agent that gathers code-based context in plan mode.
33
It doesn't draft the approach itself. It hands its findings back, and the main conversation
34
presents the plan. General purpose handles open-ended tasks that don't fit a narrower role.
35
What ties them together is that delegation is description-driven.
36
You describe the task clearly, and that description routes the work to the right subagent.
37
One more knob worth knowing. You can route each subagent to its own model. Matching model
38
capability to task cost is how you keep a multi-agent workflow both effective and affordable.
39
Structured output is a design requirement, not a nice-to-have. When you define a subagent's task,
40
you also define exactly what it should return. That means specifying a schema, JSON fields,
41
YAML structure, or named output sections. The subagent should return a success or failure
42
indicator as part of that output so the parent can check it programmatically. Results should
43
be machine-parsable, not natural language descriptions. A summary written in prose
44
might be readable by a human, but it can't be reliably parsed by the orchestrating parent
45
agent. Specify the output format in the task description before the subagent runs.
46
Don't leave it implicit. Explicit format specification is what separates a useful
47
subagent from one that returns ambiguous results. Error reporting design is as important as success
48
path design. A subagent that hits a failure condition has two options. Return an explicit
49
error indicator with enough context for the parent to understand what failed,
50
or silently return an empty result. Silent failures are the worst outcome. The parent
51
receives nothing useful, has no way to distinguish a successful empty result from a failure,
52
and can't take corrective action. Design the error path deliberately. What should the subagent
53
return when it fails? What information does the parent need to diagnose or retry? Build error
54
reporting into the output schema just like any other output field. The parent's workflow depends
55
on being able to distinguish success from failure reliably. Delegation is genuinely
56
beneficial in specific conditions. Work that's truly independent is a good fit,
57
meaning different parallel streams don't need each other's intermediate state.
58
Work that needs a different tool set than the parent is using. Work where scope isolation
59
prevents one task from contaminating another's context. But delegation is counterproductive
60
when the task is simple and would be faster in line. When the task requires the parent's
61
full context to complete. When the overhead of spawning, coordinating, and processing results
62
exceeds the time the delegation actually saves. Subagents aren't free. Every delegation decision
63
should be justified by what it gains. The first anti-pattern is spawning a
64
subagent for every subtask. It feels thorough. It's actually counterproductive. Each spawn
65
adds latency. Each spawn carries coordination complexity. Each subagent the orchestrator
66
manages adds to the overhead of running the workflow. If you're delegating trivial tasks
67
to subagents, you're spending more time on spawning and result processing than the work
68
itself would have taken in line. Reserve subagents for work that genuinely benefits from isolation,
69
parallelism, or a different permission set. If you can't articulate why a task needs its own session,
70
it probably doesn't. Do the work in line and save the delegation machinery for when it actually
71
earns its keep. The second anti-pattern is spawning a subagent without defining its output format.
72
When the output format isn't specified, the subagent returns whatever feels natural.
73
That usually means a natural language summary. Natural language summaries can't be reliably
74
parsed by the parent agent. They produce inconsistent results across runs. They
75
force the parent to interpret rather than process, which introduces errors and ambiguity into the
76
workflow. The fix is simple. Define the output format in the task description before the subagent
77
runs. Make it explicit. Specify the exact schema, the fields, the types, the success indicator.
78
A well-specified output format transforms the subagent from a black box into a predictable,
79
composable component. Pulling this together, the handoff document you write before spawning
80
is the most important design artifact in your entire agentic workflow. Next up, we'll look
81
at clod code hooks, the deterministic control layer that fires at lifecycle events no matter
82
what clod decides. I'll see you there.

--- Hook Lifecycle Events ---

1
Hello.
2
Cloud Code hooks let you run your own commands automatically at specific moments,
3
and there are quite a few of them.
4
The trick is knowing which event fires when,
5
and which one lets you step in versus just watch.
6
This lesson maps the events by cadence
7
and shows you which hooks block and which only observe. Let's jump in.
8
Let's anchor three ideas first.
9
A hook is just a command you register to run automatically
10
when a specific lifecycle event fires.
11
You aren't changing the model.
12
You are attaching your own script to a moment.
13
A lifecycle event is that named moment.
14
It might be a tool call about to happen, a session starting, or a turn ending.
15
Each event gives your hooks a chance to run,
16
and a blocking hook is one that can actually stop the action from going through.
17
The way it signals that is by exiting with code 2.
18
Hold on to those three.
19
Everything in this lesson is just events at different moments,
20
and whether the hook at that moment can block or only observe.
21
That single distinction is the whole game.
22
The cleanest way to hold all these events in your head
23
is to group them by cadence, by how often they fire.
24
There are three buckets.
25
The outermost is once per session.
26
Session start and session end fire one time each,
27
framing the whole session like bookends.
28
The middle bucket is once per turn.
29
Prompt submit and stop fire around each individual thing you ask the agent to do.
30
And the innermost, fastest bucket is every tool call.
31
Pre-tool use and post-tool use wrap each and every tool the agent runs inside its loop,
32
so those can fire many times in a single turn.
33
Session, turn, tool.
34
Outer to inner, slow to fast.
35
If you remember that nesting, the whole event set falls into place.
36
Let's start at the outermost layer, the session events.
37
These fire once as a session opens and once as it closes.
38
Session start runs when a session begins or resumes,
39
so it is your hook for the very beginning.
40
Session end runs when the session terminates right at shutdown.
41
Here is the key property.
42
Both of these are observe only.
43
They can't block anything because there is no single action for them to veto.
44
They simply mark the edges, so what are they good for?
45
Session start is where you set up context as you enter,
46
maybe loading project notes or environment details.
47
Session end is where you clean things up on the way out.
48
Think of them as set up and tear down, not as gates.
49
One layer in, we hit the turn events,
50
and these bracket each turn you take with the agent.
51
User prompts submit fires when you send a prompt before Claude even reads it.
52
Stop fires on the other side when Claude finishes responding.
53
Now here is what makes these interesting.
54
Both of them can block, which turns them into useful gates.
55
Because prompt submit runs before your prompt is processed,
56
a hook there can reject a prompt outright or reshape it first.
57
And because stop runs when Claude tries to finish,
58
a blocking hook there can prevent the agent from stopping
59
and push it to keep working.
60
So the turn layer is where you can inspect what goes in
61
and control when the agent is allowed to be done.
62
Now the innermost pair, and this is the contrast I most want you to remember.
63
Pre-tool use and post-tool use sound like a matched set,
64
but they behave very differently.
65
Post-tool use fires before a tool runs, and it can block the call.
66
That makes it your gate.
67
If you want to guard against a risky command or stop an unwanted tool entirely,
68
this is the hook, and it blocks by exiting with code 2.
69
Post-tool use fires after a tool succeeds, and here is the crucial part.
70
It can't block because the tool has already run.
71
There is nothing left to stop,
72
so you use post-tool use to react to what happened,
73
log the result, check the output, or trigger a follow-up.
74
Pre-tool guards. Post-tool reacts. Don't mix them up.
75
Beyond the regular cadences,
76
a few events fire at their own specific moments,
77
and they are worth knowing.
78
Pre-compact fires just before context compaction,
79
when Claude Code is about to summarize older context to save room.
80
It can block, so you can protect something from being summarized away.
81
Notification fires when Claude Code sends a notification,
82
and it only observes, so it is purely for alerting you,
83
maybe pinging your phone or terminal.
84
And there are permission events that fire around permission prompts.
85
A permission request hook can deny that permission,
86
acting as a blocking gate.
87
So even outside the tidy session, turn, and tool buckets,
88
the same question always applies.
89
Does this moment let me block, or only watch?
90
Let's make the blocking mechanism concrete,
91
because it comes down to the exit code your hook returns.
92
Exit 0 means success.
93
Claude Code reads any structured output your hook printed
94
and just continues normally.
95
Exit 2 is the special one. It is the blocking signal.
96
For any hook that is allowed to block,
97
exiting 2 stops the action and feeds your message back to Claude as the reason.
98
And any other exit code is treated as a non-blocking error for most events,
99
meaning the action still goes ahead anyway.
100
That last point trips people up.
101
A plain failure code of 1 doesn't block.
102
If you want to enforce a rule, you have to exit 2, specifically.
103
0 to allow, 2 to block, everything else just warns.
104
Let's pull the single most useful distinction to the front.
105
For any event, the real question is whether the hook can stop an action
106
or can only watch it.
107
On the blocking side, pre-tool use stops a tool before it runs,
108
stop keeps the agent working instead of finishing,
109
and pre-compact can halt compaction before context gets trimmed.
110
On the observe-only side, post-tool use can react but can't undo
111
because the tool already finished,
112
and session start and notification simply watch and report.
113
So the practical rule is short.
114
When you need a gate, something that can say no, pick a blocking event.
115
When you just need a record or an alert,
116
an observe-only event is exactly right,
117
and it keeps your hook out of the critical path.
118
Let's put it all together as a decision.
119
To choose the right hook, think about what you want to happen and when.
120
Do you need to guard a dangerous command before it runs?
121
Reach for pre-tool use, since it can block.
122
Want to auto-format or lint right after an edit?
123
Use post-tool use, which reacts after the fact.
124
Need to load project context the moment a session opens?
125
Session start is your hook. The pattern is always the same.
126
The cadence, session versus turn versus tool,
127
tells you when the hook fires,
128
and the blocking column tells you whether you can actually intervene or only observe.
129
Answer both of those, and the right hook picks itself.
130
Here's a quick summary.
131
Hooks fire at named moments across three cadences.
132
Session start and end bracket the session and only observe.
133
Prompt submit and stop bracket each turn and can block.
134
Pre-tool use blocks a tool, while post-tool use only reacts to what happened.
135
Exit code 2 lets a blocking hook stop an action.
136
Just remember to pick based on what you're looking to achieve.
137
And that wraps up this lesson. I'll see you in the next one.

--- Plan Mode — When and Why to Plan First ---

1
Welcome back. In this lesson we're looking at plan mode in Cloud Code.
2
Plan mode separates the thinking phase from execution.
3
Before Cloud touches a single file, it produces a structured proposal you can review and approve.
4
We'll cover when plan mode earns its place, how to trigger it, and what to look for before you say yes.
5
Let's anchor the lesson with three terms you'll use constantly.
6
Plan mode is a read-only thinking phase.
7
Cloud inspects your code base, reasons about what needs to happen, and surfaces an ordered proposal.
8
Nothing is written. The approval gate is the checkpoint where you decide whether the plan is correct.
9
You can accept it, edit it, or reject it entirely.
10
No file writes happen before you pass through.
11
And replanning is what happens when partial execution exposes something the original plan got wrong.
12
Cloud regenerates or amends the plan before continuing.
13
These three ideas—plan mode, approval gate, and replanning—form the backbone of a controlled, reversible workflow.
14
So what does plan mode actually do under the hood?
15
Cloud enters a read-only thinking phase.
16
It reads the files it thinks are relevant, reasons through the task, and produces a structured proposal.
17
That proposal isn't a commitment. It is a draft Cloud is handing to you for review.
18
The proposal includes the steps it plans to take, the files it expects to read or write, and any
19
risks or assumptions it is making explicit.
20
What it doesn't do is touch any of those files.
21
The moment you approve the plan, Cloud exits read-only mode and starts executing.
22
That transition is the key thing to internalize.
23
Thinking and doing are kept separate intentionally.
24
There are two ways to enter plan mode, and both are deliberate.
25
The first is the shift-plus-tab toggle.
26
Plan mode is a permission mode, and shift-plus-tab cycles through them.
27
Default, then accept edits, then plan.
28
When you land on plan, the whole session stays in plan mode until you cycle back out.
29
That's what you want when a longer piece of work should stay in review-first mode.
30
The second way is the slash-plan prefix.
31
You put slash-plan in front of a single request, and Cloud plans just that one prompt before
32
returning to whatever mode the session was in.
33
Use the toggle when you want to stay in plan mode.
34
Use the prefix when you want one plan without flipping the session.
35
Here's a framing that clears up a lot of confusion.
36
Plan mode isn't a separate tool you launch.
37
It's one of the permission modes the session can be in, right alongside the default mode and accept edits mode.
38
That's why both entry paths we just covered are really just ways of setting the permission mode.
39
Shift-plus-tab cycles through the modes interactively until you land on plan.
40
If you'd rather start a session already in plan mode, you pass permission mode plan on the command
41
line when you launch Cloud.
42
Once you're in plan mode and Cloud hands you a proposal, you have three choices.
43
You can approve it, you can edit it before approving, or you can reject it and ask for a different approach.
44
Approving is the specific action that flips the session out of plan mode and lets Cloud start making changes.
45
What does a well-formed plan actually look like?
46
There are three components you should expect every time.
47
First, ordered steps.
48
The plan should be a numbered sequence of discrete actions, not a paragraph of intentions.
49
Second, files touched.
50
Cloud should name every file it expects to read or write.
51
If a file is missing from this list, ask for it explicitly before approving.
52
Third, risks called out.
53
A good plan flags its own assumptions.
54
Things like, I'm assuming this interface isn't used elsewhere, or this depends on the migration having run.
55
If a plan is missing ordered steps, file names, or explicit risks, push back.
56
Ask Cloud to fill in the gaps before you approve.
57
When does plan mode actually earn its keep?
58
Three scenarios stand out.
59
Cross-file refactors are the clearest case.
60
If you're renaming a type used across dozens of files, a plan surfaces every touchpoint before execution starts.
61
Schema changes are equally risky.
62
Modifying a database schema or an API contract ripples through models, validators, and tests.
63
A plan lets you verify that all affected layers are accounted for.
64
Ambiguous requests are the third case.
65
When the right approach is genuinely unclear, plan mode externalizes Cloud's interpretation so you
66
can correct it before any damage is done.
67
In all three cases, the plan is your opportunity to catch wrong assumptions cheaply.
68
Plan mode isn't the right tool for every task.
69
There are cases where the overhead of planning adds more friction than it prevents.
70
A single file change with a clear, mechanical fix doesn't need a plan.
71
If you're renaming one variable or correcting a typo, just do it.
72
Exploratory work in a REPL where reversibility is trivially easy doesn't benefit from a formal proposal either.
73
The useful heuristic is, use plan mode when the work is hard to reverse or when it spans multiple modules.
74
If you can undo a mistake in 10 seconds, the approval gate isn't adding much.
75
If a mistake would take 30 minutes to unwind, the plan is worth it.
76
Let me connect plan mode to three related ideas.
77
Iterative refinement is the broader pattern.
78
Produce a draft, review it with a human or another agent, and revise until quality criteria are met.
79
Plan mode is one instance of this pattern applied to code changes.
80
Plan mode pairing is the practice of combining plan mode with subagents or hooks.
81
A subagent investigates the code base and returns findings.
82
The main agent turns those findings into a plan.
83
A hook validates steps before execution starts.
84
These combinations raise reliability on complex work.
85
Finally, code base exploration is what plan mode is doing while it thinks.
86
It reads the files it needs and explores the code base to build the plan.
87
Approving that plan is the step that moves the session from reading into making edits.
88
Before you approve any plan, you need to read it critically.
89
This sounds obvious, but approving without reading defeats the whole purpose.
90
There are three things to check.
91
First, does the scope match your intent?
92
Claude sometimes interprets a request more broadly than you meant,
93
and a plan that touches ten files when you expected two is a signal to narrow the task before proceeding.
94
Second, are the file paths correct for your actual project layout?
95
Claude's assumptions about directory structure aren't always accurate.
96
Third, does the plan flag risks you can already resolve from domain knowledge?
97
Catching a wrong assumption at the review stage costs seconds.
98
Catching it after half the files are rewritten costs much more.
99
So plan mode gives you visibility into Claude's reasoning before any code gets written.
100
Use it for complex, cross-file, or ambiguous work.
101
Read every plan before approving.
102
The approval gate is the developer's primary quality checkpoint.
103
And that covers when to use plan mode.
104
In the next lesson, we'll look at how to iterate on a plan once Claude proposes one.
105
Approving, editing, and replanning.
106
I'll see you there.

--- Iterating on a Plan — Approve, Edit, Re-Plan ---

1
Welcome back. This lesson is about the iteration loop that happens after Claude proposes a plan.
2
You don't have to accept a plan as is. You can edit it, reject it, or trigger a replan mid-execution.
3
We'll cover the full loop from approval through replanning, look at pairing patterns,
4
and identify the failure modes that undermine plan mode in practice. Let's jump in.
5
Let's define the three concepts that frame this lesson. The approval gate is the checkpoint where
6
you decide what happens to a proposed plan. You can accept it, edit it in line, or reject it
7
entirely. Nothing is written to disk until you pass through. Iterative refinement is the broader
8
pattern. Produce a draft, review it, revise it, and repeat until the plan correctly captures intent.
9
Plan mode is one application of this pattern to code changes. Plan mode pairing is the practice
10
of combining plan mode with other mechanisms, sub-agents that investigate before planning,
11
or hooks that validate steps before execution. These three concepts connect the approval gate
12
to the broader workflow of collaborative, human-supervised code generation. The approval
13
gate is the most important control point in the plan mode workflow, but it only works if you use
14
it properly. When Claude presents a plan, you have three options. You can accept it as is and let
15
execution begin immediately. You can edit it in line, trimming scope, reordering steps, or adding
16
something Claude omitted. Or you can reject it entirely and restate your intent more precisely.
17
The gate fails when you approve without reading. Skimming a plan and clicking through transfers
18
control back to Claude without the review the gate is designed to provide.
19
Treat the approval gate the way you treat a code review. Fast is fine. Absent isn't.
20
Editing a plan before approval is faster than triggering a full replan,
21
so it's worth understanding what you can change. The most common edit is trimming scope. Claude
22
sometimes interprets a request more broadly than you intended, and a plan that proposes touching
23
eight files when you wanted three is a signal to narrow it before proceeding. Reordering steps is
24
also common. You might know that a dependency has to be updated before a migration can run,
25
even if Claude sequenced them differently. And occasionally you'll add a step Claude omitted
26
based on your knowledge of the codebase. After you make edits, approve the revised plan. Claude
27
executes your version, not the original. What happens when execution starts and reality doesn't
28
match the plan? This is where replanning comes in. The clearest trigger is when a file Claude
29
expected to exist doesn't, or when an assumption about the codebase turns out to be wrong.
30
The right response is to stop, surface the divergence explicitly, and regenerate the plan
31
before continuing. The question is when replanning is actually necessary versus when it's overkill.
32
If the broken assumption only affects one step and the rest of the plan is still valid,
33
you might be able to patch and proceed. If the broken assumption undermines several downstream
34
steps, replanning is the right call. The decision depends on how much of the remaining work depends
35
on the assumption that broke. Plan mode pairing extends the core pattern with additional
36
reliability layers. The first pattern is sub-agent investigation. A sub-agent reads the codebase,
37
gathers context, and returns findings. The main agent uses those findings to produce a plan.
38
Investigation and planning stay separate, which means the plan is built on richer information.
39
The second pattern is hook validation. Pre-execution hooks verify plan steps before Claude
40
runs them. A hook might check that a named file exists, that a linter passes, or that a naming
41
convention is respected. The third pattern is staged human review. Instead of a single approval
42
gate at the start, you approve after each major phase. Staged gates catch drift before it compounds
43
across multiple steps. The fourth pattern is combined. Sub-agent finds, hook validates, and human
44
approves. The three layers each catch different failure classes, and stacking them yields the
45
highest reliability. The most common failure in plan mode workflows is when Claude abandons the
46
plan and improvises mid-execution. It usually starts with an unexpected file state. Claude
47
encounters something the plan didn't account for and adapts silently rather than surfacing
48
the divergence. It adds steps that weren't in the plan, adjusts its approach, and you end up
49
reviewing code that doesn't match what you approved. The fix is behavioral. Before execution starts,
50
tell Claude explicitly to stop and surface any divergence rather than working around it.
51
Something like, if you encounter anything that contradicts the plan, pause and tell me before
52
proceeding. That instruction doesn't guarantee Claude will always do it, but it reduces the
53
frequency of silent improvisation significantly. A few workflow habits make plan mode consistently
54
useful. Keep plans short. Five to eight discrete steps is the right range for a plan you can
55
actually review. Plans with 20 steps are hard to check and hard to edit. Name files explicitly
56
rather than using vague references like the relevant config file. If Claude can't name the
57
file, the plan isn't complete enough to approve. Always ask for risks. Even when you think you know
58
the code base well, asking Claude to flag assumptions surfaces things you might have
59
overlooked. These three habits, short plans, explicit file names, and explicit risk statements,
60
produce plans that are reviewable, editable, and reliable. It's worth stepping back and framing
61
the trade-off between plan mode and direct execution. Plan mode has higher upfront overhead.
62
You wait for a proposal, read it, possibly edit it, and then approve. That overhead is justified when
63
the work is multi-file, ambiguous, or hard to reverse. If the plan catches a major wrong assumption
64
before any code is written, the total time is lower than discovering the mistake after execution.
65
Direct execution has lower overhead. Claude writes code immediately, which is faster for small,
66
reversible, single file changes where the cost of a mistake is low. The decision isn't plan mode
67
always versus never. It's matching the tool to the risk profile of the work. So the full iteration
68
loop is propose, review, edit, or approve, then execute. The approval gate is your primary control
69
point. Edit plans before approving to shape scope. Re-plan when assumptions break mid-execution.
70
Watch for silent improvisation and ask Claude to surface any divergence explicitly. Short,
71
explicit plans with clearly named files and stated risks are the most reliable to review and approve.

--- Four Common Hook Events ---

1
This lesson is about hooks in Claude code. Claude fires hooks for around 30
2
lifecycle events, but we'll focus on four commonly used ones. When each one fires,
3
what they can and can't do, and how exit codes control behavior. By the end, you'll
4
pick the right hook for any scenario. Let's jump in. A hook is a user-defined
5
script registered in Claude code settings. It fires automatically at a
6
specific lifecycle event during a session. The key thing about hooks is
7
that they run at the shell level, completely outside Claude's context
8
window. Claude doesn't see them, can't override them, and can't be prompted to
9
disable them. A pre-tool use hook fires before any tool call executes. A post
10
tool use hook fires after the tool call has already completed. Understanding this
11
before versus after distinction is foundational for everything else. The
12
timing of a hook determines what it can and can't do, and choosing the wrong one
13
is the most common hook implementation mistake. What makes hooks fundamentally
14
different from Claude.md instructions or system prompt rules? Shell level
15
execution. Hooks don't live in Claude's context. They don't compete with prompt
16
instructions. They run deterministically at lifecycle events, no matter what Claude
17
decides to do. You can't prompt Claude into skipping a registered hook. You
18
can't include instructions in Claude.md that override one. This is a hard
19
architectural boundary, and it's the key thing to understand about hook security.
20
For safety enforcement and policy automation, that determinism is exactly
21
what you need. Prompt instructions can be influenced, rephrased around, or simply
22
forgotten mid-session. A registered hook fires every single time on every
23
matching lifecycle event without exception. That's what makes it reliable.
24
The pre-tool use versus post-tool use comparison is worth spending real time
25
on. Pre-tool use fires before the tool runs. At that point, the action hasn't
26
happened yet, so your hook can inspect the pending call and decide whether to
27
allow it. An exit code of two blocks, the tool completely. Other non-zero codes are
28
non-blocking errors, and the tool still runs, so to enforce a block you have to
29
exit two specifically. This is the enforcement hook. If you need to prevent
30
file writes to sensitive paths, block certain shell commands, or validate
31
parameters, pre-tool use is your only option. Post-tool use fires after the
32
tool has already executed. The action is done. You can't undo it. You can log the
33
result, trigger a notification, or kick off downstream automation, but you can't
34
block retroactively. Register post-tool use hoping to prevent something and
35
you're already too late. The stop hook has a name that sounds like it exits the
36
session, but it doesn't. It fires when Claude reaches the end of its execution
37
loop for a given turn, just before control returns to the user. Think of it
38
as the end-of-turn hook, not the end-of- session hook. One session can trigger the
39
stop hook many times, once per agent turn. This makes it useful for per-turn
40
cleanup tasks, final validation of outputs, summary logging, or dispatching a
41
notification after a multi-step run completes. Because it fires after all
42
tool calls for the turn are done, you can use it to evaluate the full state of
43
what just happened, rather than reacting to individual tool calls in isolation.
44
Let's look at the four hook types this lesson centers on. Pre-tool use fires
45
before any tool call and can block with a non-zero exit. Post-tool use fires
46
after the tool finishes, best for logging and side effects. Stop fires at the end
47
of each agent turn, good for cleanup and per-turn summaries. Notification fires
48
when Claude emits an alert, and its purpose is routing, not blocking. The
49
easiest way to hold the wider set in your head is by cadence. Some events fire
50
once per session, like session start and session end. Some fire per turn, like a
51
prompt being submitted or the turn stopping. Some fire per tool call before
52
and after, and a few fire on specific moments, like right before a compaction.
53
Learn the cadence and any new event slots into place. The notification hook
54
is the one most often misunderstood. Its job is to route alert events to external
55
systems. When Claude emits a notification during a session, your registered
56
notification hook fires, and can forward that event to Slack, PagerDuty, an email
57
webhook, or any other system your team uses. What it can't do is modify or
58
block Claude's execution flow. It's purely a routing mechanism. The exam
59
specifically tests this. If a question asks how to block an action, the answer
60
is always pre-tool use, never notification. If a question asks how to
61
surface a Claude alert to an external system, the answer is the notification
62
hook. These are distinct jobs, and mixing them up is a common trap. Exit codes are
63
how hooks communicate intent back to Claude code. Exit 0 means continue. The
64
lifecycle event proceeds as planned. Exit 2 is the blocking signal. It stops the
65
action and sends stdar back to Claude. Any other non-zero code, like 1, is a
66
non-blocking error. Claude code logs it and proceeds anyway. This is the highest
67
frequency trap in the hooks topic. If you intend to enforce a block but return a
68
generic non-zero code, like 1, the tool will still run because only exit 2
69
blocks. Conversely, an accidental exit 2 in a logging hook will block every tool
70
call it fires on. The behavior looks like Claude is refusing to act, but the
71
actual cause is a misconfigured exit code. Always test your exit code logic
72
before deploying a hook into a live session. Choosing the right hook type
73
comes down to matching timing to intent. Ask yourself, does this need to happen
74
before the action or after it? If the answer is before and you might need to
75
stop the action, pre-tool use is the only option. If the answer is after and you
76
just need to react to what happened, post-tool use is the right choice. If you
77
need end-of-turn logic that sees the complete result of a full agent cycle,
78
stop is appropriate. If you need to route an alert to an external system, that's
79
notification. The mistake to avoid is using post-tool use when you actually
80
need to prevent something. By the time post-tool use fires, the tool has already
81
run. Prevention always requires pre-tool use. Hooks are the deterministic control
82
layer in Cloud Code. They fire at lifecycle events and can't be overridden
83
by prompt instructions. Pre-tool use blocks before an action. Post-tool use
84
reacts after an action. Stop handles end-of-turn cleanup. Notification routes
85
alerts out. Exit code 0 means continue. Exit code 2 means block. So that's four
86
of the most common hook events. That wraps up this lesson. I'll see you in the next one.

--- Implementing Hooks — Shell Scripts, Exit Codes, and Gotchas ---

1
This lesson is about implementing hooks correctly. We'll look at how to register hooks in settings,
2
structure shell scripts, apply exit code conventions, and avoid the gotchas that
3
catch practitioners off guard. A misconfigured hook exit code can silently stop Claude from acting,
4
and we'll cover exactly how to prevent that. Let's jump in. Before writing a single line of
5
hook logic, you need three terms nailed down. Hook registration is how you declare a hook.
6
You add an entry to Claude Code's settings file specifying the lifecycle event and the command
7
or script to run. The exit code convention is the protocol Claude Code uses to interpret what your
8
hook decided. Zero continues, exit two blocks, and feeds stdir to Claude, and any other non-zero code
9
is a non-blocking error that is logged while the tool still runs. Hook timeout is the time limit.
10
Claude Code will wait for your hook to respond before proceeding. A hook that hangs or calls
11
a slow external service will hold up every tool call it fires on until that timeout expires.
12
Understanding all three is essential before you write your first line of hook logic.
13
Hooks are registered in Claude Code's settings file, not in Claude.md, not in a system prompt,
14
not in a tool description. The settings file is where Claude Code looks for lifecycle event
15
handlers. Each entry names the event and provides a command. That command can be a short inline shell
16
expression or a path to an external script file. You can register more than one hook for the same
17
event and they'll run in parallel, so don't rely on one finishing before another. Identical handlers
18
get deduplicated. For anything beyond a one-liner, an external script file is strongly preferred.
19
It can be tested independently, added to version control, and reviewed by teammates. Inline commands
20
are convenient for quick logging, but they become maintenance problems fast once the logic grows.
21
A pre-tool use hook script inspects the pending tool call and decides whether to allow it.
22
Claude Code passes the call context to your script as JSON on standard input. That payload
23
carries the session ID, the working directory, the event name, the tool name, and the tool input.
24
Environment variables like the project directory path are there for resolving file paths, not for
25
carrying the tool name or arguments. Your script reads the JSON, applies your policy, and exits.
26
Exit 0 and the call proceeds. Exit 2 and it's blocked. Other non-zero codes are non-blocking,
27
so the call still runs. Get the exit logic right. A code path that reaches exit 2 when it shouldn't
28
silently blocks every matching call with no error and no diagnostic. Test every exit path with sample
29
inputs before going live. Should you write an inline command or an external script file?
30
Inline commands are fine for dead simple cases. If you want to log the tool name to a file on every
31
pre-tool use event, a one-liner works perfectly. But the moment your logic involves conditionals,
32
comparisons against a policy list, or calls to external services, move it to a script file.
33
Script files can be run directly from the terminal with test inputs. You can add unit tests around
34
the exit code paths. You can put them in version control and diff changes. You can share them across
35
multiple project settings. Inline commands offer none of that. The short-term convenience of an
36
inline command usually turns into a long-term debugging problem when something goes wrong.
37
Let's look at the three most common hook gotchas. First, the accidental exit. Two, you write a hook
38
that's supposed to log a warning and let the tool call proceed. But a code path in your script exits
39
with two under certain conditions. Now every tool call that hits that condition is blocked silently.
40
If it exited one instead, nothing would be blocked because exit one is non-blocking.
41
Cloud looks unresponsive. Second, the slow hook. You register a hook that calls an external API
42
or a slow database check. Every tool call now waits for that check. If the service is slow
43
or unreachable, your session stalls. Third, the wrong event type. You register post-tool use hoping
44
to prevent a bad action, but it fires after the tool has already run. None of these fail loudly.
45
They fail silently, and that's what makes them dangerous. Exit code two and exit code one do very
46
different things, and this is the detail people get backwards. Exit code two is what you return
47
to enforce a block. It signals a policy violation that should stop the call, and Cloud Code feeds
48
the script's state error back to Cloud. Exit code one or any other non-zero code is a non-blocking
49
error. The script is signaling a problem, but Cloud Code logs it and the tool still runs.
50
So if you want to actually stop an action, you have to exit. Two, returning one and expecting a block
51
is the single most common mistake here. Document your exit code meanings directly in the script
52
as a comment. Future maintainers, including yourself six months from now, will thank you.
53
Three safety patterns come up repeatedly in hook implementations. The allow list pattern defines an
54
approved set of tool names or argument values. Anything not on the list exits non-zero and is
55
blocked. This is the more conservative default. Unknown equals blocked. The block list pattern
56
defines known dangerous values to reject. Anything not explicitly blocked exits zero and proceeds.
57
This is easier to write initially, but weaker by default, since anything you haven't thought of
58
is implicitly allowed. The dry run pattern adds a mode flag to your hook that logs what it would
59
block without actually exiting non-zero. You run in dry run mode first to observe what the hook
60
would do across real sessions, then enable enforcement once you're confident in the logic.
61
Testing hooks before deployment is non-negotiable for pre-tool use hooks
62
because a broken pre-tool use hook silently breaks Cloud's ability to act.
63
The testing process has three steps. First, run the script manually from the terminal with sample
64
inputs that cover normal cases and edge cases. Verify the exit code for each path. Second, use
65
dry run mode if your hook supports it. Run a real session and watch the logs to see what the hook
66
would have blocked. Third, add explicit messages to standard error for every blocking exit.
67
When a tool call is blocked, a diagnostic message in stderr tells you exactly which condition
68
triggered it. Without that message, a block looks identical to Cloud simply refusing to act,
69
and you'll spend significant time debugging the wrong layer. Let's recap on hooks. Hook
70
implementation comes down to registering in settings, controlling behavior with exit codes,
71
and testing every path before deployment. Exit 0 continues, exit 2 blocks. Any other non-zero code
72
is non-blocking, so an accidental exit 2 is what silently blocks tool calls. Just remember,
73
a misconfigured hook fails silently and looks identical to Cloud refusing to act.
74
That wraps up this lesson. I'll see you in the next one.

--- Real-World Hook Applications — Logging, Linting, Safety Nets ---

1
This lesson is about putting hooks to work in real engineering scenarios.
2
We'll walk through six patterns, auto-formatting on file write,
3
blocking destructive bash commands,
4
logging tool calls for audit trails,
5
sending notifications on task completion,
6
running tests automatically after edits,
7
and tracking API costs.
8
Each pattern maps to a specific hook type.
9
Let's dive in. A handful of terms anchor the patterns we'll cover.
10
An auto-format hook is a post-tool use hook
11
that runs a code formatter automatically after any file is written.
12
It keeps every file in the correct style
13
without requiring anyone to remember to run the formatter manually.
14
A safety net hook is a pre-tool use hook
15
that inspects pending bash commands for dangerous patterns
16
and blocks them before they execute.
17
An audit log hook is a post-tool use hook
18
that records every tool call with a timestamp,
19
creating a traceable history of what happened during the session.
20
These three concepts cover the most common reasons
21
developers reach for hooks.
22
The auto-format pattern is one of the most common hook use cases.
23
Developers forget to run formatters manually,
24
and inconsistent style creates noise in code reviews
25
and slows down pull request cycles.
26
The solution is a post-tool use hook
27
that fires after any write or edit call,
28
then immediately runs black, prettier,
29
or whatever formatter the project requires.
30
Post-tool use is the write hook type here
31
because the file already exists on disk when the hook fires.
32
The formatter edits the saved file,
33
and the result is style-compliant code on every write
34
with zero extra effort from the developer or from Claude.
35
It's one of the fastest wins a team can get from hooks.
36
The safety net pattern uses pre-tool use
37
because pre-tool use fires before the command executes.
38
That timing is everything.
39
The hook inspects the incoming bash command string
40
and checks it against a list of dangerous patterns,
41
rm, dash, rf, drop table, format disk,
42
and similar destructive operations.
43
If a match is found, the hook returns exit code two,
44
and Claude's execution stops immediately.
45
Exit code two is the one that blocks.
46
Exit code one and other non-zero codes
47
are treated as non-blocking errors,
48
so the command still runs.
49
A post-tool use hook can't help here
50
because post-tool use fires after the action
51
is already done.
52
Prevention requires pre-tool use.
53
Knowing which hook type to reach for based on timing
54
is one of the most important hook concepts to internalize.
55
Audit logging has several implementation flavors
56
depending on what the team needs.
57
A simple append-only log writes the tool name,
58
arguments, and timestamp as plain text
59
after each tool call.
60
It's easy to implement and readable without tooling.
61
A structured JSON log stores each entry as a JSON object,
62
which makes it easy to filter and query programmatically.
63
Remote logging pushes entries to an external endpoint
64
for centralized storage across many sessions,
65
which is useful in team environments.
66
A stop hook adds a final summary entry
67
when the agent finishes its run,
68
giving the log a clean end marker.
69
The right format depends on whether the log
70
is consumed by humans reading a terminal
71
or by machines running queries.
72
It's worth being clear about when pre-tool use
73
versus post-tool use is the right choice for logging.
74
Pre-tool use logging records intent before execution.
75
It captures what was attempted,
76
including actions that were subsequently blocked.
77
That's useful for detecting policy violations
78
because a blocked action still leaves a record
79
in a pre-tool use log.
80
Post-tool use logging records results after execution.
81
It captures what actually succeeded,
82
including output and side effects.
83
If you need a record of completed work, use post-tool use.
84
If you need to catch attempted violations,
85
pre-tool use gives you visibility into blocked actions
86
as well as completed ones.
87
Many teams run both hooks together
88
to get a complete picture of agent behavior
89
from intent all the way through execution.
90
Notification hooks use the stop lifecycle event.
91
Stop fires once when the agent reaches the end
92
of its execution loop, which makes it a natural trigger
93
for completion signals.
94
A typical implementation calls a Slack webhook,
95
sends a system notification,
96
or posts to a monitoring endpoint.
97
The payload should include the task name, elapsed time,
98
and whether the session ended cleanly or with an error.
99
This pattern is most useful for long running batch jobs
100
where the developer steps away and needs to know
101
when results are ready without pulling the terminal.
102
It decouples the developer's attention
103
from Claude's execution time,
104
which is especially valuable on tasks
105
that take 10 or 20 minutes to complete.
106
Test run hooks close the feedback loop
107
between writing code and confirming it works.
108
The hook fires post-tool use on write or edit calls,
109
then checks the file path to decide whether to run tests.
110
You don't want the full test suite
111
to execute every time Claude edits a documentation file
112
or a configuration comment.
113
Scoping the hook to test relevant paths,
114
like anything under SRC or Libby,
115
limits execution to meaningful changes.
116
When the path matches, the hook runs PyTest, Jest,
117
or whatever runner the project uses via a bash call.
118
The developer sees test results
119
immediately after each code edit,
120
with no context switch required.
121
That tight feedback loop makes it much harder
122
for a broken test to slip through unnoticed.
123
Cost tracking hooks monitor API usage at the session level.
124
A post-tool use hook parses usage
125
from the tool and transcript output where it's available,
126
recording the model name, a timestamp,
127
and any token counts the payload exposes.
128
It appends that record to a session cost file
129
or pushes it to a local database for aggregation.
130
Reviewing the data across a session surfaces,
131
which prompts are expensive
132
and whether overall usage is trending toward budget limits.
133
Teams running automated pipelines
134
use this pattern to catch runaway sessions
135
before they generate unexpected invoices.
136
It's a lightweight addition
137
that pays off quickly on high-volume workflows.
138
Without it, cost overruns often go unnoticed
139
until the billing cycle ends and the damage is already done.
140
Hooks become most useful when you match the pattern
141
to the right lifecycle event,
142
post-tool use for reactions and logging,
143
pre-tool use for safety and blocking,
144
stop for completion signals, and final cleanup.
145
That wraps it up for practical hook patterns.
146
I'll see you in the next one.

--- The Claude Agent SDK — Session Control ---

1
This lesson covers the Cloud Agent SDK, formerly called the Cloud Code SDK,
2
the programmatic interface for controlling sessions from your own code. We'll look at
3
what it provides, how to create and manage sessions, how to inject context, how to stream
4
output and tool events, and when to pick it over a direct API call. Let's jump in.
5
Start with the vocabulary. The Cloud Agent SDK, which used to be called the Cloud Code SDK,
6
is a programmatic interface that lets you integrate Cloud Code into custom applications,
7
scripts, and pipelines. Instead of typing commands into a terminal, you call SDK methods
8
from your code to start sessions, inject prompts, and handle output. A programmatic session is a
9
Cloud Code session you create and manage through the SDK, rather than the interactive CLI.
10
Your application controls when sessions start and stop, what prompts they receive,
11
and how their output is processed. Session streaming is the capability to receive tool
12
call events and output incrementally as the session runs. That gives you visibility into
13
every step without waiting for a complete response. The SDK and the command line interface expose the
14
same underlying Cloud Code capabilities. The difference is who or what is driving the session.
15
With the CLI, a developer types commands into a terminal and reads results interactively.
16
That works well for one-off tasks and exploratory work. With the SDK, your code calls methods,
17
passes prompts programmatically, and processes results however your application needs.
18
