There's no terminal involved at all. Use the CLI when a human is in the loop and driving the work.
19
Switch to the SDK when your code needs to be in the driver's seat, whether that's a script,
20
a pipeline step, or a long-running background service. This is the decision that trips up a
21
lot of developers. When do you use the SDK versus calling the Cloud API directly? The SDK is for
22
agentic sessions. It manages the full agent loop, multi-step tool use, file edits, shell commands,
23
and streaming output. You give it a prompt and it handles everything in between. The direct API is
24
for model calls. Send a prompt, get a completion. There's no session state and no built-in tool
25
orchestration unless you build it yourself. If you need the agent loop, use the SDK. If you need a
26
single model call, use the API. Creating a session programmatically starts with instantiating a
27
session object in your code. You configure it with the model you want to use, the tool categories
28
you're allowing, the working directory, and any environment variables the session needs to access.
29
Once configured, you pass prompts as plain strings. There's no terminal, no interactive input,
30
no manual step. The SDK handles the session loop from there. This approach makes it straightforward
31
to embed cloud code in Python scripts, Node.js services, CI runners, or any other runtime
32
environment that can call the SDK libraries. The session behaves the same way regardless of how
33
it's invoked. Once a session is running, you can shape its behavior by injecting context and
34
instructions at different points. At creation time, you pass a system prompt to establish role,
35
tone, and behavioral constraints before any user turn begins. You can also inject file
36
contents or directory listings so the session starts with relevant code-based knowledge
37
already loaded. Tool permissions are set at creation too, letting you explicitly allow or
38
restrict read, write, and shell access to enforce safety boundaries. And you can append follow-up
39
prompts mid-session to redirect the agent without restarting, so it keeps the context it's already
40
accumulated. Streaming is one of the most useful SDK features for production applications. Instead
41
of blocking until the session completes, your code receives events as they happen in real time.
42
Tool call events fire each time the agent reads a file, writes output, or runs a shell command.
43
That lets you log exactly what the agent is doing without waiting for the session to finish.
44
Output chunks arrive incrementally, so you can display live progress or pipe results downstream.
45
Error events surface failures immediately, giving your code a chance to react,
46
retry, or alert an operator. For long-running sessions, streaming isn't a nice-to-have.
47
It's the difference between a responsive application and one that hangs silently
48
waiting for a final result. The SDK's primary use case is embedding clod code capabilities
49
into automated systems your team already owns. Code review bots can trigger SDK sessions
50
when a pull request opens, stream the analysis, and post results directly into GitHub comments
51
without any human intervention. Documentation generators can pass source files into a session
52
and receive updated docs in return. CI and CD pipelines can launch clod code sessions as
53
explicit pipeline steps, with structured exit codes that let the rest of the pipeline react
54
to what happened. Any workflow that benefits from agentic code understanding but needs to
55
run inside a larger automated system is a strong candidate for SDK integration.
56
Let's make the SDK versus API decision concrete. Reach for the SDK when you need
57
multi-step agentic behavior, file edits, shell commands, tool orchestration, or anything that
58
involves the full agent loop running over multiple turns. Reach for the SDK when you need real-time
59
observability into what the agent is doing via streaming events. Use the direct API when all you
60
need is a single model completion with no tool use and no session state to maintain. Use the
61
direct API when you're building your own custom agent framework and want to manage the orchestration
62
yourself. The SDK is built for agentic complexity. The API is built for straightforward model calls.
63
The clod agent SDK gives you full programmatic control over agentic sessions, from creation and
64
context injection through streaming output and tool event handling. It's the right tool when
65
your code, not a human, needs to drive a multi-step workflow. So that's SDK for
66
programmatic session control. I'll see you in the next lesson.

--- Claude Code in CI/CD — Non-Interactive Mode and the -p Flag ---

1
This lesson covers Claude code in automated CICD pipelines.
2
We'll look at why non-interactive mode is required,
3
how the dash p flag enables it, how to structure tasks for automation,
4
GitHub actions integration patterns, and the security considerations that matter
5
most in automated contexts. Before we go further, let's pin down
6
the language. The dash p flag, also called print mode,
7
is a Claude code CLI flag that switches the agent to
8
non-interactive operation. When you pass dash p,
9
Claude code accepts the prompt you give it, runs the task,
10
prints the result to standard output, and exits cleanly.
11
No interactive prompt, no waiting for more input.
12
Non-interactive mode is the execution mode that dash p
13
activates. In this mode, the agent is a one-shot tool.
14
It takes a task, completes it, and exits. Pipeline job hang is what happens when
15
you forget dash p in a CICD context. Claude code waits for
16
interactive input the pipeline runner can never provide,
17
and the job blocks until it times out. Let's talk about why dash p is
18
non-negotiable in automated pipelines. When a CICD runner launches a process,
19
there's no human present to answer interactive prompts.
20
Claude code's default mode is interactive. It opens a session and
21
waits. In a runner environment with no
22
terminal, that wait never ends. The job hangs,
23
consumes runner minutes, and eventually times out.
24
The dash p flag changes this completely. It tells Claude code to run its full
25
agentic loop non-interactively. It reads the prompt,
26
does the work, calling tools across as many turns as
27
the task needs, then writes output to standard output,
28
and exits with a status code the pipeline can act on.
29
The loop still runs to completion. It just never stops to ask a human for
30
input. That's the contract automation needs.
31
In practice, you rarely pass dash p alone.
32
It comes with a small set of companion flags that make a headless run
33
predictable. Output format is the big one.
34
Pass output format JSON and Claude code returns a structured result
35
the next pipeline step can parse instead of scraping free text.
36
There's also a streaming variant, stream JSON,
37
that emits events as they happen if you want live progress.
38
Allowed tools lets you scope exactly which tools the run is permitted to
39
invoke, which matters a lot when no human is
40
watching. And if a job should build on an earlier session
41
rather than start clean, resume picks up a specific past session
42
and continue picks up the most recent one. Underneath all of them,
43
the contract stays the same. Output goes to standard output,
44
exit code zero means success, and any non-zero code means something went wrong.
45
Structuring tasks well is what makes automation reliable.
46
Four principles matter here. First, keep each invocation atomic.
47
One task, one outcome. Don't ask Claude code to lint a file and update
48
documentation in the same call. Second, write prompts that are fully
49
self-contained. The agent has no memory of previous
50
pipeline runs unless you explicitly include that
51
context in the prompt. Third, request structured output, usually
52
JSON, so the next pipeline step can parse the
53
result without brittle text matching. Fourth, design tasks to be idempotent.
54
Pipelines retry on failure, so if the same task runs twice,
55
the result should be the same both times.
56
Interactive mode and non-interactive mode are worth comparing directly.
57
Interactive mode is the default. Claude code opens a REPL session and carries on
58
a multi-turn conversation with you. It's designed for developer workstations
59
where a human is present and engaged throughout the session.
60
You can ask follow-up questions, refine tasks, and iterate in real time.
61
Non-interactive mode, activated by dash p, is the opposite. Claude code accepts
62
one prompt, does the work, and exits. There's no
63
session, no conversation, no waiting for more input. These two
64
modes are mutually exclusive for a given invocation.
65
Any scenario describing automation, scheduled jobs,
66
or CICD integration calls for non-interactive mode and the dash p
67
flag. For GitHub Actions specifically, there's
68
an official integration, and it's the path you should reach for.
69
It's the Claude code action workflow, and you set it up by running the install
70
github app command, which wires up the action and the
71
github app for you. Once it's in place, you trigger
72
Claude code by mentioning it Claude in an issue or pull request,
73
or by passing a prompt directly to the action.
74
You still store your Anthropic API key as a GitHub Actions
75
secret, so it never lands in your workflow file.
76
Now, you can run a raw Claude dash p command inside an action step,
77
but that's really just General Agent SDK automation running in CI,
78
not the documented GitHub Actions integration.
79
When a scenario points specifically at GitHub Actions,
80
reach for the official action. In automated contexts,
81
permission scoping is a security requirement, not just a best practice.
82
The principle is minimum necessary permissions. If your task only needs to
83
read files, don't grant write access. If the task
84
is purely file-based, restrict or disable network tools.
85
If Claude code only needs to touch one directory, scope it to that directory.
86
Why does this matter in automation? Because pipelines run unattended.
87
If a task behaves unexpectedly due to a bad prompt or unexpected input,
88
narrow permissions limit how much damage can happen.
89
Broad permissions in an automated context are a risk
90
you don't need to take. Let's talk about output parsing and error handling,
91
because these are where automation breaks down in practice.
92
Capture Claude code's standard output into a shell variable
93
or a file immediately. Don't assume it will always return structured data.
94
If something goes wrong, the agent may return a plain error message.
95
Check the exit code after every invocation. A non-zero exit should fail
96
the pipeline step. If you request a JSON, validate its shape
97
before using it downstream, and always set a timeout on Claude code
98
pipeline steps. A task that stalls for any reason will
99
hold up your entire pipeline until the runner's global timeout fires,
100
which is usually much longer than you want.
101
The dash p flag is the foundation of every Claude code automation pattern.
102
It converts the agent from an interactive tool into a composable
103
pipeline step. Pair it with tight permission scoping,
104
structured output, and solid error handling, and you have a reliable
105
building block for any automated workflow. Quick recap
106
though. Forget dash p and your CI jobs will hang.
107
Add it and your pipeline gets a predictable, composable step.
108
That wraps up this one. I'll see you in the next video.

--- Clarity, Specificity, and the Cost of Ambiguity ---

1
Welcome back. This lesson is about clarity and specificity in prompt instructions.
2
We'll look at why vague instructions produce inconsistent outputs,
3
why specificity is the primary lever for output quality, how the direct instruction principle
4
works, how ambiguity forces the model to fill gaps with assumptions, and what imprecise prompts
5
actually cost across a full iteration cycle. Let's get into it. Start with the vocabulary.
6
Specificity is the degree to which an instruction precisely defines the expected output.
7
When specificity is high, the model has very little room to interpret or fill gaps on its own.
8
Ambiguity is the opposite condition. When an instruction has more than one plausible
9
interpretation, the model has to choose between them. That choice is never visible to you,
10
and it may not be the choice you intended. The direct instruction principle is the practice
11
of stating what you want the model to do rather than what you want it to avoid. Negative framing
12
tells the model what to skip, but leaves the positive goal undefined, which introduces a
13
different kind of ambiguity. Let's start with why vague instructions fail. When a prompt is
14
open-ended, the model doesn't stall or ask for clarification. It selects among several valid
15
interpretations and produces output based on the one it picks. Each plausible interpretation
16
produces a different output. Run the same vague prompt twice, and you get two different results.
17
That variance isn't a model defect. It's a direct reflection of how much room the prompt leaves
18
for interpretation. The problem compounds at the back end. Debugging a vague output means
19
re-examining the prompt to find what gap the model filled, not just reading what came back.
20
Precision at the prompt stage prevents that entire class of problems.
21
Let's make the difference between abstract and concrete instructions visible with a direct
22
comparison. An abstract instruction might say, write a summary of this document. That prompt
23
forces the model to decide length, format, tone, level of detail, intended audience, and what counts
24
as the key points. Every one of those decisions is an assumption, and none of them are visible
25
to you. A concrete version of the same instruction might say, write a three-sentence summary of this
26
document for a non-technical audience, including the main finding, one supporting detail, and the
27
recommended action. The second version closes all six of those interpretation gaps in a single
28
sentence. The output becomes predictable because the decision space is closed. Specificity is the
29
single most reliable variable under your control when writing instructions. Every added dimension
30
of specificity narrows the output space further. Specifying format removes ambiguity about
31
structure. Specifying length removes ambiguity about scope. Specifying tone and perspective
32
removes ambiguity about voice. Each constraint compounds on the others. A prompt with four
33
specific constraints produces a much narrower output space than a prompt with one, and that
34
narrower space is where consistent, repeatable outputs live. When an output disappoints, the
35
right question is almost always, what did I leave unspecified that the model had to assume?
36
Identifying that gap and closing it is usually the fastest path to the output you actually want.
37
The direct instruction principle says to state what you want the model to do,
38
not what you want it to avoid. Negative framing, like don't be vague or avoid jargon, tells the
39
model what to subtract, but leaves the positive goal undefined. The model still has to guess what
40
the output should look like. Direct instructions, by contrast, map to concrete actions. Write,
41
summarize, list, compare, extract, classify. Each of those verbs specifies a clear operation.
42
Keeping one goal per instruction also matters. Compound instructions that combine multiple
43
objectives split the model's focus and reduce the reliability of each individual goal.
44
State one goal, then layer constraints on top of it. When a prompt contains ambiguity, the model
45
doesn't pause and ask for clarification. It resolves the ambiguity by selecting the most
46
statistically likely interpretation and proceeds with that choice. The model isn't doing anything
47
wrong here. It's doing exactly what it was designed to do, but the interpretation it picks may not
48
match what you intended, and neither party is aware that a different interpretation was even
49
possible. The output looks plausible because it is plausible, it just isn't what you wanted.
50
That's the particular danger of ambiguity. It doesn't produce obviously broken outputs that
51
are easy to identify. It produces subtly wrong outputs that look fine on first read.
52
Let's talk about the actual cost of ambiguous prompts, because it shows up in places that
53
are easy to underestimate. Variance in outputs means you can't build reliable expectations
54
about what a prompt will return. Unpredictability makes quality evaluation harder, because you have
55
to account for the fact that the same prompt might perform differently tomorrow. Debugging
56
ambiguous outputs is expensive. You have to go back to the prompt, identify what gap the model filled,
57
decide what you actually wanted, and rewrite. Do that across dozens of iterations, and the time
58
adds up fast. The cost of ambiguity is hidden at writing time and fully paid at review time,
59
which is the worst possible accounting. There are four reliable ways to turn a vague
60
instruction into a specific one. Add a format. Tell the model whether you want a bullet list,
61
numbered steps, a table, or a paragraph. That single constraint eliminates a major category
62
of interpretation gaps. Add a length. Specify word count, sentence count, or a natural bound like
63
in one paragraph. Add an audience. Naming who will read the output determines tone,
64
vocabulary level, and assumed background knowledge. Add a perspective, first person,
65
third person, or a named role like senior analyst. Each of these dimensions is a constraint you can
66
apply independently, and any one of them meaningfully narrows the output space.
67
Specificity is the fastest and most reliable path from a vague prompt to a consistent,
68
high-quality output. Every ambiguity in an instruction is a gap the model fills
69
with an assumption you can't see. Direct instructions, concrete constraints, and
70
positive framing close those gaps before the model has a chance to make choices for you.
71
So that's clarity and specificity. Next up, decomposing instructions and designing
72
the constraints that keep outputs reliable. I'll see you there.

--- Instruction Decomposition and Constraint Design ---

1
Welcome back. Let me walk you through instruction decomposition and constraint design.
2
Breaking complex instructions into ordered steps, defining scope, encoding format and length, and tone constraints,
3
knowing when to split one instruction into several, and testing whether an instruction is specific
4
enough to be reliable.
5
These are the mechanics we're covering in this lesson. Let's jump into it.
6
Before we go further, let's pin down the language. Instruction decomposition means breaking a
7
complex task into a sequence of simpler,
8
ordered sub-instructions, each with a single clear goal. Decomposition reduces ambiguity and makes
9
outputs much easier to verify at each stage.
10
Scope definition is the practice of explicitly naming what to include, what to exclude, and what to
11
assume in an instruction.
12
Scope definition closes the most common interpretation gaps without requiring you to specify every
13
detail of the output.
14
Constraint encoding is the translation of quality requirements into specific instruction parameters.
15
Format, length, tone, and perspective are the four primary constraint types, and each one closes a
16
different category of interpretation gap.
17
Why does decomposition matter? When a single instruction contains multiple goals, the model has to
18
balance them simultaneously.
19
That competition reduces the quality of every individual outcome. A task split into clear sequential
20
steps consistently produces better results
21
than the same task crammed into one large prompt, and it's also much easier to manage and review.
22
Each step can be checked independently before the next one runs. If a step produces unexpected output,
23
you know exactly where the problem occurred and can fix it there.
24
That precision is impossible when everything happens in a single call.
25
Decomposition converts one large, hard-to-debug prompt into a series of smaller, verifiable steps.
26
Ordered decomposition means assigning each phase of a task its own instruction, with a clear sequence between them.
27
For a three-phase task, step one might extract the relevant information from the source.
28
Step two summarizes the extracted content according to a specific format.
29
Step three reviews the summary for gaps or inconsistencies.
30
Each step has one goal, one expected output type, and one criterion you can use to verify it.
31
When phase one completes and you check the extraction, you know the input to phase two is sound before you run it.
32
That sequencing is what makes complex tasks tractable.
33
You're solving three simple problems in order, not one complicated problem all at once.
34
Scope definition is one of the most underused tools in instruction writing.
35
Most interpretation gaps come from things that weren't said, rather than things that were said badly.
36
Scope definition addresses that directly by forcing you to name the edges of the task explicitly.
37
What should the output include? List those elements by name. What should it exclude?
38
State that directly rather than hoping the model infers it.
39
What can the model treat as already established context?
40
Name those assumptions so the model doesn't hedge or re-explain background you've already covered.
41
And where does the task end?
42
A clear stopping point prevents the model from continuing past the intended output.
43
Defining all four of those boundaries turns an open-ended task into a closed one.
44
The comparison between a single complex instruction and a decomposed one is worth making concrete.
45
Consider a contract analysis task. A single instruction might say,
46
analyze this contract, identify the key risks, summarize each party's obligations,
47
and flag any clauses that conflict with standard practice.
48
That's four distinct goals in one call.
49
The model will attempt all four, but each one gets reduced attention,
50
and the outputs are harder to check individually.
51
Decomposed, the same task becomes three separate calls.
52
List every obligation per party, assess each obligation as standard or unusual,
53
then summarize only the unusual ones. Each step has one goal.
54
The output of each step feeds into the next. Every step is verifiable on its own.
55
Constraints are parameters that narrow the output space without changing what the core task is asking for.
56
There are four primary types. Format specifies the structure of the output.
57
Bullet list, numbered steps, table, or prose paragraph.
58
Length specifies the quantity, word count, sentence count, or a natural boundary like in two paragraphs.
59
Tone specifies the register, formal, informal, technical, plain language, or a specific stylistic target.
60
Perspective specifies the point of view, first person, third person, or a named role or persona.
61
Each type closes a different category of interpretation gap.
62
Applying all four to a single instruction isn't over-engineering.
63
It's closing the four most common sources of output variance in one pass.
64
Not every task needs decomposition, but certain signals tell you when splitting is the right move.
65
If the task has distinct phases that produce different kinds of output,
66
those phases should be separate instructions.
67
If you need to verify one phase before the next phase can run correctly, decompose.
68
If the instruction requires more than two or three constraints to be even roughly precise,
69
that's a sign the task is doing too much in one call.
70
And if past attempts with a single prompt consistently missed part of the task,
71
that's the clearest signal of all.
72
When you find yourself stacking constraint after constraint to keep a single instruction from going wrong,
73
splitting is almost always faster and more reliable than trying to make one large prompt airtight.
74
Testing for specificity means asking structured questions about your instruction before you run it.
75
The interpretation test asks,
76
Could a reasonable person read this in more than one way?
77
If yes, find the ambiguity and close it.
78
The gap test asks,
79
What would the model have to assume to complete this?
80
List those assumptions explicitly, then decide which ones to resolve in the instruction
81
and which ones are safe to leave implicit.
82
The output preview asks you to mentally trace through what a plausible response looks like.
83
If two different outputs both seem like reasonable answers to your instruction,
84
that's the signal you need another constraint.
85
The scope check asks whether the instruction has a clear stopping point.
86
If it could go on indefinitely, add a boundary.
87
Decomposing instructions and encoding constraints are the two core mechanics of writing prompts
88
that behave predictably at scale.
89
Ordering steps produce verifiable outputs.
90
Scope definitions close interpretation gaps before the model encounters them.
91
Format, length, tone, and perspective constraints
92
each eliminate a category of variance in the model.
93
Bottom line, decomposed instructions are easier to verify, debug, and improve.
94
Coming up, the system prompt.
95
Its purpose, structure, and the practices that make it reliable.
96
I'll see you there.

--- The System Prompt — Purpose, Structure, and Best Practices ---

1
When you're building any application on Clod, the system prompt is what shapes every response in a
2
session. We'll cover what it does, how to structure it, and the practices that make it reliable.
3
It lives before the human turn, it's invisible to end users, and getting it right is one of the
4
highest leveraged things you can do in Clod. Let's jump in. There's a small vocabulary that frames
5
every system prompt decision, so let's lock it in first. The system prompt is the special
6
instruction block passed to the model before any human message. It establishes role, behavior,
7
constraints, and background context. End users never see it, but it shapes every reply. The user
8
turn is the human message in each exchange. It carries the immediate request and any data specific
9
to that request. Persistent context is information that applies to every exchange in the session,
10
placed once in the system prompt rather than repeated in each user turn.
11
These three concepts define the architecture of how you communicate with the model at scale.
12
One of the most important architectural decisions is knowing what goes in the system prompt
13
versus the user turn. The system prompt is for things that stay constant, the model's role,
14
standing behavioral rules, formatting requirements, and background knowledge the model needs for every
15
response. The user turn is for things that change, the specific request, dynamic data, per request
16
variables. Mixing these two has real consequences. If you put dynamic data in the system prompt,
17
you lose the ability to cache it efficiently and inflate your token count every time. If you put
18
standing rules in the user turn, they can be inadvertently overridden or ignored in a multi-turn
19
conversation. Keep the boundary clean. A well-structured system prompt has five sections,
20
each doing a distinct job. Role defines who the model is, what expertise it brings, how it
21
communicates, what professional identity it holds. Context provides the background information the
22
model needs for the whole session. Domain knowledge, company policy, reference data.
23
Instructions specify what the model should do. Task steps, response format, workflow behavior.
24
Constraints define what the model should never do. Prohibited topics, tone restrictions, length
25
limits. Examples show how instructions translate to actual outputs. Each section answers a different
26
question, and when a section is missing, the model fills that gap with defaults that may not match
27
your intent. Persistent context versus per request context is one of the most common points
28
of confusion when designing prompt architectures. Persistent context belongs in the system prompt
29
because it applies identically to every turn. A support bot's policy document is persistent.
30
Its response format rules are persistent. The customer's specific account balance is per
31
request. It changes per user, per query, and has no place in the system prompt.
32
Putting per request data in the system prompt means that data is resent and reprocessed on
33
every single exchange, driving up token costs unnecessarily. Injecting it in the user turn
34
scopes it precisely to the exchange where it matters. There is a real tension between writing
35
a thorough system prompt and controlling token cost. A thorough prompt covers edge cases, spells
36
out formatting rules, and includes examples. It reduces ambiguity and produces more consistent
37
responses. But the full system prompt is resent with every request, so every additional word adds
38
to your per request token, count, and latency. A minimal prompt is cheaper and faster. The risk is
39
that the model fills unaddressed gaps with its own defaults, and those defaults may not match
40
what you need. The practical approach is to start with what you need for correctness,
41
then trim language that is redundant or overly prescriptive, measuring consistency at each step.
42
The order of sections inside a system prompt matters more than most people expect.
43
Lead with role, because it frames how the model interprets every instruction that follows.
44
If the model knows it is a financial compliance analyst before it reads the instructions,
45
those instructions land differently than if role comes last. Instructions should come before
46
constraints, so desired behavior is defined before limits are placed on it. Examples should come last,
47
illustrating rules already stated, rather than front-loading patterns the model has to reverse
48
engineer into principles. And any high-priority instructions should sit near the top of the
49
relevant section, because models weight content at the beginning of a long context window more
50
heavily than content buried near the end. Conflicting instructions are one of the
51
hardest bugs to catch in a system prompt, because they don't always surface on the obvious test
52
inputs. A common example, telling the model to be concise while also instructing it to always explain
53
its reasoning. For simple tasks, these directives coexist fine. For complex tasks, they pull the
54
model in opposite directions, and the output becomes inconsistent depending on which instruction
55
the model weighted more heavily. The fix is to use conditional phrasing. Be concise unless the user
56
explicitly asks for an explanation. That disambiguates the competing rules, rather than
57
leaving the model to resolve them implicitly. Audit your prompt for pairs of rules that could
58
simultaneously apply and rewrite them as conditionals. System prompt testing should be treated
59
as seriously as code testing. The single happy path test case isn't enough. You need to test across
60
diverse phrasings of the same request to confirm that behavior is consistent regardless of how users
61
phrase things. You need adversarial inputs that directly try to contradict your constraints to
62
verify those constraints hold. You need boundary conditions. Very long inputs, empty inputs,
63
sudden topic switches in the middle of a session. And every time you edit the system prompt, you
64
need a regression pass to confirm that changes improved what you intended and didn't break what
65
was already working. A system prompt that hasn't been tested under pressure is an unknown risk.
66
Quick recap. The system prompt sets persistent context. The user turn carries per request context.
67
Structure your prompt with five sections. Role, context, instructions, constraints, and examples.
68
Put stable information in the system prompt and dynamic data in user turns. Order sections with
69
role first and high priority rules near the top. And that's the system prompt. Next, we'll look at
70
role assignment, persona design, and injection context. I'll see you there.

--- Role Assignment, Persona Design, and Context Setting ---

1
This lesson is about identity and grounding, how you tell the model who it is and what it knows.
2
We'll compare role assignment to persona design, look at how context injection anchors responses
3
to specific facts, and build a framework for designing identities that stay consistent
4
across the full range of real-world user inputs. Role assignment is the instruction that defines
5
the model's functional expertise, the professional identity and knowledge framing. You want it to
6
operate from. It typically goes at the very start of the system prompt because it frames everything
7
else. Persona extends role assignment by adding a named identity and specific personality traits.
8
Where role assignment says you are a financial analyst, persona says you are Jordan,
9
a plain-spoken analyst who avoids jargon and uses numbered lists. Context injection is the
10
act of placing background information directly into the prompt. Documents, records, policies,
11
or retrieved data the model needs to reason over. These three concepts work together to define who
12
the model is and what it knows. Role assignment changes the model's behavior in concrete,
13
measurable ways. When you tell Claude it is a senior security analyst, its responses shift
14
toward technical framing, risk-focused reasoning, and precise domain vocabulary. When you tell it
15
to be a customer support agent, it shifts toward empathetic language, solution-oriented structure,
16
and a lower assumed technical literacy in the reader. The same question phrased identically
17
produces noticeably different outputs depending on role. This isn't a coincidence. The role statement
18
activates different patterns of knowledge framing and communication style. For practitioners,
19
role assignment is the fastest and most reliable lever for aligning default model behavior
20
with a specific use case without rewriting every instruction from scratch.
21
The distinction between role assignment and persona is one that the CCAF curriculum draws
22
explicitly because it has practical architectural implications. Role assignment is purely functional.
23
It tells the model what expertise to bring and how to reason. It requires no name and no
24
personality description. Persona goes further. It gives the model a name, a voice, and specific
25
stylistic constraints. A persona might include, you are Aria. You write in a warm encouraging tone.
26
You never use bullet points. You always end responses with an open question.
27
These are stylistic and identity specifications that go well beyond functional expertise.
28
Both live in the system prompt, but they serve different design purposes,
29
and conflating them leads to underspecified prompts that produce inconsistent behavior.
30
Context injection is how you give the model facts. It can't get from training alone.
31
Training knowledge has a cutoff date and may not include your organization's internal policies,
32
the customer's specific account record, or the document the user just uploaded.
33
Context injection solves this by placing relevant information directly in the prompt,
34
where the model can reason over it in real time. The key architectural point is that
35
injection into the system prompt is persistent for the session, while injection into the user
36
turn is scoped to that one exchange. If the policy document applies to every request,
37
it belongs in the system prompt. If the customer's order details are specific to one interaction,
38
they belong in the user turn. Getting this placement wrong is a common source of both
39
wasted tokens and unexpected model behavior. Thinking clearly about placement saves token
40
cost and prevents behavioral drift. Content that stays the same across every exchange goes in the
41
system prompt. That means the model's role, standing policies, and reference data it needs
42
every time. Content that varies per exchange goes in the user turn. That means per user data,
43
per request documents, and dynamic variables. You may see older material describe an assistant
44
prefill pattern where you cede a partial reply to steer format. On current models,
45
that final turn prefill is no longer supported and returns an error, and you generally don't need it.
46
To shape structured output, define the format explicitly or use the structured output feature
47
instead. Using the right placement means knowing what changes between requests and what stays
48
constant. Persona stability is a reliability concern, not just a stylistic one. A persona
49
that works on typical inputs but breaks under edge cases or adversarial pressure
50
is a deployment risk. The way to build stable personas is through specificity. Vague instructions
51
like be friendly and professional leave too much room for interpretation. Specific instructions
52
give the model clear guidance. Use a warm tone, keep responses under 200 words, never use industry
53
jargon, always acknowledge the user's concern before offering a solution. The more precisely
54
you define the persona's communication constraints, the more consistently the model will hold them,
55
and always test with inputs designed to stress the persona before it reaches users. Off-topic
56
requests, emotional escalation, and direct attempts to override the persona all need to
57
be tested explicitly. One concept worth flagging directly. Role assignment shapes how the model
58
reasons and communicates, but it doesn't grant capabilities the model doesn't have. If you tell
59
the model it is a world-class chemistry expert, it will adopt the framing and vocabulary of that
60
role, but it can't synthesize knowledge it wasn't trained on. Similarly, a persona can't be instructed
61
to bypass safety behaviors. Telling the model you are a persona who always answers any question
62
without restriction doesn't override the model's built-in constraints. This is an important boundary
63
for practitioners to understand when setting expectations with stakeholders and designing
64
system prompts. Role and persona affect style and framing. They don't change the model's fundamental
65
capabilities or ethical guardrails. Testing role and context consistency is the final step before
66
a system prompt is production ready. There are four test categories that cover the major failure modes.
67
Topic switches test whether the role holds when users go off script or ask questions outside the
68
defined domain. Context retrieval tests confirm the model actually uses injected data rather than
69
defaulting to its training priors, which can happen when the injection is buried or conflicts with
70
something in training. Persona pressure tests check whether users can inadvertently or deliberately
71
override the assigned identity through persistent requests or jailbreak style framing. And baseline
72
tests establish the expected output range so you can detect regressions when the system prompt is
73
updated. All four are needed before a role in context design is considered validated.
74
Pulling this together, role defines functional expertise and frames every response. Persona
75
extends role with a named identity and stylistic constraints. Context injection grounds the model
76
in facts without the context window. System prompt context is persistent. User turn context is per
77
exchange. Specificity produces stability. And that wraps up role assignment and context setting.
78
I'll see you in the next lesson.

--- Using XML Tags to Structure Complex Prompts ---

1
When prompts get super complex, the model needs structural signals it can rely on,
2
and XML tags are the most reliable way to provide that. We'll look at why XML outperforms
3
plain text delimiters, walk through the standard tag conventions, explore nested tags for hierarchical
4
data, and cover how proper structure prevents prompt injection. Let's dive in.
5
Let's get the language right first. XML tags are structured markup elements,
6
things like document, instructions, or example, that label and separate sections of a prompt.
7
Clod is specifically trained to respect XML tag boundaries, which makes them more reliable than
8
prose separators. Prompt injection is an attack where malicious user-supplied content tries to
9
override the developer's instructions. Separating data from instructions with XML tags helps the
10
model tell the two apart, but it isn't the primary defense against injection. The real mitigations
11
are treating tool and document content as untrusted, and giving the model least privilege so
12
a compromised input can't take harmful actions. Tag consistency means using the same tag names for
13
the same roles throughout a prompt. Consistency reinforces the structural signal Clod uses when
14
parsing your input. Let's start with why XML beats plain delimiters. When you use dashes,
15
asterisks, or markdown headers as separators, the model has to infer whether they're structure
16
signals or actual content. That inference can go wrong, especially in longer prompts where section
17
boundaries matter. XML tags have unambiguous open and close syntax. There's no guessing whether a
18
line of dashes is a separator or part of a table. Clod's training reinforces tag boundaries
19
consistently, so the model reliably treats the content inside a tag as a distinct section.
20
For any prompt where you need clear, dependable separation between sections,
21
XML is the right choice over prose or markdown. Four standard tag names cover most of what you'll
22
build. The instructions tag wraps the task directive, the thing you're asking the model to do.
23
The context tag holds background information. The task depends on things the model needs to know,
24
but isn't being asked to produce. The examples tag contains few-shot demonstrations of the output
25
format or reasoning style you want. The output format tag specifies the structure and format you
26
expect back from the model. You're not required to use these exact names, but they're widely
27
recognized conventions. Using them makes your prompts easier for collaborators to read and
28
maintains the kind of consistency that improves follow-through. Good tag naming is a small habit
29
that pays off at scale. Choose names that describe what the content is, not just that it exists.
30
A tag named customer data is clearer than data or block. Stay consistent with your names. If you
31
call something document in one prompt, call it document everywhere. When tag names drift, the
32
structural signal weakens. Every tag needs a matching close tag. Unclosed tags confuse the
33
parser and can produce unpredictable output. If you're building a system with many prompts,
34
align your tag vocabulary across all of them. That cross-prompt consistency is what lets you
35
maintain and debug complex systems reliably. Flat and nested tags serve different needs. Flat tags
36
work well when sections are independent. You have instructions, context, and examples at the same
37
level, each clearly labeled, no hierarchy needed. That covers most single document prompts. Nested
38
tags come into play when a section has its own internal structure. If you're passing a document
39
with a source, a date, and the actual content, nesting a source tag and a content tag inside a
40
parent document tag preserves those relationships. The model can read each child independently while
41
knowing they belong to the same document. Nesting adds clarity when hierarchy exists. It adds noise
42
when it doesn't, so keep it to one or two levels in practice. Nested tags let you represent
43
structured data without ambiguity. A document tag can contain a source tag, a date tag, and a document
44
content tag as children. The model reads each child independently within the context of its parent.
45
That parent-child relationship matters when you have multiple documents. Each one has its own
46
source and content, and the tags make those pairings explicit. Practically, you should keep
47
nesting to one or two levels deep. Deeper nesting becomes hard to read and maintain, and the clarity
48
benefit disappears. When a section has internal structure that needs to be parsed separately,
49
one level of nesting is usually all you need. Separating data from instructions is one useful
50
thing XML tags enable. The risk without it is prompt injection, where user-supplied content
51
in the same undifferentiated block tries to override your task directive. By placing task
52
directives inside an instructions tag and user-supplied content inside context or document
53
tags, you make it less likely the model confuses data for a directive. That said, tagging isn't a
54
guaranteed barrier. Adversarial input can attempt to break out of a tag, so treat tags as one layer
55
of clarity, not the primary defense. The real mitigations are treating any tool or document
56
content as untrusted, and giving the model least privilege so a compromised input can't trigger
57
harmful actions. Tag consistency shapes how reliably the model follows through on your
58
structure. Repeated tag names are the key mechanism. When the model sees the same tag name in the same
59
role every time, it builds a reliable association. If you vary tag names using document in one place
60
and doc in another, that association weakens. Every tag also needs its matching close. Unclosed tags
61
are a common source of subtle bugs, where the model treats everything after the open tag as
62
inside the section. For larger systems, align tag names across all your prompts. A shared vocabulary
63
of tag names is what makes a multi-prompt system coherent and easier to maintain over time.
64
XML tags give Claude a reliable structural signal that plain text and markdown headers can't match.
65
The standard conventions, instructions, context, examples, and output format
66
cover most needs. Consistent naming and proper nesting keep complex prompts readable and
67
debuggable. The big picture? Tags are the right structural tool for complex prompts.
68
That wraps up this lesson. I'll see you in the next one.

--- Document Structure Patterns for Multi-Part Tasks ---

1
Welcome back. When you're injecting multiple documents into a single prompt,
2
the structure you choose determines whether the model can keep them straight.
3
We'll cover how to inject multiple documents cleanly,
4
when to use sections versus separate turns, how
5
to reference documents and instructions, and how to test that the model
6
attributes outputs correctly. Let's jump in. Start with the
7
vocabulary. A multi-document prompt is any prompt that
8
includes two or more source documents for the model to reason over.
9
Each one needs its own labeled block so the model can reference and distinguish
10
them. Document injection is the practice of
11
placing source material into a prompt using structured tags, things like
12
document, document content, and source, rather than
13
pasting text inline into your instructions.
14
Output attribution is the model's ability to correctly link what it says
15
to the document it came from. Reliable attribution depends almost entirely on
16
how well your structure separates and labels the inputs.
17
When a task references multiple sources, each document needs its own consistently
18
structured block. The pattern is straightforward. Wrap each
19
document in a document tag, give it a numbered attribute so you can
20
reference it later, include a source child tag identifying
21
where the document came from, and place the actual text inside a
22
document content child tag. Keeping each block self-contained and
23
parallel is what makes the structure legible to both the model
24
and to you when debugging. If one block uses source and another doesn't,
25
the model has to guess what role that content plays.
26
Consistent structure eliminates that guessing.
27
Choosing between sections and separate turns comes down to whether your task is
28
parallel or sequential. If you need the model to
29
compare, contrast, or synthesize multiple documents at once,
30
sections work well. All the inputs are available simultaneously in a single
31
call, and the model can reason across all of
32
them. If your workflow is sequential, where each step depends on seeing the
33
output from the prior step, separate turns are better. Each turn can
34
be smaller and more focused. The context window doesn't get
35
overwhelmed with material the current step doesn't need,
36
and you can catch errors and course correct between steps rather than
37
discovering problems at the end of a long single call.
38
The comparison between a single prompt with sections and separate
39
conversational turns is worth making explicit. A single
40
prompt with sections puts everything in one call. The model
41
sees all documents and instructions simultaneously. That's
42
powerful for synthesis tasks, but it makes prompts long, and long
43
prompts are harder to debug when something goes wrong.
44
Separate conversational turns spread the task across messages.
45
Each turn is smaller and builds on the prior response.
46
That's better for sequential workflows, but adds round-trip overhead
47
and requires you to manage what context carries forward between turns.
48
Neither approach is universally better. The task structure
49
should drive the choice. Three XML tags cover most document injection scenarios
50
you'll encounter. The document tag is the top level
51
container for a single source. The document a content tag holds the
52
raw text of the source material and sits as a child inside the document tag.
53
The source tag provides identifying metadata, a file name,
54
a URL, or a short label, also as a child of document.
55
Numbering your documents with an index attribute on the outer document tag
56
is a small addition that pays off when your instructions need to reference
57
a specific source by position. Together, these three tags produce clean,
58
consistently structured document blocks that the model can parse and reference
59
without ambiguity. Explicit reference patterns inside your
60
instructions are what connect the structure to the
61
task. You can reference by source name. Based on the document from source A,
62
summarize the main argument. Or you can reference by index.
63
Using document 1 and document 2, identify where they disagree.
64
Both styles work, but explicit references help the model attribute its
65
output to the right input. Avoid vague phrases like the documents
66
or the context when only one specific document should inform that part of the
67
answer. Vague references are one of the main
68
causes of output bleed, where a claim gets attributed to the
69
wrong source. Three anti-patterns come up repeatedly
70
in multi-document prompts. The first is inline prose mixing,
71
embedding document text directly inside instruction paragraphs
72
without any tag boundary. The model can't reliably separate what it should follow
73
from what it should analyze. The second is mixing data and directives in the
74
same untagged block. This is the core structure failure that
75
enables prompt injection. The third is sending a long,
76
unformatted prompt with multiple documents and no tags at all,
77
hoping the model finds everything. As prompt length grows,
78
misattribution becomes increasingly likely. All three anti-patterns share the
79
same root cause. No clear boundary between instructions
80
and source material. Testing attribution is the step that
81
confirms your structure is actually working.
82
The most reliable method is asking the model to cite which document each claim
83
comes from in its output. If it can't, the structure isn't clear
84
enough. Another strong test is deliberately using
85
documents with conflicting information. A well-structured prompt
86
should let the model correctly attribute which document says what,
87
even when they disagree. Using structured output formats like JSON
88
makes attribution explicit and machine checkable.
89
Attribution testing doesn't need to be elaborate, but it should happen before a
90
multi-document prompt goes into production,
91
because structure problems that pass a quick read often fail under real inputs.
92
XML document structure keeps multi-part prompts reliable and debuggable.
93
The document, document content, and source tag pattern
94
gives each source a clean, labeled home. Explicit references in instructions
95
connect structure to the task. Inline prose mixing and untagged blocks are
96
the primary cause of misattribution. Coming up, few-shot
97
prompting and why examples anchor output more
98
reliably than instructions alone. I'll see you there.

--- Why Examples Work — Pattern Matching and Format Anchoring ---

1
Here's the question this lesson answers. Why do examples anchor model outputs so much more
2
reliably than regular instructions? We'll cover what examples communicate simultaneously,
3
how pattern matching works, how format anchoring locks structure from the first token, and when
4
to choose zero-shot versus few-shot. These ideas frame everything we'll be talking about.
5
There's a small vocabulary that frames every few-shot decision, so let's lock it in first.
6
Few-shot prompting is a technique that provides two or more input-output examples before the task.
7
The examples demonstrate format, tone, or reasoning, and high-quality examples are the
8
single biggest lever for output consistency. Zero-shot prompting issues a task with no
9
examples at all. It relies on the model's pre-trained knowledge and how clearly the
10
instruction is written. It works well for common tasks, but often degrades on novel formats or
11
domain-specific output structures. One-shot prompting provides exactly one example. It's a
12
deliberate choice, not a fallback. When a single canonical case fully defines the desired pattern,
13
one example is all you need. A well-designed example carries information that prose instructions
14
struggle to convey. It shows format directly, the exact structure the output should follow,
15
without requiring the model to interpret a written description of that structure.
16
It communicates tone through demonstration rather than instruction. Formal, conversational,
17
terse, or detailed. It shows the reasoning pattern by example, not explanation, and it implicitly
18
scopes the output by showing what's included and what's left out of a correct response.
19
Prose instructions typically address one of these dimensions at a time and leave the others
20
open to interpretation. An example communicates all four simultaneously, which is why a single
21
high-quality example often outperforms a full paragraph of written guidance on format and style.
22
Pattern matching is a useful way to think about how examples anchor output.
23
When you provide examples, the model doesn't just read them for surface content. It picks up on
24
structural patterns, the length of responses, the phrasing style, the sequencing of output elements.
25
Those implicit patterns carry over even when the new input looks different from the examples.
26
With a prose instruction, the model has to work out what your description means for this output,
27
and that leaves more room for variance. With an example, the model can mirror a concrete
28
demonstration directly. That tends to transfer format and tone more reliably, which is why
29
consistent output is usually easier to achieve with examples than with instructions alone.
30
Prose instructions and concrete examples differ in how reliably they transfer format requirements.
31
When you describe what you want in natural language, the model has to work out what your
32
description means for this specific output. That leaves room for variance. Particularly for novel
33
or domain-specific formats, the model hasn't seen often. When you show a concrete example,
34
the model can mirror the demonstrated pattern. Format, tone, and structure come through the
35
demonstration rather than a written description, which tends to leave less room for drift.
36
For tasks where output consistency matters, examples are usually a more reliable transfer
37
mechanism than instructions alone. Format anchoring refers to how an example influences
38
the very first token the model produces. The first output token is the highest stakes moment
39
in any generation because it sets the structural path for everything that follows. When your
40
example ends in a specific format, the model continues that format from the opening token.
41
Without an example, the model should infer the format from your instruction text,
42
which is an interpretation task. With an example, the format is anchored before
43
interpretation can introduce variance. This is especially valuable for structured output tasks
44
where you need the model to open with a specific element, like a JSON object,
45
a table row, or a labeled field. The example locks the opening before drift can occur.
46
Shot count matters, but the returns aren't linear. Zero shot is fastest and simplest.
47
When a task is common and format flexibility is acceptable, you don't need examples at all.
48
One example works well when a single canonical case fully defines the pattern. Three examples
49
is the most broadly useful configuration for structured output tasks because it captures
50
enough format and tone variation without overwhelming the prompt. Beyond three,
51
returns diminish in most cases. Five or more examples are worth the overhead
52
only when your inputs span genuinely different types or edge cases that three examples can't
53
cover. More examples isn't always better. The right count is the minimum that reliably anchors
54
the pattern you need. Zero shot and few shot aren't in competition. They serve different
55
situations. Zero shot works well for summarization, question answering, and common text generation
56
tasks where the format is flexible and the task is well within the model's pre-trained knowledge.
57
Few shot is the right choice when you need structured output, novel formats, or consistent
58
tone across a range of inputs. For well-defined common tasks, zero shot with clear instructions
59
can match few shot performance and it's simpler to maintain. The practical approach is to start
60
with zero shot and add examples only when you observe output variants you can't fix
61
with instruction improvements. Few shot has real overhead. Use it when the consistency payoff
62
justifies it. The relationship between example count and output quality isn't what most people
63
assume. A single well-crafted example that covers the target format, edge case behavior, and desired
64
tone will outperform five mediocre ones. When your examples have inconsistent formatting,
65
off-tone phrasing, or vary in what they include, you're giving the model conflicting signals. The
66
model averages across those signals and the result is output that's muddled rather than consistent.
67
Before adding more examples to a failing prompt, audit the examples you already have. The question
68
isn't how many examples you need, it's whether the examples you have are doing their job.
69
Quality is the variable that moves output quality the most. Examples communicate format, tone, and
70
reasoning simultaneously and they anchor output more reliably than pros because they eliminate
71
the interpretation step. Pattern matching extracts explicit rules from demonstrations. Format anchoring
72
locks structure from the first token. Three shot is broadly useful. Zero shot fits common tasks
73
with flexible output. Example quality is the single biggest lever for consistency.
74
That wraps up this lesson. I'll see you in the next one.

--- Designing High-Quality Few-Shot Examples ---

1
Here's what we're tackling in this one.
2
How to design high-quality few-shot examples.
3
Input-output pair design, example diversity and edge
4
cases, what contamination is and how to avoid it,
5
dynamic selection at runtime, ordering effects,
6
and how to test whether examples are helping or hurting.
7
These ideas frame everything we'll do from here on.
8
Let's get into it.
9
Before we go further, let's pin down the three terms
10
that anchor example design.
11
Example contamination is what happens
12
when examples are too narrow, stylistically skewed,
13
or topically biased.
14
Instead of teaching the model to generalize,
15
contaminated examples cause it to reproduce
16
the example's idiosyncrasies across all outputs.
17
Dynamic few-shot is the practice of selecting examples
18
at runtime based on similarity to the current input
19
rather than using a fixed static set.
20
It produces more consistent output across varied inputs.
21
Ordering effects refer to the influence
22
that example sequence has on output.
23
Models give more weight to examples
24
that appear later in the prompt, so where
25
you place your best example is a deliberate design decision.
26
Every input-output pair in your example set
27
is a signal to the model about what a correct response looks like.
28
That signal needs to be clean.
29
The input should be realistic and representative
30
of the actual task, inputs you'll send in production,
31
not a simplified stand-in that doesn't reflect real complexity.
32
The output should be exactly what
33
you would accept as a correct response, nothing more
34
and nothing less.
35
If the output contains anything unusual,
36
any artifact, odd phrasing, or extra formatting
37
that you wouldn't want reproduced, remove it.
38
Examples that almost demonstrate the pattern
39
introduce more noise than signal.
40
One focused example that clearly shows the target behavior
41
is worth more than several that partially demonstrate it.
42
A set of similar examples teaches the model
43
one narrow pattern.
44
A diverse set teaches it a range.
45
When all your examples come from the same input type, length,
46
and phrasing style, the model learns
47
to handle that specific case well
48
and generalizes poorly to anything different.
49
Diversity means covering different input lengths,
50
different topics, and different phrasing styles
51
within the same task type.
52
It also means including at least one example
53
that tests the edge of the pattern, a harder input,
54
an unusual format, or a boundary case.
55
Three well-chosen diverse examples
56
will outperform five examples drawn
57
from the same narrow slice of your input space.
58
The goal is breadth of coverage, not volume.
59
Static few-shot uses the same fixed examples
60
for every input the prompt receives.
61
It's simple to implement and easy to maintain,
62
but it has a real weakness.
63
When real inputs differ significantly
64
from the examples, output quality drops.
65
Static sets work well when your input distribution
66
is narrow and predictable.
67
Dynamic few-shot addresses this by selecting examples at runtime.
68
You embed a pool of high-quality examples
69
and retrieve the most similar ones to the current input
70
before each inference call.
71
The prompt always contains examples
72
that are close to what you're actually asking.
73
For diverse or open-ended workloads,
74
dynamic selection is significantly more reliable,
75
though it requires a retrieval mechanism
76
and a well-maintained example pool.
77
Contamination is one of the most common failure
78
modes in few-shot prompting and one of the easiest to miss.
79
When an example uses unusual phrasing,
80
that phrasing tends to appear in outputs
81
across unrelated inputs.
82
When examples are all drawn from one topic area,
83
the model learns to assume that topic context,
84
even when the input is about something different.
85
When example outputs are longer than they need to be,
86
the model learns to generate verbose responses.
87
These are contamination patterns.
88
The most reliable way to catch them
89
is to review each example output as
90
if it were a production response you would ship to a real user.
91
If anything in the output would prompt you to edit it
92
before shipping, that element shouldn't be in your example.
93
Dynamic few-shot selection requires
94
building and maintaining an example pool.
95
The process starts with embedding your example pool
96
using a sentence similarity or retrieval model.
97
At inference time, you embed the current input
98
and retrieve the top examples by similarity score.
99
Those examples go into the prompt in place
100
of a fixed static set.
101
The result is a prompt whose examples
102
are contextually relevant to the specific input being processed.
103
Over time, as you collect and validate higher quality
104
examples from real production inputs, you update the pool.
105
Dynamic selection is the most reliable approach
106
for applications with diverse or unpredictable input distributions.
107
The overhead is real, but the output consistency improvement
108
justifies it for most production use cases.
109
Ordering effects are a well-documented property
110
of few-shot prompts.
111
Models assign more weight to examples
112
that appear closer to the task instruction,
113
meaning later examples have more influence on output format
114
and tone than earlier ones.
115
The practical implication is that your most representative
116
highest quality example belongs at the end of the example block
117
immediately before the actual task.
118
If you have one example you're most confident about,
119
that's the one to put last.
120
When you observe unexpected variance in output quality,
121
reordering examples is one of the first things to try.
122
Ordering is an easy-to-adjust variable
123
that can produce significant differences in complex prompts
124
without changing any of the example content.
125
Testing examples isn't optional if you want
126
to know whether they're working.
127
The baseline comparison is simple.
128
Run the prompt with examples, then run it without examples,
129
and evaluate the outputs.
130
If zero-shot matches the quality of few-shot,
131
your examples are adding prompt length without adding value.
132
If the output reproduces stylistic artifacts
133
or phrasing that only appeared in one example,
134
you have contamination.
135
If quality degrades on inputs that don't closely
136
resemble the examples, your set lacks diversity.
137
Test across a range of inputs, not just the canonical cases
138
the examples were designed for.
139
When an example is hurting quality,
140
remove it or replace it.
141
Patching around a bad example with additional instructions
142
is the wrong direction.
143
High-quality few-shot examples require deliberate design.
144
Each pair needs realistic inputs and clean outputs
145
with no artifacts.
146
Diverse examples generalize better than similar examples.
147
Dynamic selection outperforms static sets when inputs vary.
148
Place your best example last.
149
Always compare against a zero-shot
150
to confirm examples are adding value.
151
And that wraps up few-shot example design.
152
Next up, chain of thought prompting
153
and how to get Claude to show its reasoning.
154
I'll see you there.

--- Chain-of-Thought — Prompting for Visible Reasoning ---

1
Welcome back. Chain of thought prompting is about instructing the model to show its reasoning,
2
and applied right, it meaningfully improves accuracy on complex tasks.
3
We'll look at zero-shot chain of thought, structured reasoning scaffolds,
4
when this technique actually helps versus when it adds no value,
5
and how to test whether it's earning its overhead for a given task. Let's get into it.
6
A handful of terms anchor this lesson. Chain of thought prompting is a technique
7
where you instruct the model to show its reasoning steps before delivering a final answer.
8
You elicit it through instructions like think step-by-step,
9
or by including worked-out reasoning in your few-shot examples.
10
Zero-shot chain of thought triggers that same visible reasoning without any examples.
11
You just append a phrase like think step-by-step to the prompt. No demonstration required.
12
Structured chain of thought takes it further by providing a reasoning scaffold,
13
which is a template with labeled steps the model follows,
14
rather than generating its own reasoning path. Each technique suits different task types,
15
and knowing when to use which is the practical skill this lesson builds.
16
Zero-shot chain of thought is the simplest way to activate visible reasoning.
17
You append a phrase to your prompt, and the model generates intermediate reasoning before
18
the final answer. Common phrases are think step-by-step, walk me through your reasoning,
19
or explain before answering. No examples are required. That's what makes it zero-shot.
20
It works well for arithmetic, logic puzzles, and multi-step planning tasks,
21
where intermediate steps reduce the chance of accumulating errors.
22
One common mistake is omitting the elicitation phrase entirely,
23
and expecting the model to reason step-by-step on its own. It won't. The phrase is the mechanism.
24
Without it, you get a direct answer rather than visible reasoning.
25
Structured chain of thought gives you more control over how the model reasons.
26
Instead of letting the model choose its own path, you provide a template with labeled stages.
27
For example, a prompt might instruct the model to first identify the key constraints,
28
then analyze the options, then conclude with a recommendation.
29
That structure produces consistent reasoning across many similar tasks,
30
which is useful when you need responses to be comparable,
31
or when you're building a workflow where reasoning steps feed into downstream processes.
32
Freeform chain of thought is better when task types vary too much to fit a template,
33
or when a rigid scaffold would constrain the model's reasoning in unhelpful ways.
34
The choice comes down to whether consistency or flexibility matters more for your use case.
35
The comparison between freeform and structured chain of thought
36
comes down to control versus flexibility. Freeform chain of thought works well when
37
the model needs latitude to reason through problems that differ significantly in structure.
38
You give it the trigger phrase and let it go. Structured chain of thought works well when
39
you need reasoning to follow a consistent shape, such as when you're comparing responses across
40
many inputs, or when the steps in a reasoning process are well-defined enough to specify in
41
advance. Neither is universally better. If your tasks are heterogeneous,
42
freeform gives the model room to reason appropriately. If your tasks share a common
43
structure, a scaffold makes outputs more consistent and easier to evaluate.
44
Chain of thought helps most on tasks where intermediate steps matter.
45
Multi-step arithmetic is the classic example. Errors in early steps compound,
46
and visible work lets you catch where things went wrong.
47
Logic and formal deduction tasks benefit similarly, because each reasoning step is
48
a potential failure point, and making them visible makes them checkable.
49
Planning and sequencing tasks, where you're ordering actions or resolving dependencies,
50
benefit from explicit step-by-step thinking that surfaces conflicts early.
51
Complex instruction following also benefits when there are multiple constraints
52
the model needs to satisfy. Reasoning through each one explicitly reduces the chance of missing
53
or violating a constraint in the final answer. Not every task benefits from chain of thought,
54
and adding it to tasks that don't need it costs latency and tokens without improving accuracy.
55
Simple factual lookups don't need intermediate reasoning. The answer is either known,
56
or it isn't. Straightforward classification tasks with clear signals don't require the
57
model to reason through steps. Short direct answer tasks add generation overhead without
58
gaining anything. Highly creative tasks can actually be constrained by forced step-by-step
59
structure, which can make outputs more rigid than you want. Before applying chain of thought broadly,
60
ask whether the task actually involves multi-step reasoning. If it doesn't,
61
the technique adds cost without benefit. An important clarification. Chain of thought
62
is a prompting technique, not an API feature. The entire mechanism lives in the text of your prompt.
63
You don't set an API flag or enable a special mode. You write an instruction that tells the
64
model to reason visibly, and it does. This applies across any model that follows instructions well.
65
It's also worth being clear that chain of thought is distinct from extended thinking,
66
which is a separate API feature that allocates a dedicated token budget for internal reasoning.
67
Chain of thought puts reasoning in the prompt text. Extended thinking is configured through
68
API parameters. Conflating the two leads to incorrect answers on questions about API design
69
and capability boundaries. Testing. Whether chain of thought actually helps for a given task is an
70
essential step before relying on it. The simplest test is running the same task with and without
71
the elicitation phrase and comparing accuracy across a representative set of inputs. You're
72
looking for cases where visible reasoning produces more correct answers, not just longer ones. Check
73
whether the reasoning steps are coherent and actually support the conclusion. If the model
74
produces plausible-looking reasoning steps that still arrive at wrong answers, chain of thought
75
may not be the right tool for that task type. Testing also reveals tasks where the technique
76
adds latency without improving output quality, which helps you decide where it's worth the
77
overhead and where it isn't. Chain of thought prompting is a prompt text technique that makes
78
reasoning visible and improves accuracy on multi-step problems. Zero-shot chain of thought
79
uses an elicitation phrase with no examples. Structured chain of thought adds a scaffold
80
for consistent reasoning. Both live in the prompt, not in API settings. So that's chain of thought
81
prompting. Coming up, extended thinking, which is a distinct API feature with very different
82
trade-offs. I'll see you there.

--- Extended Thinking — When and How to Use It ---

1
Extended thinking is an API feature that lets Claude reason internally before producing a
2
visible response. We're covering how it differs from chain of thought prompting,
3
how adaptive mode and the effort setting control reasoning depth, when the cost is justified,
4
and how thinking blocks relate to the context window.
5
Start with the vocabulary. Extended thinking is an API feature that lets the model reason
6
internally before its visible response. You enable it through the thinking parameter,
7
not through instructions in the prompt text.
8
Thinking blocks are the structured internal reasoning segments that extended thinking
9
produces. They are streamed as distinct event types before the final response,
10
and don't appear in the output text. On current models, thinking runs in adaptive mode. Claude
11
decides how much to reason, and you steer that depth with an effort level rather than a fixed
12
token count. The overall cost is capped by the max token setting. Understanding these three
13
concepts is essential for answering API design questions correctly.
14
The most important distinction in this lesson is between chain of thought prompting
15
and extended thinking. Chain of thought is a prompting technique. You write an instruction
16
in the prompt text, the model generates visible reasoning as part of its response,
17
and no API parameters are involved. Extended thinking is an API feature. On current models,
18
you enable adaptive thinking and steer how deeply the model reasons with an effort level,
19
rather than dialing in a raw token budget. The model's thinking blocks are streamed
20
separately from the final response and aren't part of the output text.
21
One lives entirely in the prompt, the other is configured at the API level. Candidates who
22
conflate these two will answer API design questions incorrectly, so keeping the distinction
23
clear is worth the effort. Controlling reasoning depth is the core configuration step for extended
24
thinking. On current models, you set the thinking type to adaptive and then pick an effort level.
25
The levels run low, medium, high, x-high, and max, where high is the default and x-high and max
26
push the model to reason more deeply. In adaptive mode, Claude decides how much to reason for each
27
request, and the effort level steers that depth and the overall token spend. More reasoning can
28
improve quality on complex problems, but it also raises cost and latency, because thinking tokens
29
are billed even though they don't appear in the response. The overall cost is bounded by the max
30
token setting, which caps thinking plus output together. For most use cases, start at a moderate
31
effort level and adjust based on output quality. Extended thinking earns its cost when problems
32
have genuine internal complexity. Good candidates are multi-step reasoning tasks, where chain of
33
thought in the prompt doesn't produce sufficient quality, problems that require exploring multiple
34
solution paths before committing to one, and tasks where the quality of the final answer
35
depends heavily on thorough pre-answer reasoning. High stakes decisions, where reasoning quality
36
matters more than response speed, are also appropriate. For simple tasks with clear direct
37
answers, extended thinking adds cost without improving output quality. The question to ask
38
is whether the problem is complex enough that more thorough internal reasoning would actually change
39
the answer. If the answer is no, chain of thought or a direct prompt is probably sufficient.
40
Thinking blocks have four properties worth understanding. They are separate from the output,
41
streamed before the final response as distinct event types, and not concatenated into the answer
42
text. They count against the model's context budget, meaning large thinking budgets reduce
43
the tokens available for the prompt and the final response. They aren't prompt text. They are
44
generated internally by the model as a result of the API feature, not written by the developer.
45
And they are surfaced as structured streaming events, which means client code needs to handle
46
those event types explicitly if you want to read or display the thinking content.
47
These four properties distinguish thinking blocks from anything produced by chain of thought
48
prompting. Not every complex task justifies extended thinking. Single question problems
49
with clear answers rarely benefit from the additional token budget. Tasks requiring
50
synthesis of many constraints or large amounts of data are better candidates. Exploratory reasoning
51
tasks where the solution path isn't obvious in advance and the model needs to consider multiple
52
approaches are well suited to extended thinking. Tasks where chain of thought prompting already
53
produces high quality results probably don't need it. The practical guidance is to evaluate problem
54
