Instead, include the failure reason explicitly. Something like, your previous response was
33
missing the required summary field. Here is the output you returned. Please revise it to include
34
a one sentence summary. That kind of specificity gives the model something to act on. Paste the
35
original output alongside the failure message so the model can compare what it produced with what
36
you needed. Concrete feedback drives concrete improvement. Vague feedback drives vague revisions
37
that look different on the surface but fail the same validation check again. Retry budgets aren't
38
optional. Without a hard cap, a validation failure can turn into an infinite loop that drains cost
39
and blocks the pipeline. So let's talk about how to design a budget well. Set a small cap.
40
Two to three attempts covers the vast majority of recoverable failures. If a model can't succeed in
41
three tries, the problem is almost certainly a prompt design issue, not a one-off hiccup. Log
42
every attempt. Logging what failed and why on each retry is how you identify systemic problems in your
43
prompts over time. And escalate explicitly when the budget runs out. Don't silently return an
44
empty result. Route to a human review queue or return a structured error that downstream systems
45
can handle. Silence is the most dangerous failure mode of all. Sometimes a model gets stuck in a
46
pattern. It produces output that fails the same validation check on every attempt with only
47
superficial differences. Identical retries don't help here. What you need is variance. One technique
48
is a small temperature bump on each retry. Start the first retry at the model's default temperature.
49
Raise it slightly on the second attempt to push the model toward different phrasing and structure.
50
Another technique is prompt variation. Reframe the instruction slightly on each retry.
51
Not enough to change what you're asking for, but enough to break the model out of the same failure
52
path. Exponential backoff is also important when you're hitting rate limits under failure conditions.
53
Don't hammer the API with rapid retries. Space them out so you don't add a rate limit failure
54
on top of a validation failure. This slide introduces additional vocabulary worth knowing.
55
The reviewer pattern is a second model call that critiques the first call's output
56
before your application ever sees it. We'll cover this in depth in the next lesson,
57
but it's worth naming here because it's the next level up from a simple retry loop.
58
The escalation path is the defined action you take when retries run out. This should be explicit
59
in your architecture. Either return a structured error, flag the item for human review, or fall
60
back to a known safe default. Don't leave it undefined. And semantic drift is something to
61
watch for in longer retry chains. If you run too many retries without a strong anchor, the output
62
can satisfy your format checks, but drift further from the intended meaning with each attempt.
63
More retries isn't always better. Knowing when to escalate instead of retrying is a judgment call,
64
but there are clear signals. The most reliable signal is a semantic failure that repeats across
65
multiple attempts. If the model produces structurally valid output that consistently
66
misses the intended meaning, that's not a random failure. That's a signal your prompt isn't giving
67
the model what it needs to succeed. Escalate when that pattern appears. Another signal is when the
68
format passes, but the content meaning is consistently wrong. Schema checks give you a
69
green light, but the actual answer is off. That's a semantic validation failure, and no number of
70
retries is likely to fix it without changing the prompt. Treat repeated semantic failures as design
71
feedback, not bad luck. They're telling you something important about your instruction design.
72
Let me leave you with the single most common validation mistake I see in production systems.
73
Teams implement schema validation. They confirm the output is valid JSON with the right fields,
74
and they ship. But schema-only validation gives false confidence. A JSON object can be perfectly
75
structured and completely wrong. A required field can contain a string that technically satisfies
76
the type check but says the opposite of what was intended. You need at least one semantic check
77
on top of your schema checks. It doesn't have to be a full second model call. Sometimes a simple
78
rule works. Does the output mention at least one of the required topics? Is the score within a
79
plausible range? Passing schema validation is the floor. Build checks that actually catch the
80
failures your users will notice. Reliable pipelines need all three validation layers,
81
not just schema checks. Cap your retries, include the failure reason in every retry prompt,
82
and escalate when the same semantic failure repeats across attempts. Build your budgets
83
before you deploy, not after the first runaway loop. And that covers validation gates. In the
84
next lesson, we'll look at multi-pass review, where a second model call critiques the first
85
before the user ever sees it. I'll see you in that lesson.

--- Multi-Pass Review — When One Pass Is Not Enough ---

1
This lesson goes beyond the single retry loop and looks at what happens when you add a second model
2
call specifically to critique the first.
3
We'll cover the reviewer pattern, how to design reviewer prompts that actually work,
4
when multi-pass review is worth the cost, and how to close the loop with a revision.
5
Let's get into it.
6
Three terms to anchor this lesson.
7
The reviewer pattern is a second model call whose only job is to critique the output of the first call.
8
It's not generating new content, it's evaluating existing content against a defined standard.
9
Self-review is when you use the same model for both the generation and the review.
10
It's cheaper and faster, but the model tends to be generous with its own output.
11
You get confirmation bias built into the architecture.
12
Cross-review uses a different model for the reviewer role, often a stronger model than the one that generated the output.
13
This gives you a genuinely independent perspective, which is more valuable in high-stakes situations.
14
These three concepts define the reviewer pattern space.
15
Most teams start with self-review and upgrade to cross-review only where the stakes justify it.
16
The reviewer pattern inserts a second model call into your pipeline between generation and delivery.
17
Here's how it flows in practice.
18
The first call generates the output using your primary prompt.
19
The second call, the reviewer, receives that output along with an explicit checklist and produces a structured critique.
20
If the critique identifies issues, a third call takes the original output and the critique together
21
and produces a revised version.
22
That revised version is what your application delivers to the user.
23
The key insight is that the reviewer call isn't doing generation.
24
It's doing evaluation.
25
Its job is to be a strict, systematic reader that checks the output against the same standard every time.
26
That consistency is what makes the pattern valuable.
27
A human reviewer is inconsistent.
28
A well-prompted reviewer model isn't.
29
The choice between self-review and cross-review comes down to cost versus independence.
30
Self-review is the cheaper path.
31
You use the same model, so you're not paying for a more expensive reviewer.
32
The latency is similar to any other chained call.
33
The problem is confirmation bias.
34
Models tend to rate their own outputs favorably.
35
I've seen self-review passes approve outputs that a human would immediately flag,
36
simply because the model recognized its own phrasing as intentional.
37
Cross-review changes the dynamic.
38
A different model, especially a stronger one, doesn't share that affinity for the original output.
39
It evaluates more independently.
40
That independence is what you're paying for.
41
In regulated domains, healthcare, finance, legal, that independence is often not optional.
42
It's a requirement for the output to be trustworthy at all.
43
The reviewer prompt is where most multi-pass implementations break down.
44
The temptation is to write something like,
45
Review this output and identify any issues.
46
That produces vague, inconsistent critiques that don't drive useful revisions.
47
The better approach is an explicit checklist.
48
List exactly what the reviewer should check.
49
Does the output include a conclusion section?
50
Are all figures consistent with the context provided?
51
Does the tone match the specified audience?
52
Each check item should have a clear pass or fail condition.
53
Ask the reviewer to return a structured critique.
54
Which check failed?
55
A brief explanation of why?
56
And a suggested correction.
57
That structure makes the critique immediately actionable in the revision step.
58
Vague feedback from a reviewer produces vague revisions.
59
Specific feedback produces specific improvements.
60
Reviewer checklists typically fall into three categories.
61
And you should build all three into your reviewer prompt.
62
Completeness checks ask whether all required sections, fields, or elements are present.
63
Missing content is the most common failure in long-form generation.
64
And it's easy to check systematically.
65
Accuracy checks ask whether the facts, figures, and claims are consistent with the input context.
66
Did the model hallucinate a number?
67
Did it contradict something in the source material?
68
This category is harder to check automatically and often benefits from a stronger reviewer model.
69
Tone and format checks ask whether the output matches the required style, voice, and structure.
70
Is it too formal? Too casual?
71
Is the JSON structured correctly?
72
Covering all three categories in your checklist gives the reviewer a complete picture, not just a partial one.
73
Multipass review isn't free.
74
It adds latency from an extra model call and cost from both the reviewer call and, if issues are
75
found, the revision call.
76
So when does that cost make sense?
77
It makes sense when the output is high stakes.
78
If an error in the generated content has real downstream consequences, legal liability, clinical
79
misguidance, financial miscalculation,
80
then a review pass is cheap insurance.
81
It makes sense in regulated domains where accuracy is a compliance requirement, not just a quality preference.
82
And it makes sense for long-form content where a missing section is easy to overlook on a quick read.
83
The cost is almost never justified for low stakes, easily corrected output.
84
If a human can spot and fix an error in 30 seconds with no real harm done, skip the review pass.
85
Just as important as knowing when to use multipass review is knowing when not to.
86
Draft summaries, brainstorming outputs, and exploratory generation are low stakes by design.
87
The user expects to revise them.
88
Adding a review pass to that kind of output inflates cost and latency without a proportional quality benefit.
89
High volume, low cost generation is another case where review doesn't scale.
90
If you're generating thousands of short outputs, a review call on each one multiplies your API cost significantly.
91
You need to weigh that against the probability and cost of an error getting through.
92
And if a human already reviews the output before it reaches a decision point, you may already have a
93
quality gate in place.
94
Don't add a model reviewer on top of a human reviewer without asking whether the model pass is actually adding value.
95
Here's an architectural point that teams often miss.
96
Multipass review is quality control for inherently variable output.
97
It isn't a substitute for a clear original prompt.
98
I see this anti-pattern regularly.
99
A team has a primary prompt that produces inconsistent output.
100
Instead of fixing the prompt, they add a reviewer pass to catch the inconsistencies.
101
The reviewer adds cost and latency, but the root cause is still the weak original prompt.
102
Some inconsistencies slip through anyway.
103
The right sequence is to fix the prompt first.
104
Get the primary generation to a reliable baseline, then add a review pass as a quality control layer
105
for the genuine variability that remains.
106
Using review as a substitute for prompt engineering is like adding a proofreader to compensate for
107
unclear writing instructions.
108
Fix the instructions first.
109
The critique is only half the loop.
110
You need a revision pass to close it.
111
Here's what that looks like in practice.
112
Your revision prompt receives two things.
113
The original output from the generation call and the structured critique from the reviewer call.
114
It asks the model to produce a revised version that addresses each point in the critique.
115
This is where the structure of the reviewer prompt pays off.
116
If the critic returned a list of specific failures with suggested corrections, the revision model
117
has clear targets.
118
If the critique was vague, the revision will be vague too.
119
After the revision, run the same checklist again.
120
Validate the revision the same way you validated the original.
121
If the revision passes, deliver it.
122
If it still fails, you're back to the retry budget decision we covered in the first lesson.
123
The two lessons connect directly.
124
Multipass review adds a meaningful quality control layer that single pass generation just can't match.
125
Use explicit checklists.
126
Choose self or cross review based on the stakes involved.
127
And always close the loop with a concrete revision pass.
128
That wraps up multipass review.
129
Coming up next, we'll close out prompt engineering with how to actually evaluate prompts, test data
130
sets, grading, and regression testing.
131
I'll see you there.

--- Building a Prompt Evaluation Workflow ---

1
Welcome back. Eval workflows are how you turn prompt iteration from guesswork into measurable
2
engineering, and most teams skip them until something breaks in production. We'll cover
3
why manual spot checking falls apart at scale, how a structured eval loop works, the metrics
4
that actually matter, and how eval-driven iteration becomes a deployment gate. Let's jump in.
5
A handful of terms anchor this lesson. Prompt evaluation is the systematic process of testing
6
prompt variants against a labeled dataset to measure output quality, consistency, and regression
7
risk. It includes defining metrics, building test datasets, and running graded comparisons
8
before deploying changes. Regression testing is the practice of running the full eval suite
9
after a prompt change to detect whether cases that previously passed now fail. The goal is
10
to catch regressions before they reach production rather than discovering them from user reports.
11
An eval metric is a quantitative measure used to compare prompt versions, with common examples
12
being pass rate, regression rate, latency per call, and cost per eval run. These three concepts
13
together define the scaffolding that makes systematic evaluation possible. Manual spot
14
checking has a structural problem. It depends on the reviewer's intuition rather than consistent
15
criteria. When you review five or ten outputs and they look reasonable, that observation tells you
16
almost nothing about how the prompt performs across the full input space. Edge cases and
17
adversarial inputs are rarely included in informal reviews, which means the failures that matter most
18
in production are precisely the ones least likely to appear in spot checks. There is also no record
19
kept, so when a future prompt change introduces a regression, there is no documented baseline to
20
compare against. Systematic evaluation addresses all of these gaps by replacing subjective impression
21
with measurable, repeatable, documented results that travel with the prompt through every change cycle.
22
The contrast between ad hoc testing and a systematic eval workflow is essentially a contrast
23
between speed and reliability. Ad hoc spot checking is fast. You make a change, look at a few outputs,
24
and decide it looks better. The problem is that this approach produces no coverage of edge cases,
25
no regression detection, and no historical record to reference after the fact.
26
A systematic eval workflow is slower to set up but produces consistent documented evidence. You
27
have a data set, a run script, a grading step, and a comparison against a baseline. That evidence is
28
what makes a deploy decision defensible. The upfront investment in building the workflow
29
pays off every time you run it because each iteration adds to a record of measured improvements
30
rather than a sequence of impressions. The core eval loop has four steps that repeat on every
31
iteration. First, the data set. A curated set of inputs with known expected outputs or grading
32
criteria. Second, the run. Each input in the data set is sent through the current prompt and the
33
responses are collected. Third, the grade. Each response is scored against the expected output or
34
criteria producing a pass or fail result for every case. Fourth, the compare. The aggregated scores
35
for the new prompt version are measured against the baseline established by the prior version.
36
Each pass through this loop produces a documented result. That result either justifies promoting the
37
new version or sends you back to the change step. The loop is the mechanism that turns testing from
38
a one-time check into a continuous improvement process. Four metrics cover the majority of what
39
you need to evaluate a prompt change. Pass rate is the percentage of eval cases where the output
40
meets the grading criteria and is the primary indicator of overall prompt quality. Regression
41
rate is the share of cases that previously passed but now fail after the change. Any increase in
42
regression rate is a signal to investigate before proceeding. Latency is the average response time
43
per eval case which matters when a prompt change affects how the model processes the task in ways
44
that could slow output. Cost per eval is the total token spend divided by the number of cases which
45
tracks whether the prompt is staying within operational budget constraints. Together these
46
four metrics give you a complete picture of quality, stability, cost, and speed. Eval-driven
47
prompt iteration replaces guesswork with a structured four-step cycle. You make a change
48
to the prompt based on observed failures or new requirements. You run the full eval suite against
49
the updated prompt. You compare pass rate and regression rate against the prior version to
50
measure the net effect. You deploy only when the new version passes without introducing new
51
regressions. This cycle converts every deploy decision into a documented judgment backed by
52
measured evidence. The discipline here is in not skipping any step even for small changes that seem
53
obviously safe. Small changes to prompts can have non-obvious effects on cases that look unrelated
54
to the modification. The only way to catch that is to run the suite. Regression testing protects all
55
cases that currently work, not just the specific inputs related to the change you are making.
56
Every prompt change runs the full suite. A test case that newly fails after a change is a regression
57
regardless of whether fixing that case was the intent of the change. Regression rate tracks the
58
fraction of previously passing cases that now fail and that number needs to be zero or declining
59
before a change is promoted. A prompt modification that fixes the target failures but introduces
60
regressions elsewhere isn't a net improvement. It has traded one set of problems for another.
61
Catching regressions before deploy is always cheaper than finding them in production
62
because production regressions affect real users and require emergency fixes under pressure.
63
Knowing when to run evals is as important as knowing how to run them. The most important rule
64
is to run a full eval before every deployment. Any prompt change that touches production requires it
65
regardless of how small the change appears or how confident you are in the modification.
66
Evals should also run after dataset updates because adding new test cases changes what
67
the baseline metrics represent. Rerunning against both versions recalibrates the comparison.
68
Running evals on a regular schedule independent of any change cycle catches model level drift.
69
Models can shift in behavior across provider updates even when the prompt hasn't changed
70
and scheduled eval runs surface those shifts before they cause production problems.
71
Systematic evaluation turns every prompt change into a documented evidence-backed decision.
72
The core loop is dataset, run, grade, repair. Pass rate, regression rate, latency, and cost per eval
73
carry the comparison. Regression testing ensures fixes don't come at the cost of cases that were
74
already working. That's the eval workflow from dataset to comparison. Next up, generating test
75
datasets and using models to grade outputs. I'll see you there.

--- Test Dataset Generation and Model-Based Grading ---

1
Welcome back. Test dataset quality determines what your eval workflow can actually measure.
2
We're covering how to source and build test datasets, what properties make a dataset useful,
3
how code-based grading works, how model-based grading works, and where it introduces risk,
4
and how to calibrate a model grader against human judgments. Let's jump into it.
5
Let's get the language right first. A test dataset is a curated collection of input prompts
6
with corresponding ground truth or reference outputs used to evaluate model and prompt
7
performance. It covers typical cases, edge cases, and adversarial inputs. Code-based grading is an
8
evaluation technique that uses programmatic assertions, regular expressions, schema validators,
9
or unit tests to determine whether an output meets correctness criteria. It is fast and
10
deterministic. Model-based grading is an evaluation technique where a separate model scores the output
11
of the model under test against criteria or reference answers. It scales to subjective or
12
open-ended tasks that code-based checks can't handle. These three terms define the building
13
blocks of any eval pipeline. Test datasets come from three distinct sources and each has different
14
strengths. Handcrafted cases are written manually by someone who understands the prompt's intended
15
scope and known failure modes. They are precise but slow to produce at volume. Synthetic cases
16
are generated by a model from a seed set of topics, patterns, or example inputs. They scale
17
quickly and can cover a wide variety of surface area. Synthetic generation needs to be reviewed
18
though to avoid low quality or redundant cases. Production sampled cases come from real inputs
19
drawn from system logs with outputs reviewed and labeled after the fact. Production samples carry
20
the most real-world validity because they reflect actual user behavior. Most mature eval suites
21
combine all three. A common bootstrap path is to start with a handcrafted set, expand with
22
synthetic generation, and add production samples once the system is live. The quality of a test
23
dataset is ultimately about coverage across the input space. The prompt is designed to handle.
24
Typical cases represent inputs. The prompt handles correctly under normal conditions.
25
These are necessary but not sufficient on their own. Edge cases push the boundaries of the prompt's
26
intended scope. Inputs that are unusual, ambiguous, or at the limit of what the prompt was designed
27
for. Adversarial inputs are cases deliberately constructed to expose weak handling, trigger
28
refusals at the wrong time, or produce malformed output. Volume also matters. If a dataset has only
29
15 cases, a pass rate of 85% means two failures, which is a poor statistical basis for a deployment
30
decision. A dataset that covers only typical inputs will consistently miss the failures that
31
cause the most damage in production. Code-based and model-based grading each occupy a different
32
part of the eval space. Code-based grading applies when correctness can be expressed as a rule.
33
It is fast, deterministic, and cheap per case. Exact match checks work when the expected output
34
is a fixed string or value. Regex works when acceptable outputs fall within a describable
35
pattern. Schema validation checks whether the output parses correctly against a defined JSON
36
schema. Unit tests treat the output as input to a set of programmatic assertions. The limitation of
37
code-based grading is that it only applies to output types where correctness is definable
38
programmatically. For open-ended or subjective outputs, code-based grading can't work, and
39
model-based grading is the alternative that scales to those cases. Code-based grading has four
40
main techniques. Exact match is the simplest. The output equals an expected string, number,
41
or value. It is reliable when the expected output is deterministic, but breaks down when acceptable
42
outputs vary in phrasing or format. Regex allows a range of acceptable outputs to be described as a
43
pattern, which handles variation better than exact match while still being fully deterministic.
44
Schema validation checks whether the model's output is a valid instance of a defined JSON
45
schema, which is the standard approach for evaluating structured data outputs.
46
Unit tests are the most flexible option. The output is passed to a set of programmatic assertions
47
that check whatever properties matter for the specific task. Code-based grading is preferred
48
whenever it applies because the determinism and speed make it easy to run at scale. Model-based
49
grading works by giving a separate grader, model the original input, the output under evaluation,
50
and a scoring rubric that defines what a passing response looks like. The grader model returns a
51
score, a pass or fail label, or a brief rationale explaining the judgment. The rubric is the key
52
design artifact. It needs to be specific enough that the grader applies it consistently across
53
cases. One important detail is that the grading prompt is itself a prompt that needs to be tested
54
and version controlled. If the grading prompt is changed without evaluation, the grader's behavior
55
can drift silently, making historical comparisons unreliable. Model-based grading scales to any
56
output type that a code check can't evaluate, which makes it the standard approach for tasks
57
like summarization, tone assessment, or complex reasoning evaluation. Model-based grading
58
introduces three risks that don't appear in code-based grading. Greater bias is the tendency
59
for the grader model to favor outputs stylistically similar to its own generation defaults,
60
which can inflate scores for certain formats or tones even when the underlying quality doesn't
61
justify a higher mark. Untested grading prompts are a systemic risk. The grading prompt can drift
62
or be changed without anyone treating it as a prompt change that requires its own evaluation.
63
The most consequential risk is treating model-graded scores as ground truth.
64
Without calibration against human judgments, model-graded scores overstate the precision of
65
the measurement. These risks don't disqualify model-based grading, but they mean it needs
66
to be managed with the same discipline applied to the prompts it is evaluating. Calibration is how
67
you connect model-graded scores to human judgment so that the scores mean something reliable.
68
The process starts by sampling a representative subset of eval cases and having humans label each
69
one independently. The same cases are then run through the model grader. Agreement rate,
70
the fraction of cases where the grader and the human reach the same verdict,
71
is the primary calibration metric. Systematic disagreements reveal where the rubric is
72
underspecified or where the grader model is applying a different standard than what human
73
reviewers would apply. The rubric is adjusted to close those gaps and calibration is rerun.
74
A calibrated model grader is reliable at scale because you know how closely its scores
75
track human judgment. An uncalibrated grader introduces silent errors that propagate
76
through every downstream metric comparison. Test datasets and grading method selection
77
determine what your eval workflow can measure. Cover typical edge and adversarial inputs.
78
Code-based grading handles structured output. Model-based grading handles subjective tasks
79
but requires calibration. Apply the same rigor to the grading prompt as to any other.
80
Coming up, how Claude selects tools and the role of descriptions in agentic systems. I'll see you there.

--- How the Context Window Works — Tokens, Limits, and Tradeoffs ---

1
Here's what we're tackling in this lesson. The context window. What it is, what counts against
2
it, how different content tokenizes, and what the limit looks like across Claude model tiers.
3
Understanding this constraint in detail is what separates systems that work at scale
4
from ones that break under load. Let's dive in. Start with the vocabulary. The context window
5
is everything the model can see in a single inference call. That means the system prompt,
6
the conversation history, any documents you've injected, tool definitions, and the model's own
7
output. Everything outside that window is completely invisible. Tokens are the atomic
8
unit the model processes, roughly four characters or three quarters of an English word each.
9
And the context window limit is the hard ceiling on total token count, combining input and output.
10
Exceed it and your request fails or gets truncated. These three concepts are the
11
foundation for everything else in this lesson. One of the most common mistakes I see in production
12
systems is treating the context window as if it only contains the user's message.
13
In reality, every component of the request contributes tokens. Your system prompt might be
14
500 words. A multi-turn conversation with 20 exchanges adds up fast. If you're using RAG,
15
the retrieved document chunks can be thousands of tokens before the user says a single word.
16
Tool schemas, injected metadata, and memory summaries all add up. And then the model's own
17
output tokens count too, at a higher price per token on most tiers. In well-instrumented production
18
systems, the actual user message is often a small fraction of total context usage.
19
Input and output tokens serve different functions and have different cost profiles.
20
Input tokens are everything you send. The system prompt, conversation history, documents,
21
tool definitions, and the current user message. In RAG and agentic pipelines, input is almost
22
always the dominant share of total context usage. Output tokens are what the model generates in
23
return. Output tokens typically cost more per token than input, and they also count against
24
the context window limit. This matters for chain of thought reasoning. When you enable extended
25
thinking or scratchpad reasoning, the model can generate a lot of output tokens that eat into the
26
available budget before it ever produces a final answer. Tokenization isn't uniform, and this affects
27
your cost and your architecture. Plain English Prose tokenizes efficiently, roughly one token per
28
four characters, or about three quarters of a word. Code tends to be similarly efficient because
29
programming keywords and common identifiers are well represented in the tokenizer vocabulary.
30
Structured formats like JSON and XML are less efficient because all those brackets,
31
quotes, colons, and newlines each consume tokens. Non-English text can be significantly less
32
efficient. Some languages require two to four times as many tokens as the same semantic content
33
in English. When you're choosing a data format for injected content, tokenization efficiency is a real
34
factor. Claude comes in multiple model tiers, and it's worth being precise about what separates
35
them. Context window size isn't the main dividing line. Haiku is the exception here. It carries a
36
smaller 200,000 token window and is optimized for speed and cost on lighter tasks like single
37
turn classification, short document summarization, and fast response chatbots. Sonnet and Opus are
38
different. Both share the same large 1 million token window, so the choice between them isn't
39
about how much fits. It's about capability and cost. Sonnet gives you strong performance at a
40
lower price for multi-document retrieval and everyday agentic pipelines. Opus is the most
41
capable tier for the hardest reasoning and long horizon work, so match the tier to the task's
42
difficulty and your budget, not to a bigger window. Overflow shows up in two distinct places, and the
43
API tells you about them differently. The first case is input that's too large. If the tokens you
44
send already exceed the window, the request never runs. You get a 400 error, a rejection saying the
45
prompt is too long before a single output token is generated. The second case is subtler. Your input
46
fits, generation starts, and then the model runs out of room partway through its own response.
47
Here, the request doesn't error out. It stops early, and the stop reason comes back as model
48
context window exceeded. That's a truncated answer, not a failed call, so your handling
49
code has to check for it explicitly. Treat the upfront rejection and the midstream stop as two
50
separate cases. Three concepts that will come up again in system design discussions. A context
51
budget is a deliberate allocation plan. You decide in advance how many tokens each component gets.
52
System prompt gets 500. History gets 2,000. Retrieved chunks get 4,000. Output headroom
53
gets 1,000. You enforce these limits in code, not by hoping the content fits.
54
Context rot is Anthropic's term for what happens as total tokens grow. The model's attention dilutes
55
across the larger context, so recall and instruction following degrade even when the answer is sitting
56
right there in the prompt. You'll also hear the external phrase lost in the middle. That's the
57
narrower, position-based version of the same idea, that content in the middle gets attended to less
58
than content at the edges. All three point to the same principle, budget proactively.
59
Given these constraints, what does a well-designed context-constrained system look like?
60
First, conversation history should be summarized rather than carried verbatim. Instead of
61
accumulating every exchange, periodically compress older turns into a concise summary and keep only
62
recent turns in full. Second, for RAG pipelines, chunk and retrieve only the most relevant document
63
sections rather than injecting entire documents. Third, set explicit output token limits on every
64
request to preserve input headroom and prevent runaway generation. Fourth, monitor token usage
65
per request in your logging infrastructure so you can catch gradual growth before it becomes a
66
production incident. These aren't optional best practices. They're what separates a prototype from
67
a system that runs reliably at scale. The bottom line, the context window is the fundamental
68
constraint on every clod inference call. Tokens measure everything, and inputs and outputs both
69
count. Model tiers differ in window sizes, and filling the window without a plan leads to degradation
70
and sometimes failure. Budget deliberately. That's context window mechanics. I'll see you in the next lesson.

--- Context Window Economics — Cost, Speed, and Quality ---

1
When you're building systems that have to run at scale and stay financially sustainable,
2
context window economics is what you need to understand. We'll cover how token pricing works,
3
why larger contexts create real cost and latency trade-offs, and why filling the context window
4
with marginal content can hurt quality. Let's get into it. A handful of terms anchor this lesson.
5
Context window economics is the cost-benefit analysis of how much content to include in a
6
request. You're balancing retrieval accuracy and response quality against per-token pricing and
7
latency. Larger contexts cost more and can degrade model focus, so the optimization isn't always to
8
use more. Token pricing is straightforward. You pay a per-token rate for input tokens and a
9
separate, usually higher, per-token rate for output tokens. Latency in this context means
10
time to first token, the elapsed time from sending the request to when the model starts
11
generating. All three of these concepts interact, and we'll trace those interactions throughout this
12
lesson. Token pricing applies to everything in your request, not just the message you type.
13
Your system prompt, the conversation history, injected documents, tool schemas, metadata,
14
and then the model's response. All of it is billed at per-token rates. Output tokens are priced higher
15
than input tokens on most Claude tiers, which matters when you're designing for chain-of-thought
16
reasoning or extended agentic loops that generate a lot of text. The practical implication is that
17
costs compound across components. A 10,000-token system prompt repeated across 1,000 daily requests
18
is 10 million input tokens per day before a single user message is processed. Large stable
19
prompts are often the biggest optimization target in production. API cost reduction.
20
The large versus small context trade-off isn't just about whether your content fits.
21
It's about the cost and latency implications of every token you include. A large context
22
enables long document analysis and rich retrieval, but it costs more per request and increases the
23
time before the model starts responding. A small context is cheaper and faster, but it forces you
24
to be disciplined about what you include. You may need chunking strategies, conversation
25
summarization, or aggressive retrieval filtering to stay within limits. Neither approach is
26
universally better. The right choice depends on what the task actually requires, not on what the
27
maximum limit allows. The discipline of working within a smaller context often produces better
28
results than filling a large one with marginal content. Time-to-first token is the latency
29
metric that matters most for user-facing applications. It measures how long the user
30
waits before seeing any output. That waiting time scales with the amount of input the model needs
31
to process before it starts generating. More tokens in means more attention computation before
32
output starts. Streaming responses help with perceived latency because the user sees tokens
33
arrive incrementally, but total generation time still scales with context size. One important
34
optimization is KV cache. When a request shares a stable prefix with a previous request, the model
35
can reuse the cached key value computation for that prefix and skip reprocessing it. This is
36
the mechanism behind prompt caching, which we'll cover in a later slide. There are three main
37
levers for controlling context cost in production systems. First is system prompt size. Many
38
production system prompts accumulate instructions over time without anyone auditing for redundancy.
39
A quarterly review that cuts the system prompt from 2,000 tokens to 800 tokens reduces baseline
40
cost on every single request. Second is history policy. Carrying full verbatim conversation history
41
grows linearly with session length. Summarizing older turns into a compact representation
42
caps the history budget while preserving continuity. Third is retrieval precision.
43
If your rag pipeline retrieves five loosely relevant chunks when one precisely relevant
44
chunk would do, you're paying for four times the tokens without a quality benefit. High
45
precision retrieval is a quality and cost improvement simultaneously. Here's the insight
46
that the CCAF exam specifically tests. More context isn't always better. Irrelevant tokens
47
aren't neutral filler. They compete with relevant content for the model's attention.
48
If you retrieve 10 document chunks when only two are actually relevant to the query,
49
the model has to process and attend to 10 chunks. The noise from the eight irrelevant chunks dilutes
50
the signal from the two good ones. Contradictory passages in context can cause the model to produce
51
inconsistent responses. Very long contexts can reduce the precision with which the model follows
52
instructions in the system prompt. The practical rule is more volume doesn't compensate for low
53
precision. The highest quality responses often come from contexts that are tightly curated,
54
not maximally large. Three quality mechanisms worth knowing by name. Attention dilution is
55
what happens when a high proportion of context tokens are irrelevant or redundant. The model's
56
attention capacity is finite and noise competes with signal. Retrieval precision is the fraction
57
of retrieved chunks that are genuinely relevant. High precision is better for quality and cost
58
simultaneously, which is why investing in good retrieval infrastructure pays compound dividends.
59
Prompt caching is the API feature that stores the computed KV cache for a stable prefix
60
so subsequent requests can reuse it. The cached tokens are billed at a reduced rate
61
and time to first token drops because the prefix computation is skipped. These three
62
concepts together define the efficiency frontier of context window design. Prompt caching deserves
63
a closer look because it's one of the highest return optimizations in the API economics toolkit.
64
The basic mechanism, you mark a prefix as cacheable, usually a long system prompt or a large
65
reference document. The first request that uses that prefix computes the KV cache and stores it.
66
Every subsequent request that shares the same prefix reuses the cached computation instead of
67
reprocessing the tokens. The benefits are twofold. Cached input tokens are billed at a significantly
68
lower rate, often a fraction of the standard input price, and time to first token decreases
69
because the model skips the prefix computation step. For applications with thousands of daily
70
requests sharing a stable system prompt caching can reduce API costs substantially. Let's pull
71
this all together. Token pricing applies to every component, input and output alike. Larger contexts
72
increase cost and time to first token. Irrelevant tokens don't just increase cost, they also reduce
73
quality. And prompt caching amortizes stable prefixes across requests. Spend your context
74
budget on Signal. That wraps up context window economics. Next we'll dig into the lost in the
75
middle effect and why middle content gets missed. I'll see you there.

--- Why Summaries Lose Facts ---

1
In this lesson, we're looking at why history summarization loses facts and what you can do about it.
2
We'll cover which categories of information are most at risk, how loss compounds across turns,
3
and the extract-first pattern that breaks the cycle.
4
By the end, you'll know exactly what gets dropped and why. Let's get into it.
5
Before we talk solutions, let's lock in three terms you'll use throughout this topic.
6
History summarization is what happens when the context window fills up and the model condenses prior
7
turns into a shorter narrative.
8
That narrative preserves the gist but drops specifics, and it does that silently.
9
You don't get a warning. The persistent fact block is the fix, a structured region of the prompt
10
that holds extracted facts and survives the summarization pass intact.
11
And fact extraction is the step that pulls those facts out before the summarizer can consume them.
12
These three concepts form the foundation for everything else in this lesson, and the next one, so
13
take a moment to make sure they're solid.
14
Summarization exists for a good reason. Long conversations accumulate more tokens than the context
15
window can hold, so something has to give.
16
The model compresses older turns into a shorter narrative. The trade-off is that fluency improves
17
and specificity drops.
18
The compressed version reads naturally and preserves the overall flow of the conversation.
19
What it loses are the exact values, the specific number you mentioned, the exact name of the tool
20
you chose, the precise threshold you set.
21
And here's what makes this particularly tricky. The model doesn't flag what it compressed. It just
22
continues the conversation as if everything is still intact.
23
That silent loss is what we need to design around, and it's the source of most long-session reliability failures.
24
Let's make the trade-off concrete with a comparison. On the fluency side, the compressed summary
25
reads cleanly, and the model can continue responding coherently.
26
Relationships between ideas stay intact, and the overall arc of the conversation is preserved.
27
That's genuinely useful.
28
On the specificity side, the exact number you entered for a rate limit is gone. The name of the
29
specific dataset you referenced is gone.
30
The decision you made three turns ago about which approach to use is gone.
31
The model has no way to distinguish between a token budget of 5 and 12 and one of 8192 if the
32
specific value was dropped in compression.
33
That distinction matters enormously when your application depends on it, and the model won't tell
34
you the value is missing.
35
Not all information is equally vulnerable to summarization loss. Think in four categories. Identifiers go first.
36
User IDs, ticket numbers, version strings, and session tokens look like opaque strings to a
37
summarizer, but they're critical to application logic.
38
Numeric limits are next. Token budgets, rate limits, retry thresholds, and cost caps frequently get
39
compressed down to something like, a limit was mentioned.
40
Named entities, meaning specific people, tools, datasets, and services, get generalized or dropped entirely.
41
And decisions, the choices that constrain later steps, get softened into a vague narrative about the
42
direction of the conversation.
43
These four categories are where you should focus your extraction logic, because they're what the
44
summarizer consistently discards.
45
You can recognize fact loss from its symptoms even before you know the root cause.
46
Constraint amnesia is when a rule, the user stated early in the session, gets silently ignored several turns later.
47
The model doesn't say it forgot the rule, it just stops applying it.
48
Entity confusion is subtler. The model substitutes a plausible name or number for the one the user
49
actually provided. It sounds confident, and it's wrong.
50
Decision drift is when a committed choice gets reopened as if it were still under discussion.
51
The user said, we're using approach A, and three turns later the model is evaluating approach B again.
52
All three symptoms trace back to the same root cause, facts that weren't extracted before
53
summarization consumed them.
54
Here's what makes this problem worse over time.
55
Each summarization pass starts from the output of the previous one. The first pass compresses raw
56
turns and drops minor details.
57
The second pass compresses a summary and drops a bit more. The third pass compresses a summary of a summary.
58
By that point, only the broadest narrative survives. This is compounding loss, and it accelerates
59
faster than most developers expect.
60
A constraint stated clearly at turn 5 might be completely gone by turn 20, not because any single
61
pass was careless, but because each pass had a little less to work with than the one before it.
62
Long sessions don't just get longer. They behave qualitatively differently from short ones, and you
63
need to design for that explicitly.
64
When we get to the next lesson, we'll go deep on building the persistent fact block, but I want you
65
to understand the two core update strategies now.
66
An append-only block grows by adding new facts. It never overwrites what's already there.
67
That's simple to implement, and it preserves the full history of what was stated, but the block
68
grows with every turn, and you need a size management strategy.
69
Replace on conflict takes a different approach. When a new value arrives for a fact that already has
70
an entry, the new value takes over.
71
The block stays smaller, but you lose the earlier value permanently, and the extract-first pattern
72
ties both strategies together.
73
You run extraction before summarization, so facts land in the block before the compressor can discard them.
74
The extract-first pattern is a simple reordering of operations that breaks the loss cycle entirely.
75
Instead of summarizing a turn and then trying to recover what was lost, you extract first.
76
Step 1. As a new turn arrives, run a fact extraction pass against the raw turn text before anything else happens.
77
Step 2. Write the extracted facts into the persistent block.
78
Step 3. Summarize the turn for the rolling history.
79
By the time the summarizer runs, the facts are already safe in the block.
80
The summarizer can be as aggressive as it needs to be about compressing the narrative.
81
It no longer has sole custody of the facts. This pattern is the foundation of every reliable long-session architecture,
82
and it costs almost nothing to implement once the extraction prompt is written.
83
Summarization is unavoidable, but fact loss is a design choice.
84
Extract first. Write to the persistent block. Then let the summarizer do its job on the narrative.
85
The categories and symptoms we covered here tell you exactly what to extract.
86
And that covers why summaries lose facts.
87
In the next lesson, we'll design a persistent fact block, what goes in it, how to update it, and where to inject it.
88
I'll see you there.

--- Designing a Persistent Fact Block ---

1
Welcome back. This lesson is about building the persistent fact block. We've established why
2
summaries lose facts. Now we'll design the system that prevents it. How to write the extraction
3
prompt, which update strategy to choose, where to inject the block, how to keep it from growing
4
unbounded, and the one failure mode that catches almost everyone. Let's get into it.
5
Let's start with vocabulary because these terms drive implementation decisions. An append-only
6
block is the simpler approach. New facts get added as new entries and nothing gets overwritten.
7
You preserve the full history of what was stated across the session, but the block grows with every
8
turn and you need a strategy for bounding it. Replace on conflict is the alternative. When a
9
new value arrives for a fact that already has an entry, the new value takes over. The block stays
10
smaller but you lose the earlier value permanently. Fact aging is the mechanism for pruning stale
11
entries. You track when each fact was last referenced and remove entries that haven't
12
been touched in a set number of turns. These three concepts shape every architectural decision that
13
follows. The extraction prompt is where most implementations either succeed or quietly fall
14
apart. The key insight is that generic prompts produce generic output. If you tell the model to
15
extract important facts, it decides what's important and its judgment won't match yours.
16
Instead, specify the categories explicitly. Tell it to extract identifiers, numeric limits,
17
named entities, and decisions. Tell it to return JSON with typed fields. Tell it to output null for
18
any category where nothing was stated in the turn. That null output is important. It tells you the
19
extractor ran and found nothing rather than leaving you uncertain about whether the category was
20
checked at all. Structured output with explicit nulls is far easier to validate downstream than
21
a freeform summary and it makes schema enforcement straightforward. The choice between append-only
22
and replace-on-conflict comes down to one question. Does the history of a fact's values matter? For
23
some facts, the answer is yes. If a user changed their token budget mid-session, you might want to
24
know that happened and why. Append-only preserves that story. For other facts, the answer is no.
25
If the user corrected the name of the tool they're using, you just need the current correct name.
26
Replace-on-conflict gives you that without carrying the old incorrect value forward.
27
In practice, many implementations use a hybrid, a set of immutable facts that are always append-only,
28
and a set of mutable facts where the latest value wins. The extraction schema drives which category
29
each fact falls into, so the schema design and the update strategy are tightly coupled.
30
Where you inject the persistent block in the request determines how reliably the model treats
31
it as authoritative. Three options exist. Placing it at the top of the user turn keeps it visible,
32
but it competes with the user's actual question for attention and can feel cluttered in the
33
conversation. Injecting it as a separate system block sets it apart from the conversational flow
34
and signals that it's structural information rather than part of the dialogue. That's the
35
most reliable option for most architectures. A third approach places the block in line,
36
just before the query, which gives it high salience at the cost of verbosity.
37
For most production systems, a dedicated system block gives you the clearest separation
38
between what the model knows as context and what the user is asking right now.
39
Size management is unavoidable in any long-running session. Left unmanaged,
40
a persistent fact block will consume an increasing share of your token budget.
41
Four strategies address this. Capping the block sets a hard token limit and forces you to
42
prioritize. When the cap is reached, new facts can't be added until old ones are removed. Aging
43
out, stale facts tracks the last turn each fact was referenced and removes entries that haven't
44
appeared recently. Merging redundant entries consolidates multiple facts about the same entity
45
under a single key to reduce duplication. Priority tiers separate permanent facts,
46
like the session goal or the user's name, from ephemeral facts that can be pruned freely.
47
Most production systems combine two or three of these strategies,
48
rather than relying on any single one to do all the work.
49
Conflicts happen in any session where the same piece of information gets stated more than once,
50
often with different values. A user mentions a rate limit of 100 requests per minute early
51
in a session and then says, actually, the limit is 500. Three turns later. If your block doesn't
52
detect the conflict, one of two things happens. Either the old value stays and the correction is
53
lost, or the new value wins silently and you have no record that the value changed.
54
Neither outcome is great. A better approach detects the conflict before writing, logs both
55
values, applies replace on conflict for the block itself, and escalates to the user when
56
the conflict is semantically significant, like a budget or a security threshold. Silent overwrites
57
high data quality problems that surface later as hard-to-debug application errors.
58
After extraction runs, you need a downstream validation step to confirm
59
that what landed in the block matches what the user actually said. Schema enforcement
60
checks that extracted values conform to expected types and ranges before they're written. A numeric
61
field shouldn't contain a string. A turn number shouldn't be negative. A round-trip check goes
62
further. Re-extract facts from the block's output and compare them back to the source turn.
63
Discrepancies between the re-extracted version and the original turn reveal extraction errors
64
before they propagate into application logic. These checks add a small amount of latency,
65
but they catch the category of failure that's hardest to debug later. A fact that was extracted
66
and written but wasn't quite right, silently shaping every response that followed it without
67
any visible error. I've seen this failure more than any other, so let's spend a moment on it.
68
A team builds the persistent fact block. They write the extraction prompt, implement the update
69
strategy, inject the block into the system message. Then they forget to add an instruction telling the
70
model to actually consult it. The block is there. The facts are accurate. And the model generates its
71
response entirely from general knowledge and conversational history because nothing in the
72
prompt said to check the fact block. The block becomes an expensive no-op. The fix has to be
73
explicit. In every request, include an instruction telling the model where the fact block is and what
74
to do with it. Something like, use the facts in the persistent context section above to ground your
75
response. Without that instruction, you have a block, but not a working system. A persistent
76
fact block only works when every layer is intentional. The extraction prompt, the update
77
strategy, the injection point, the size management, and the instruction to consult it. Get all five
78
right and fact loss becomes a largely solved problem. And that covers persistent fact blocks.

--- The Memory Tool for Persistence ---

1
Welcome back. Compaction can keep a long session alive, but detail gets lost along the way.
2
So where do the facts you can't lose actually live? This lesson covers the memory tool,
3
how a model saves information across conversations. We'll see how it works,
4
the mindset behind it, and how it differs from memory inside the chat.
5
Let's anchor three terms. The memory tool is a tool the model uses to read and write files
6
under a dedicated memory path, so facts persist across separate conversations.
7
A client-side tool is the key idea behind it. That means the model requests an action,
8
and your own application actually performs it against storage you control.
9
The model never reaches into your files directly, it only asks.
10
In-context memory is the contrast. That's anything the model knows purely because
11
it's sitting in the current transcript. It feels like memory, but it vanishes the
12
moment that context is cleared or summarized. Keep those three straight,
13
because the whole lesson turns on the gap between the first and the last.
14
So how does the memory tool actually work? The flow is a request and a response. The model
15
decides it wants to do something with memory, say view the folder or create a file, and it issues
16
that request. Your application receives it, runs the operation against whatever storage you've set
17
up, and returns the result back to the model. The files themselves live under a memory path
18
that you fully control. It could be a folder on disk or keys in a database, whatever fits your
19
system. The upshot is that the model can build up notes over many turns and many sessions without
20
having to hold all of that in its context at once. It reads back what it needs when it needs it.
21
What can it actually do with those files? Three things. At a foundations level, it can read,
22
meaning it views the memory folder and opens files to recall what an earlier session wrote down.
23
It can write, creating or editing files to save facts, progress, and decisions that are worth
24
keeping around. And it can organize, renaming or deleting files so the store stays coherent
25
instead of turning into a junk drawer. Those map to the same operations you'd expect on any set
26
of files. The important shift in thinking is that the model is now an active caretaker of a
27
durable notebook, not just a talker that forgets everything the instant the conversation ends.
28
Here's why this matters so much. The entire point of the tool is survival across the boundaries
29
that normally clear context. When a brand new session starts, it can reread what the previous
30
session wrote. Facts outlast a context reset or a completely fresh conversation. Work resumes from
31
recorded state rather than starting over from nothing. Think about what that changes. Without
32
it, every new session is an amnesiac, rediscovering the same ground again and again. With it, the store
33
becomes the thread that ties separate sessions together into one continuous effort. That
34
continuity is the real product here, and it's why persistence, not cleverness, is the headline
35
feature of this tool. The tool ships with a specific design instruction, and it's worth taking
36
to heart. The mindset is assume interruption. Assume the context window could reset at any moment
37
for any reason, which means anything you haven't written to memory is at risk of being lost the
38
instant that happens. The practical rule that follows is simple. Check your memory first before
39
doing anything else, and then record your progress as you go rather than waiting until the end. When
40
you design an agent this way, an interruption stops being a disaster. It becomes a resume
41
instead of a restart. That single shift in assumption is what makes long-running work
42
robust. Let's put the two kinds of memory side by side because the distinction is the heart of this
43
lesson. The memory tool writes facts to a durable store that sits outside the chat. Those facts
44
survive resets, and the model reads them back on demand in later sessions. In-context memory is the
45
opposite. Those facts are held only in the current transcript. They're convenient right now and cost
46
nothing extra to use, but they're gone the moment that context is summarized or cleared.
47
So the question to ask about any fact is simple. Does it need to survive? If yes,
48
it belongs in the memory tool. If it's only useful for the current turn, in-context is fine.
49
Where does this tool fit best? Three situations stand out. Long projects are the obvious one.
50
Multi-session work, like building a feature over several days, benefits most because each
51
session can resume from recorded state instead of re-exploring everything. Learned preferences
52
are another. When a user tells you once how they like something done, that fact can be saved and
53
reused rather than re-asked on every visit. And progress logs are the third. A running record of
54
what's done and what's still left lets an interrupted agent pick up cleanly right where it left off.
55
If your use case has any of those shapes, the memory tool is likely a strong fit worth reaching
56
for. One last connection before we close. The two features we've covered in these lessons
57
are partners, not rivals. Compaction keeps the active context small on its own by summarizing
58
the older conversation server side. The memory tool preserves exactly the kind of specifics a
59
summary might drop. Put them together and you get long runs that don't blow past the window and
60
don't lose the essentials either. The clean mental model is a division of labor. Let compaction handle
61
the bulk of the history automatically, while the memory tool guards the handful of facts that
62
absolutely have to survive. Lean context on one side, durable state on the other. Let's summarize
63
what we covered. The memory tool keeps facts across separate conversations. It's client side,
64
so the model asks and your code stores the files. Written facts persist across resets and new
65
sessions. Design with the assume interruption mindset, and it contrasts within context memory,
66
which vanishes when context clears. That wraps up this lesson on memory. I'll see you in the next one.

--- The Tool-Output Bloat Problem ---

1
In this lesson, we're looking at a problem that quietly degrades agent performance.
2
Tool outputs that return far more data than the agent actually needs.
3
We'll cover why this bloat hurts accuracy,
4
how the costs compound across multiple calls,
5
and the architecture mistake that leaves bloat in place
6
even when teams think they've fixed it.
7
Let's get into it.
8
Let's anchor three terms before we go further.
9
Tool output trimming is the practice of reducing what a tool returns
10
to only the fields the calling agent actually needs
11
before that output enters context.
12
You apply this at the tool wrapper, not the display layer.
13
Signal-to-noise ratio describes how much of the context window
14
holds genuinely relevant content versus filler.
15
A high ratio means the model is working with focused, useful information.
16
And context window pressure is what happens when accumulated tokens
17
crowd useful content out of the regions where the model pays closest attention.
18
All three ideas connect.
19
Bloat lowers the ratio and increases the pressure.
20
Picture a weather API that returns 80 fields.
21
Temperature, humidity, barometric pressure, pollen count, UV index,
22
moon phase, historic averages, and dozens more.
23
Your agent needs three of them.
24
But unless you trim, all 80 land in context.
25
Now multiply that across an order management system, a user profile lookup,
26
and a permissions check all in the same workflow.
27
Each call deposits its full payload into the shared context.
28
The agent's attention has to navigate all of it.
29
And most of it is completely irrelevant to what the agent is trying to accomplish.
30
That gap between what's returned and what's needed
31
is exactly the definition of the bloat problem.
32
Here's the clearest way to see the difference.
33
With an untrimmed output, all 80 fields enter the context window.
34
The model reasons over timestamps, metadata, pagination cursors,
35
deprecation flags, and audit stamps.
36
It doesn't need.
37
Signal gets diluted.
38
Even when the model's logic is completely sound,
39
it can land on a wrong answer because it's attending to noise
40
instead of the relevant fields.
41
With a trimmed output, only the three needed fields enter context.
42
The model focuses entirely on what's relevant to the current task.
43
Reasoning quality improves.
44
And the token count for the next request drops immediately.
45
Same data source, very different outcomes for the agent.
46
The bloat problem gets significantly worse when you chain tool calls together,
47
which is exactly what most agentic workflows do.
48
Each call deposits its unreduced payload into the shared context window.
49
After five calls, the context holds five separate layers of noise
50
stacked on top of each other.
51
When the agent reaches step six,
52
it has to reason through all that accumulated clutter from every prior step.
53
The relevant signal at step one is buried under four more noisy payloads
54
by the time you need it again.
55
A single untrimmed call is an annoyance you can work around.
56
A chain of untrimmed calls is a reliability failure
57
that compounds with every additional step you add to the workflow.
58
There are two cost dimensions to bloat
59
that teams often overlook until they're already paying for them.
60
First is token cost.
61
Every extra field in context is a token you'll pay for on the next request.
62
If your tool output carries 500 irrelevant tokens
63
and you make 100 calls per session,
64
that's 50,000 tokens of pure waste per session.
65
Bloat you accept today becomes a recurring charge on every downstream call.
66
Second is latency.
67
Larger context takes more time to process and generate from.
68
Even when the extra tokens don't change the final answer,
69
they slow down every response.
70
Trimming is one of the few optimizations
71
that improves cost, speed, and accuracy simultaneously.
72
Research on transformer attention has shown
73
that models attend most strongly to content positioned
74
near the start and end of the context window.
75
Content buried in the middle receives measurably weaker attention.
76
This phenomenon is called the lost in the middle effect,
77
and it has direct practical consequences for agent design.
78
When verbose tool outputs push key facts
79
toward the center of a long context, accuracy drops.
80
It's not because the model reasoned badly or has a logic problem.
81
It's because the critical signal ended up in a low attention zone
82
where the model simply doesn't focus as strongly.
83
Trimming keeps your relevant signal in high attention positions
84
where it will actually be weighted appropriately by the model.
85
Let's add three more terms that come up
86
when you actually implement trimming.
87
Reference indirection means storing the full tool response
88
somewhere external, such as a cache or retrieval store,
89
and handing the agent only a compact identifier
90
pointing to that stored payload.
91
If the agent needs more detail, it fetches on demand.
92
Many agents never need to.
93
A trim audit log is a structured record
94
of every field your trim layer discarded on each call.
95
This log becomes essential when a downstream step
96
asks for a field that got dropped.
97
You look at the log rather than guessing.
98
And field-level filtering is the most direct trim approach.
99
You declare a fixed allow list of fields to keep,
100
and everything else gets dropped at the tool wrapper
101
before entering context.
102
One of the most common mistakes I see in production agent architectures
103
is trimming at the display layer instead of the model layer.
104
Teams build a clean dashboard that shows
105
only the three relevant fields to human reviewers.
106
The interface looks polished, and the team
107
feels like they've solved the bloat problem.
108
But the model is still receiving the full 80-field payload
109
in its context window every single call.
110
The display trim hides the noise from humans,
111
but it does nothing for the model.
112
The agent is still reasoning over all that filler.
113
The fix has to happen at the tool wrapper itself
114
before the output ever enters context.
115
Downstream display can be handled separately.
116
Model-side trim has to come first.
117
Verbose tool outputs silently hurt agent performance
118
in ways that are easy to miss until the workflow
119
is already in production.
120
The bloat compounds, the costs accumulate,
121
and accuracy erodes not because the model is wrong,
122
but because the signal is buried.
123
That covers why verbose tool outputs hurt.
124
In the next lesson, we'll work through three trim strategies,
125
filtering, summarizing, and reference indirection.
126
I'll see you there.

--- Trim Strategies — Filter, Summarize, Reference ---

1
Now that we understand why verbose tool outputs hurt,
2
let's look at three concrete strategies for trimming them.
3
We'll cover field-level filtering,
4
model-driven summarization, and reference indirection.
5
We'll also look at the design framework
6
for choosing between them, and the anti-pattern
7
that can make trimming worse than not trimming at all.
8
Let's get into it.
9
The three core trim strategies each
10
address a different scenario.
11
Field-level filtering is a deterministic approach
12
where you declare a fixed allow list of fields
13
and drop everything else at the tool wrapper.
14
It's the cheapest option when the schema is stable.
15
Model-driven summarization routes the raw output
16
through a small model that compresses it
17
into what the main agent actually needs.
18
It's more flexible, but adds one model call.
19
And reference indirection stores
20
the full payload externally,
21
handing the agent only a compact identifier it can use
22
to fetch more detail if and when the task requires it.
23
Each strategy has a distinct cost profile,
24
a distinct latency profile, and a distinct best use case.
25
Understanding all three gives you the full toolkit.
26
Field-level filtering is the most straightforward trim
27
you can apply, and it should be your default
28
when conditions allow.
29
You configure the tool wrapper with an allow list
30
of the specific fields your agent needs for its task.
31
When the tool returns its response,
32
the wrapper runs a deterministic JSON path extraction,
33
and returns only those allow-listed fields.
34
No model is involved, no extra latency is added,
35
and the reduction is guaranteed every time.
36
The key requirement is that the tool schema
37
has to be stable and versioned.
38
If fields move, get renamed,
39
or get nested differently across API versions,
40
a fixed allow list will silently miss them,
41
and your agent will start failing
42
in ways that are hard to trace.
43
For predictable versioned APIs,
44
filtering is the best
45
and most cost-effective starting point.
46
The choice between filtering and summarization
47
comes down almost entirely to schema stability.
48
When the tool's response structure
49
is predictable and versioned, filtering works perfectly.
50
You declare the fields once,
51
the wrapper runs the same extraction every time,
52
and there's nothing to adapt to.
53
But when the schema changes across providers,
54
across API versions, or when the tool produces
55
dynamic output structures that vary by call,
56
a fixed field list breaks down.
57
A field you relied on yesterday may be renamed,
58
moved to a nested object,
59
or combined with another field today.
60
In that case, routing the raw output
61
through a small summarization model
62
gives you the adaptability
63
that a static allow list simply can't provide.
64
The model reads whatever structure arrives
65
and returns what your agent needs.
66
Model-driven summarization adds one lightweight step
67
between the tool call and the main agent.
68
You route the raw payload
69
through a small, inexpensive model,
70
along with a task-scoped prompt
71
that describes what the main agent is trying to accomplish.
72
That small model returns a compact, relevant summary
73
focused on the task at hand.
74
The summary, not the raw payload,
75
enters the main agent's context.
76
Yes, you're adding one model call
77
and a small amount of latency,
78
but the arithmetic usually works out strongly in your favor.
79
If the raw payload is 2,000 tokens
80
and the summary is 150,
81
you've removed 1,850 tokens of context pressure
82
in exchange for one small model call.
83
For schemas that change frequently,
84
that trade is worth it every time.
85
Here's a simple decision framework
86
for choosing between the three strategies.
87
If the schema is stable and versioned,
88
start with field-level filtering.
89
It's free to run, fully deterministic,
90
and requires no model involvement at all.
91
If the schema is dynamic or varies across providers
92
or versions, use model-driven summarization.
93
The small model call adapts to any structure
94
the tool returns and extracts what you need regardless.
95
If the payload is very large
96
and the agent may not need most of it
97
to complete its current task,
98
reach for reference indirection.
99
Store the full response in an external cache,
100
hand the agent a compact identifier,
101
and let it fetch only if the task actually requires it.
102
In practice, many agent tasks complete
103
without that fetch ever happening,
104
which means the payload never touches
105
the context window at all.
106
Reference indirection is the right strategy
107
when payloads are very large
108
and the agent may not need them
109
to complete its current task.
110
Instead of placing the full response
111
into the context window,
112
your trim layer writes it
113
to an external retrieval store or cache
114
and returns only a compact identifier to the agent.
115
The agent proceeds with its reasoning.
116
If it reaches a point where more detail is needed
117
to answer a question or take an action,
118
it issues a targeted fetch call using that identifier
119
and retrieves exactly what it needs.
120
In practice, many agents complete their full workflows
121
without ever issuing that fetch.
122
Reference indirection turns what would be
123
a guaranteed and expensive context cost
124
into a conditional one that only occurs
125
when the agent genuinely requires the detail.
126
Let's name three concepts that are important
127
for managing trim in production.
128
A trim audit log is a structured record
129
of every field your trim layer discards.
130
When a downstream agent step fails
131
because it needed a field that got dropped,
132
the audit log tells you exactly what happened
133
without requiring a recall to the tool.
134
Over trimming is the anti-pattern
135
of dropping fields the agent later needs,
136
which forces an expensive recall to recover them.
137
And the trim layer is the wrapper component
138
that sits between the raw tool response
139
and the agent's context,
140
where all of this reduction work happens.
141
The trim audit log deserves its own dedicated attention
142
because it's the kind of thing that's easy to skip
143
during initial development and genuinely painful to add
144
after a production incident.
145
The rule is simple.
146
Every time your trim layer discards a field,
147
it should write a log entry recording the field name,
148
the call timestamp, and what fields were kept.
149
When a downstream workflow step fails
150
or produces a wrong answer,
151
your first debugging check should be the trim log.
152
Did we drop something the agent needed?
153
You'll often find the issue there immediately.
154
You'll also find that reviewing logs over time
155
reveals whether your Allow List has become too narrow
156
as the workflow evolved.
157
Fields that agents repeatedly attempt to access
158
but that aren't present in context
159
are a clear signal that the Allow List needs updating.
160
The right trim strategy depends on
161
how stable your schema is,
162
how large the payloads are,
163
and how likely the agent is to need the full content.
164
Filter when you can, summarize when you have to,
165
use indirection for the large stuff,
166
and always log what you drop.
167
And that wraps up context preservation.
168
Coming up, we shift focus to escalation,
169
how agents recognize when they need to hand off
170
to a human or another agent.
171
I'll see you there.

--- When to Escalate — Trigger Design ---

1
Welcome back. In this lesson we're looking at escalation trigger design. Agents that try to
2
handle everything on their own eventually produce bad outputs silently or get stuck in retry loops.
3
We'll cover the four main trigger categories, the difference between hard and soft triggers,
4
and how to give an agent a clean way to escalate deliberately. Let's start with three terms you'll
5
need for this topic. An escalation trigger is an explicit condition that causes an agent to hand
6
off control rather than keep executing. The key word is explicit. Vague instructions like
7
escalate when unsure are too ambiguous for a model to act on reliably.
8
A confidence gate is a specific kind of trigger that fires when the agent's own confidence score
9
falls below a threshold you define in the system prompt. An out-of-policy detection fires when the
10
requested action hits a deny list or falls outside an allow list. These three mechanisms cover most
11
of what you'll need to build a trigger set that actually works in production. Every escalation
12
system fails in one of two directions. The first failure mode is never escalating. The agent keeps
13
going, produces output, and the output reaches users even when it's wrong. This is the silent
14
failure. It's the harder one to catch because there's no alert, no flag, just a bad result that
15
