conversation up to that point and distills it into a compact block, then continues from that summary.
18
It doesn't keep your recent turns word-for-word unless you opt-in. There's a client-managed
19
setting, pause after compaction, where your own code reinserts the recent messages verbatim.
20
But that's on you to wire up, not automatic. From your seat, you keep talking to the model
21
exactly as before. Why does this feature exist at all? Because every long agent run collides with
22
the same wall. The window is finite. Old turns pile up, and eventually they crowd out room for
23
new ones. Without some kind of help, a session just stalls once the history overflows the space
24
available. Compaction buys back room so the work can continue past that point. I want you to see
25
the intent clearly. The goal is a longer, effective session with as little loss as possible of what
26
actually mattered. It's not magic, and it isn't free, but it turns a hard stop into a soft one.
27
That alone is worth understanding as a foundation. Now the contrast that matters. Earlier in the
28
course, we taught an application-level rolling summary. That's where your own code watches how
29
long the conversation is getting and, at some threshold, calls the model to summarize and trim
30
the history itself. You own the timing. You own the prompt. You own what gets kept.
31
Server-side compaction moves the heavy lifting into the platform. You still opt in and set the
32
threshold, but once it's enabled, the platform does the summarizing for you at that point.
33
Both solve the same core problem. The real question is who writes the summarize loop?
34
With your rolling summary, you do. With compaction, the platform does, and you trade some control for
35
a lot less to build. That trade shows up as three things worth naming. First, there's less to build.
36
Compaction runs inside the platform, so you skip writing, testing, and tuning your own
37
summarize and trim loop. For a lot of applications, that's a genuine relief. Second, there's less
38
control. Because the platform decides what the summary keeps, you give up fine-grained say over
39
exactly which details survive, so plan for some to be dropped. Third, it isn't free. The summary
40
still costs a step to produce. None of these is a flaw. They're just the shape of the deal.
41
Knowing what you're getting is the foundation's level point here.
42
Here's the catch I most want to land. Any summary is lossy by nature. That's not a
43
bug in compaction. It's simply what summarizing means. A fact that doesn't make it into the
44
summary is gone from the model's context. Exact numbers, identifiers, a snippet of code the model
45
wrote earlier, all of that can quietly drop out when the history is compressed. And once it's gone,
46
the model can't recover it, because the raw turns it came from are no longer there to read.
47
So the lesson is blunt. Never treat the transcript as your only copy of something you actually need.
48
If it matters, it has to live somewhere the summary can't erase.
49
The fix is a habit, not a setting you flip on. As you work, write your durable facts to a file
50
or a database. Then re-read that store when you need them, instead of trusting that an old turn
51
is still sitting in context. Let compaction do what it's good at, which is handling the
52
bulk of the conversation, while your own store holds the handful of things that truly can't be
53
lost. Think of it as a division of labor. The platform keeps the session flowing. You keep the
54
essentials safe outside it. This pairs naturally with a tool built exactly for that job, the memory
55
tool, which is where we're headed next. Before we wrap, three practical signals to file away.
56
First, long sessions are where compaction earns its keep. Extended chats and multi-step agent
57
runs fill the window fastest, so that's where it matters most, and it's the case you'll reach for
58
it. Second, treat the summarized history as a gist, not a ledger. It's a good faith recap,
59
not a perfect record. So anything you truly need should live somewhere else that a summary can't
60
touch. Third, cite the docs. Trigger points and the exact identifiers around this feature
61
evolve over time. When you need current specifics, confirm them in the compaction documentation,
62
rather than memorizing a number that may already have quietly changed underneath you.
63
Let's recap. Compaction is the built-in way to survive a filling context window. By default,
64
it summarizes the whole conversation on the server and continues from that summary. Unlike
65
the rolling summary, your own code would trigger. The catch is that summaries are lossy, so facts
66
can disappear. Keep durable state outside the transcript. That wraps up this lesson.
67
I'll see you in the next one.

--- Per-Field Confidence — Beyond a Single Score ---

1
In this lesson we're looking at per-field confidence scores. When a model
2
extracts structured data, it can emit a separate score for every field it fills
3
in. We'll cover why that matters, how to elicit these scores through schema
4
design, and how routing policy uses them to send only uncertain fields to a human
5
reviewer. Let's dive in. Let's ground the lesson in three concepts before we go
6
further. Field-level confidence is a score the model emits for each
7
individual extracted field, rather than for the whole record. That granularity is
8
what makes targeted routing possible. The routing threshold is the cutoff. Fields
9
that score above it get auto accepted and flow downstream, while fields that
10
fall below it get queued for a human reviewer to check. Calibration is the
11
process of verifying whether those scores actually match the model's real
12
world accuracy on labeled data. These three ideas work together throughout the
13
lesson, and each one builds on the previous. The problem with a single
14
record-level confidence score is that it hides where the model is actually
15
struggling. Imagine a record that scores 0.85 overall. That sounds solid, but what
16
if the date field inside that record is sitting at 0.40? The aggregate number
17
masked the weak spot entirely. A human reviewer who gets the whole record still
18
has to wade through every high confidence field to find the one that
19
actually needed attention. That wasted effort scales badly in a high-volume
20
extraction pipeline. Per-field scores fix this by surfacing exactly where the
21
uncertainty lives, so downstream routing can act precisely on the problem field
22
rather than re-inspecting the entire record. The contrast between record-level
23
and field-level scoring is worth making explicit. With a record-level score, one
24
number covers the full extraction. If that number falls below your threshold,
25
the entire record goes to human review, even when only a single field is
26
actually uncertain. That sends reviewers a lot of work they didn't need to do.
27
Field-level scoring changes the routing unit from the record to the individual
28
value. Each field carries its own score, so routing acts on each value
29
independently. A customer name at 0.95 passes straight through. An invoice date
30
at 0.38 goes to a reviewer. The reviewer touches only the field that needs help,
31
and every other field continues downstream without interruption. Here's
32
the thing to understand before you build any of this. There is no native
33
confidence field inside the model. Claude doesn't hand you a calibrated
34
probability from its internal state. What you get is a verbalized self-report, a
35
number the model generates because you asked it to. So getting per-field
36
confidence comes down to prompt and schema design. You define a structured
37
output where each field has a value key and a confidence key where the model
38
writes its own estimate on a zero-to-one scale. A self-reported score is just the
39
model's opinion of itself, and that opinion can be badly wrong. The number
40
means nothing until you check it against a labeled, held-out set. That calibration
41
is what turns a self-report into something you can route on. Choose which
42
high-stakes fields get a confidence sibling based on downstream risk. Once
43
the model returns scores for each field, your routing logic reads them against a
44
threshold. Fields that score at or above the cutoff are auto-accepted and move
45
downstream without any human involvement. Fields that fall below the cutoff are
46
flagged and queued for a reviewer. In some systems there is also a borderline
47
zone near the threshold where a secondary check applies, or where a
48
tighter threshold kicks in for specific field types. The exact policy depends on
49
the business stakes and the reviewer capacity available, but the mechanism is
50
the same. Compare the score to a threshold and route accordingly. In
51
production you will almost never see a record where every field is high
52
confidence or every field is low confidence. The typical case is mixed. A
53
record might have a customer name at 0.95, a total amount at 0.88, and an
54
invoice date at 0.38. The name and amount flow straight through. The date
55
goes to the review queue. This partial routing is the real value of per field
56
scoring. Reviewers handle only the uncertain portion of the work rather
57
than re-examining entire records that are mostly correct. That keeps throughput
58
high while still catching the fields that actually need attention. Routing
59
policy has two basic modes and the choice between them has real throughput
60
consequences. Per field routing makes an independent decision for each field
61
based on its own score. A reviewer touches only the values that actually
62
need attention. Per record routing sends the entire record to review whenever any
63
field falls below the threshold, regardless of how many fields are
64
actually uncertain. Per field routing is more efficient, but it requires downstream
65
systems that can accept partially reviewed records. Per record routing is
66
simpler to implement and easier to audit, which is why some compliance heavy
67
environments prefer it even at the cost of higher reviewer workload. Understanding
68
both modes helps you choose the right policy for your context. There is a
69
failure mode that trips up a lot of teams when they first deploy per field
70
confidence scoring. They take the model's confidence scores at face value and set
71
thresholds based on those numbers without ever checking whether the scores
72
are accurate. A model might confidently report 0.90 on a field type where it is
73
actually only correct 60% of the time. If routing trusts that 0.90 and auto
74
accepts the field, errors flow straight into production. Calibration, which we
75
will cover in the next lesson, is how you catch this mismatch and make the scores
76
trustworthy before they drive real decisions. Per field confidence scoring
77
shifts the routing unit from the whole record to the individual extracted value.
78
High confidence fields auto accept and only the uncertain ones go to review.
79
But model emitted scores need calibration before you can trust them to
80
drive real routing decisions. That covers per field confidence. I'll see you in the next lesson.

--- Calibrating with a Validation Set ---

1
Welcome back. In this lesson we talk about calibration, verifying that a model's confidence
2
scores actually match its real accuracy. We'll cover what calibration means, how to build a
3
labeled validation set, how to run the calibration check, and how to read a reliability diagram to
4
spot miscalibration. Let's get into it. Three concepts frame everything in this lesson.
5
A validation set is a held out collection of labeled samples used to measure how accurate
6
the model's extractions really are. A reliability diagram is a plot that compares what the model
7
says it is confident about against what it actually gets correct, and the gap between those
8
two things tells you whether the model is trustworthy. The routing threshold is the score
9
cutoff you set based on the calibration result rather than on the raw model output. These three
10
tools work together to turn confidence scores into something you can actually act on. Calibration has
11
a specific meaning worth stating precisely. A calibrated model is one where the score it reports
12
matches its actual accuracy. If the model says 0.90 on a field type, a calibrated model is correct
13
on that field type 90% of the time. That alignment is what you're checking for. An overconfident
14
model says 0.90 but is only correct 60% of the time. It thinks it's doing well, but it's feeding
15
errors into your auto accept pipeline without any flag. An underconfident model says 0.60 but is
16
actually correct 90% of the time. That wastes reviewer capacity on work that didn't need
17
checking. Both miscalibrations are worth fixing, but overconfidence is the more dangerous failure
18
because it silently lets errors through. Building a useful validation set requires three things.
19
First, the samples have to be representative of your actual production documents,
20
not cherry-picked easy cases. If your production data includes handwritten forms, scanned invoices,
21
and digital submissions, the validation set should include all of those. Second, every sample needs a
22
ground truth label for each field you are measuring. A human expert creates those labels by
23
checking the correct value against the source document. Third, the set stays held out. You never
24
use it to train the model or adjust the prompt because that would contaminate the measurement
25
and make the calibration result unreliable. The contrast between an overconfident and a
26
well-calibrated model shows up clearly in how routing behaves, and it's worth walking through
27
both cases. When the model is overconfident, it reports high scores on fields it frequently gets
28
wrong. Routing trusts those scores, auto accepts the fields, and errors flow into production without
29
anyone catching them. The system looks efficient on paper but is silently degrading output quality.
30
When the model is well calibrated, a field scored at 0.85 is correct roughly 85% of the time.
31
The routing threshold you set at 0.90 actually corresponds to 90% accuracy in practice.
32
That alignment is what makes per-field routing genuinely reliable,
33
not just a mechanism for moving work around. Running the calibration check is a four-step
34
process. Start by grouping the validation predictions into buckets based on their confidence
35
scores. A bucket for 0.0 to 0.1, another for 0.1 to 0.2, and so on up to 1.0. Then for each bucket,
36
measure the fraction of predictions in that range that match the ground truth label.
37
That fraction is the empirical accuracy for that confidence range. Next, plot each bucket's
38
midpoint confidence on the x-axis against its empirical accuracy on the y-axis. Draw a diagonal
39
line from the bottom left corner to the top right. The diagonal represents perfect calibration.
40
Finally, find the gaps. Bins above the diagonal indicate overconfidence and bins below show
41
underconfidence. The distance from your plotted points to the diagonal tells you how miscalibrated
42
the model is and where threshold adjustment is needed. The reliability diagram is the primary
43
tool for communicating calibration results to anyone who sets routing policy. The x-axis runs
44
from 0.0 to 1.0 and shows the model's reported confidence. The y-axis runs from 0.0 to 1.0
45
and shows the empirical accuracy measured on the validation set. A perfectly calibrated model
46
traces the diagonal exactly. Confidence of 0.70 maps to accuracy of 0.70. When the plotted line
47
curves above the diagonal, the model is overconfident in that range. When it curves below,
48
the model is underconfident. A diagram that hugs the diagonal across all confidence ranges
49
tells you the scores are trustworthy enough to drive routing decisions directly.
50
Once you have the reliability diagram, adjusting the routing threshold is straightforward. Start
51
with your policy's accuracy requirement. If the business needs at least 95% accuracy on auto
52
accepted fields, find the 0.95 level on the i-axis of the diagram. Trace horizontally to where your
53
calibration curve crosses that accuracy level, then read down to the x-axis to find the corresponding
54
confidence value. Set that confidence value as your routing threshold. Fields scoring at or above
55
it will achieve roughly 95% accuracy based on the calibration data. Fields below it go to review.
56
The threshold now reflects real performance rather than the model's self-assessment.
57
Calibration results don't stay valid indefinitely. When a team upgrades to a newer model version,
58
the confidence distribution shifts because the new model generates different scores for the
59
same inputs. When a prompt is revised, even a subtle change to phrasing or examples can move
60
scores across the threshold boundary. Both situations require rerunning the calibration
61
check on the same validation set. The validation set itself stays fixed, so the comparison is fair.
62
Treating calibration as a recurring operational task, scheduled alongside model updates and
63
prompt changes, keeps the routing thresholds honest over time rather than slowly drifting
64
out of alignment. Calibration is what makes confidence scores actionable. A validation set
65
provides labeled ground truth. The calibration check reveals where the model is over or under
66
confident, and the reliability diagram makes those gaps visible. Because models and prompts change,
67
calibration is a recurring task, not a one-time setup. Coming up, we'll look at how to use those
68
calibrated scores in practice, stratified sampling, and routing low confidence output
69
to human review. I'll see you there.

--- Stratified Sampling — Why Random Misses What Matters ---

1
Hello. This lesson is about stratified sampling for AI output review.
2
We'll look at why random sampling misses what matters, how to stratify by confidence,
3
by category, and by stakes, and how combining all three dimensions maximizes the real errors
4
your reviewers catch per hour. Let's get into it.
5
Before we go further, let's anchor three terms.
6
Stratified sampling is a review selection strategy that deliberately oversamples
7
the outputs most likely to be wrong. Instead of picking items at random,
8
you weight selection toward low confidence outputs, rare categories, and high stakes decisions.
9
Stakes weighting is the second concept. It says the importance of an error matters as
10
much as the model's uncertainty. A confident wrong answer on a high
11
stakes decision is just as dangerous as an uncertain one on a routine task.
12
The third term is reviewer burnout. When reviewers see a flood of correct outputs,
13
they stop reading carefully. Burnout is a system design failure,
14
not a people problem, and stratification is one of the main tools to prevent it.
15
Here's the core problem with random sampling. If your model is 95% accurate, then a random
16
sample of 100 outputs will contain roughly 95 correct ones and only 5 errors. Your reviewers
17
spend most of their time confirming what the model already knows, and the actual failure cases
18
show up infrequently enough that patterns are hard to spot.
19
Now scale that up. In a system processing 10,000 outputs per day, random review at a 1% rate gives
20
you 100 reviews, of which maybe 5 contain real errors. You're burning reviewer time on low value
21
confirmations. The errors you do catch are statistically isolated, so you can't identify
22
the underlying cause. Random sampling gives you coverage on paper while hiding the actual failure
23
distribution. Let's compare the two approaches directly. Random sampling is simple to implement.
24
Every output has the same probability of selection, and no additional infrastructure
25
is needed beyond a counter or a random number check. The downside is that the signal your
26
reviewers receive is diluted by the model's existing accuracy. Stratified sampling requires
27
more setup. You need confidence scores, category labels, and a sampling policy that specifies rates
28
for each stratum. That's real engineering work, but the payoff is significant. Reviewers see a
29
concentrated stream of uncertain, rare, or high stakes outputs. The error density in the queue
30
goes up. Reviewers catch more real problems per hour and can identify patterns across cases.
31
The upfront investment in stratification pays off quickly at any reasonable review volume.
32
The most common starting point for stratification is the confidence score. Most models, whether
33
classifiers or generative systems, produce some measure of output certainty. A classifier gives
34
you a probability distribution. A generative model might produce a log probability or a calibration
35
score from a separate head. Whatever the source, you set a threshold, say 0.70, and any output
36
below that threshold gets sampled at a higher rate. Outputs above the threshold still enter
37
the review pool, but at a much lower rate. The threshold itself is tunable. If reviewers have
38
excess capacity, lower it to pull in more borderline cases. If they're overwhelmed,
39
raise it to focus only on the lowest confidence outputs. Confidence stratification alone will
40
dramatically improve your reviewers error yield compared to a purely random baseline.
41
Stratification actually has three distinct dimensions, and effective systems use all three.
42
The first is confidence. Anything below your threshold goes into a high priority review lane.
43
The second is category balance. I want to spend a moment on this one because it's easy to overlook.
44
If 80% of your model's outputs fall into one category and only 5% fall into a rare but
45
important one, random sampling will reflect that imbalance. Reviewers will see mostly the common
46
category, and the rare one will be underrepresented even if it has a much higher error rate.
47
Per-category quotas fix this. The third dimension is stakes. Some outputs need review regardless of
48
confidence because the consequences of an error are severe. These three dimensions work together,
49
and we'll look at how to combine them next. Category-based stratification is about
50
protecting against class imbalance in the review queue. Imagine a content moderation system with
51
five output categories. Four of them are common, one is rare. If you sample randomly, the rare
52
category might generate only two or three reviews per day. If that rare category has a 20% error
53
rate versus 2% for the common ones, you're dramatically under-investing in the highest risk
54
outputs. The solution is per-category sample quotas. You define a minimum number of reviews
55
per category per day, independent of volume. This guarantees that even low-frequency categories get
56
consistent coverage. It also helps you detect drift. If a category that was previously accurate
57
suddenly starts producing more errors, you'll catch the shift in your review data rather than
58
in production incident reports. Let's talk about how to combine the three stratification dimensions
59
effectively. Confidence cross-category means you're prioritizing items that score low on the
60
model's confidence and come from under-represented categories. These items have two independent
61
signals, suggesting they deserve attention. You don't need both signals to be present to trigger
62
review, but when they overlap, you should prioritize those items highest in the queue.
63
Confidence cross-stakes creates a separate lane. High-stakes outputs go to human review regardless
64
of confidence score. You're not betting on the model being right just because it's confident.
65
Full three-way stratification uses all three simultaneously. You'll need a scoring function
66
that weights each dimension and ranks outputs for review. The specifics depend on your system,
67
but the principle is consistent. Concentrate reviewer attention on outputs where errors have
68
the highest expected cost. Reviewer burnout deserves its own focus because it's a system
69
design failure that looks like a personnel problem. When reviewers process hundreds of
70
outputs per shift and the vast majority are correct, they develop a pattern of approving
71
quickly. That's rational behavior given the signal they're receiving. But it means that when a real
72
error does appear, the reviewer is less likely to catch it. Their cognitive threshold has been
73
recalibrated by the volume of correct outputs. Stratification directly counters this by raising
74
the error density of the review queue. When reviewers know that a meaningful fraction of
75
every batch contains real issues, they stay engaged. The goal isn't to make the job harder.
76
It's to make every review session worth the reviewer's full attention rather than a rote
77
confirmation exercise. Stratified sampling transforms review from a coverage exercise
78
into a targeted quality signal. By concentrating reviewer attention on uncertain, rare, and high
79
stakes outputs, you catch more real errors per hour and build a clearer picture of where the
80
model actually struggles. And that covers stratified sampling. In the next lesson,
81
we'll move from sampling theory to the practical review queue, how the routing actually works,
82
what reviewers see, and how corrections feed back into calibration. I'll see you there.

--- Routing Low-Confidence Output Through a Review Queue ---

1
Welcome back. This lesson covers the review queue as an operational system. We'll look at how low
2
confidence outputs get routed in, what the reviewer interface should show, how the approve, edit, and
3
reject actions feed back into model improvement, and how to handle latency constraints and bypass
4
scenarios. Let's get into it. Three terms frame this lesson. The review queue is a dedicated work
5
surface, separate from your production pipeline, where low confidence outputs land for human
6
inspection. It's not just a list of items. It's a system with priority ordering, context presentation,
7
and action capture. The reviewer action set is what people do once they see an item. There are
8
exactly three options. Approve the output as correct, reject it entirely, or edit it to produce the
9
correct answer. That constraint is intentional. Ambiguous action sets slow reviewers down and
10
produce inconsistent records. The bypass policy is the rule that lets certain outputs skip the
11
queue. It exists because not every situation can wait for a reviewer, but bypass should be logged
12
and audited just like a normal review action. Let's look at the queue architecture from the top.
13
The queue sits between the model and whatever uses its output, whether that's an end user,
14
a downstream system, or an automated process. When the model produces an output, a routing layer
15
checks the confidence score against your stratification policy. If the output falls
16
below threshold or hits a high stakes classification, it goes to the queue instead of being released.
17
High confidence, low stakes outputs continue downstream without interruption. The queue holds
18
flagged items in a prioritized list with the lowest confidence and highest stakes outputs
19
surfacing first. Items stay in the queue until a reviewer acts on them. The two operational
20
metrics you track from day one are queue depth, how many items are waiting, and queue latency,
21
how long items wait before review. The reviewer interface is where the system either succeeds
22
or fails in practice. If reviewers have to hunt for context, they'll make faster decisions with
23
less information, and the quality of those decisions will suffer. A well-designed interface
24
puts everything on one screen. The original input that generated the model's output appears on the
25
left or top. The model's output and its confidence score appear alongside it. If a secondary system
26
or rule engine has generated a suggested correction, that shows up too. The reviewer reads the input,
27
evaluates the output against it, considers the suggestion, and makes a decision, all without
28
navigating away. That single screen constraint keeps reviews fast and keeps decision quality
29
high. Anything that forces a tab switch or a search adds friction and degrades throughput.
30
The three reviewer actions each have a distinct meaning in the system's record.
31
Approve means the reviewer has confirmed the output is correct and it can be released downstream.
32
The approval is logged with a timestamp and reviewer identity. Edit means the output was
33
wrong or incomplete and the reviewer has provided the correct version. The edited output gets
34
released and the original plus correction pair goes into a data set for future training or
35
calibration. Reject means the output is wrong and no corrected version should be released.
36
Maybe the input itself was ambiguous or the correct answer requires information the reviewer
37
doesn't have. Reject discards the item from the release path and flags the input type for further
38
analysis. All three actions close the loop differently but all three generate a logged record.
39
Four design principles separate a functional review queue from one that reviewers game or ignore.
40
The first is a single action surface. Reviewers should be able to approve,
41
edit, or reject without opening another tool, searching a knowledge base, or switching context.
42
Every additional step adds latency and introduces inconsistency. The second is context first. Input,
43
output, and confidence score all need to be visible before the reviewer takes any action.
44
Don't bury the confidence score in a metadata panel. Put it where reviewers see it immediately.
45
The third is queue priority. High stakes and low confidence items should surface at the top of the
46
reviewers list automatically. Reviewers should never have to sort their own queue. The fourth
47
is the audit trail which we'll cover in detail when we get to auditability. The most underused
48
feature of a review queue is the feedback loop it creates for model improvement. Every reviewer
49
action is a labeled data point. When a reviewer approves an output you've confirmed the model's
50
behavior on that specific input type at that confidence level. That's a positive training
51
signal. When a reviewer edits an output you've captured the correct input output pair. Edit
52
corrections are high value training data because they come from real production inputs, not from
53
curated data sets, and they're labeled by a domain expert who examined the specific failure.
54
When a reviewer rejects an output you've identified an input class the model handles poorly.
55
Aggregated rejects point directly at the areas where the model needs additional training or
56
where the input pre-processing needs rethinking. The queue pays for itself through the data it
57
generates. Adding a review step creates latency and you need to decide how to handle that before
58
the system goes to production. Synchronous review means the downstream consumer waits for a reviewer
59
decision before receiving the output. This is appropriate when the downstream process can
60
tolerate variable response times but it creates a hard dependency on reviewer availability.
61
If no reviewer is active the pipeline stalls. Asynchronous review returns a placeholder to
62
the downstream consumer immediately and fulfills the actual output once a review decision is made.
63
This keeps the pipeline moving but requires the downstream system to handle deferred fulfillment.
64
Some use cases work well with async, others don't. A medical documentation system that needs the
65
correct output before a clinician signs off can't use a placeholder. Design the latency model
66
explicitly and early before downstream integrations are built around assumptions.
67
The bypass policy handles the cases where waiting for a reviewer isn't possible. There are three
68
main scenarios where bypass is appropriate. Emergency override applies when a time-critical
69
situation means that review delay causes more harm than the risk of an unreviewed output.
70
This should be rare and tightly defined. A high confidence floor bypass allows outputs above a
71
validated confidence ceiling to skip the queue automatically. The threshold has to be validated
72
empirically, not set arbitrarily, and every bypass should still be logged. The third scenario is
73
reviewer unavailability. If the queue has no active reviewers and the business needs continuity,
74
a fallback rule can release outputs with a warning flag. In all three cases the bypass action gets
75
logged with the same completeness as a normal review decision. Bypass isn't an escape from
76
accountability. A review queue is the infrastructure that makes human oversight scalable, auditable,
77
and useful for model improvement. Routing, reviewer interface, action capture, and bypass policy
78
all have to work together for the system to hold up under production load. And that wraps up human
79
review routing. And coming up we'll talk about provenance tracking,
80
where every claim in a synthesized answer maps back to its source. I'll see you there.

--- Claim-to-Source Mappings — Why Every Sentence Needs a Source ---

1
In this lesson we're going to talk about provenance tracking.
2
When an AI model synthesizes information from multiple sources, the output reads confidently.
3
But can you trace any single sentence back to where it came from? We'll cover claim-to-source
4
mapping, inline citations, and the verification workflows that make synthesis actually auditable.
5
Let's establish three terms before we go further. Provenance tracking is the practice of attaching
6
a verifiable source pointer to every claim in synthesized output. It's not enough for a model
7
to be fluent. Each sentence needs a traceable origin. Claim-to-source mapping is the data
8
structure that makes that possible. It links each output claim to the specific source documents it
9
came from. And inline citations are how you expose that mapping in the readable output.
10
An inline citation sits next to the specific claim it supports. That's a fundamentally different
11
approach from a bibliography at the end of a paragraph, which tells you what sources exist,
12
but not which sentence came from which source. These three ideas work together.
13
Here's the core problem. A large language model is excellent at producing fluent,
14
confident-sounding prose. When it synthesizes 10 source documents into a single report,
15
the output reads like it was written by someone who read everything and understands it.
16
But the sources themselves are invisible. As a reviewer, you have no direct way to know which
17
sentence came from which document. That means you can't efficiently check whether a claim is accurate.
18
Errors compound across a long document because each incorrect claim becomes a building block
19
for the next. And when a regulator or an auditor asks you to show them where a conclusion came from,
20
you are stuck. Provenance tracking solves this by making sources visible at the claim level.
21
The choice between inline citations and end-of-paragraph lists isn't just cosmetic.
22
It determines how granular your accountability is. Inline citations sit next to each claim.
23
A reviewer can point to any sentence in the output, follow the source pointer,
24
and verify that the cited document actually supports that sentence. That's per-claim
25
accountability. End-of-paragraph citation lists tell you which sources informed a paragraph,
26
but they don't tell you which specific sentence came from which source.
27
That's paragraph-level accountability at best. For high-stakes synthesis like regulatory filings,
28
clinical summaries, or legal analysis, you need the inline approach. End-of-paragraph lists are
29
acceptable only when the cost of a wrong claim is low and review time is limited.
30
Let's get concrete about what a claim-to-source mapping schema actually looks like.
31
At minimum, each record in the schema holds three things. First, the claim text itself.
32
That's the exact sentence or assertion from the output. Second, a list of source identifiers.
33
Those are the document IDs, URLs, or chunk references for every source that supports
34
that claim. Third, an optional confidence score for each source. You don't need to display all
35
of this in the readable output. The schema lives in the background. But because each claim is now
36
a first-class data object, you can query it, audit it, and update it independently of the
37
surrounding text. That's the architectural shift provenance tracking requires.
38
There are four concrete reasons organizations should invest in provenance tracking. The first
39
is trust. When stakeholders can verify a sample of claims, they are more willing to act on the
40
synthesis. They don't need to verify everything, just enough to build confidence. The second is
41
auditability. A regulator can request a trace of any fact to its origin without re-running the
42
model. The third is compliance. GDPR transparency and access obligations in Articles 13 through 15
43
and the EU AI Act increasingly push organizations to demonstrate where automated conclusions came
44
from, not just what they concluded. The fourth is error correction. When a claim is flagged as wrong,
45
you follow its source pointer directly to the problem. You don't need to re-synthesize the
46
whole document to fix one sentence. A verification workflow puts the provenance schema to work.
47
The core idea is sampling. You don't verify every claim. Instead, you select a representative
48
sample weighted toward higher-risk claims, and you confirm each one against its source pointer.
49
The process looks like this. Pick a claim from the output, follow the source identifiers to the
50
cited documents, read the relevant passage, and judge whether the synthesized claim accurately
51
reflects what the source says. There's no magic percentage. The right sample size depends on how
52
common errors are, how much error you can tolerate, and the confidence level you need. A risk-weighted
53
sample of claims can catch systematic problems, including fabricated citations and distorted
54
paraphrases, without requiring a human to read every source document in full. It's the practical
55
bridge between provenance architecture and real accountability. I want to define three more terms
56
that come up constantly in provenance discussions. A source identifier is a unique, stable reference
57
to a specific document, chunk, or passage. Stable means it doesn't break when the document is updated
58
or moved. If your identifiers are unstable, your provenance trail is unreliable. A fabricated
59
citation is when the model invents a source reference that either doesn't match the actual
60
document content or doesn't exist at all. This is one of the most dangerous failure modes in synthesis,
61
and a sampling audit is the structured review process I described in the last slide. It checks
62
a subset of claims against their source pointers to validate accuracy at scale. Together, these
63
three concepts define the failure modes and the remedy. Fabricated citations deserve extra
64
attention because they're particularly hard to detect without a verification workflow. Here's
65
what happens. The model produces a fluent claim and attaches a real-looking source reference.
66
The document title looks plausible, the authors sound credible, the date is recent. But when a
67
reviewer follows the pointer, one of two things is true. Either the document doesn't support the
68
claim that was made, or the document doesn't exist at all. Reviewers who trust the citation
69
format without verifying it will accept the claim as sourced. This is a systematic risk in any
70
synthesis pipeline that doesn't enforce source verification. The fix is simple but requires
71
discipline. Every citation in the schema should be verified against the actual source before the
72
output is finalized. Provenance tracking turns synthesis from a black box into an auditable
73
process. When every claim carries a source pointer, reviewers can verify, regulators can audit,
74
and errors can be corrected one claim at a time. That covers claim to source mapping.
75
I'll see you in the next one.

--- Conflict Annotation — Surfacing Disagreement in Synthesis ---

1
In this lesson, we're looking at what happens when your source documents disagree.
2
A well-designed synthesis pipeline doesn't just pick one source and move on.
3
It detects the conflict, annotates it, and applies an explicit resolution strategy.
4
We'll cover conflict detection, annotation patterns, resolution rules,
5
and how to log disagreements even after they're resolved.
6
Let's jump into it.
7
Let's define the three central concepts for this lesson.
8
Conflict annotation is the practice of surfacing disagreement in the output rather than hiding it.
9
When two sources disagree, the annotated synthesis tells the reader that a conflict exists.
10
It doesn't pretend one source was the only one that spoke.
11
A resolution strategy is the explicit rule you use to decide between conflicting sources.
12
Prefer newer, prefer authoritative, and escalate to human are the three main options,
13
and we'll walk through each.
14
The averaging anti-pattern is the failure mode.
15
It's what happens when a system silently blends conflicting sources or picks one without logging the choice.
16
The reader gets a confident-sounding claim with no indication that another source said something different.
17
That's the problem we're solving.
18
The averaging anti-pattern is subtle because the output still looks good.
19
The prose is fluent, the claim sounds certain,
20
and there's nothing in the text to suggest that another source disagreed.
21
But here's what actually happened.
22
The model encountered two incompatible claims.
23
Instead of flagging the conflict, it chose one, discarded the other, and presented its choice as settled fact.
24
The reader has no way to know a choice was made.
25
They can't evaluate whether the right source was selected.
26
And the discarded source may have been more accurate, more recent, or more authoritative.
27
Downstream decisions get made on a foundation of false certainty.
28
This is why conflict annotation isn't optional in high-stakes synthesis.
29
The cost of hidden disagreement is higher than the cost of surfacing it.
30
Conflict detection is the upstream step that makes annotation possible.
31
Before your pipeline synthesizes anything, it needs a mechanism to identify when two sources make
32
incompatible claims about the same fact.
33
The practical approach combines two techniques.
34
First, semantic similarity.
35
You find passages from different sources that are discussing the same topic.
36
High similarity means they're making claims about the same thing.
37
Second, entailment checking.
38
You test whether one passage contradicts the other, supports it, or is simply unrelated.
39
When two passages are semantically similar but entailment scoring says they contradict each other,
40
you have a detected conflict.
41
The key insight is that detection should happen before synthesis, not after.
42
If you wait until the output is already written, you're auditing a problem that's already been hidden.
43
The comparison between detected and undetected conflicts comes down to what the reader is allowed to know.
44
When a conflict is detected and annotated, the output tells the reader something like,
45
Source A reports that the policy was updated in 2022.
46
Source B shows the policy was last revised in 2019.
47
This difference hasn't been resolved.
48
The reader now knows uncertainty exists.
49
They can investigate, escalate, or accept the ambiguity with full knowledge.
50
When a conflict goes undetected or unannounced, the reader sees a confident assertion with no
51
indication that another source said something different.
52
One of those two reading experiences produces informed decisions.
53
The other produces confident mistakes.
54
Conflict annotation is the difference between an honest synthesis and a misleading one.
55
Once a conflict is detected, your pipeline needs a rule for resolving it.
56
There are three main strategies.
57
Prefer Newer says that when two sources conflict, the more recent one wins.
58
This works well for domains where facts evolve over time.
59
Regulatory requirements, software versions, and pricing data all fit this pattern.
60
Prefer Authoritative says that designated high-trust sources outrank secondary ones.
61
You need to define an authority hierarchy for your source corpus in advance.
62
Authoritative sources might be official standards, bodies, regulatory agencies, or primary research.
63
Escalate to Human is the third option.
64
When neither Prefer Newer nor Prefer Authoritative can cleanly resolve a conflict, you route the
65
decision to a human reviewer.
66
This is expensive but appropriate when the stakes are high enough that an automated rule could get it wrong.
67
Here's a rule that's easy to overlook.
68
When you resolve a conflict, you should log it even though the conflict is now closed.
69
The resolution trail needs to be preserved for future reference.
70
Your conflict log should record the conflicting claims and their source identifiers,
71
the resolution strategy that was applied, and which claim was selected alongside the one that was discarded.
72
Why does this matter after the fact?
73
Because future reviewers need to know that a choice was made.
74
If the conflict log doesn't exist, a reviewer reading the final output three months later will see a
75
confident claim and assume there was consensus.
76
With the log, they see that two sources disagreed, a rule was applied, and a specific choice was recorded.
77
That's the difference between an auditable system and an opaque one.
78
Let's add three supporting concepts to the vocabulary.
79
A conflict log is the persistent record of every detected disagreement in a synthesis run.
80
It stores the competing claims, the sources they came from, the resolution strategy applied, and the outcome.
81
It's not just a debugging tool.
82
It's part of the audit trail.
83
An entailment check is the semantic test used during conflict detection.
84
It asks whether one passage of text contradicts, supports, or is neutral toward another passage.
85
Entailment models are available as standalone components and can be integrated into preprocessing pipelines.
86
Source authority ranking is the predefined hierarchy that powers the prefer authoritative strategy.
87
Without a defined ranking, prefer authoritative has nothing to evaluate.
88
Building that ranking before you need it is part of responsible synthesis architecture.
89
Conflict annotation is only useful if readers actually engage with it.
90
That means the presentation has to be calibrated to the stakes and the audience.
91
For high stakes claims, like legal conclusions or clinical findings, surface the conflict in line.
92
Put it right next to the claim it affects.
93
For medium risk conflicts, use a footnote or an expandable section.
94
The main body stays readable, but the detail is available one click away.
95
For full document review, generate a conflict summary at the end.
96
This gives senior reviewers a consolidated list of all detected disagreements without forcing them
97
to read the entire document.
98
The goal across all of these approaches is the same.
99
Readers who need to know about a conflict should be able to find it without digging,
100
and readers who don't need it shouldn't be buried in noise.
101
The core principle of conflict annotation is that synthesis which hides disagreement is making a
102
choice the reader can never see or challenge.
103
Detection finds the conflicts.
104
Annotation surfaces them clearly in the output.
105
Resolution strategies provide explicit, repeatable rules for choosing.
106
And conflict logs preserve the audit trail even after a decision is made.
107
Synthesis without prominence is opinion, not analysis.
